Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa

Homage to the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Enlightened One.

Saṃyuttanikāye

The Connected Discourses

Khandhavagga-aṭṭhakathā

Commentary on the Division of the Aggregates

1. Khandhasaṃyuttaṃ

1. Connected Discourses on the Aggregates

1. Nakulapituvaggo

1. The Chapter on Nakula’s Father

1. Nakulapitusuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Sutta on Nakula’s Father

1. Khandhiyavaggassa [V 2.0219][P 2.0249][M 2.0229] paṭhame bhaggesūti evaṃnāmake janapade. Susumāragireti susumāragiranagare. Tasmiṃ kira māpiyamāne susumāro saddamakāsi, tenassa “susumāragira”ntveva nāmaṃ akaṃsu. Bhesakaḷāvaneti bhesakaḷāya nāma yakkhiniyā adhivutthattā evaṃladdhanāme vane. Tadeva migagaṇassa [V 2.0220] abhayatthāya dinnattā migadāyoti vuccati. Bhagavā tasmiṃ janapade taṃ nagaraṃ nissāya tasmiṃ vanasaṇḍe viharati. Nakulapitāti nakulassa nāma dārakassa pitā.

1. In the first [sutta] of the Division on the Aggregates: ‘In Bhaggā’ means in a country so named. ‘At Susumāragira’ means in the city of Susumāragira. It is said that while that [city] was being laid out, a crocodile made a sound, hence they gave it the name “Susumāragira.” ‘In the Bhesakaḷā Wood’ means in a wood that received its name because it was inhabited by a yakkhaṇī named Bhesakaḷā. That very place, because it was given for the safety of herds of deer, is called the Deer Park. The Blessed One, while dwelling in that country and depending on that city [for alms], was staying in that grove. ‘Nakula’s father’ means the father of a boy named Nakula.

Jiṇṇoti jarājiṇṇo. Vuḍḍhoti vayovuḍḍho. Mahallakoti jātimahallako. Addhagatoti tiyaddhagato. Vayoanuppattoti tesu tīsu addhesu pacchimavayaṃ anuppatto. Āturakāyoti gilānakāyo. Idañhi sarīraṃ suvaṇṇavaṇṇampi niccapaggharaṇaṭṭhena āturaṃyeva nāma[M 2.0230]. Visesena panassa jarāturatā byādhāturatā maraṇāturatāti tisso āturatā honti. Tāsu kiñcāpi eso mahallakattā jarāturova, abhiṇharogatāya panassa byādhāturatā idha adhippetā. Abhikkhaṇātaṅkoti abhiṇharogo nirantararogo. Aniccadassāvīti tāya āturatāya icchiticchitakkhaṇe āgantuṃ asakkonto kadācideva daṭṭhuṃ labhāmi, na sabbakālanti attho. Manobhāvanīyānanti manavaḍḍhakānaṃ[P 2.0250]. Yesu hi diṭṭhesu kusalavasena cittaṃ vaḍḍhati, te sāriputtamoggallānādayo mahātherā manobhāvanīyā nāma. Anusāsatūti punappunaṃ sāsatu. Purimañhi vacanaṃ ovādo nāma, aparāparaṃ anusāsanī nāma. Otiṇṇe vā vatthusmiṃ vacanaṃ ovādo nāma, anotiṇṇe tantivasena vā paveṇivasena vā vuttaṃ anusāsanī nāma. Apica ovādoti vā anusāsanīti vā atthato ekameva, byañjanamattameva nānaṃ.

‘Aged’ means worn out by old age. ‘Elderly’ means advanced in age. ‘Senior’ means senior by birth. ‘Gone on in the path’ means having gone through the three stages of life. ‘Having reached [old] age’ means having reached the final age among those three stages. ‘With an afflicted body’ means with a sickly body. For this body, even if it has a golden hue, is indeed called ‘afflicted’ because of its nature of constantly oozing. In particular, one has three afflictions: the affliction of old age, the affliction of sickness, and the affliction of death. Among these, although he is afflicted by old age due to being senior, here it is the affliction of sickness, due to frequent illness, that is intended. ‘Frequently ailing’ means having frequent illness, continuous illness. ‘An infrequent seer’ means: due to that affliction, being unable to come at any desired moment, [he thinks,] “I get to see [the Buddha] only occasionally, not all the time”—this is the meaning. ‘Inspiring to the mind’ means those who cause the mind to grow. For when they are seen, the mind grows by way of the wholesome; such great elders as Sāriputta and Moggallāna are called ‘inspiring to the mind.’ ‘May he instruct’ means may he instruct again and again. For the first utterance is called ‘advice,’ and subsequent utterances are called ‘instruction.’ Or, an utterance when a matter has arisen is called ‘advice’; what is said before a matter has arisen, either according to the texts or according to tradition, is called ‘instruction.’ Furthermore, whether ‘advice’ or ‘instruction,’ they are one in meaning; only the expression is different.

Āturo hāyanti āturo hi ayaṃ, suvaṇṇavaṇṇo piyaṅgusāmopi samāno niccapaggharaṇaṭṭhena āturoyeva. Aṇḍabhūtoti aṇḍaṃ viya bhūto dubbalo. Yathā kukkuṭaṇḍaṃ vā mayūraṇḍaṃ vā geṇḍukaṃ viya gahetvā khipantena vā paharantena vā na sakkā kīḷituṃ, tāvadeva bhijjati, evamayampi kāyo kaṇṭakepi khāṇukepi pakkhalitassa bhijjatīti aṇḍaṃ viya bhūtoti aṇḍabhūto. Pariyonaddhoti sukhumena chavimattena pariyonaddho. Aṇḍañhi sāratacena pariyonaddhaṃ, tena ḍaṃsamakasādayo nilīyitvāpi chaviṃ chinditvā yūsaṃ paggharāpetuṃ na sakkonti. Imasmiṃ pana chaviṃ chinditvā yaṃ icchanti, taṃ karonti. Evaṃ sukhumāya chaviyā pariyonaddho. Kimaññatra bālyāti bālabhāvato aññaṃ kimatthi? Bāloyeva ayanti attho. Tasmāti yasmā ayaṃ kāyo evarūpo, tasmā.

‘The afflicted body’ is to be parsed as ‘for this body is afflicted.’ Even though it may have a golden hue or the color of a priyangu flower, it is indeed afflicted due to its nature of constantly oozing. ‘Become like an egg’ means having become like an egg, it is fragile. Just as a chicken egg or a peacock egg, when taken like a ball, cannot be played with by throwing or striking it, for it breaks right away, so too this body breaks when one stumbles on a thorn or a stump; thus, because it has become like an egg, it is called ‘egg-like.’ ‘Covered over’ means covered over by a mere thin skin. For an egg is covered over by a hard shell, so that gadflies, mosquitoes, and so forth, even after alighting on it, cannot pierce the shell and cause the fluid to ooze out. But in this [body], having pierced its skin, they do what they wish. Thus, it is covered over by a thin skin. ‘What else but foolishness?’ means what is there other than the state of being a fool? The meaning is: this person is just a fool. ‘Therefore’ means: because this body is of such a nature, therefore...

Tenupasaṅkamīti rañño cakkavattissa upaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā anantaraṃ pariṇāyakaratanassa upaṭṭhānaṃ gacchanto rājapuriso viya, saddhammacakkavattissa bhagavato upaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā, anantaraṃ dhammasenāpatissa [V 2.0221] apacitiṃ kātukāmo yenāyasmā sāriputto, tenupasaṅkami. Vippasannānīti suṭṭhu pasannāni. Indriyānīti manacchaṭṭhāni indriyāni. Parisuddhoti niddoso. Pariyodātoti [M 2.0231] tasseva vevacanaṃ. Nirupakkilesatāyeva hi esa pariyodātoti vutto, na setabhāvena. Etassa ca pariyodātataṃ disvāva [P 2.0251] indriyānaṃ vippasannataṃ aññāsi. Nayaggāhapaññā kiresā therassa.

‘He approached him’ means: just as a royal servant, having gone to attend upon a wheel-turning monarch, would then go to attend upon the jewel of a chief minister, so too, having gone to attend upon the Blessed One, the wheel-turner of the true Dhamma, he then, wishing to pay respect to the General of the Dhamma, approached the Venerable Sāriputta where he was. ‘Serene’ means exceedingly serene. ‘Faculties’ means the faculties with the mind as the sixth. ‘Purified’ means faultless. ‘Luminous’ is a synonym for that very word. Indeed, it is only because of freedom from defilements that this [complexion] is called ‘luminous,’ not because of whiteness. And it was only upon seeing its luminosity that he knew the serenity of his faculties. This, it is said, is the elder’s wisdom that grasps the method.

Kathañhi no siyāti kena kāraṇena na laddhā bhavissati? Laddhāyevāti attho. Iminā kiṃ dīpeti? Satthuvissāsikabhāvaṃ. Ayaṃ kira satthu diṭṭhakālato paṭṭhāya pitipemaṃ, upāsikā cassa mātipemaṃ paṭilabhati. Ubhopi “mama putto”ti satthāraṃ vadanti. Bhavantaragato hi nesaṃ sineho. Sā kira upāsikā pañca jātisatāni tathāgatassa mātāva, so ca, gahapati, pitāva ahosi. Puna pañca jātisatāni upāsikā mahāmātā, upāsako mahāpitā, tathā cūḷāmātā cūḷapitāti. Evaṃ satthā diyaḍḍhaattabhāvasahassaṃ tesaṃyeva hatthe vaḍḍhito. Teneva te yaṃ neva puttassa, na pitu santike kathetuṃ sakkā, taṃ satthu santike nisinnā kathenti. Imināyeva ca kāraṇena bhagavā “etadaggaṃ, bhikkhave, mama sāvakānaṃ upāsakānaṃ vissāsikānaṃ yadidaṃ nakulapitā gahapati, yadidaṃ nakulamātā gahapatānī”ti (a. ni. 1.257) te etadagge ṭhapesi. Iti so imaṃ vissāsikabhāvaṃ pakāsento kathañhi no siyāti āha. Amatena abhisittoti nassidha aññaṃ kiñci jhānaṃ vā vipassanā vā maggo vā phalaṃ vā “amatābhiseko”ti daṭṭhabbo, madhuradhammadesanāyeva pana “amatābhiseko”ti veditabbo. Dūratopīti tiroraṭṭhāpi tirojanapadāpi.

‘How could it not be so for us?’ means: “For what reason would it not have been obtained? It was indeed obtained.” This is the meaning. What does this indicate? His state of intimacy with the Teacher. It is said that from the time he first saw the Teacher, this man felt a father’s love, and the female lay disciple, his wife, felt a mother’s love. Both would say of the Teacher, “He is my son.” For their affection had carried over from a past existence. It is said that the female lay disciple had been the Tathāgata’s mother for five hundred lifetimes, and he, the householder, had been his father. Then for another five hundred lifetimes, the female lay disciple was his grandmother and the male lay disciple his grandfather, and likewise his aunt and uncle. Thus, for one and a half thousand lifetimes, the Teacher had grown up in their very hands. For that very reason, sitting in the Teacher’s presence, they would speak of things they could not speak of even in the presence of their son or father. And for this very reason, the Blessed One placed them in the foremost position, saying, “Monks, foremost among my lay disciples who are intimate are the householder Nakulapitā and the housewife Nakulamātā” (Aṅguttara Nikāya 1.257). Thus, explaining this state of intimacy, he said, “How could it not be so for us?” ‘Anointed with the deathless’ means: here, nothing else—no jhāna, or insight, or path, or fruit—should be seen as the ‘anointing with the deathless.’ Rather, only the sweet Dhamma teaching should be understood as the ‘anointing with the deathless.’ ‘From afar’ means even from another region, even from another country.

Assutavā puthujjanoti idaṃ vuttatthameva. Ariyānaṃ adassāvītiādīsu ariyāti ārakattā kilesehi, anaye na iriyanato, aye iriyanato, sadevakena ca lokena araṇīyato buddhā ca paccekabuddhā ca buddhasāvakā ca vuccanti. Buddhā eva vā idha ariyā. Yathāha – “sadevake, bhikkhave, loke…pe… tathāgato ariyo”ti vuccatīti (saṃ. ni. 5.1098). Sappurisānanti ettha pana paccekabuddhā tathāgatasāvakā ca sappurisāti veditabbā. Te hi lokuttaraguṇayogena sobhanā purisāti [P 2.0252] sappurisā. Sabbeva vā ete dvedhāpi vuttā. Buddhāpi hi ariyā ca sappurisā ca, paccekabuddhā buddhasāvakāpi. Yathāha –

The phrase 'an uninstructed ordinary person' has the meaning already stated. In the phrases beginning with 'one who does not see the noble ones,' the term 'noble ones' (ariyā) refers to Buddhas, Paccekabuddhas, and the disciples of the Buddha, so called because they are far (ārakattā) from the defilements, because they do not proceed (na iriyanato) in what is wrong, because they proceed (iriyanato) in what is right, and because they are worthy of being approached (araṇīyato) by the world with its gods. Or, in this context, only the Buddhas are 'noble ones.' As it is said: "Monks, in the world with its gods... the Tathāgata is called the 'Noble One'" (SN 5.437). As for 'good persons' (sappurisā), here it should be understood that Paccekabuddhas and the disciples of the Tathāgata are 'good persons.' For they are 'good persons' because they are splendid persons (sobhanā purisā) through their connection with supramundane qualities. Or, all of these may be spoken of in both ways. For Buddhas are both noble ones and good persons, and so too are Paccekabuddhas and the disciples of the Buddha. As it is said:

“Yo [V 2.0222][M 2.0232] ve kataññū katavedi dhīro,

Kalyāṇamitto daḷhabhatti ca hoti;

Dukhitassa sakkacca karoti kiccaṃ,

Tathāvidhaṃ sappurisaṃ vadantī”ti. (jā. 2.17.78);

“One who is grateful, who repays what has been done, is wise, a good friend, and firm in devotion; who diligently does the task for one in distress—such a one they call a good person.”

“Kalyāṇamitto daḷhabhatti ca hotī”ti ettāvatā hi buddhasāvako vutto, kataññutādīhi paccekabuddhabuddhāti. Idāni yo tesaṃ ariyānaṃ adassanasīlo, na ca dassane sādhukārī, so “ariyānaṃ adassāvī”ti veditabbo. So ca cakkhunā adassāvī, ñāṇena adassāvīti duvidho. Tesu ñāṇena adassāvī idha adhippeto. Maṃsacakkhunā hi dibbacakkhunā vā ariyā diṭṭhāpi adiṭṭhāva honti tesaṃ cakkhūnaṃ vaṇṇamattaggahaṇato na ariyabhāvagocarato. Soṇasiṅgālādayopi cakkhunā ariye passanti, na cete ariyānaṃ dassāvino nāma.

By the phrase 'a good friend and firm in devotion,' a disciple of the Buddha is referred to; by the qualities beginning with 'grateful,' Paccekabuddhas and Buddhas are referred to. Now, one who is not in the habit of seeing those noble ones, and who does not properly revere seeing them, is to be understood as 'one who does not see the noble ones.' And this person is twofold: one who does not see with the physical eye, and one who does not see with wisdom. Among these, the one who does not see with wisdom is intended here. For even if the noble ones are seen with the physical eye or the divine eye, they are as if unseen, because those eyes only grasp their mere appearance, their range not being the state of nobility. Even dogs, jackals, and others see the noble ones with their physical eyes, but they are not called 'seers of the noble ones.'

Tatridaṃ vatthu – cittalapabbatavāsino kira khīṇāsavattherassa upaṭṭhāko vuḍḍhapabbajito ekadivasaṃ therena saddhiṃ piṇḍāya caritvā, therassa pattacīvaraṃ gahetvā, piṭṭhito āgacchanto theraṃ pucchi – “ariyā nāma, bhante, kīdisā”ti? Thero āha – “idhekacco mahallako ariyānaṃ pattacīvaraṃ gahetvā vattapaṭivattaṃ katvā sahacarantopi neva ariye jānāti, evaṃ dujjānāvuso, ariyā”ti. Evaṃ vuttepi so neva aññāsi. Tasmā na cakkhunā dassanaṃ dassanaṃ, ñāṇena dassanameva dassanaṃ. Yathāha – “kiṃ te, vakkali, iminā pūtikāyena diṭṭhena? Yo kho, vakkali, dhammaṃ passati, so maṃ passati. Yo maṃ passati, so dhammaṃ passatī”ti (saṃ. ni. 3.87). Tasmā cakkhunā passantopi ñāṇena ariyehi diṭṭhaṃ aniccādilakkhaṇaṃ apassanto, ariyādhigatañca dhammaṃ anadhigacchanto ariyakaradhammānaṃ ariyabhāvassa ca adiṭṭhattā “ariyānaṃ adassāvī”ti veditabbo.

On this, there is the following story: It is said that an elderly ordained one, an attendant of the Arahant Elder who lived on Cittalapabbata, was once walking for alms with the elder. Carrying the elder’s bowl and robe, he followed behind and asked the elder, “Venerable sir, what are the noble ones like?” The elder replied, “Here, a certain old person, while carrying the bowl and robe of the noble ones, performing duties for them, and even accompanying them, still does not know the noble ones. So hard to know, friend, are the noble ones.” Even when this was said, he still did not understand. Therefore, seeing with the physical eye is not seeing; only seeing with wisdom is seeing. As it is said: “What good is it to you, Vakkali, to see this foul body? One who sees the Dhamma, Vakkali, sees me. One who sees me sees the Dhamma” (SN 22.87). Thus, even if one sees with the physical eye but does not see with wisdom the characteristics of impermanence and so on as seen by the noble ones, and does not attain the Dhamma attained by the noble ones, then due to not having seen the dhammas that make one noble and the state of nobility, one should be understood as 'one who does not see the noble ones.'

Ariyadhammassa akovidoti, satipaṭṭhānādibhede ariyadhamme akusalo. Ariyadhamme avinītoti ettha pana –

‘Unskilled in the noble Dhamma’ means one is unskilled in the noble Dhamma, which has divisions such as the establishments of mindfulness. As for ‘untrained in the noble Dhamma’:

“Duvidho vinayo nāma, ekamekettha pañcadhā;

Abhāvato tassa ayaṃ, avinītoti vuccati”.

“Discipline is said to be twofold, and each of these is fivefold. Due to the absence of that, this one is called ‘untrained’.”

Ayañhi [V 2.0223][P 2.0253][M 2.0233] saṃvaravinayo pahānavinayoti duvidho vinayo. Ettha ca duvidhepi vinaye ekameko vinayo pañcadhā bhijjati. Saṃvaravinayopi hi sīlasaṃvaro satisaṃvaro ñāṇasaṃvaro khantisaṃvaro vīriyasaṃvaroti pañcavidho. Pahānavinayopi tadaṅgappahānaṃ, vikkhambhanappahānaṃ samucchedappahānaṃ paṭippassaddhippahānaṃ nissaraṇappahānanti pañcavidho.

For this discipline is twofold: the discipline of restraint and the discipline of abandoning. And here, in both kinds of discipline, each one is divided into five. The discipline of restraint is fivefold: restraint by morality, restraint by mindfulness, restraint by knowledge, restraint by patience, and restraint by energy. The discipline of abandoning is also fivefold: abandoning by substitution of opposites, abandoning by suppression, abandoning by cutting off, abandoning by tranquillization, and abandoning by escape.

Tattha “iminā pātimokkhasaṃvarena upeto hoti samupeto”ti (vibha. 511) ayaṃ sīlasaṃvaro. “Rakkhati cakkhundriyaṃ, cakkhundriye saṃvaraṃ āpajjatī”ti ayaṃ (dī. ni. 1.213; ma. ni. 1.295; saṃ. ni. 4.239; a. ni. 3.16) satisaṃvaro.

Therein, the phrase “is endowed and fully endowed with this Pātimokkha restraint” (Vb 244) refers to restraint by morality. The phrase “guards the eye faculty, undertakes restraint in the eye faculty” (DN 1.70; MN 1.180; etc.) refers to restraint by mindfulness.

“Yāni sotāni lokasmiṃ, (ajitāti bhagavā)

Sati tesaṃ nivāraṇaṃ;

Sotānaṃ saṃvaraṃ brūmi,

Paññāyete pidhīyare”ti. (su. ni. 1041; cūḷani. ajitamāṇavapucchāniddesa.4) –

“Whatever currents there are in the world, Ajita,” said the Blessed One, “mindfulness is their prevention. I declare the restraint of the currents; by wisdom they are closed off.” (Snp 5.6)

Ayaṃ ñāṇasaṃvaro. “Khamo hoti sītassa uṇhassā”ti (ma. ni. 1.24; a. ni. 4.114; 6.58) ayaṃ khantisaṃvaro. “Uppannaṃ kāmavitakkaṃ nādhivāsetī”ti (ma. ni. 1.26; a. ni. 4.114; 6.58) ayaṃ vīriyasaṃvaro. Sabbopi cāyaṃ saṃvaro yathāsakaṃ saṃvaritabbānaṃ vinetabbānañca kāyaduccaritādīnaṃ saṃvaraṇato “saṃvaro” vinayanato “vinayo”ti vuccati. Evaṃ tāva saṃvaravinayo pañcadhā bhijjatīti veditabbo.

This is restraint by knowledge. The phrase “is patient with cold and heat” (MN 1.10; AN 4.114; etc.) refers to restraint by patience. The phrase “does not tolerate an arisen thought of sensual desire” (MN 1.11; AN 4.114; etc.) refers to restraint by energy. And all this restraint is called ‘restraint’ (saṃvara) because it restrains bodily misconduct and so on, which are to be respectively restrained and disciplined; it is called ‘discipline’ (vinaya) because it disciplines them. Thus, it should be understood that the discipline of restraint is divided fivefold.

Tathā yaṃ nāmarūpaparicchedādīsu vipassanāñāṇesu paṭipakkhabhāvato dīpālokeneva tamassa, tena tena vipassanāñāṇena tassa tassa anatthassa pahānaṃ. Seyyathidaṃ – nāmarūpavavatthānena sakkāyadiṭṭhiyā, paccayapariggahena ahetuvisamahetudiṭṭhīnaṃ, tasseva aparabhāgena kaṅkhāvitaraṇena kathaṃkathībhāvassa, kalāpasammasanena “ahaṃ mamā”ti gāhassa, maggāmaggavavatthānena amagge maggasaññāya, udayadassanena ucchedadiṭṭhiyā, vayadassanena sassatadiṭṭhiyā, bhayadassanena sabhaye abhayasaññāya, ādīnavadassanena assādasaññāya, nibbidānupassanāya abhiratisaññāya[M 2.0234], muccitukamyatāñāṇena amuccitukāmatāya. Upekkhāñāṇena anupekkhāya, anulomena [V 2.0224] dhammaṭṭhitiyaṃ nibbāne ca paṭilomabhāvassa, gotrabhunā saṅkhāranimittagāhassa pahānaṃ, etaṃ tadaṅgappahānaṃ nāma.

Furthermore, there is the abandoning of this or that unwholesome state by means of this or that insight knowledge among the insight knowledges, such as the determination of mind-and-matter, due to its being the opposing factor, just as darkness is dispelled by the light of a lamp. This is as follows: by the determination of mind-and-matter, the abandoning of identity view; by the comprehension of conditions, of the views of causelessness and dissimilar cause; by the overcoming of doubt, which is a later part of that same comprehension, of the state of uncertainty; by the comprehension of groups, of the grasping of 'I' and 'mine'; by the determination of path and not-path, of the perception of the not-path as path; by the knowledge of contemplation of arising, of the annihilation view; by the knowledge of contemplation of dissolution, of the eternity view; by the knowledge of contemplation of fear, of the perception of safety in what is fearful; by the knowledge of contemplation of danger, of the perception of gratification; by the knowledge of contemplation of disenchantment, of the perception of delight; by the knowledge of desire for deliverance, of the state of not desiring deliverance; by the knowledge of equanimity towards formations, of non-equanimity; by conformity knowledge, of the state of opposition to the stability of things and to Nibbāna; by change-of-lineage, of the grasping of the sign of formations. This is called abandoning by substitution of opposites.

Yaṃ [P 2.0254] pana upacārappanābhedena samādhinā pavattibhāvanivāraṇato ghaṭappahāreneva udakapiṭṭhe sevālassa, tesaṃ tesaṃ nīvaraṇādidhammānaṃ pahānaṃ, etaṃ vikkhambhanappahānaṃ nāma. Yaṃ catunnaṃ ariyamaggānaṃ bhāvitattā taṃtaṃmaggavato attano santāne “diṭṭhigatānaṃ pahānāyā”tiādinā nayena (dha. sa. 277; vibha. 628) vuttassa samudayapakkhikassa kilesagaṇassa accantaṃ appavattibhāvena pahānaṃ, idaṃ samucchedappahānaṃ nāma. Yaṃ pana phalakkhaṇe paṭippassaddhattaṃ kilesānaṃ, etaṃ paṭippassaddhippahānaṃ nāma.

Furthermore, the abandoning of those respective things such as the hindrances by means of concentration distinguished as access and absorption, which prevents their actual occurrence—like the removal of moss on the surface of water by striking it with a pot—is called abandoning by suppression. The abandoning of the mass of defilements belonging to the truth of origin, spoken of in the way beginning with 'for the abandoning of views,' which occurs by way of their complete non-arising again in the continuity of one who possesses that respective path due to the four noble paths having been developed, is called abandoning by eradication. Furthermore, the state of the defilements being calmed at the moment of fruition is called abandoning by tranquillization.

Yaṃ sabbasaṅkhatanissaṭattā pahīnasabbasaṅkhataṃ nibbānaṃ, etaṃ nissaraṇappahānaṃ nāma. Sabbampi cetaṃ pahānaṃ yasmā cāgaṭṭhena pahānaṃ, vinayaṭṭhena vinayo, tasmā “pahānavinayo”ti vuccati. Taṃtaṃpahānavato vā tassa tassa vinayassa sambhavatopetaṃ “pahānavinayo”ti vuccati. Evaṃ pahānavinayopi pañcadhā bhijjatīti veditabbo.

That Nibbāna which, due to being the escape from all conditioned things, is where all conditioned things are abandoned, is called abandoning by escape. And all this abandoning is called 'abandonment-discipline' because it is 'abandoning' in the sense of relinquishment and 'discipline' in the sense of removal. Or, because for one who possesses that respective abandoning, that respective discipline arises, it is called 'abandonment-discipline.' Thus, it should be known that abandonment-discipline is also divided fivefold.

Evamayaṃ saṅkhepato duvidho, bhedato ca dasavidho vinayo bhinnasaṃvarattā pahātabbassa ca appahīnattā yasmā etassa assutavato puthujjanassa natthi, tasmā abhāvato tassa ayaṃ “avinīto”ti vuccatīti. Esa nayo sappurisānaṃ adassāvī sappurisadhammassa akovido sappurisadhamme avinītoti etthāpi. Ninnānākaraṇañhi etaṃ atthato. Yathāha –

Thus, this discipline—twofold in brief and tenfold in detail—does not exist for this unlearned worldling, due to having broken restraint and due to not having abandoned what is to be abandoned. Therefore, on account of its absence, this person is called 'undisciplined.' This same method should be applied in the passage: 'one who does not see good people, is not skilled in the Dhamma of good people, and is undisciplined in the Dhamma of good people.' For this is indeed not different in meaning. As it is said:

“Yeva te ariyā, teva te sappurisā. Yeva te sappurisā, teva te ariyā. Yo eva so ariyānaṃ dhammo, so eva so sappurisānaṃ dhammo. Yo eva so sappurisānaṃ dhammo, so eva so ariyānaṃ dhammo. Yeva te ariyavinayā, teva te sappurisavinayā. Yeva te sappurisavinayā[M 2.0235], teva te ariyavinayā. Ariyeti vā sappuriseti vā, ariyadhammeti vā sappurisadhammeti vā, ariyavinayeti vā sappurisavinayeti vā esese eke ekaṭṭhe same samabhāge tajjāte taññevā”ti.

‘Whoever are the noble ones, they are the good people. Whoever are the good people, they are the noble ones. Whatever is the Dhamma of the noble ones, that is the Dhamma of the good people. Whatever is the Dhamma of the good people, that is the Dhamma of the noble ones. Whatever are the disciplines of the noble ones, they are the disciplines of the good people. Whatever are the disciplines of the good people, they are the disciplines of the noble ones. Whether it is “noble one” or “good person,” “Dhamma of the noble ones” or “Dhamma of the good people,” “discipline of the noble ones” or “discipline of the good people”—this is that, it is one, has a single meaning, is the same, has an equal share, is of that same nature, is just that.’

Rūpaṃ attato samanupassatīti idhekacco rūpaṃ attato samanupassati, “yaṃ rūpaṃ, so ahaṃ[V 2.0225], yo ahaṃ, taṃ rūpa”nti rūpañca [P 2.0255] attañca advayaṃ samanupassati. Seyyathāpi nāma telappadīpassa jhāyato yā acci, so vaṇṇo. Yo vaṇṇo, sā accīti acciñca vaṇṇañca advayaṃ samanupassati, evameva idhekacco rūpaṃ attato samanupassati…pe… advayaṃ samanupassatīti evaṃ rūpaṃ “attā”ti diṭṭhipassanāya passati. Rūpavantaṃ vā attānanti arūpaṃ “attā”ti gahetvā chāyāvantaṃ rukkhaṃ viya taṃ rūpavantaṃ samanupassati. Attani vā rūpanti arūpameva “attā”ti gahetvā pupphasmiṃ gandhaṃ viya attani rūpaṃ samanupassati. Rūpasmiṃ vā attānanti arūpameva “attā”ti gahetvā karaṇḍake maṇiṃ viya taṃ attānaṃ rūpasmiṃ samanupassati. Pariyuṭṭhaṭṭhāyīti pariyuṭṭhānākārena abhibhavanākārena ṭhito, “ahaṃ rūpaṃ, mama rūpa”nti evaṃ taṇhādiṭṭhīhi gilitvā pariniṭṭhapetvā gaṇhanako nāma hotīti attho. Tassa taṃ rūpanti tassa taṃ evaṃ gahitaṃ rūpaṃ. Vedanādīsupi eseva nayo.

‘One regards form as self’: Here, a certain person regards form as self, thinking, ‘Whatever is form, that I am; whatever I am, that is form,’ and thus regards form and self as non-dual. Just as, for a burning oil lamp, whatever is the flame, that is the color, and whatever is the color, that is the flame, and one thus regards the flame and the color as non-dual; in the very same way, a certain person here regards form as self and regards it as non-dual. Thus, one sees form as ‘self’ with the seeing that is wrong view. ‘Or self as possessing form’: Taking the formless as ‘self,’ one regards that self as possessing form, like a tree possessing a shadow. ‘Or form in self’: Taking just the formless as ‘self,’ one regards form in the self, like fragrance in a flower. ‘Or self in form’: Taking just the formless as ‘self,’ one regards that self in form, like a jewel in a casket. ‘One who stands obsessed’: This means one stands in the manner of obsession, in the manner of overwhelming, thinking, ‘I am form, my form.’ Thus, having swallowed and brought it to completion with craving and views, one is called a grasper—this is the meaning. ‘That form of theirs’: For that person, that form is thus grasped. This same method applies to feeling and the rest.

Tattha “rūpaṃ attato samanupassatī”ti suddharūpameva attāti kathitaṃ. “Rūpavantaṃ vā attānaṃ, attani vā rūpaṃ, rūpasmiṃ vā attānaṃ, vedanaṃ attato…pe… saññaṃ… saṅkhāre… viññāṇaṃ attato samanupassatī”ti imesu sattasu ṭhānesu arūpaṃ attāti kathitaṃ. “Vedanāvantaṃ vā attānaṃ, attani vā vedanaṃ, vedanāya vā attāna”nti evaṃ catūsu khandhesu tiṇṇaṃ tiṇṇaṃ vasena dvādasasu ṭhānesu rūpārūpamissako attā kathito. Tattha “rūpaṃ attato samanupassati, vedanaṃ… saññaṃ… saṅkhāre… viññāṇaṃ attato samanupassatī”ti imesu pañcasu ṭhānesu ucchedadiṭṭhi kathitā, avasesesu sassatadiṭṭhīti evamettha pannarasa bhavadiṭṭhiyo pañca vibhavadiṭṭhiyo honti, tā sabbāpi maggāvaraṇā, na saggāvaraṇā, paṭhamamaggavajjhāti veditabbā.

Herein, in the passage ‘one regards form as self,’ it is said that mere form is the self. In these seven instances—‘one regards self as possessing form, or form as in the self, or the self as in form,’ and ‘one regards feeling as self… perception… formations… consciousness as self’—it is said that the formless is the self. In the twelve instances across the four aggregates, by way of three for each, such as ‘one regards self as possessing feeling, or feeling as in the self, or the self as in feeling,’ it is said that the self is a mixture of form and the formless. Therein, in these five instances—‘one regards form as self, feeling… perception… formations… consciousness as self’—the annihilation view is spoken of; in the remaining instances, the eternalism view. Thus, herein there are fifteen views of being and five views of non-being. All of them are obstacles to the path, but not obstacles to heaven, and they are to be understood as destroyed by the first path.

Evaṃ [M 2.0236] kho, gahapati, āturakāyo ceva hoti āturacitto cāti kāyo nāma buddhānampi āturoyeva. Cittaṃ pana rāgadosamohānugataṃ āturaṃ nāma, taṃ idha dassitaṃ. No ca āturacittoti idha nikkilesatāya cittassa anāturabhāvo [P 2.0256] dassito. Iti imasmiṃ sutte lokiyamahājano āturakāyo ceva āturacitto cāti dassito, khīṇāsavā āturakāyā anāturacittā, satta sekhā neva āturacittā, na anāturacittāti veditabbā. Bhajamānā pana anāturacittataṃyeva bhajantīti. Paṭhamaṃ.

Thus, householder, in the passage “one has a sick body and a sick mind,” the body, indeed, is sick even for Buddhas. The mind, however, when accompanied by lust, hatred, and delusion, is called sick; that is what is shown here. And in the passage “but not sick in mind,” the mind’s unailing state is shown here by its freedom from defilements. Thus, in this discourse, the great mass of worldlings is shown to have both a sick body and a sick mind; those whose cankers are destroyed have sick bodies but unailing minds; the seven trainees should be understood as being neither sick-minded nor unailing-minded. But when considered, they partake only of the state of an unailing mind. The first.

2. Devadahasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Devadaha Sutta

2. Dutiye [V 2.0226] devadahanti devā vuccanti rājāno, tesaṃ maṅgaladaho, sayaṃjāto vā so dahoti, tasmā “devadaho”ti vutto. Tassa avidūre nigamo devadahantveva napuṃsakaliṅgavasena saṅkhaṃ gato. Pacchābhūmagamikāti pacchābhūmaṃ aparadisāyaṃ niviṭṭhaṃ janapadaṃ gantukāmā. Nivāsanti temāsaṃ vassāvāsaṃ. Apalokitoti āpucchito. Apalokethāti āpucchatha. Kasmā theraṃ āpucchāpeti? Te sabhāre kātukāmatāya. Yo hi ekavihāre vasantopi santikaṃ na gacchati pakkamanto anāpucchā pakkamati, ayaṃ nibbhāro nāma. Yo ekavihāre vasantopi āgantvā passati, pakkamanto āpucchati, ayaṃ sabhāro nāma. Imepi bhikkhū bhagavā “evamime sīlādīhi vaḍḍhissantī”ti sabhāre kātukāmo āpucchāpeti.

2. In the second sutta, regarding ‘Devadaha’: kings are called ‘devas,’ and it was their auspicious lake; or, that lake arose by itself, therefore it was called ‘Devadaha.’ Not far from it, a market town came to be known as ‘Devadaha,’ in the neuter gender. ‘Those who go to the land behind’ means those who wish to go to the country established in the western direction, the land behind. ‘They reside’ refers to the three-month rains-residence. ‘Apalokito’ means ‘one who has been asked for leave.’ ‘Apaloketha’ means ‘you should ask for leave.’ Why does the Blessed One have them ask leave of the Elder? Because of His wish to make them considerate. For a monk who, though living in the same monastery, does not approach his seniors and departs without asking leave, is called ‘inconsiderate.’ A monk who, while living in the same monastery, comes to see his seniors and asks for leave when departing, is called ‘considerate.’ The Blessed One, wishing to make these monks considerate, thinking, ‘In this way, these monks will grow in virtue and other qualities,’ has them ask leave.

Paṇḍitoti dhātukosallādinā catubbidhena paṇḍiccena samannāgato. Anuggāhakoti āmisānuggahena ca dhammānuggahena cāti dvīhipi anuggahehi anuggāhako. Thero kira aññe bhikkhū viya pātova piṇḍāya agantvā sabbabhikkhūsu gatesu sakalaṃ saṅghārāmaṃ anuvicaranto asammaṭṭhaṭṭhānaṃ sammajjati, achaḍḍitaṃ kacavaraṃ chaḍḍeti, saṅghārāme dunnikkhittāni mañcapīṭhadārubhaṇḍamattikābhaṇḍāni paṭisāmeti. Kiṃ kāraṇā? “Mā aññatitthiyā vihāraṃ paviṭṭhā disvā paribhavaṃ akaṃsū”ti. Tato gilānasālaṃ gantvā gilāne assāsetvā “kenattho”ti pucchitvā yena attho hoti, tadatthaṃ [M 2.0237] tesaṃ daharasāmaṇere ādāya bhikkhācāravattena vā sabhāgaṭṭhāne vā bhesajjaṃ pariyesitvā tesaṃ datvā, “gilānupaṭṭhānaṃ nāma buddhapaccekabuddhehi vaṇṇitaṃ, gacchatha sappurisā appamattā hothā”ti te pesetvā sayaṃ piṇḍāya caritvā upaṭṭhākakule vā bhattakiccaṃ katvā vihāraṃ gacchati. Idaṃ tāvassa nibaddhavāsaṭṭhāne āciṇṇaṃ.

‘Wise’ means endowed with the fourfold wisdom, such as skill in the elements. ‘Supportive’ means one who supports with two kinds of support: material support and support of the Dhamma. It is said that the Elder, unlike other monks, would not go for alms early in the morning. After all the monks had gone, he would walk around the entire monastery, sweeping places that were not swept, discarding rubbish that had not been discarded, and tidying up improperly placed beds, stools, wooden items, and earthenware in the monastery. For what reason? So that ‘adherents of other sects, upon entering the monastery and seeing it, might not speak disparagingly.’ Then he would go to the infirmary, comfort the sick, and ask, ‘What is needed?’ For whatever was needed, he would take their young novices and search for medicine either by going on alms round or in suitable places. After giving it to them, he would send them off, saying, ‘Attending to the sick has been praised by the Buddhas and Paccekabuddhas. Go, good sirs, be diligent.’ Then he himself would go for alms, take his meal at a supporter’s home, and return to the monastery. This, for a start, was his regular practice in his place of residence.

Bhagavati [P 2.0257] pana cārikaṃ caramāne “ahaṃ aggasāvako”ti upāhanaṃ āruyha chattaṃ gahetvā purato purato na gacchati. Ye pana tattha mahallakā vā ābādhikā vā atidaharā vā, tesaṃ rujjanaṭṭhānāni telena makkhāpetvā pattacīvaraṃ attano daharasāmaṇerehi gāhāpetvā taṃdivasaṃ vā dutiyadivasaṃ vā te gaṇhitvāva gacchati. Ekadivasañhi taññeva āyasmantaṃ ativikāle sampattattā senāsanaṃ alabhitvā, cīvarakuṭiyaṃ nisinnaṃ disvā, satthā punadivase bhikkhusaṅghaṃ sannipātāpetvā, hatthivānaratittiravatthuṃ kathetvā, “yathāvuḍḍhaṃ senāsanaṃ dātabba”nti sikkhāpadaṃ [V 2.0227] paññāpesi. Evaṃ tāvesa āmisānuggahena anuggaṇhāti. Ovadanto panesa satavārampi sahassavārampi tāva ovadati, yāva so puggalo sotāpattiphale patiṭṭhāti, atha naṃ vissajjetvā aññaṃ ovadati. Iminā nayena ovadato cassa ovāde ṭhatvā arahattaṃ pattā gaṇanapathaṃ atikkantā. Evaṃ dhammānuggahena anuggaṇhāti.

Furthermore, when the Blessed One was travelling on a journey, he would not, thinking, ‘I am the chief disciple,’ walk at the very front wearing sandals and holding a sunshade. Instead, for those there who were elderly, sick, or very young, he would have their painful spots anointed with oil, have their bowl and robe carried by his own young novices, and would take them along that day or the next. For one day, having seen that the Venerable One, having arrived very late, could not obtain a lodging and was sitting in the robe hut, the Teacher on the next day assembled the Sangha of monks, told the story of the elephant, the monkey, and the partridge, and laid down the training rule: ‘Lodgings should be given according to seniority.’ Thus, in the first place, he supports with material support. When instructing, however, he instructs a person a hundred times, even a thousand times, until that person is established in the fruit of stream-entry. Then, dismissing them, he instructs another. In this way, of those who followed his instruction, the number who attained Arahantship surpassed any counting. Thus he supports with the support of the Dhamma.

Paccassosunti te bhikkhū “amhākaṃ neva upajjhāyo, na ācariyo na sandiṭṭhasambhatto. Kiṃ tassa santike karissāmā”ti? Tuṇhībhāvaṃ anāpajjitvā “evaṃ, bhante”ti satthu vacanaṃ sampaṭicchiṃsu. Eḷagalāgumbeti gacchamaṇḍapake. So kira eḷagalāgumbo dhuvasalilaṭṭhāne jāto. Athettha catūhi pādehi maṇḍapaṃ katvā tassa upari taṃ gumbaṃ āropesuṃ, so taṃ maṇḍapaṃ chādesi. Athassa heṭṭhā iṭṭhakāhi paricinitvā vālikaṃ okiritvā āsanaṃ paññāpayiṃsu. Sītalaṃ divāṭṭhānaṃ udakavāto vāyati. Thero tasmiṃ nisīdi. Taṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ “eḷagalāgumbe”ti.

`Paccassosuṃ` means: those monks, thinking, 'He is neither our preceptor, nor our teacher, nor a familiar companion. What shall we do in his presence?', did not remain silent but accepted the Teacher's words, saying, 'Very well, venerable sir.' `Eḷagalāgumbe` means 'in a thicket-pavilion'. It is said that this Eḷagalāgumba thicket grew in a place with constant water. There, having built a pavilion with four posts, they placed that thicket on top, and it shaded the pavilion. Underneath it, they arranged bricks, sprinkled sand, and prepared a seat. It was a cool place for the daytime, where a breeze from the water blew. The Elder sat there. With reference to this, it is said, 'in Eḷagalāgumbe'.

Nānāverajjagatanti ekassa rañño rajjato nānāvidhaṃ rajjagataṃ. Virajjanti aññaṃ rajjaṃ. Yathā hi sadesato añño videso, evaṃ nivuttharajjato aññaṃ [M 2.0238] rajjaṃ virajjaṃ nāma, taṃ verajjanti vuttaṃ. Khattiyapaṇḍitāti bimbisārakosalarājādayo paṇḍitarājāno. Brāhmaṇapaṇḍitāti caṅkītārukkhādayo paṇḍitabrāhmaṇā. Gahapatipaṇḍitāti cittasudattādayo paṇḍitagahapatayo. Samaṇapaṇḍitāti sabhiyapilotikādayo paṇḍitaparibbājakā[P 2.0258]. Vīmaṃsakāti atthagavesino. Kiṃvādīti kiṃ attano dassanaṃ vadati, kiṃ laddhikoti attho. Kimakkhāyīti kiṃ sāvakānaṃ ovādānusāsaniṃ ācikkhati? Dhammassa cānudhammanti bhagavatā vuttabyākaraṇassa anubyākaraṇaṃ. Sahadhammikoti sakāraṇo. Vādānuvādoti bhagavatā vuttavādassa anuvādo. “Vādānupāto”tipi pāṭho, satthu vādassa anupāto anupatanaṃ, anugamananti attho. Imināpi vādaṃ anugato vādoyeva dīpito hoti.

`Nānāverajjagataṃ` means having gone to various other realms from the realm of a single king. `Virajjaṃ` is said of another realm. Just as a place other than one's own is called a foreign place (`videsa`), so too a realm other than the one in which one lives is called `virajjaṃ`. That `virajjaṃ` is called `verajjaṃ`. `Khattiyapaṇḍitā` refers to wise kings such as Bimbisāra and the Kosala king. `Brāhmaṇapaṇḍitā` refers to wise brahmins such as Caṅkī and Tārukkha. `Gahapatipaṇḍitā` refers to wise householders such as Citta and Sudatta. `Samaṇapaṇḍitā` refers to wise wanderers such as Sabhiya and Pilotika. `Vīmaṃsakā` means those who seek the meaning. `Kiṃvādī` means: what is his view? What is his doctrine? This is the meaning. `Kimakkhāyī` means: what advice and instruction does he teach his disciples? `Dhammassa cānudhammaṃ` means a secondary explanation of the exposition spoken by the Blessed One. `Sahadhammiko` means with reason. `Vādānuvādo` means a teaching that is in accordance with the teaching spoken by the Blessed One. There is also the reading `Vādānupāto`; the meaning is conformity to the Teacher's teaching, following after it. By this also, a teaching that follows the teaching is indeed explained.

Avigatarāgassātiādīsu taṇhāvaseneva attho veditabbo. Taṇhā hi rajjanato rāgo, chandiyanato chando, piyāyanaṭṭhena pemaṃ, pivitukāmaṭṭhena pipāsā, anudahanaṭṭhena pariḷāhoti vuccati. Akusale cāvuso, dhammetiādi kasmā āraddhaṃ? Pañcasu khandhesu avītarāgassa ādīnavaṃ[V 2.0228], vītarāgassa ca ānisaṃsaṃ dassetuṃ. Tatra avighātoti niddukkho. Anupāyāsoti nirupatāpo. Apariḷāhoti niddāho. Evaṃ sabbattha attho veditabbo. Dutiyaṃ.

In the phrases beginning with 'for one whose lust has not departed,' the meaning should be understood by way of craving. For craving is called `rāga` (lust) because of its nature of attachment, `chando` (desire) because of its nature of wishing, `pemaṃ` (affection) because of its nature of loving, `pipāsā` (thirst) because of its nature of wanting to drink, and `pariḷāho` (burning) because of its nature of burning. Regarding 'Friends, in unwholesome states,' and so forth: for what reason was this commenced? To show the danger for one whose lust concerning the five aggregates has not been removed, and the benefit for one whose lust has been removed. Therein, 'without vexation' means without suffering. 'Without tribulation' means without anguish. 'Without burning' means without fever. Thus, the meaning should be understood in every case. The second.

3. Hāliddikānisuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Hāliddikāni Sutta

3. Tatiye avantīsūti avantidakkhiṇāpathasaṅkhāte avantiraṭṭhe. Kuraraghareti evaṃnāmake nagare. Papāteti ekato papāte. Tassa kira pabbatassa ekaṃ passaṃ chinditvā pātitaṃ viya ahosi. “Pavatte”tipi pāṭho, nānātitthiyānaṃ laddhipavattaṭṭhāneti attho. Iti thero tasmiṃ raṭṭhe taṃ nagaraṃ nissāya tasmiṃ pabbate viharati. Hāliddikānīti evaṃnāmako. Aṭṭhakavaggiye māgaṇḍiyapañheti aṭṭhakavaggikamhi māgaṇḍiyapañho nāma atthi, tasmiṃ pañhe. Rūpadhātūti rūpakkhandho adhippeto. Rūpadhāturāgavinibaddhanti rūpadhātumhi rāgena vinibaddhaṃ[P 2.0259]. Viññāṇanti kammaviññāṇaṃ. Okasārīti gehasārī ālayasārī.

3. In the third, `avantīsu` means in the Avanti country, known as Avantidakkhināpatha. `Kuraraghare` means in a city named Kuraraghara. `Papāte` means on a precipice on one side. It is said that one side of that mountain was as if it had been cut and made to fall. There is also a reading `pavatte`, meaning a place where the views of various sectarians prevailed. Thus, the elder dwells on that mountain, relying on that city in that country. `Hāliddikāni` refers to one so named. `Aṭṭhakavaggiye māgaṇḍiyapañhe` means in the Māgaṇḍiya Question of the Aṭṭhakavagga. `Rūpadhātu` means the form aggregate is intended. `Rūpadhāturāgavinibaddhaṃ` means bound by lust for the form element. `Viññāṇaṃ` means kamma-consciousness. `Okasārī` means one who resorts to a home, one who resorts to an abode.

Kasmā [M 2.0239] panettha “viññāṇadhātu kho, gahapatī”ti na vuttanti? Sammohavighātatthaṃ. “Oko”ti hi atthato paccayo vuccati, purejātañca kammaviññāṇaṃ pacchājātassa kammaviññāṇassapi vipākaviññāṇassapi vipākaviññāṇañca vipākaviññāṇassapi kammaviññāṇassapi paccayo hoti, tasmā “kataraṃ nu kho idha viññāṇa”nti? Sammoho bhaveyya, tassa vighātatthaṃ taṃ agahetvā asambhinnāva desanā katā. Apica ārammaṇavasena catasso abhisaṅkhāraviññāṇaṭṭhitiyo vuttāti tā dassetumpi idha viññāṇaṃ na gahitaṃ.

Why was it not said here, 'Indeed, householder, the consciousness element'? To dispel confusion. For `oko` is, in meaning, called a condition. And pre-nascent kamma-consciousness is a condition for post-nascent kamma-consciousness and for resultant consciousness; and resultant consciousness is a condition for resultant consciousness and for kamma-consciousness. Therefore, confusion would arise, such as, 'Which consciousness is intended here?' To dispel that confusion, the teaching was given without taking up that consciousness, thus it was made unmixed. Furthermore, in order to show the four stations of consciousness for formations that have been spoken of by way of the object, consciousness was not taken up here.

Upayupādānāti taṇhūpayadiṭṭhūpayavasena dve upayā, kāmupādānādīni cattāri upādānāni ca. Cetaso adhiṭṭhānābhinivesānusayāti akusalacittassa adhiṭṭhānabhūtā ceva abhinivesabhūtā ca anusayabhūtā ca. Tathāgatassāti sammāsambuddhassa. Sabbesampi hi khīṇāsavānaṃ ete pahīnāva, satthu pana khīṇāsavabhāvo loke atipākaṭoti uparimakoṭiyā evaṃ vuttaṃ. Viññāṇadhātuyāti idha viññāṇaṃ kasmā gahitaṃ? Kilesappahānadassanatthaṃ. Kilesā hi na kevalaṃ catūsuyeva khandhesu pahīnā pahīyanti, pañcasupi pahīyantiyevāti kilesappahānadassanatthaṃ gahitaṃ. Evaṃ kho, gahapati, anokasārī hotīti evaṃ kammaviññāṇena okaṃ asarantena anokasārī nāma hoti.

`Upayupādānā` refers to two supports—namely, the support of craving and the support of views—and the four clingings, beginning with clinging to sensual pleasures. `Cetaso adhiṭṭhānābhinivesānusayā` means that which are the basis of resolution, the basis of insistence, and the underlying tendencies of an unwholesome mind. `Tathāgatassa` means of the Sammāsambuddha. For all those whose taints are destroyed, these are indeed abandoned. However, the Teacher’s state of having destroyed the taints is supremely evident in the world; thus, it is stated in the highest terms. Regarding `viññāṇadhātuyā`, why is consciousness taken up here? To show the abandoning of defilements. For defilements are not abandoned only in the four aggregates, but are abandoned in all five; thus, it is taken up to show the abandoning of defilements. `Evaṃ kho, gahapati, anokasārī hoti` means: thus, a person, by means of kamma-consciousness that does not resort to a home, is called an `anokasārī`.

Rūpanimittaniketavisāravinibandhāti [V 2.0229] rūpameva kilesānaṃ paccayaṭṭhena nimittaṃ, ārammaṇakiriyasaṅkhātanivāsanaṭṭhānaṭṭhena niketanti rūpanimittaniketaṃ. Visāro ca vinibandho ca visāravinibandhā. Ubhayenapi hi kilesānaṃ patthaṭabhāvo ca vinibandhanabhāvo ca vutto, rūpanimittanikete visāravinibandhāti rūpanimittaniketavisāravinibandhā, tasmā rūpanimittaniketamhi uppannena [P 2.0260] kilesavisārena ceva kilesabandhanena cāti attho. Niketasārīti vuccatīti ārammaṇakaraṇavasena nivāsanaṭṭhānaṃ sārīti vuccati. Pahīnāti te rūpanimittaniketakilesavisāravinibandhā pahīnā.

As for ‘form-sign-dwelling-spreading-and-binding’ (rūpanimittaniketavisāravinibandhā): form itself is a ‘sign’ (nimitta) in the sense of being a condition for the defilements. It is a ‘dwelling’ (niketa) in the sense of being a temporary dwelling place, which is reckoned as the activity of taking an object. Thus, it is a ‘form-sign-dwelling’ (rūpanimittaniketa). ‘Spreading’ (visāra) and ‘binding’ (vinibandha) are ‘spreading-and-binding’ (visāravinibandhā). By both of these terms, the state of spreading of the defilements and their state of binding are indeed spoken of. ‘Spreading-and-binding in the form-sign-dwelling’ (rūpanimittanikete visāravinibandhā) becomes ‘form-sign-dwelling-spreading-and-binding’ (rūpanimittaniketavisāravinibandha). Therefore, the meaning is: by the spreading of defilements and by the binding of defilements that have arisen in the form-sign-dwelling. As for ‘one who resorts to a dwelling’ (niketasārī), it is said: by way of taking an object, one who resorts to a dwelling place is spoken of. As for ‘abandoned’ (pahīnā): those form-sign-dwelling-defilement-spreading-and-bindings are abandoned.

Kasmā panettha pañcakkhandhā “okā”ti vuttā, cha ārammaṇāni “niketa”nti? Chandarāgassa balavadubbalatāya. Samānepi hi etesaṃ ālayaṭṭhena visayabhāve okoti niccanivāsanaṭṭhānagehameva vuccati, niketanti [M 2.0240] “ajja asukaṭṭhāne kīḷissāmā”ti katasaṅketaṭṭhānaṃ nivāsaṭṭhānaṃ uyyānādi. Tattha yathā puttadāradhanadhaññapuṇṇagehe chandarāgo balavā hoti, evaṃ ajjhattikesu khandhesu. Yathā pana uyyānaṭṭhānādīsu tato dubbalataro hoti, evaṃ bāhiresu chasu ārammaṇesūti chandarāgassa balavadubbalatāya evaṃ desanā katāti veditabbo.

Why, here, are the five aggregates called ‘homes’ (okā), and the six sense objects ‘dwellings’ (niketa)? Because of the strength and weakness of desire and lust. For although for these the state of being an object is the same in the sense of being a basis for clinging, ‘home’ (oka) is indeed called a permanent dwelling place, a house, while ‘dwelling’ (niketa) refers to a temporary dwelling place, a place appointed by agreement, such as a park, saying, ‘Today we will play in such-and-such a place.’ Therein, just as desire and lust are strong in a house full of children, wife, wealth, and grain, so too are they strong regarding the internal aggregates. But just as desire and lust are weaker than that in places like parks, so too are they weaker regarding the six external sense objects. Thus, it should be understood that the teaching was given in this way because of the strength and weakness of desire and lust.

Sukhitesu sukhitoti upaṭṭhākesu dhanadhaññalābhādivasena sukhitesu “idānāhaṃ manāpaṃ bhojanaṃ labhissāmī”ti gehasitasukhena sukhito hoti, tehi pattasampattiṃ anubhavamāno viya carati. Dukkhitesu dukkhitoti tesaṃ kenacideva kāraṇena dukkhe uppanne sayaṃ dviguṇena dukkhena dukkhito hoti. Kiccakaraṇīyesūti kiccasaṅkhātesu karaṇīyesu. Tesu yogaṃ āpajjatīti upayogaṃ sayaṃ tesaṃ kiccānaṃ kattabbataṃ āpajjati. Kāmesūti vatthukāmesu. Evaṃ kho, gahapati, kāmehi aritto hotīti evaṃ kilesakāmehi aritto hoti anto kāmānaṃ bhāvena atuccho. Sukkapakkho tesaṃ abhāvena ritto tucchoti veditabbo.

As for ‘happy among the happy’ (sukhitesu sukhito): when his supporters are happy by way of gains of wealth, grain, and so on, he becomes happy with household-based happiness, thinking, ‘Now I will obtain agreeable food.’ He behaves as if he himself were experiencing the prosperity they have attained. As for ‘sorrowful among the sorrowful’ (dukkhitesu dukkhito): when sorrow arises for them for some reason, he himself becomes sorrowful with a twofold sorrow. As for ‘in duties and obligations’ (kiccakaraṇīyesu): in obligations that are called ‘duties’. As for ‘he applies himself to them’ (tesu yogaṃ āpajjati): he greatly exerts himself; he himself undertakes the state of having to perform their duties. As for ‘in sensual pleasures’ (kāmesu): in material sensual pleasures. As for ‘Thus, householder, he is not empty of sensual pleasures’ (evaṃ kho, gahapati, kāmehi aritto hoti): thus he is not empty of the defilements of sensual pleasure, not hollow within due to the presence of sensual pleasures. The bright side should be understood as being empty and hollow due to their absence.

Purakkharānoti vaṭṭaṃ purato kurumāno. Evaṃrūpo siyantiādīsu dīgharassakāḷodātādīsu rūpesu “evaṃrūpo nāma bhaveyya”nti pattheti. Sukhādīsu vedanāsu evaṃvedano nāma; nīlasaññādīsu saññāsu evaṃ sañño nāma; puññābhisaṅkhārādīsu saṅkhāresu evaṃsaṅkhāro nāma; cakkhuviññāṇādīsu [P 2.0261] viññāṇesu “evaṃ viññāṇo nāma bhaveyya”nti pattheti.

As for ‘placing before’ (purakkharāno): one who places the round of existence before oneself. In passages such as ‘May I be of such a form,’ regarding forms such as long, short, black, and white, one wishes, ‘May I be of such-and-such a form!’ Regarding feelings such as pleasure, ‘of such a feeling’; regarding perceptions such as the perception of blue, ‘of such a perception’; regarding formations such as meritorious formations, ‘of such a formation’; and regarding consciousnesses such as eye-consciousness, one wishes, ‘May I be of such-and-such a consciousness!’

Apurakkharānoti [V 2.0230] vaṭṭaṃ purato akurumāno. Sahitaṃ me, asahitaṃ teti tuyhaṃ vacanaṃ asahitaṃ asiliṭṭhaṃ, mayhaṃ sahitaṃ siliṭṭhaṃ madhurapānasadisaṃ. Adhiciṇṇaṃ te viparāvattanti yaṃ tuyhaṃ dīghena kālena paricitaṃ suppaguṇaṃ, taṃ mama vādaṃ āgamma sabbaṃ khaṇena viparāvattaṃ nivattaṃ. Āropito te vādoti tuyhaṃ doso mayā āropito. Cara vādappamokkhāyāti taṃ taṃ ācariyaṃ upasaṅkamitvā uttari pariyesanto imassa vādassa mokkhāya cara āhiṇḍāhi. Nibbeṭhehi vā sace pahosīti atha sayameva pahosi, idheva nibbeṭhehīti. Tatiyaṃ.

As for ‘not placing before’ (apurakkharāno): one who does not place the round of existence before oneself. As for ‘Mine is coherent, yours is incoherent’ (sahitaṃ me, asahitaṃ te): your speech is incoherent, unpolished; mine is coherent, polished, like a sweet drink. As for ‘What has been long practiced by you is reversed’ (adhiciṇṇaṃ te viparāvattaṃ): that which has been practiced and well-mastered by you for a long time, all of that, upon encountering my statement, is in a moment reversed, turned back. As for ‘Your statement has been charged’ (āropito te vādo): a fault has been imputed to you by me. As for ‘Go for release from this statement’ (cara vādappamokkhāya): approaching this and that teacher and searching for something further, go, wander about for the sake of release from this charge. As for ‘Or unravel it, if you are able’ (nibbeṭhehi vā sace pahosi): if you yourself are able, unravel it right here. The third.

4. Dutiyahāliddikānisuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Second Hāliddikāni Sutta

4. Catutthe [M 2.0241] sakkapañheti cūḷasakkapañhe, mahāsakkapañhepetaṃ vuttameva. Taṇhāsaṅkhayavimuttāti taṇhāsaṅkhaye nibbāne tadārammaṇāya phalavimuttiyā vimuttā. Accantaniṭṭhāti antaṃ atikkantaniṭṭhā satataniṭṭhā. Sesapadesupi eseva nayo. Catutthaṃ.

4. In the fourth, as for the Sakkapañha, this was indeed spoken in the Cūḷasakkapañha Sutta and in the Mahāsakkapañha Sutta. As for ‘liberated through the exhaustion of craving’ (taṇhāsaṅkhayavimuttā): in Nibbāna, the exhaustion of craving, they are liberated by the liberation of fruition which has that as its object. As for ‘utterly perfected’ (accantaniṭṭhā): having gone beyond the end, they are perpetually accomplished. This same method is to be understood for the remaining terms as well. The fourth.

5. Samādhisuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Samādhi Sutta

5. Pañcame samādhinti idaṃ bhagavā te bhikkhū cittekaggatāya parihāyante disvā, “cittekaggataṃ labhantānaṃ imesaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ phātiṃ gamissatī”ti ñatvā āha. Abhinandatīti pattheti. Abhivadatīti tāya abhinandanāya “aho piyaṃ iṭṭhaṃ kantaṃ manāpa”nti vadati. Vācaṃ abhinandantopi ca taṃ ārammaṇaṃ nissāya evaṃ lobhaṃ uppādento abhivadatiyeva nāma. Ajjhosāya tiṭṭhatīti gilitvā pariniṭṭhapetvā gaṇhāti. Yā [P 2.0262] rūpe nandīti yā sā rūpe balavapatthanāsaṅkhātā nandī. Tadupādānanti taṃ gahaṇaṭṭhena upādānaṃ. Nābhinandatīti na pattheti. Nābhivadatīti patthanāvasena na “iṭṭhaṃ kanta”nti vadati. Vipassanācittena cetasā “aniccaṃ dukkha”nti vacībhedaṃ karontopi nābhivadatiyeva. Pañcamaṃ.

5. In the fifth, the Blessed One spoke this on ‘concentration’ (samādhi) having seen those monks falling away from one-pointedness of mind and knowing, ‘For these monks, when they attain one-pointedness of mind, their meditation subject will grow.’ ‘Delights in’ (abhinandati) means one desires. ‘Applauds’ (abhivadati) means, with that delight, one says, ‘Oh, how dear, desirable, charming, and agreeable!’ And even without uttering the words, one who, relying on that object, generates greed in this way is indeed called one who applauds. ‘Clings and persists’ (ajjhosāya tiṭṭhati) means having swallowed, having brought to completion, one grasps. ‘The delight in form’ (yā rūpe nandī) is that delight in form which is called strong yearning. ‘That is clinging’ (tadupādānaṃ) means it is clinging (upādāna) in the sense of grasping. ‘Does not delight in’ (nābhinandati) means one does not desire. ‘Does not applaud’ (nābhivadati) means, by way of desire, one does not say, ‘desirable, charming.’ Even one who, with a mind of insight, makes a verbal utterance, thinking, ‘impermanent, suffering,’ is not one who applauds. The fifth.

6. Paṭisallāṇasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Paṭisallāṇa Sutta

6. Chaṭṭhe paṭisallāṇeti idaṃ bhagavā te bhikkhū kāyavivekena parihāyante disvā “kāyavivekaṃ labhantānaṃ imesaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ phātiṃ gamissatī”ti ñatvā āha. Chaṭṭhaṃ.

6. In the sixth, the Blessed One spoke this on ‘seclusion’ (paṭisallāṇa) having seen those monks falling away from bodily seclusion, and knowing, ‘For these monks, when they attain bodily seclusion, their meditation subject will grow.’ The sixth.

7. Upādāparitassanāsuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Upādāparitassanā Sutta

7. Sattame [V 2.0231] upādāparitassananti gahaṇena uppannaṃ paritassanaṃ. Anupādāaparitassananti aggahaṇena aparitassanaṃ. Rūpavipariṇāmānuparivattiviññāṇaṃ hotīti “mama rūpaṃ vipariṇata”nti vā “ahu vata metaṃ, dāni vata me natthī”ti vā ādinā nayena kammaviññāṇaṃ rūpassa bhedānuparivatti hoti. Vipariṇāmānuparivattijāti vipariṇāmassa anuparivattito vipariṇāmārammaṇacittato [M 2.0242] jātā. Paritassanā dhammasamuppādāti taṇhāparitassanā ca akusaladhammasamuppādā ca. Cittanti kusalacittaṃ. Pariyādāya tiṭṭhantīti pariyādiyitvā tiṭṭhanti. Uttāsavāti sauttāso. Vighātavāti savighāto sadukkho. Apekkhavāti sālayo. Upādāya ca paritassatīti gaṇhitvā paritassako nāma hoti. Na rūpavipariṇāmānuparivattīti khīṇāsavassa kammaviññāṇameva natthi, tasmā rūpabhedānuparivatti na hotīti vattuṃ vaṭṭati. Sattamaṃ.

7. In the seventh [sutta], 'agitation through clinging' means the agitation—which is craving and wrong view—that arises through grasping. 'Non-agitation through non-clinging' means the absence of agitation through not grasping. 'Consciousness follows the alteration of form' means that kamma-consciousness, associated with craving and wrong view, follows the destruction of form in such ways as, 'My form has altered,' or, 'Alas, this was mine; alas, now it is not mine.' 'Born from following the alteration' means born from a mind that follows the alteration, a mind that has alteration as its object. 'Agitations and the arising of states' means both the agitation that is craving and the arising of other unwholesome states. 'Mind' means the wholesome mind. 'They stand having overcome' means they stand having exhausted [the wholesome mind]. 'Fearful' means one has terror. 'Distressed' means one has distress and suffering. 'Concerned' means one has attachment. 'And one is agitated through clinging' means that having grasped with the grasp of craving and wrong view, one is called one who is agitated. 'Consciousness does not follow the alteration of form' means that for one whose taints are destroyed, kamma-consciousness itself does not exist; therefore, it is fitting to say that consciousness does not follow the destruction of form. Seventh.

8. Dutiyaupādāparitassanāsuttavaṇṇanā

8. The Commentary to the Second Discourse on Clinging and Agitation.

8. Aṭṭhame taṇhāmānadiṭṭhivasena desanā katā. Iti paṭipāṭiyā catūsu suttesu vaṭṭavivaṭṭameva kathitaṃ. Aṭṭhamaṃ.

8. In the eighth, the teaching was given by means of craving, conceit, and views. Thus, in sequence, only the round of existence and its cessation were taught in the four discourses. The eighth.

9. Kālattayaaniccasuttavaṇṇanā

9. The Commentary on the Discourse on the Impermanence of the Three Times.

9. Navame [P 2.0263] ko pana vādo paccuppannassāti paccuppannamhi kathāva kā, aniccameva taṃ. Te kira bhikkhū atītānāgataṃ aniccanti sallakkhetvā paccuppanne kilamiṃsu, atha nesaṃ ito atītānāgatepi “paccuppannaṃ anicca”nti vuccamāne bujjhissantīti ajjhāsayaṃ viditvā satthā puggalajjhāsayena imaṃ desanaṃ desesi. Navamaṃ.

9. In the ninth: What is there to say of the present? It is simply impermanent. It is said that those monks, having marked the past and future as impermanent, grew weary regarding the present. Then, the Teacher, knowing their inclination that they would understand when it was said, 'The present is impermanent'—which for them was more easily knowable than the past and future—taught this discourse in accordance with the individuals' disposition. The ninth.

10-11. Kālattayadukkhasuttādivaṇṇanā

10-11. The Commentary on the Discourses beginning with the Discourse on Suffering in the Three Times.

10-11. Dasamekādasamāni [V 2.0232] dukkhaṃ anattāti padehi visesetvā tathārūpeneva puggalajjhāsayena kathitānīti. Dasamekādasamāni.

10-11. The tenth and eleventh discourses are distinguished by the terms 'suffering' and 'not-self,' and were taught in just such a manner according to the disposition of the individuals. The tenth and eleventh.

Nakulapituvaggo paṭhamo.

The Nakulapitu Chapter, the First.

2. Aniccavaggo

2. The Chapter on Impermanence

1-10. Aniccasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-10. The Commentary on the Anicca Sutta and Others

12-21. Aniccavagge pariyosānasuttaṃ pucchāvasikaṃ, sesāni tathā tathā bujjhanakānañca vasena desitānīti. Paṭhamādīni.

12-21. In the Chapter on Impermanence, the concluding discourse is based on a question, while the rest are taught in accordance with those who would understand in various ways. The first and others.

Aniccavaggo dutiyo.

The Impermanence Chapter, the Second.

3. Bhāravaggo

3. The Burden Chapter

1. Bhārasuttavaṇṇanā

1. The Commentary to the Burden Discourse.

22. Bhāravaggassa [M 2.0243] paṭhame pañcupādānakkhandhātissa vacanīyanti pañcupādānakkhandhā iti assa vacanīyaṃ, evaṃ vattabbaṃ bhaveyyāti attho. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, bhāroti ye ime pañcupādānakkhandhā, ayaṃ bhāroti vuccati. Kenaṭṭhenāti? Parihārabhāriyaṭṭhena. Etesañhi ṭhāpanagamananisīdāpananipajjāpananhāpanamaṇḍanakhādāpanabhuñjāpanādiparihāro bhāriyoti parihārabhāriyaṭṭhena bhāroti vuccati. Evaṃnāmoti tisso dattotiādināmo. Evaṃgottoti kaṇhāyano vacchāyanotiādigotto. Iti vohāramattasiddhaṃ puggalaṃ “bhārahāro”ti katvā dasseti. Puggalo [V 2.0233] hi paṭisandhikkhaṇeyeva [P 2.0264] khandhabhāraṃ ukkhipitvā dasapi vassāni vīsatipi vassasatampīti yāvajīvaṃ imaṃ khandhabhāraṃ nhāpento bhojento mudusamphassamañcapīṭhesu nisīdāpento nipajjāpento pariharitvā cutikkhaṇe chaḍḍetvā puna paṭisandhikkhaṇe aparaṃ khandhabhāraṃ ādiyati, tasmā bhārahāroti jāto.

22. In the first of the Burden Chapter, 'the five aggregates of clinging' is what should be said. The meaning is that it should be stated as 'the five aggregates of clinging.' 'This, monks, is called the burden' means that these five aggregates of clinging are called the burden. In what sense? In the sense of being burdensome to maintain. For the maintenance of these—placing, moving, seating, lying down, bathing, adorning, feeding, consuming, and so on—is burdensome; therefore, it is called a burden in the sense of being burdensome to maintain. 'Such a name' means a name such as Tissa or Datta. 'Such a clan' means a clan such as Kaṇhāyana or Vacchāyana. Thus, he shows the person, established merely by convention, as the 'burden-bearer.' For a person, having taken up the burden of the aggregates at the very moment of rebirth-linking, maintains this burden of the aggregates—bathing it, feeding it, seating it on soft couches and chairs, laying it down—for ten years, twenty years, or even a hundred years, for as long as life lasts. Then, at the moment of death, having discarded it, one again takes up another burden of aggregates at the moment of rebirth-linking. Therefore, one becomes a 'burden-bearer.'

Ponobhavikāti punabbhavanibbattikā. Nandīrāgasahagatāti nandirāgena saha ekattameva gatā. Tabbhāvasahagatañhi idha adhippetaṃ. Tatra tatrābhinandinīti upapattiṭṭhāne vā rūpādīsu vā ārammaṇesu tattha tattha abhinandanasīlāva. Kāmataṇhādīsu pañcakāmaguṇiko rāgo kāmataṇhā nāma, rūpārūpabhavarāgo jhānanikanti sassatadiṭṭhisahagato rāgoti ayaṃ bhavataṇhā nāma, ucchedadiṭṭhisahagato rāgo vibhavataṇhā nāma. Bhārādānanti bhāragahaṇaṃ. Taṇhāya hi esa bhāraṃ ādiyati. Asesavirāganirodhotiādi sabbaṃ nibbānasseva vevacanaṃ. Tañhi āgamma taṇhā asesato virajjati nirujjhati cajiyati paṭinissajjiyati vimuccati, natthi cettha kāmālayo vā diṭṭhālayo vāti nibbānaṃ etāni nāmāni labhati. Samūlaṃ taṇhanti taṇhāya avijjā mūlaṃ nāma. Abbuyhāti arahattamaggena taṃ samūlakaṃ uddharitvā. Nicchāto parinibbutoti nittaṇho parinibbuto nāmāti vattuṃ vaṭṭatīti. Paṭhamaṃ.

'Leading to renewed existence' means productive of renewed existence. 'Accompanied by delight and lust' means it has gone to oneness with delight and lust. For here, 'accompanied by that state' is intended. 'Delighting in this or that' means having the nature of delighting in this or that, whether in a place of rebirth or in objects such as form. Among the cravings, lust for the five strands of sensual pleasure is called 'craving for sensual pleasures.' Lust for form and formless existence, delight in jhāna, and lust accompanied by the view of eternalism—this is called 'craving for existence.' Lust accompanied by the view of annihilation is called 'craving for non-existence.' 'The taking up of the burden' means the grasping of the burden. For it is through craving that one takes up this burden. 'The remainderless fading away and cessation,' and so on, are all synonyms for Nibbāna. For, having reached it, craving without remainder fades away, ceases, is given up, is relinquished, is released; and in it there is no abode of sensuality or abode of views. Therefore, Nibbāna obtains these names. 'Craving with its root' means that ignorance is the root of craving. 'Having pulled it up' means having uprooted that craving with its root by means of the path of Arahantship. 'Without craving, fully extinguished' means it is fitting to say that one who is without craving is called 'fully extinguished.' The first.

2. Pariññasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Discourse on Full Understanding

23. Dutiye [M 2.0244] pariññeyyeti parijānitabbe, samatikkamitabbeti attho. Pariññanti accantapariññaṃ, samatikkamanti attho. Rāgakkhayotiādi nibbānassa nāmaṃ. Tañhi accantapariññā nāma. Dutiyaṃ.

23. In the second [discourse], `pariññeyye` means 'to be thoroughly comprehended' and 'to be transcended'; this is the meaning. `Pariññaṃ` means 'complete full understanding' and 'complete transcendence,' which is Nibbāna; this is the meaning. 'The destruction of lust,' and so on, is a name for Nibbāna. For that is indeed called 'complete full understanding.' The second.

3. Abhijānasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Discourse on Direct Knowledge

24. Tatiye abhijānanti abhijānanto. Iminā ñātapariññā kathitā, dutiyapadena tīraṇapariññā, tatiyacatutthehi pahānapariññāti imasmiṃ sutte tisso pariññā kathitāti. Tatiyaṃ.

24. In the third [discourse], 'one understands fully' (abhijānanti) means 'understanding fully' (abhijānanto). By this, the full understanding as knowing (ñātapariññā) is taught; by the second term, the full understanding as investigating (tīraṇapariññā); by the third and fourth terms, the full understanding as abandoning (pahānapariññā). Thus, in this discourse, the three kinds of full understanding are taught. The third.

4-9. Chandarāgasuttādivaṇṇanā

4–9. Commentary on the Discourses on Sensual Desire, and so on

25-30. Catutthādīni [V 2.0234][P 2.0265] dhātusaṃyutte vuttanayeneva veditabbāni. Paṭipāṭiyā panettha pañcamachaṭṭhasattamesu cattāri saccāni kathitāni, aṭṭhamanavamesu vaṭṭavivaṭṭaṃ. Catutthādīni.

The fourth [discourse] and so on should be understood in the same way as stated in the Connected Discourses on Elements. Here, in sequence, the four truths are taught in the fifth, sixth, and seventh [discourses]; the round of existence and the cessation of the round are taught in the eighth and ninth. The fourth and so on.

10. Aghamūlasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Discourse on the Root of Misery

31. Dasame aghanti dukkhaṃ. Evamettha dukkhalakkhaṇameva kathitaṃ. Dasamaṃ.

31. In the tenth discourse, `aghaṃ` means suffering. Thus, here, only the characteristic of suffering has been taught. The tenth.

11. Pabhaṅgusuttavaṇṇanā

11. Commentary on the Discourse on the Perishable

32. Ekādasame pabhaṅgunti pabhijjanasabhāvaṃ. Evamidha aniccalakkhaṇameva kathitanti. Ekādasamaṃ.

32. In the eleventh discourse, `pabhaṅgu` means having the nature of breaking up. Thus, here, only the characteristic of impermanence has been taught. The eleventh.

Bhāravaggo tatiyo.

The Third Chapter: On the Burden.

4. Natumhākavaggo

4. The Chapter on 'Not Yours'

1. Natumhākasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on ‘Not Yours’

33. Natumhākavaggassa paṭhame pajahathāti chandarāgappahānena pajahatha. Tiṇādīsu tiṇaṃ nāma antopheggu bahisāraṃ tālanāḷikerādi. Kaṭṭhaṃ [M 2.0245] nāma antosāraṃ bahipheggu khadirasālasākapanasādi. Sākhā nāma rukkhassa bāhā viya nikkhantā. Palāsaṃ nāma tālanāḷikerapaṇṇādi. Paṭhamaṃ.

33. In the first discourse of the Chapter on 'Not Yours,' ‘pajahatha’ means ‘you should abandon’ through the abandonment of sensual desire. Among ‘grass and so on,’ grass refers to what is pithless inside and has substance outside, such as the palm and coconut. Wood refers to what has substance inside and is pithless outside, such as acacia, sal, teak, and jackfruit. A branch is what extends from a tree like an arm. A leaf refers to things like palm and coconut leaves. The first.

2. Dutiyanatumhākasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Second Discourse on ‘Not Yours’

34. Dutiyaṃ [V 2.0235] vinā upamāya bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayena vuttaṃ. Dutiyaṃ.

34. The second discourse was spoken according to the disposition of those who are to understand without a simile. The second.

3. Aññatarabhikkhusuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Discourse concerning a Certain Monk

35. Tatiye rūpañce, bhante, anusetīti yadi rūpaṃ anuseti. Tena saṅkhaṃ gacchatīti kāmarāgādīsu yena anusayena taṃ rūpaṃ anuseti, teneva anusayena “ratto duṭṭho mūḷho”ti paṇṇattiṃ gacchati. Na tena saṅkhaṃ gacchatīti tena abhūtena anusayena “ratto duṭṭho mūḷho”ti saṅkhaṃ na gacchatīti. Tatiyaṃ.

35. In the third discourse, the phrase ‘And in form, venerable sir, it lies latent’ means: if a latent tendency lies latent in form. ‘He is reckoned by that’ means by whichever latent tendency—such as that for sensual desire—that lies latent in relation to that form, by that very latent tendency a person is designated as ‘attached, hostile, or deluded.’ ‘He is not reckoned by that’ means that by a non-existent, unarisen latent tendency, one is not designated as ‘attached, hostile, or deluded.’ The third.

4. Dutiyaaññatarabhikkhusuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Second Discourse on a Certain Monk

36. Catutthe [P 2.0266] taṃ anumīyatīti taṃ anusayitaṃ rūpaṃ marantena anusayena anumarati. Na hi ārammaṇe bhijjamāne tadārammaṇā dhammā tiṭṭhanti. Yaṃ anumīyatīti yaṃ rūpaṃ yena anusayena anumarati. Tena saṅkhaṃ gacchatīti tena anusayena “ratto duṭṭho mūḷho”ti saṅkhaṃ gacchati. Atha vā yanti karaṇavacanametaṃ, yena anusayena anumīyati, tena “ratto duṭṭho mūḷho”ti saṅkhaṃ gacchatīti attho. Catutthaṃ.

36. In the fourth discourse, ‘it perishes along with it’ means that form in which a latent tendency inheres perishes along with the perishing latent tendency. For when the object perishes, the mental states that take it as their object do not remain. ‘That which perishes along with it’ refers to the form that perishes along with a certain latent tendency. ‘He is reckoned by that’ means he is reckoned by that latent tendency as ‘lustful, hostile, or deluded.’ Alternatively, ‘yaṃ’ is an instrumental term: by whichever latent tendency it perishes along with, by that he is reckoned as ‘lustful, hostile, or deluded.’ This is the meaning. The fourth.

5-6. Ānandasuttādivaṇṇanā

5–6. Commentary on the Discourse to Ānanda, and so forth

37-38. Pañcame ṭhitassa aññathattaṃ paññāyatīti dharamānassa jīvamānassa jarā paññāyati. Ṭhitīti hi jīvitindriyasaṅkhātāya anupālanāya nāmaṃ. Aññathattanti jarāya. Tenāhu porāṇā –

In the fifth discourse, ‘alteration is discerned in what is standing’ means aging is discerned in what is present, what is living. For ‘standing’ (ṭhiti) is a term for preservation, which is reckoned as the life faculty. ‘Alteration’ (aññathatta) means aging. Thus, the ancients said:

“Uppādo jāti akkhāto, bhaṅgo vutto vayoti ca;

Aññathattaṃ jarā vuttā, ṭhitī ca anupālanā”ti.

‘Arising’ is called birth; ‘dissolution’ is said to be decay; ‘alteration’ is declared as aging; and ‘standing’ is preservation.

Evaṃ [V 2.0236] ekekassa khandhassa uppādajarābhaṅgasaṅkhātāni tīṇi lakkhaṇāni honti yāni sandhāya vuttaṃ “tīṇimāni, bhikkhave, saṅkhatassa saṅkhatalakkhaṇānī”ti (a. ni. 3.47).

Thus, each aggregate has three characteristics—arising, aging, and dissolution—which are referred to in the statement, ‘Monks, these are the three characteristics of the conditioned’ (AN 3.47).

Tattha [M 2.0246] saṅkhataṃ nāma paccayanibbatto yo koci saṅkhāro. Saṅkhāro ca na lakkhaṇaṃ, lakkhaṇaṃ na saṅkhāro, na ca saṅkhārena vinā lakkhaṇaṃ paññāpetuṃ sakkā, nāpi lakkhaṇaṃ vinā saṅkhāro, lakkhaṇena pana saṅkhāro pākaṭo hoti. Yathā hi na ca gāvīyeva lakkhaṇaṃ, lakkhaṇameva na gāvī, nāpi gāviṃ muñcitvā lakkhaṇaṃ paññāpetuṃ sakkā, nāpi lakkhaṇaṃ muñcitvā gāviṃ, lakkhaṇena pana gāvī pākaṭā hoti, evaṃsampadamidaṃ veditabbaṃ.

Here, ‘the conditioned’ means any formation whatsoever that is produced by conditions. The formation is not the characteristic, nor is the characteristic the formation; nor can a characteristic be made known apart from a formation, nor a formation apart from a characteristic; but the formation becomes manifest through the characteristic. Just as a cow is not the characteristic, nor is the characteristic the cow; nor can a characteristic be made known apart from a cow, nor a cow apart from a characteristic; but the cow becomes manifest through the characteristic—so should this complete example be understood.

Tattha saṅkhārānaṃ uppādakkhaṇe saṅkhāropi uppādalakkhaṇampi kālasaṅkhāto tassa khaṇopi paññāyati. “Uppādopī”ti [P 2.0267] vutte saṅkhāropi jarālakkhaṇampi kālasaṅkhāto tassa khaṇopi paññāyati. Bhaṅgakkhaṇe saṅkhāropi taṃlakkhaṇampi kālasaṅkhāto tassa khaṇopi paññāyati. Apare pana vadanti “arūpadhammānaṃ jarākhaṇo nāma na sakkā paññāpetuṃ, sammāsambuddho ca ‘vedanāya uppādo paññāyati, vayo paññāyati, ṭhitāya aññathattaṃ paññāyatī’ti vadanto arūpadhammānampi tīṇi lakkhaṇāni paññāpeti, tāni atthikkhaṇaṃ upādāya labbhantī”ti vatvā –

Here, at the moment of the arising of formations, the formation itself, the characteristic of arising, and that moment reckoned as time are discerned. When ‘arising also’ is stated, the formation itself, the characteristic of aging, and that moment reckoned as time are discerned. At the moment of dissolution, the formation itself, that characteristic, and that moment reckoned as time are discerned. Others, however, say: ‘The moment of aging for immaterial phenomena cannot be made known. Yet the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One, while stating, “The arising of feeling is discerned, its passing away is discerned, the alteration of what is standing is discerned,” makes known the three characteristics even for immaterial phenomena. Those are obtained by taking the moment of existence as the basis’—and having said this:

“Atthitā sabbadhammānaṃ, ṭhiti nāma pavuccati;

Tasseva bhedo maraṇaṃ, sabbadā sabbapāṇina”nti. –

‘The existence of all phenomena is called “standing” (ṭhiti); its dissolution is death, always for all beings.’

Imāya ācariyagāthāya tamatthaṃ sādhenti. Atha vā santativasena ṭhānaṃ ṭhitīti veditabbanti ca vadanti. Yasmā pana sutte ayaṃ viseso natthi, tasmā ācariyamatiyā suttaṃ apaṭibāhetvā suttameva pamāṇaṃ kattabbaṃ. Chaṭṭhaṃ uttānameva. Pañcamachaṭṭhāni.

They establish that meaning through this teachers’ verse. Alternatively, they also say that ‘standing’ (ṭhiti) should be understood as duration by way of continuity. However, since this distinction is not present in the discourse, therefore, without setting aside the discourse on the basis of a teacher's opinion, the discourse itself should be taken as the authority. The sixth discourse is indeed clear. The fifth and sixth discourses.

7-10. Anudhammasuttādivaṇṇanā

7–10. Commentary on the Discourse on Conformity and So Forth

39-42. Sattame dhammānudhammappaṭipannassāti navannaṃ lokuttaradhammānaṃ anulomadhammaṃ pubbabhāgapaṭipadaṃ paṭipannassa. Ayamanudhammoti ayaṃ anulomadhammo hoti. Nibbidābahuloti ukkaṇṭhanabahulo [V 2.0237] hutvā. Parijānātīti tīhi pariññāhi parijānāti. Parimuccatīti maggakkhaṇe uppannāya pahānapariññāya parimuccati. Evaṃ imasmiṃ sutte maggova kathito hoti, tathā ito paresu tīsu. Idha pana anupassanā aniyamitā, tesu [M 2.0247] niyamitā. Tasmā idhāpi sā tattha niyamitavaseneva niyametabbā. Na hi sakkā tīsu aññataraṃ anupassanaṃ vinā nibbindituṃ parijānituṃ vāti. Sattamādīni.

In the seventh discourse, 'one who practices the Dhamma in conformity with the Dhamma' (dhammānudhammappaṭipannassa) means one who practices the preliminary practice that is the Dhamma in conformity with the nine supramundane Dhammas. 'This is the Dhamma in conformity' (ayamanudhammo) means this is the conforming practice. 'Abounding in disenchantment' (nibbidābahulo) means being full of weariness. 'Fully comprehends' (parijānāti) means one fully comprehends through the three kinds of full comprehension. 'Is liberated' (parimuccati) means one is liberated at the moment of the path by the full comprehension of abandoning. Thus, in this discourse, only the path is taught, and likewise in the next three. Here, however, the contemplation is unspecified, whereas in those it is specified. Therefore, here too it should be specified in the same way as it is specified there. For it is not possible to become disenchanted or to fully comprehend without one or another of the three contemplations. The seventh and following discourses.

Natumhākavaggo catuttho.

The Fourth Chapter: On 'Not Yours'.

5. Attadīpavaggo

5. The Chapter on Oneself as an Island

1. Attadīpasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on Oneself as an Island

43. Attadīpavaggassa [P 2.0268] paṭhame attadīpāti attānaṃ dīpaṃ tāṇaṃ leṇaṃ gatiṃ parāyaṇaṃ patiṭṭhaṃ katvā viharathāti attho. Attasaraṇāti idaṃ tasseva vevacanaṃ. Anaññasaraṇāti idaṃ aññassa saraṇapaṭikkhepavacanaṃ. Na hi añño aññassa saraṇaṃ hoti aññassa vāyāmena aññassa asijjhanato, vuttampi cetaṃ –

43. In the first discourse of the Attadīpa Vagga, the meaning of 'be an island to yourselves' (attadīpā) is: dwell having made the self, which is the Dhamma, an island, a protection, a shelter, a destination, a final resort, and a foundation. 'Take yourselves as a refuge' (attasaraṇā) is a synonym for that very term. 'Take no other refuge' (anaññasaraṇā) is a statement rejecting another refuge. For truly, another cannot be a refuge for another, since one’s attainment does not come about through another’s effort. And this has been said:

“Attā hi attano nātho,

Ko hi nātho paro siyā”ti. (dha. pa. 160);

“For self is the protector of self; what other protector could there be?” (Dhp. 160)

Tenāha “anaññasaraṇā”ti. Ko panettha attā nāma? Lokiyalokuttaro dhammo. Tenevāha – “dhammadīpā dhammasaraṇā anaññasaraṇā”ti. Yonīti kāraṇaṃ – “yoni hesā, bhūmija, phalassa adhigamāyā”tiādīsu (ma. ni. 3.227) viya. Kiṃpahotikāti kiṃpabhutikā, kuto pabhavantīti attho? Rūpassa tvevāti idaṃ tesaṃyeva sokādīnaṃ pahānadassanatthaṃ āraddhaṃ. Na paritassatīti na gaṇhāti na gahati. Tadaṅganibbutoti tena vipassanaṅgena kilesānaṃ nibbutattā tadaṅganibbuto. Imasmiṃ sutte vipassanāva kathitā. Paṭhamaṃ.

Therefore He said, 'take no other refuge.' What, then, is 'self' here? The Dhamma, both mundane and supramundane. For that very reason He said, 'have the Dhamma as an island, the Dhamma as a refuge, no other refuge.' 'Source' (yoni) means cause, as in such phrases as, 'Bhūmija, this is a source for the attainment of the fruit' (MN 3.227). 'Of what origin?' (kiṃpahotikā) means 'of what beginning?' (kiṃpabhutikā); the meaning is, 'from where do they arise?' The teaching beginning 'only of form...' (rūpassa tveva) was undertaken to show the abandoning of that very sorrow and so forth. 'Does not tremble' (na paritassati) means one does not grasp, does not cling. 'Extinguished by that factor' (tadaṅganibbuto): one is so called because the defilements are extinguished by that factor of insight. In this discourse, only insight is taught. The first.

2. Paṭipadāsuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Paṭipadā Sutta

44. Dutiye [V 2.0238] dukkhasamudayagāminī samanupassanāti yasmā sakkāyo dukkhaṃ, tassa ca samudayagāminī paṭipadā nāma “rūpaṃ attato samanupassatī”ti evaṃ [M 2.0248] diṭṭhisamanupassanā vuttā, tasmā dukkhasamudayagāminī samanupassanāti ayamettha attho hoti. Dukkhanirodhagāminī samanupassanāti ettha saha vipassanāya catumaggañāṇaṃ “samanupassanā”ti vuttaṃ. Iti imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭavivaṭṭaṃ kathitaṃ. Dutiyaṃ.

44.
