In the second [sutta], regarding 'the contemplation leading to the origin of suffering': since the existing body (sakkāya) is suffering, and the practice leading to its origin—namely, 'one repeatedly regards form as self'—is taught as a contemplation that is a view, therefore, the meaning here is 'the contemplation leading to the origin of suffering'. Regarding 'the contemplation leading to the cessation of suffering': here, the knowledge of the four paths together with insight is called 'contemplation'. Thus, in this sutta, the round of existence and the escape from the round are taught. The second.

3. Aniccasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Anicca Sutta

45. Tatiye sammappaññāya daṭṭhabbanti saha vipassanāya maggapaññāya daṭṭhabbaṃ. Virajjati vimuccatīti maggakkhaṇe virajjati, phalakkhaṇe vimuccati. Anupādāya āsavehīti anuppādanirodhena [P 2.0269] niruddhehi āsavehi agahetvā iti vimuccati. Rūpadhātuyātiādi paccavekkhaṇadassanatthaṃ vuttaṃ. Saha phalena paccavekkhaṇadassanatthantipi vadantiyeva. Ṭhitanti upari kattabbakiccābhāvena ṭhitaṃ. Ṭhitattā santussitanti pattabbaṃ pattabhāvena santuṭṭhaṃ. Paccattaṃyeva parinibbāyatīti sayameva parinibbāyati. Tatiyaṃ.

45. In the third [sutta], 'to be seen with right wisdom' means it should be seen with the wisdom of the path together with the wisdom of insight. 'Becomes dispassionate, is liberated' means: at the moment of the path, one becomes dispassionate; at the moment of fruition, one is liberated. 'Without clinging, from the taints' means: one is liberated by not grasping, the taints having ceased through the cessation of non-arising. The passage beginning with 'Regarding the form element' was spoken for the purpose of showing the knowledge of reviewing. The teachers also say it is for the purpose of showing the knowledge of reviewing together with the fruition. 'Established' means: it is established due to the absence of any further task to be done. 'Being established, content' means: content because that which was to be attained has been attained. 'Attains Parinibbāna by oneself alone' means: one attains the Parinibbāna of the defilements by oneself alone. The third.

4. Dutiyaaniccasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Second Anicca Sutta

46. Catutthe pubbantānudiṭṭhiyoti pubbantaṃ anugatā aṭṭhārasa diṭṭhiyo na honti. Aparantānudiṭṭhiyoti aparantaṃ anugatā catucattālīsa diṭṭhiyo na honti. Thāmaso parāmāsoti diṭṭhithāmaso ceva diṭṭhiparāmāso ca na hoti. Ettāvatā paṭhamamaggo dassito. Idāni saha vipassanāya tayo magge ca phalāni ca dassetuṃ rūpasmintiādi āraddhaṃ. Atha vā diṭṭhiyo nāma vipassanāya eva pahīnā, idaṃ pana upari saha vipassanāya cattāro magge dassetuṃ āraddhaṃ. Catutthaṃ.

46. In the fourth [sutta], as for 'views that follow upon the past': the eighteen views that follow upon the past portion of the aggregates do not arise. As for 'views that follow upon the future': the forty-four views that follow upon the future portion of the aggregates do not arise. As for 'tenacity and grasping': neither the tenacity of views nor the misapprehension of views arises. By this much, the first path has been shown. Now, in order to show the three paths together with insight, and the fruits, the text beginning with 'regarding form' is undertaken. Alternatively, views are indeed abandoned by insight alone, but this is undertaken in order to show the four paths above, together with insight. The fourth.

5. Samanupassanāsuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Samanupassanā Sutta

47. Pañcame [V 2.0239] pañcupādānakkhandhe samanupassanti etesaṃ vā aññataranti paripuṇṇagāhavasena pañcakkhandhe samanupassanti, aparipuṇṇagāhavasena etesaṃ aññataraṃ. Iti ayañceva samanupassanāti iti ayañca diṭṭhisamanupassanā. Asmīti cassa avigataṃ hotīti yassa ayaṃ samanupassanā atthi, tasmiṃ asmīti taṇhāmānadiṭṭhisaṅkhātaṃ papañcattayaṃ avigatameva hoti. Pañcannaṃ [M 2.0249] indriyānaṃ avakkanti hotīti tasmiṃ kilesajāte sati kammakilesapaccayānaṃ pañcannaṃ indriyānaṃ nibbatti hoti.

47. In the fifth [sutta], as for 'they contemplate the five aggregates of clinging or any one of them': by way of complete grasping, they contemplate the five aggregates as self; by way of incomplete grasping, they contemplate any one of them as self. As for 'Thus this is contemplation': Thus, this is also a contemplation which is a view. As for 'And for him, "I am" has not departed': for the person who has this contemplation, the threefold proliferation—called craving, conceit, and views, which arises as 'I am'—has certainly not departed. As for 'There is an arising of the five faculties': when that net of defilements exists, there is the manifestation of the five faculties, which have kamma and defilements as their condition.

Atthi, bhikkhave, manoti idaṃ kammamanaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Dhammāti ārammaṇaṃ. Avijjādhātūti javanakkhaṇe avijjā. Avijjāsamphassajenāti avijjāsampayuttaphassato [P 2.0270] jātena. Apica manoti bhavaṅgakkhaṇe vipākamanodhātu, āvajjanakkhaṇe kiriyamanodhātu. Dhammādayo vuttappakārāva. Asmītipissa hotīti taṇhāmānadiṭṭhivasena asmīti evampissa hoti. Ito paresu ayamahamasmīti rūpādīsu kiñcideva dhammaṃ gahetvā “ayaṃ ahamasmī”ti attadiṭṭhivasena vuttaṃ. Bhavissanti sassatadiṭṭhivasena. Na bhavissanti ucchedadiṭṭhivasena. Rūpī bhavissantiādīni sabbāni sassatameva bhajanti. Athetthāti atha tenevākārena ṭhitesu etesu indriyesu. Avijjā pahīyatīti catūsu saccesu aññāṇabhūtā avijjā pahīyati. Vijjā uppajjatīti arahattamaggavijjā uppajjati. Evamettha asmīti taṇhāmānadiṭṭhiyo. Kammassa pañcannañca indriyānaṃ antare eko sandhi, vipākamanaṃ pañcindriyapakkhikaṃ katvā pañcannañca indriyānaṃ kammassa ca antare eko sandhīti. Iti tayo papañcā atīto addhā, indriyādīni paccuppanno addhā, tattha kammamanaṃ ādiṃ katvā anāgatassa paccayo dassitoti. Pañcamaṃ.

As for 'There is, monks, the mind': this was spoken in reference to the kamma-mind. As for 'dhammā': this refers to the object. As for 'the element of ignorance': this is ignorance at the moment of impulsion. As for 'born of contact accompanied by ignorance': this means born from contact associated with ignorance. Furthermore, as for 'mind': at the moment of the life-continuum, it is the resultant mind-element; at the moment of adverting, it is the functional mind-element. 'Dhammā', etc., are as described. As for 'For him there is also "I am"': by the power of craving, conceit, and views, 'I am' thus also occurs for him. In the subsequent phrases, as for 'I am this': having taken some phenomenon among form, etc., it is said through the power of the view of self, 'This is I'. 'I will be' is said through the power of the eternalist view. 'I will not be' is said through the power of the annihilationist view. All phrases beginning with 'I will be possessed of form' partake only of eternalism. As for 'Then here': then, in these faculties that are established in that very manner. As for 'ignorance is abandoned': ignorance, which is non-knowledge regarding the four truths, is abandoned. As for 'knowledge arises': the knowledge of the Arahant path arises. Thus, here 'I am' refers to craving, conceit, and views. Between kamma and the five faculties, there is one connection. Making the resultant mind to be on the side of the five faculties, there is one connection between the five faculties and kamma. Thus, the three proliferations are the past time; the faculties and so on are the present time; and among them, beginning with the kamma-mind, the condition for the future is shown. The fifth.

6. Khandhasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Khandha Sutta

48. Chaṭṭhe rūpakkhandho kāmāvacaro, cattāro khandhā catubhūmakā. Sāsavanti āsavānaṃ ārammaṇabhāvena paccayabhūtaṃ. Upādāniyanti tatheva ca upādānānaṃ paccayabhūtaṃ. Vacanattho panettha – ārammaṇaṃ katvā pavattehi saha āsavehīti sāsavaṃ. Upādātabbanti upādāniyaṃ. Idhāpi [V 2.0240] rūpakkhandho kāmāvacaro, avasesā tebhūmakā vipassanācāravasena vuttā. Evamettha rūpaṃ rāsaṭṭhena khandhesu paviṭṭhaṃ, sāsavarāsaṭṭhena upādānakkhandhesu. Vedanādayo sāsavāpi atthi, anāsavāpi atthi. Te rāsaṭṭhena sabbepi khandhesu paviṭṭhā, tebhūmakā panettha sāsavaṭṭhena upādānakkhandhesu paviṭṭhāti. Chaṭṭhaṃ.

48. In the sixth, the aggregate of form pertains to the sense-sphere; the four aggregates pertain to the four planes. 'With taints' means being a condition by way of being an object for the taints. 'Subject to clinging' means likewise being a condition for the clingings. The meaning of the term here is this: it is called 'with taints' because it occurs together with taints that arise having taken it as an object. It is called 'subject to clinging' because it is to be clung to by the clingings. Here too, the aggregate of form pertains to the sense-sphere; the remaining aggregates, pertaining to the three planes, are spoken of in terms of the domain of insight meditation. Thus, herein, form is included in the aggregates in the sense of being a mass, and in the aggregates of clinging in the sense of being a tainted mass. Feeling and the others exist both with taints and without taints. In the sense of being a mass, all of them are included in the aggregates. Herein, those pertaining to the three planes are included in the aggregates of clinging in the sense of being an object for the taints. Sixth.

7-8. Soṇasuttādivaṇṇanā

7-8. Commentary on the Soṇa Sutta, etc.

49-50. Sattame [M 2.0250][P 2.0271] seyyohamasmīti visiṭṭho uttamo ahamasmi. Kimaññatra yathābhūtassa adassanāti yathābhūtassa adassanato aññaṃ kiṃ bhaveyya? Adassanaṃ aññāṇameva bhaveyyāti attho. Idānissa te parivaṭṭaṃ vajirabhedadesanaṃ ārabhanto taṃ kiṃ maññasi soṇotiādimāha. Aṭṭhamaṃ uttānameva. Sattamaaṭṭhamāni.

In the seventh: 'I am better' means 'I am special, I am excellent.' As for 'What else other than not seeing things as they are?': What other cause could there be for such a view, apart from not seeing reality as it is? The meaning is: Not seeing, which is just ignorance, would be the cause for such a view. Now, commencing for him the diamond-smasher discourse with its three cycles, the Blessed One said, 'What do you think, Soṇa?' and so on. The eighth is self-evident. Seventh and eighth.

9-10. Nandikkhayasuttādivaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the Nandikkhaya Sutta, etc.

51-52. Navamadasamesu nandikkhayā rāgakkhayo, rāgakkhayā nandikkhayoti idaṃ nandīti vā rāgoti vā imesaṃ atthato ninnānākaraṇatāya vuttaṃ. Nibbidānupassanāya vā nibbindanto nandiṃ pajahati, virāgānupassanāya virajjanto rāgaṃ pajahati. Ettāvatā vipassanaṃ niṭṭhapetvā “rāgakkhayā nandikkhayo”ti idha maggaṃ dassetvā “nandirāgakkhayā cittaṃ vimutta”nti phalaṃ dassitanti. Navamadasamāni.

In the ninth and tenth, 'from the destruction of delight, the destruction of lust; from the destruction of lust, the destruction of delight'—this is said because 'delight' and 'lust' are not different in their intrinsic meaning. Alternatively, through the contemplation of disenchantment, one, becoming disenchanted, abandons delight; through the contemplation of dispassion, one, becoming dispassionate, abandons lust. Having by this much brought insight to completion, the path is shown here with 'from the destruction of lust, the destruction of delight,' and the fruit is shown with 'through the destruction of delight and lust, the mind is liberated.' Ninth and tenth.

Attadīpavaggo pañcamo.

The Chapter on the Island for Oneself, the fifth.

Mūlapaṇṇāsako samatto.

The Mūlapaṇṇāsaka is completed.

6. Upayavaggo

6. The Chapter on Approach

1. Upayasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Upaya Sutta

53. Upayavaggassa [V 2.0241] paṭhame upayoti taṇhāmānadiṭṭhivasena pañcakkhandhe upagato. Viññāṇanti kammaviññāṇaṃ. Āpajjeyyāti kammaṃ javāpetvā paṭisandhiākaḍḍhanasamatthatāya vuddhiādīni āpajjeyya. Viññāṇupayanti padassa aggahaṇe kāraṇaṃ vuttameva. Vocchijjatārammaṇanti [P 2.0272] paṭisandhiākaḍḍhanasamatthatāya abhāvena ārammaṇaṃ vocchijjati. Patiṭṭhā viññāṇassāti kammaviññāṇassa patiṭṭhā na hoti. Tadappatiṭṭhitanti taṃ appatiṭṭhitaṃ. Anabhisaṅkhacca vimuttanti paṭisandhiṃ anabhisaṅkharitvā vimuttaṃ. Paṭhamaṃ.

53. In the first of the Upayavagga: 'one who approaches' is one who approaches the five aggregates by way of craving, conceit, and view. 'Consciousness' means kamma-consciousness. 'It would attain' means that having impelled kamma, due to its capacity to draw forth rebirth-linking, it would attain growth, and so on. The reason for not taking the term 'consciousness as an approach' has already been stated. 'The object would be cut off' means that the object is cut off due to the absence of the capacity to draw forth rebirth-linking. 'An establishment for consciousness' means there is no establishment for kamma-consciousness. 'That being unestablished' means that consciousness is unestablished. 'Liberated without conditioning' means it is liberated without creating rebirth-linking. First.

2. Bījasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Explanation of the Discourse on Seeds

54. Dutiye bījajātānīti bījāni. Mūlabījanti vacaṃ vacattaṃ haliddaṃ siṅgiveranti evamādi. Khandhabījanti assattho nigrodhoti evamādi. Phalubījanti [M 2.0251] ucchu veḷu naḷoti evamādi. Aggabījanti ajjukaṃ phaṇijjakanti evamādi. Bījabījanti sālivīhiādi pubbaṇṇañceva muggamāsādi aparaṇṇañca. Akhaṇḍānīti abhinnāni. Bhinnakālato paṭṭhāya bījaṃ bījatthāya na upakappati. Apūtikānīti udakatemanena apūtikāni. Pūtibījañhi bījatthāya na upakappati. Avātātapahatānīti vātena ca ātapena ca na hatāni, nirojataṃ na pāpitāni. Nirojañhi kasaṭaṃ bījaṃ bījatthāya na upakappati. Sārādānīti gahitasārāni patiṭṭhitasārāni. Nissārañhi bījaṃ bījatthāya na upakappati. Sukhasayitānīti cattāro māse koṭṭhe pakkhittaniyāmeneva sukhaṃ sayitāni. Pathavīti heṭṭhā patiṭṭhānapathavī. Āpoti uparisnehanaāpo. Catasso viññāṇaṭṭhitiyoti kammaviññāṇassa ārammaṇabhūtā rūpādayo cattāro khandhā. Te hi ārammaṇavasena patiṭṭhābhūtattā pathavīdhātusadisā. Nandirāgo sinehanaṭṭhena āpodhātusadiso. Viññāṇaṃ sāhāranti sappaccayaṃ kammaviññāṇaṃ. Tañhi bījaṃ viya pathaviyaṃ ārammaṇapathaviyaṃ viruhati. Dutiyaṃ.

54. In the second (discourse), 'seed-types' means seeds. 'Root-seeds' are such as sweet flag (vaca), vacatta, turmeric, and ginger. 'Stem-seeds' are such as the assattha tree and the banyan tree. 'Joint-seeds' are such as sugarcane, bamboo, and reeds. 'Tip-seeds' are such as ajjuka and phaṇijjaka. 'Seed-seeds' are early grains such as Sāli and Vīhi rice, and later grains (pulses) such as green gram and black gram. 'Unbroken' means not split. From the time it is broken, a seed is not suitable for its purpose as a seed. 'Not rotten' means not rotted by being soaked in water. Indeed, a rotten seed is not suitable for its purpose as a seed. 'Not damaged by wind and sun' means not destroyed by wind and sun, not brought to a state of being without nutritive essence. Indeed, a seed without nutritive essence, which is mere chaff, is not suitable for its purpose as a seed. 'Possessing essence' means having taken in and established its essence. Indeed, a seed without essence is not suitable for its purpose as a seed. 'Well-lain' means having lain comfortably in a granary for three or four months, simply by virtue of having been placed there. 'Earth' is the earth below that serves as a foundation. 'Water' is the water from above that moistens. 'The four stations of consciousness' are the four aggregates, beginning with form, which are the object of kamma-consciousness. Indeed, because they are a foundation by way of being an object, they are like the earth element. 'Delight and lust', because of their moistening nature, are like the water element. 'Consciousness with its nutriment' means kamma-consciousness together with its conditions. For that kamma-consciousness, like a seed sprouting in the physical earth, sprouts in the earth that is its object. The second.

3. Udānasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Explanation of the Udāna Sutta

55. Tatiye [V 2.0242][P 2.0273] udānaṃ udānesīti balavasomanassasamuṭṭhānaṃ udānaṃ udāhari. Kiṃ nissāya panesa bhagavato uppannoti. Sāsanassa niyyānikabhāvaṃ. Kathaṃ? Evaṃ kirassa ahosi, “tayome upanissayā – dānūpanissayo sīlūpanissayo bhāvanūpanissayo cā”ti. Tesu dānasīlūpanissayā dubbalā, bhāvanūpanissayo balavā. Dānasīlūpanissayā hi tayo magge ca phalāni ca pāpenti, bhāvanūpanissayo arahattaṃ pāpeti. Iti dubbalūpanissaye patiṭṭhito bhikkhu ghaṭento vāyamanto pañcorambhāgiyāni bandhanāni chetvā tīṇi maggaphalāni nibbatteti, “aho sāsanaṃ niyyānika”nti āvajjentassa ayaṃ udapādi.

55. In the third (sutta), 'he uttered an inspired utterance' means he uttered an inspired utterance that arose from strong joy. Relying on what, then, did this arise for the Blessed One? Relying on the Dispensation's quality of leading out [of saṃsāra]. How so? It is said that it occurred to him thus: 'These are my three supports: the support of generosity, the support of virtue, and the support of development.' Among these, the supports of generosity and virtue are weak, while the support of development is strong. For the supports of generosity and virtue lead one to the three paths and fruits, whereas the support of development leads one to Arahantship. Thus, a monk established on the weak supports, by striving and making effort, having cut off the five lower fetters, brings into being the three paths and fruits. While reflecting, 'Ah, the Dispensation is indeed a means of release!' this inspired utterance arose.

Tattha “dubbalūpanissaye ṭhatvā ghaṭamāno tīṇi maggaphalāni pāpuṇātī”ti imassatthassāvibhāvanatthaṃ milakattherassa vatthu veditabbaṃ – so kira gihikāle pāṇātipātakammena jīvikaṃ kappento araññe pāsasatañceva adūhalasatañca yojesi. Athekadivasaṃ aṅgārapakkamaṃsaṃ khāditvā pāsaṭṭhānesu vicaranto pipāsābhibhūto ekassa araññavāsittherassa [M 2.0252] vihāraṃ gantvā therassa caṅkamantassa avidūre ṭhitaṃ pānīyaghaṭaṃ vivari, hatthatemanamattampi udakaṃ nāddasa. So kujjhitvā āha – “bhikkhu, bhikkhu tumhe gahapatikehi dinnaṃ bhuñjitvā bhuñjitvā supatha, pānīyaghaṭe añjalimattampi udakaṃ na ṭhapetha, na yuttameta”nti. Thero “mayā pānīyaghaṭo pūretvā ṭhapito, kiṃ nu kho eta”nti? Gantvā olokento paripuṇṇaghaṭaṃ disvā pānīyasaṅkhaṃ pūretvā adāsi. So dvattisaṅkhapūraṃ pivitvā cintesi – “evaṃ pūritaghaṭo nāma mama kammaṃ āgamma tattakapālo viya jāto. Kiṃ nu kho anāgate attabhāve bhavissatī”ti? Saṃviggacitto dhanuṃ chaḍḍetvā, “pabbājetha maṃ, bhante”ti āha. Thero tacapañcakakammaṭṭhānaṃ ācikkhitvā taṃ pabbājesi.

Herein, to clarify the meaning of 'by striving while established on the weak support, one attains the three paths and fruits,' the story of the Elder Milaka should be known. It is said that in his household life, he made a living by killing living beings, and in the forest he set up a hundred snares and a hundred stone-press traps. Then one day, after eating meat cooked on charcoal, he was wandering around the places where the snares were set. Overcome by thirst, he went to the dwelling of a certain forest-dwelling elder. He opened a water pot placed not far from where the elder was doing walking meditation, but he did not see even enough water to wet his hand. Angered, he said: 'Monk, monk! You eat and eat what is given by householders and then you just sleep. You do not even keep a handful of water in the water pot. This is not right!' The Elder thought, 'I filled the water pot and placed it here. What could this be?' Going to look, he saw the pot was full, so he filled a conch shell with water and gave it to him. After drinking thirty-two conch-shellfuls, he thought: 'This pot, though it was full, became like a red-hot iron bowl on account of my kamma. What on earth will become of me in a future existence?' With a mind struck by spiritual urgency, he threw down his bow and said, 'Venerable sir, please ordain me!' The Elder taught him the meditation subject of the five parts ending with the skin and ordained him.

Tassa samaṇadhammaṃ karontassa bahūnaṃ migasūkarānaṃ māritaṭṭhānaṃ pāsaadūhalānañca yojitaṭṭhānaṃ upaṭṭhāti. Taṃ anussarato sarīre dāho uppajjati, kūṭagoṇo viya kammaṭṭhānampi vīthiṃ na paṭipajjati. So “kiṃ karissāmi bhikkhubhāvenā”ti[P 2.0274]? Anabhiratiyā pīḷito therassa santikaṃ gantvā vanditvā āha – “na sakkomi, bhante, samaṇadhammaṃ kātu”nti. Atha naṃ thero “hatthakammaṃ karohī”ti āha. So “sādhu, bhante”ti vatvā udumbarādayo allarukkhe chinditvā [V 2.0243] mahantaṃ rāsiṃ katvā, “idāni kiṃ karomī”ti pucchi? Jhāpehi nanti. So catūsu disāsu aggiṃ datvā jhāpetuṃ asakkonto, “bhante, na sakkomī”ti āha. Thero “tena hi apehī”ti pathaviṃ dvidhā katvā avīcito khajjopanakamattaṃ aggiṃ nīharitvā tattha pakkhipi. So tāva mahantaṃ rāsiṃ sukkhapaṇṇaṃ viya khaṇena jhāpesi. Athassa thero avīciṃ dassetvā, “sace vibbhamissasi, ettha paccissasī”ti saṃvegaṃ janesi. So avīcidassanato paṭṭhāya pavedhamāno “niyyānikaṃ, bhante, buddhasāsana”nti pucchi, āmāvusoti. Bhante, buddhasāsanassa niyyānikatte sati milako attamokkhaṃ karissati, mā cintayitthāti. Tato paṭṭhāya samaṇadhammaṃ karoti ghaṭeti, tassa vattapaṭivattaṃ pūreti, niddāya bādhayamānāya tintaṃ palālaṃ sīse ṭhapetvā pāde soṇḍiyaṃ otāretvā nisīdati. So ekadivasaṃ pānīyaṃ [M 2.0253] parissāvetvā ghaṭaṃ ūrumhi ṭhapetvā udakamaṇikānaṃ pacchedaṃ āgamayamāno aṭṭhāsi. Atha kho thero sāmaṇerassa imaṃ uddesaṃ deti –

As he practiced the duties of a monk, the places where he had killed many deer and boars, and where he had set snares and traps, came to his mind. Remembering them, a burning sensation arose in his body, and like a deceitful ox, his meditation subject did not proceed on the straight path. He thought, 'What shall I do with this state of being a bhikkhu?' Afflicted by discontent, he went to the Elder, paid homage, and said, 'Venerable sir, I cannot practice the duties of a monk.' The Elder then said to him, 'First, do some manual labor.' He replied, 'Very well, venerable sir,' and after cutting down moist trees such as the udumbara and making a large pile, he asked, 'Now what shall I do?' 'Burn it,' said the Elder. Unable to set it on fire after applying fire in the four directions, he said, 'Venerable sir, I cannot.' The Elder said, 'Then step aside.' Splitting the earth in two, he drew out a fire the size of a firefly from the Avīci hell and threw it there. In an instant, it burned up that great pile as if it were dry leaves. Then the Elder showed him the Avīci hell and instilled a sense of spiritual urgency in him, saying, 'If you disrobe, you will be cooked here.' From the moment he saw the Avīci hell, he stood trembling and asked, 'Venerable sir, is the Buddha's teaching a means of deliverance?' 'Yes, friend,' replied the Elder. 'Venerable sir,' he said, 'since the Buddha's teaching is a means of deliverance, Milaka will work out his own liberation. Do not be concerned.' From then on, he practiced the duties of a monk; he strove and made an effort. He fulfilled his duties major and minor for the Elder. When drowsiness oppressed him, he would place damp straw on his head, immerse his feet in a pool of water, and remain seated. One day, after filtering drinking water, he placed the water pot on his thigh and stood waiting for the dripping of the water to cease. Then the Elder gave this recitation passage to the novice:

“Uṭṭhānavato satīmato,

Sucikammassa nisammakārino;

Saññatassa dhammajīvino,

Appamattassa yasobhivaḍḍhatī”ti. (dha. pa. 24);

For one who is energetic, mindful, pure in conduct, discerning, self-restrained, living by the Dhamma, and heedful—glory increases.

So catuppadikampi taṃ gāthaṃ attaniyeva upanesi – “uṭṭhānavatā nāma mādisena bhavitabbaṃ. Satimatāpi mādiseneva…pe… appamattenapi mādiseneva bhavitabba”nti. Evaṃ taṃ gāthaṃ attani upanetvā tasmiṃyeva padavāre ṭhito pañcorambhāgiyāni saṃyojanāni chinditvā anāgāmiphale patiṭṭhāya haṭṭhatuṭṭho –

He applied that four-lined verse to himself: 'Indeed, one like me must be energetic. One like me must be mindful... One like me must be heedful.' Thus applying that verse to himself, standing right there at that very spot, he cut off the five lower fetters and became established in the fruit of non-returning, thrilled and elated.

“Allaṃ palālapuñjāhaṃ, sīsenādāya caṅkamiṃ;

Pattosmi tatiyaṃ ṭhānaṃ, ettha me natthi saṃsayo”ti. –

I carried a heap of wet straw on my head and walked. I have attained the third stage; of this there is no doubt.

Imaṃ udānagāthaṃ āha. Evaṃ [P 2.0275] dubbalūpanissaye ṭhito ghaṭento vāyamanto pañcorambhāgiyāni saṃyojanāni chinditvā tīṇi maggaphalāni nibbatteti. Tenāha bhagavā – “no cassaṃ, no ca [V 2.0244] me siyā, nābhavissa, na me bhavissatīti evaṃ adhimuccamāno bhikkhu chindeyya orambhāgiyāni saṃyojanānī”ti.

He spoke this inspired verse. Thus, one established on a weak support, by striving and making an effort, cuts off the five lower fetters and brings forth the three paths and fruits. Therefore the Blessed One said: 'A bhikkhu who is resolved thus, "It would not be, it would not be for me; it will not be, it will not be for me," would cut off the lower fetters.'

Tattha no cassaṃ, no ca me siyāti sace ahaṃ na bhaveyyaṃ, mama parikkhāropi na bhaveyya. Sace vā pana me atīte kammābhisaṅkhāro nābhavissa, idaṃ me etarahi khandhapañcakaṃ na bhaveyya. Nābhavissa, na me bhavissatīti idāni pana tathā parakkamissāmi, yathā me āyatiṃ khandhābhinibbattako kammasaṅkhāro na bhavissati, tasmiṃ asati āyatiṃ paṭisandhi nāma na me bhavissati. Evaṃ adhimuccamānoti evaṃ adhimuccanto bhikkhu dubbalūpanissaye ṭhito pañcorambhāgiyāni saṃyojanāni chindeyya. Evaṃ vutteti evaṃ sāsanassa niyyānikabhāvaṃ āvajjentena bhagavatā [M 2.0254] imasmiṃ udāne vutte. Rūpaṃ vibhavissatīti rūpaṃ bhijjissati. Rūpassa vibhavāti vibhavadassanena sahavipassanena. Sahavipassanakā hi cattāro maggā rūpādīnaṃ vibhavadassanaṃ nāma. Taṃ sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Evaṃ adhimuccamāno, bhante, bhikkhu chindeyyāti, bhante, evaṃ adhimuccamāno bhikkhu chindeyyeva pañcorambhāgiyāni saṃyojanāni. Kasmā na chindissatīti?

Herein, 'I might not be, it might not be mine' means: If I were not to exist, my requisites too would not exist. Or, if in the past there had been no kamma-formations for me, this present fivefold aggregate would not exist for me. 'There will not be, there will not be for me' means: Now, however, I will strive in such a way that in the future there will be no kamma-formation producing aggregates for me; and when that is absent, in the future there will be no rebirth for me. 'By resolving thus' means: A monk established on a weak foundation, resolving in this way, could cut off the five lower fetters. 'When this was said' refers to when this inspired utterance was spoken by the Blessed One as he was reflecting on the liberating nature of the Dispensation. 'Form will cease to be' means form will break up. 'The cessation of form' means by seeing the cessation, together with insight. For the four paths accompanied by insight are called 'the seeing of the cessation' of form and so forth. This was said in reference to that. 'By resolving thus, venerable sir, a monk would cut off' means: Venerable sir, a monk resolving thus would indeed cut off the five lower fetters. Why would he not cut them off?

Idāni upari maggaphalaṃ pucchanto kathaṃ pana, bhantetiādimāha. Tattha anantarāti dve anantarāni āsannānantarañca dūrānantarañca. Vipassanā maggassa āsannānantaraṃ nāma, phalassa dūrānantaraṃ nāma. Taṃ sandhāya “kathaṃ pana, bhante, jānato kathaṃ passato vipassanānantarā ‘āsavānaṃ [P 2.0276] khayo’ti saṅkhaṃ gataṃ arahattaphalaṃ hotī”ti pucchati. Atasitāyeti atasitabbe abhāyitabbe ṭhānamhi. Tāsaṃ āpajjatīti bhayaṃ āpajjati. Tāso hesoti yā esā “no cassaṃ, no ca me siyā”ti evaṃ pavattā dubbalavipassanā, sā yasmā attasinehaṃ pariyādātuṃ na sakkoti, tasmā assutavato puthujjanassa tāso nāma hoti. So hi “idānāhaṃ ucchijjissāmi, na dāni kiñci bhavissāmī”ti attānaṃ papāte patantaṃ viya passati aññataro brāhmaṇo viya. Lohapāsādassa kira heṭṭhā tipiṭakacūḷanāgatthero tilakkhaṇāhataṃ dhammaṃ parivatteti. Atha aññatarassa brāhmaṇassa ekamante ṭhatvā dhammaṃ suṇantassa saṅkhārā suññato upaṭṭhahiṃsu. So papāte patanto viya hutvā vivaṭadvārena tato palāyitvā gehaṃ pavisitvā, puttaṃ ure sayāpetvā, “tāta, sakyasamayaṃ āvajjento manamhi naṭṭho”ti āha. Na heso bhikkhu tāsoti esā evaṃ pavattā balavavipassanā sutavato ariyasāvakassa na tāso [V 2.0245] nāma hoti. Na hi tassa evaṃ hoti “ahaṃ ucchijjissāmī”ti vā “vinassissāmī”ti vāti. Evaṃ pana hoti “saṅkhārāva uppajjanti, saṅkhārāva nirujjhantī”ti. Tatiyaṃ.

Now, wishing to ask about the higher path and fruit, he said, 'How then, venerable sir?' and so forth. Therein, 'immediately after' is twofold: proximate immediacy and distant immediacy. Insight is called the proximate immediacy of the path; it is called the distant immediacy of the fruit. With reference to this, he asks: 'How then, venerable sir, for one who knows, for one who sees, does the fruit of Arahantship—which has come to be reckoned as "the destruction of the taints"—arise with insight as its distant immediacy?' 'Not to be dreaded' means in a place not to be dreaded, not to be feared. 'He falls into dread' means he falls into fear. 'This is dread' refers to that weak insight which proceeds thus: 'I might not be, it might not be mine.' Because it is unable to completely consume self-love, it is therefore called 'dread' for an uninstructed worldling. For he sees himself as if falling from a precipice, thinking, 'Now I will be annihilated! Now I will become nothing!'—like a certain brahmin. It is said that beneath the Lohapāsāda, the Elder Tipiṭaka Cūḷanāga was reviewing the Dhamma marked by the three characteristics. Then, for a certain brahmin who was standing to one side listening to the Dhamma, the formations appeared as empty. Becoming as if he were falling from a precipice, he fled from there through an open door, entered his house, placed his son on his chest, and said, 'Dear son, while reflecting on the doctrine of the Sakyan sage, my mind was almost lost.' 'This is not dread for a monk': this powerful insight, thus arisen, is not called 'dread' for a learned noble disciple. For it does not occur to him, 'I will be annihilated,' or 'I will perish.' Rather, it occurs to him thus: 'Formations alone arise; formations alone cease.' The third.

4. Upādānaparipavattasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Discourse on the Revolution of Clinging

56. Catutthe catuparivaṭṭanti ekekasmiṃ khandhe catunnaṃ parivaṭṭanavasena. Rūpaṃ abbhaññāsinti rūpaṃ dukkhasaccanti abhiññāsiṃ. Evaṃ sabbapadesu catusaccavaseneva [M 2.0255] attho veditabbo. Āhārasamudayāti ettha sacchandarāgo kabaḷīkārāhāro āhāro nāma. Paṭipannāti sīlato paṭṭhāya yāva arahattamaggā paṭipannā honti. Gādhantīti patiṭṭhahanti. Ettāvatā sekkhabhūmiṃ kathetvā idāni asekkhabhūmiṃ kathento ye ca kho keci, bhikkhavetiādimāha. Suvimuttāti arahattaphalavimuttiyā suṭṭhu vimuttā. Kevalinoti sakalino [P 2.0277] katasabbakiccā. Vaṭṭaṃ tesaṃ natthi paññāpanāyāti yena te avasiṭṭhena vaṭṭena paññāpeyyuṃ, taṃ nesaṃ vaṭṭaṃ natthi paññāpanāya. Atha vā vaṭṭanti kāraṇaṃ, paññāpanāya kāraṇaṃ natthīti. Ettāvatā asekkhabhūmivāro kathito. Catutthaṃ.

56. In the fourth, 'fourfold revolution' is by way of revolving the four truths in each aggregate. 'I directly knew form' means: I directly knew form as the Noble Truth of Suffering. In this way, the meaning should be understood in all instances by way of the Four Truths. In 'with the origin of nutriment,' physical food together with desire and lust is called nutriment. 'Those who are practicing' are those practicing from the level of virtue up to the path of Arahantship. 'They find a footing' means they are established. Having thus far spoken of the plane of the trainee, now, speaking of the plane of the non-trainee, he said, 'And whoever, monks...' and so on. 'Well-liberated' means they are thoroughly liberated by the liberation of the fruit of Arahantship. 'Accomplished' means they are complete, having done all that was to be done. 'There is no round for their designation' means that remaining round by which they might be designated does not exist for their designation. Or alternatively, 'round' means cause; there is no cause for designation. Thus far, the section on the plane of the non-trainee has been spoken. The fourth.

5. Sattaṭṭhānasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Discourse on the Seven Bases

57. Pañcame sattaṭṭhānakusaloti sattasu okāsesu cheko. Vusitavāti vusitavāso. Uttamapurisoti seṭṭhapuriso. Sesamettha vuttanayeneva veditabbaṃ. Idaṃ pana suttaṃ ussadanandiyañceva palobhanīyañcāti veditabbaṃ. Yathā hi rājā vijitasaṅgāmo saṅgāme vijayino yodhe uccaṭṭhāne ṭhapetvā tesaṃ sakkāraṃ karoti. Kiṃ kāraṇā? Etesaṃ sakkāraṃ disvā sesāpi sūrā bhavituṃ maññissantīti, evameva bhagavā appameyyaṃ kālaṃ pāramiyo pūretvā mahābodhimaṇḍe kilesamāravijayaṃ katvā sabbaññutaṃ patto sāvatthiyaṃ jetavanamahāvihāre nisīditvā imaṃ suttaṃ desento khīṇāsave ukkhipitvā thomesi vaṇṇesi. Kiṃ kāraṇā? Evaṃ avasesā sekkhapuggalā arahattaphalaṃ pattabbaṃ maññissantīti. Evametaṃ suttaṃ khīṇāsavānaṃ ukkhipitvā pasaṃsitattā ussadanandiyaṃ, sekkhānaṃ palobhitattā palobhanīyanti veditabbaṃ.

57. In the fifth, 'skilled in the seven bases' means one who is expert in seven areas. 'One who has lived the life' means one who has lived the noble life. 'Supreme person' means a most excellent person. The rest here should be understood in the way already stated. This discourse, however, should be understood as being both exalting and gladdening, and enticing. For it is just as a king, victorious in battle, honors his victorious warriors by placing them in high positions. For what reason? Thinking, 'Seeing the honor given to these, the others too will resolve to become heroes.' In the very same way, the Blessed One—having fulfilled the perfections for an incalculable period of time, achieved victory over the defilements at the seat of great awakening, and attained omniscience—while seated in the Jetavana Mahāvihāra at Sāvatthī and teaching this discourse, exalted, praised, and extolled those whose taints are destroyed. For what reason? Thinking, 'In this way, the remaining trainees will consider the fruit of Arahantship as something to be attained.' Thus, this discourse, because it exalts and praises those whose taints are destroyed, is exalting and gladdening; and because it entices the trainees, it is to be understood as enticing.

Evaṃ [V 2.0246] kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu sattaṭṭhānakusalo hotīti ettāvatā cettha maggaphalapaccavekkhaṇavasena desanaṃ niṭṭhapetvāpi puna kathañca, bhikkhave, bhikkhu tividhūpaparikkhī hotīti idaṃ “khīṇāsavo yasmiṃ ārammaṇe satatavihārena viharati, taṃ satto vā puggalo vā na hoti, dhātuādimattameva pana hotī”ti evaṃ khīṇāsavassa satatavihārañca, “imesu dhammesu kammaṃ katvā ayaṃ āgato”ti āgamanīyapaṭipadañca dassetuṃ vuttaṃ. Tattha dhātuso upaparikkhatīti dhātusabhāvena passati oloketi. Sesapadadvayepi eseva nayo. Pañcamaṃ.

With the words, "Thus, monks, a bhikkhu is skilled in the seven bases," although the discourse was concluded at this point by way of reviewing the path and fruit, this was said again: "And how, monks, is a bhikkhu one who investigates in three ways?" This was said to show both the constant dwelling of one whose cankers are destroyed—thus: "In whatever object one whose cankers are destroyed dwells with constant abiding, that object is not a being or a person, but is merely the elements, and so on"—and the path by which one has come—thus: "Having completed the work with regard to these phenomena, this one has come." Therein, "he investigates as to the elements" (dhātuso upaparikkhati) means he sees, he observes, with the nature of elements. This same method should be understood for the other two phrases as well. The fifth is finished.

6. Sammāsambuddhasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Explanation of the Discourse on the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One

58. Chaṭṭhe [M 2.0256][P 2.0278] ko adhippayāsoti ko adhikapayogo. Anuppannassāti imañhi maggaṃ kassapasammāsambuddho uppādesi, antarā añño satthā uppādetuṃ nāsakkhi, iti bhagavā anuppannassa maggassa uppādetā nāma. Nagaropamasmiñhi avaḷañjanaṭṭhānesu purāṇamaggo jāto, idha avattamānaṭṭhena anuppannamaggo nāma. Asañjātassāti tasseva vevacanaṃ. Anakkhātassāti akathitassa. Maggaṃ jānātīti maggaññū. Maggaṃ viditaṃ pākaṭaṃ akāsīti maggavidū. Magge ca amagge ca kovidoti maggakovido. Maggānugāti maggaṃ anugacchantā. Pacchā samannāgatāti ahaṃ paṭhamaṃ gato, sāvakā pacchā samannāgatā. Chaṭṭhaṃ.

58. In the sixth discourse, 'what is the meaning?' means what is the further application. Regarding 'of the unarisen': because Kassapa, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened Buddha, caused this path to arise, and no other teacher in between was able to cause it to arise, the Blessed One is therefore called the originator of the unarisen path. Indeed, in the simile of the city, the ancient path arose in unused places; here, on account of its not existing, it is called the unarisen path. 'Of the unproduced' is a synonym for that very term. Regarding 'of the undeclared': of what was not taught. Because he knows the path, he is a Path-Knower (Maggaññū). Because he made the path known and manifest, he is a Path-Discoverer (Maggavidū). Because he is skilled in the path and in what is not the path, he is a Path-Expert (Maggakovido). Regarding 'followers of the path': they follow the path. Regarding 'endowed with it afterwards': I went first; the disciples were endowed with it afterwards. The sixth is finished.

7. Anattalakkhaṇasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Explanation of the Anattalakkhaṇa Sutta

59. Sattame pañcavaggiyeti aññāsi koṇḍaññattherādike pañca jane purāṇupaṭṭhāke. Āmantesīti āsāḷhipuṇṇamadivase dhammacakkappavattanato paṭṭhāya anukkamena sotāpattiphale patiṭṭhite “idāni nesaṃ āsavakkhayāya dhammaṃ desessāmī”ti pañcamiyaṃ pakkhassa āmantesi. Etadavocāti etaṃ “rūpaṃ, bhikkhave, anattā”tiādinayappavattaṃ anattalakkhaṇasuttaṃ avoca. Tattha anattāti pubbe vuttehi catūhi kāraṇehi anattā. Taṃ kiṃ maññatha, bhikkhaveti idaṃ kasmā āraddhaṃ? Ettakena ṭhānena anattalakkhaṇameva kathitaṃ, na aniccadukkhalakkhaṇāni, idāni tāni dassetvā samodhānetvā tīṇipi lakkhaṇāni dassetuṃ idamāraddhanti veditabbaṃ. Tasmāti yasmā ime pañcakkhandhā aniccā dukkhā anattā, tasmā. Yaṃkiñci rūpantiādīsu vitthārakathā [V 2.0247] visuddhimagge paññābhāvanādhikāre khandhaniddese vuttāva. Sesaṃ sabbattha vuttānusāreneva veditabbaṃ. Imasmiṃ pana sutte anattalakkhaṇameva kathitanti. Sattamaṃ.

59. In the seventh discourse, regarding 'the group of five': the five persons headed by the Elder Aññāsi Koṇḍañña, who were his former attendants. Regarding 'he addressed them': when they were established in the fruit of stream-entry in due course, starting from the setting in motion of the Wheel of Dhamma on the full-moon day of Āsāḷhī, he thought, 'Now I will teach the Dhamma to them for the destruction of the taints,' and addressed them on the fifth day of the waning fortnight. Regarding 'he said this': he spoke this Anattalakkhaṇa Sutta, which proceeds in the manner beginning, 'Form, O monks, is not-self.' Therein, regarding 'not-self': it is not-self for the four reasons stated previously. Why was this, beginning 'What do you think, O monks?', undertaken? By this much, only the characteristic of not-self has been taught, not the characteristics of impermanence and suffering. It should be understood that this was undertaken now in order to show those other characteristics, and, having combined them, to show all three characteristics. Regarding 'therefore': because these five aggregates are impermanent, suffering, and not-self, therefore... Regarding the passage beginning 'Whatever form...', the detailed explanation has been stated in the Visuddhimagga, in the section on the Development of Wisdom, in the Exposition of the Aggregates. The remainder should be understood everywhere in accordance with what has already been said. However, in this discourse, only the characteristic of not-self is taught. The seventh is finished.

8. Mahālisuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Mahāli Sutta

60. Aṭṭhame ekantadukkhantiādīni dhātusaṃyutte vuttanayāneva. Aṭṭhamaṃ.

60. In the eighth, the terms beginning with 'entirely suffering' are just in the manner explained in the Dhātusaṃyutta. The eighth is finished.

9. Ādittasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Āditta Sutta

61. Navame [M 2.0257][P 2.0279] ādittanti ekādasahi aggīhi ādittaṃ pajjalitaṃ. Iti dvīsupi imesu suttesu dukkhalakkhaṇameva kathitaṃ. Navamaṃ.

61. In the ninth, 'ablaze' means ablaze and flaming with the eleven fires. Thus, in both these suttas, only the characteristic of suffering is taught. The ninth is finished.

10. Niruttipathasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Niruttipatha Sutta

62. Dasame niruttiyova niruttipathā, atha vā niruttiyo ca tā niruttivasena viññātabbānaṃ atthānaṃ pathattā pathā cāti niruttipathā. Sesapadadvayepi eseva nayo. Tīṇipi cetāni aññamaññavevacanānevāti veditabbāni. Asaṃkiṇṇāti avijahitā, “ko imehi attho”ti vatvā achaḍḍitā. Asaṃkiṇṇapubbāti atītepi na jahitapubbā. Na saṃkīyantīti etarahipi “kimetehī”ti na chaḍḍīyanti. Na saṃkīyissantīti anāgatepi na chaḍḍīyissanti. Appaṭikuṭṭhāti appaṭibāhitā. Atītanti attano sabhāvaṃ bhaṅgameva vā atikkantaṃ. Niruddhanti desantaraṃ asaṅkamitvā tattheva niruddhaṃ vūpasantaṃ. Vipariṇatanti vipariṇāmaṃ gataṃ naṭṭhaṃ. Ajātanti anuppannaṃ. Apātubhūtanti apākaṭībhūtaṃ.

62. In the tenth, the very expressions are the paths of language. Or alternatively, because those expressions, by means of expression, are the way to the meanings that are to be understood, they are also paths; thus they are 'paths of language.' This same method should be understood for the remaining two terms. And these three should be understood as synonyms for one another. 'Unconfused' means not abandoned; not discarded by saying, “What is the use of these?” 'Not confused previously' means not abandoned previously in the past. 'They are not being confused' means even now they are not discarded by saying, “What to do with these?” 'They will not be confused' means in the future, too, they will not be discarded. 'Unblamed' means not rejected. 'Past' means having passed beyond its own nature or having passed beyond dissolution. 'Ceased' means having ceased and been pacified right there without moving to another place. 'Changed' means having undergone change, destroyed. 'Unborn' means not arisen. 'Unmanifest' means not become apparent.

Ukkalāti ukkalajanapadavāsino. Vassabhaññāti vasso ca bhañño ca. Dvepi hi te mūladiṭṭhigatikā. Ahetukavādātiādīsu “natthi hetu natthi paccayo”ti gahitattā ahetukavādā. “Karoto na karīyati pāpa”nti gahitattā akiriyavādā. “Natthi dinna”ntiādigahaṇato natthikavādā. Tattha ime dve janā, tisso diṭṭhiyo, kiṃ ekekassa diyaḍḍhā hotīti? Na tathā, yathā pana eko bhikkhu paṭipāṭiyā cattāripi jhānāni nibbatteti[V 2.0248], evamettha ekeko tissopi diṭṭhiyo nibbattesīti veditabbo. “Natthi hetu natthi paccayo”ti punappunaṃ āvajjentassa āharantassa abhinandantassa assādentassa maggadassanaṃ viya hoti. So micchattaniyāmaṃ okkamati, so ekantakāḷakoti [P 2.0280] vuccati. Yathā pana ahetukadiṭṭhiyaṃ, evaṃ “karoto na karīyati pāpaṃ, natthi dinna”nti imesupi ṭhānesu micchattaniyāmaṃ okkamati.

Ukkalā means the inhabitants of the Ukkala country. Vassabhaññā means Vassa and Bhañña. For both of them were holders of a root-view. In the passages beginning with 'proponents of non-causality,' because they held the view that 'there is no cause, there is no condition,' they are proponents of non-causality. Because they held the view that 'for one who acts, evil is not done,' they are proponents of non-action. Because they held such views as 'there is nothing in what is given,' they are proponents of nihilism. In this regard, there are these two people and three views. Does each one have one and a half? It is not so. But just as one monk successively produces all four jhānas, so here it should be understood that each one produced all three views. For one who repeatedly adverts to, brings to mind, delights in, and savors the idea that 'there is no cause, there is no condition,' it becomes like seeing the path. He enters the fixed course of wrongness; he is called one who is utterly black. And just as with the view of non-causality, so too in these cases—'for one who acts, evil is not done,' and 'there is nothing in what is given'—he enters the fixed course of wrongness.

Na garahitabbaṃ nappaṭikkositabbaṃ amaññiṃsūti ettha “yadetaṃ atītaṃ nāma, nayidaṃ atītaṃ, idamassa anāgataṃ vā paccuppannaṃ vā”ti vadanto garahati nāma. Tattha dosaṃ dassetvā “kiṃ iminā garahitenā”ti? Vadanto [M 2.0258] paṭikkosati nāma. Ime pana niruttipathe tepi accantakāḷakā diṭṭhigatikā na garahitabbe na paṭikkositabbe maññiṃsu. Atītaṃ pana atītameva, anāgataṃ anāgatameva, paccuppannaṃ paccuppannameva kathayiṃsu. Nindāghaṭṭanabyārosaupārambhabhayāti viññūnaṃ santikā nindābhayena ca ghaṭṭanabhayena ca dosāropanabhayena ca upārambhabhayena ca. Iti imasmiṃ sutte catubhūmikakhandhānaṃ paṇṇatti kathitāti. Dasamaṃ.

Here, in the passage 'they thought it should not be blamed, not rejected,' one who says, 'This which is called the past is not the past; this could be the future or the present,' is called one who blames. Having shown a fault in that, one who says, 'What is the use of this that has been blamed?' is called one who rejects. But regarding these paths of expression, even they, the holders of the view who were utterly black, did not think they should be blamed or rejected. Instead, they spoke of the past as just the past, the future as just the future, and the present as just the present. 'Due to fear of blame, striking, animosity, and reproach' means from the wise, due to fear of blame, fear of striking, fear of having faults imputed, and fear of reproach. Thus, in this sutta, the concept concerning the aggregates of the four planes has been taught. The tenth.

Upayavaggo chaṭṭho.

The Upaya Chapter, the Sixth.

7. Arahantavaggo

7. The Arahant Chapter

1. Upādiyamānasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Sutta on Clinging

63. Arahantavaggassa paṭhame upādiyamānoti taṇhāmānadiṭṭhivasena gaṇhamāno. Baddho mārassāti mārassa pāsena baddho nāma. Mutto pāpimatoti pāpimato pāsena mutto nāma hoti. Paṭhamaṃ.

63. In the first [sutta] of the Arahant Chapter, 'clinging' means grasping by way of craving, conceit, and views. 'Bound by Māra' means bound by Māra's snare. 'Freed from the Evil One' means freed from the Evil One's snare. The first.

2. Maññamānasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Sutta on Conceiving

64. Dutiye maññamānoti taṇhāmānadiṭṭhimaññanāhi maññamāno. Dutiyaṃ.

64. In the second [sutta], 'conceiving' means one who conceives by means of the conceivings of craving, conceit, and views. The second.

3. Abhinandamānasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Sutta on Delighting

65. Tatiye [V 2.0249] abhinandamānoti taṇhāmānadiṭṭhiabhinandanāhiyeva abhinandamāno. Tatiyaṃ.

65. In the third [sutta], 'delighting' means one who delights precisely by means of the delights of craving, conceit, and views. The third.

4-5. Aniccasuttādivaṇṇanā

4-5. Commentary on the Suttas on Impermanence, etc.

66-68. Catutthe chandoti taṇhāchando. Pañcamachaṭṭhesupi eseva nayo. Catutthādīni.

In the fourth [sutta], 'desire' means the desire that is craving. In the fifth and sixth as well, this is the method. The fourth, etc.

7. Anattaniyasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Sutta on Not Belonging to a Self

69. Sattame [P 2.0281] anattaniyanti na attano santakaṃ, attano parikkhārabhāvena suññatanti attho. Sattamaṃ.

69. In the seventh [sutta], 'not belonging to a self' means not one's own possession; the meaning is that it is devoid of being one's own requisite. The seventh.

8-10. Rajanīyasaṇṭhitasuttādivaṇṇanā

8-10. Commentary on the Suttas on What is Established as Provocative of Lust, etc.

70-72. Aṭṭhame [M 2.0259] rajanīyasaṇṭhitanti rajanīyena ākārena saṇṭhitaṃ, rāgassa paccayabhāvena ṭhitanti attho. Navamadasamāni rāhulasaṃyutte vuttanayeneva veditabbānīti. Aṭṭhamādīni.

In the eighth [sutta], 'what is established as provocative of lust' means established in a manner that is provocative of lust; the meaning is that it stands as a condition for passion. The ninth and tenth should be understood by the same method as spoken of in the Rāhula Saṃyutta. The eighth, etc.

Arahantavaggo sattamo.

The Arahant Chapter, the Seventh.

8. Khajjanīyavaggo

8. The Chapter on Being Devoured

1-3. Assādasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-3. Commentary on the Suttas on Gratification, etc.

73-75. Khajjanīyavaggassa [V 2.0250] ādito tīsu suttesu catusaccameva kathitaṃ. Paṭhamādīni.

In the first three suttas at the beginning of the Chapter on Being Devoured, only the Four Noble Truths are taught. The first, etc.

4. Arahantasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Sutta on Arahants

76. Catutthe yāvatā, bhikkhave, sattāvāsāti, bhikkhave, yattakā sattāvāsā nāma atthi. Yāvatā bhavagganti yattakaṃ bhavaggaṃ nāma atthi. Ete aggā ete seṭṭhāti ete aggabhūtā ceva seṭṭhabhūtā ca. Yadidaṃ arahantoti ye ime arahanto nāma. Idampi suttaṃ purimanayeneva ussadanandiyañca palobhanīyañcāti veditabbaṃ.

76. In the fourth [sutta], 'Monks, as far as there are abodes of beings' means, monks, as many as there are so-called abodes of beings. 'As far as the peak of existence' means as much as there is a so-called peak of existence. 'These are the highest, these are the best' means these have become the highest and have become the best. 'That is, the arahants' means those who are called arahants. This sutta, too, should be understood by the same method as the previous one, as being both uplifting and alluring.

Athāparaṃ etadavocāti tadatthaparidīpanāhi ceva visesatthaparidīpanāhi ca gāthāhi etaṃ “sukhino vata arahanto”tiādivacanaṃ avoca. Tattha sukhinoti jhānasukhena maggasukhena phalasukhena ca sukhitā. Taṇhā tesaṃ na vijjatīti tesaṃ apāyadukkhajanikā taṇhā na vajjati. Evaṃ te imassapi taṇhāmūlakassa abhāvena sukhitāva. Asmimāno samucchinnoti navavidho asmimāno [P 2.0282] arahattamaggena samucchinno. Mohajālaṃ padālitanti ñāṇena avijjājālaṃ phālitaṃ.

'Then he spoke this further' means he spoke this statement, 'Happy, indeed, are the arahants,' etc., with verses that explain its meaning and explain its special meaning. Therein, 'happy' means they are happy with the bliss of jhāna, the bliss of the path, and the bliss of the fruit. 'Craving is not found in them' means that for them, the craving that generates the suffering of the lower realms does not exist. Thus, they are happy through the absence of this suffering which has craving as its root. 'The conceit "I am" is eradicated' means the nine-fold conceit 'I am' is eradicated by the path of Arahantship. 'The net of delusion is torn apart' means the net of ignorance is split by knowledge.

Anejanti ejāsaṅkhātāya taṇhāya pahānabhūtaṃ arahattaṃ. Anupalittāti taṇhādiṭṭhilepehi alittā. Brahmabhūtāti seṭṭhabhūtā. Pariññāyāti tīhi pariññāhi parijānitvā. Sattasaddhammagocarāti saddhā hirī [M 2.0260] ottappaṃ bāhusaccaṃ āraddhavīriyatā upaṭṭhitassatitā paññāti ime satta saddhammā gocaro etesanti sattasaddhammagocarā.

'Unagitated' means Arahantship, which is the state of having abandoned craving, which is known as agitation. 'Unsmeared' means not smeared by the smears of craving and views. 'Become Brahma' means having become the best. 'Having fully understood' means having fully understood by means of the three kinds of full understanding. 'Whose pasture is the seven true qualities' means these seven true qualities—faith, moral shame, moral dread, great learning, aroused energy, established mindfulness, and wisdom—are their pasture; therefore, they are those 'whose pasture is the seven true qualities'.

Sattaratanasampannāti sattahi bojjhaṅgaratanehi samannāgatā. Anuvicarantīti lokiyamahājanāpi [V 2.0251] anuvicarantiyeva. Idha pana khīṇāsavānaṃ nirāsaṅkacāro nāma gahito. Tenevāha “pahīnabhayabheravā”ti. Tattha bhayaṃ bhayameva, bheravaṃ balavabhayaṃ. Dasahaṅgehi sampannāti asekkhehi dasahi aṅgehi samannāgatā. Mahānāgāti catūhi kāraṇehi mahānāgā. Samāhitāti upacārappanāhi samāhitā. Taṇhā tesaṃ na vijjatīti “ūno loko atitto taṇhādāsoti kho, mahārāja, tena bhagavatā”ti (ma. ni. 2.305) evaṃ vuttā dāsakārikā taṇhāpi tesaṃ natthi. Iminā khīṇāsavānaṃ bhujissabhāvaṃ dasseti.

'Endowed with the seven treasures' means endowed with the seven treasures of the enlightenment factors. 'They wander'—worldly people also just wander. Here, however, the fearless conduct of those whose taints are destroyed is taken. Therefore, he said, 'they have abandoned fear and terror.' Therein, 'fear' is just fear; 'terror' is strong fear. 'Endowed with the ten factors' means endowed with the ten factors of the non-learner. 'Great Nāgas' means they are great nāgas for four reasons. 'Composed' means composed through access and absorption concentration. 'Craving is not found in them' means that for them, even the enslaving craving spoken of by the Blessed One thus, 'The world, great king, is deficient, insatiable, a slave to craving,' does not exist. By this, he shows the state of freedom of those whose taints are destroyed.

Asekhañāṇanti arahattaphalañāṇaṃ. Antimoyaṃ samussayoti pacchimo ayaṃ attabhāvo. Yo sāro brahmacariyassāti sāro nāma phalaṃ. Tasmiṃ aparapaccayāti tasmiṃ ariyaphale, na aññaṃ pattiyāyanti, paccakkhatova paṭivijjhitvā ṭhitā. Vidhāsu na vikampantīti tīsu mānakoṭṭhāsesu na vikampanti. Dantabhūminti arahattaṃ. Vijitāvinoti rāgādayo vijetvā ṭhitā.

'The knowledge of the non-learner' means the knowledge of the fruit of Arahantship. 'This is his final frame' means this is the final state of existence. 'Which is the essence of the spiritual life'—the essence is called the fruit. 'In that, they are not dependent on another' means, in that noble fruit, they do not have to trust another; they stand having penetrated it by direct experience. 'They do not tremble in the threefold ways' means they do not tremble in the three divisions of conceit. 'The ground of the tamed' means Arahantship. 'The victors' means they stand having conquered passion and so on.

Uddhantiādīsu uddhaṃ vuccati kesamatthako, apācīnaṃ pādatalaṃ, tiriyaṃ vemajjhaṃ. Uddhaṃ vā atītaṃ, apācīnaṃ anāgataṃ, tiriyaṃ paccuppannaṃ. Uddhaṃ vā vuccati devaloko, apācīnaṃ apāyaloko, tiriyaṃ manussaloko. Nandī tesaṃ na vijjatīti etesu ṭhānesu saṅkhepato vā atītānāgatapaccuppannesu khandhesu tesaṃ taṇhā natthi. Idha vaṭṭamūlakataṇhāya [P 2.0283] abhāvo dassito. Buddhāti catunnaṃ saccānaṃ buddhattā buddhā.

In the phrases beginning with 'upward,' 'upward' refers to the crown of the head, 'downward' to the sole of the foot, and 'across' to the middle. Alternatively, 'upward' is the past, 'downward' is the future, and 'across' is the present. Or, 'upward' refers to the world of the devas, 'downward' to the world of woe, and 'across' to the human world. 'Delight is not found in them' means that in these domains, or in brief, in the aggregates of the past, future, and present, they have no craving. Here, the absence of craving, the root of the round of existence, is shown. 'Buddhas' means they are Buddhas because of having understood the four truths.

Idaṃ panettha sīhanādasamodhānaṃ – “vimuttisukhenamhā sukhitā, dukkhajanikā no taṇhā pahīnā, pañcakkhandhā pariññātā, dāsakārikataṇhā ceva vaṭṭamūlikataṇhā ca pahīnā, anuttaramhā asadisā, catunnaṃ saccānaṃ buddhattā buddhā”ti bhavapiṭṭhe ṭhatvā abhītanādasaṅkhātaṃ sīhanādaṃ nadanti khīṇāsavāti. Catutthaṃ.

Herein, this is the summary of the lion's roar: Those whose taints are destroyed, standing on the summit of existence, roar the lion's roar, known as the fearless proclamation, saying: 'We are happy with the bliss of liberation. Our craving that generates suffering is abandoned. The five aggregates are fully understood. The craving that enslaves and the craving that is the root of the round of existence are both abandoned. We are unsurpassed, without an equal. Because of having understood the four truths, we are Buddhas.' The fourth.

5. Dutiyaarahantasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Explanation of the Second Arahant Sutta.

77. Pañcamaṃ [M 2.0261] vinā gāthāhi suddhikameva katvā kathiyamānaṃ bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayena vuttaṃ. Pañcamaṃ.

77. The fifth discourse was expounded as a pure prose text, without verses, spoken according to the inclination of those who were to understand. The fifth.

6. Sīhasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Explanation of the Lion Sutta.

78. Chaṭṭhe [V 2.0252] sīhoti cattāro sīhā – tiṇasīho, kāḷasīho, paṇḍusīho, kesarasīhoti. Tesu tiṇasīho kapotavaṇṇagāvisadiso tiṇabhakkho ca hoti. Kāḷasīho kāḷagāvisadiso tiṇabhakkhoyeva. Paṇḍusīho paṇḍupalāsavaṇṇagāvisadiso maṃsabhakkho. Kesarasīho lākhārasaparikammakateneva mukhena agganaṅguṭṭhena catūhi ca pādapariyantehi samannāgato, matthakatopissa paṭṭhāya lākhātūlikāya katvā viya tisso rājiyo piṭṭhimajjhena gantvā antarasatthimhi dakkhiṇāvattā hutvā ṭhitā, khandhe panassa satasahassagghanikakambalaparikkhepo viya kesarabhāro hoti, avasesaṭṭhānaṃ parisuddhaṃ sālipiṭṭhasaṅkhacuṇṇapicuvaṇṇaṃ hoti. Imesu catūsu sīhesu ayaṃ kesarasīho idha adhippeto.

78. In the sixth discourse, there are four kinds of lions: the grass lion, the black lion, the tawny lion, and the maned lion. Among these, the grass lion is like a dove-colored cow and feeds on grass. The black lion is like a black cow and also feeds on grass. The tawny lion is like a cow the color of a withered leaf and feeds on flesh. The maned lion is endowed with a mouth, a tail-tip, and four foot-tips as if treated with red lac. From the top of its head, as if drawn with a lac-dyed brush, three stripes run down the middle of its back, turning to the right between its thighs and remaining there. On its shoulders, it has a mass of mane like a shawl worth a hundred thousand, while the rest of its body is pure, having the color of rice flour, conch powder, or cotton. Among these four lions, it is this maned lion that is intended here.

Migarājāti migagaṇassa rājā. Āsayāti vasanaṭṭhānato suvaṇṇaguhato vā rajatamaṇiphalikamanosilāguhato vā nikkhamatīti vuttaṃ hoti. Nikkhamamāno panesa catūhi kāraṇehi nikkhamati andhakārapīḷito vā ālokatthāya, uccārapassāvapīḷito vā tesaṃ vissajjanatthāya, jighacchāpīḷito vā gocaratthāya, sambhavapīḷito vā assaddhammapaṭisevanatthāya. Idha pana gocaratthāya nikkhantoti adhippeto.

The term 'migarājā' means king of the herd of beasts. 'Āsayā' means that he emerges from his dwelling place, whether from a golden cave or a cave of silver, gems, crystal, or realgar—this is what is said. When emerging, he comes out for four reasons: oppressed by darkness, for the sake of light; oppressed by excrement and urine, for the purpose of discharging them; oppressed by hunger, for the sake of finding food; or oppressed by sexual urge, for the purpose of sexual intercourse. Here, however, it is intended that he emerges for the sake of finding food.

Vijambhatīti [P 2.0284] suvaṇṇatale vā rajatamaṇiphalikamanosilātalānaṃ vā aññatarasmiṃ dve pacchimapāde samaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā purimapāde purato pasāretvā sarīrassa pacchābhāgaṃ ākaḍḍhitvā purimabhāgaṃ abhiharitvā piṭṭhiṃ nāmetvā gīvaṃ ukkhipitvā asanisaddaṃ karonto viya nāsapuṭāni pothetvā sarīralaggaṃ rajaṃ vidhunanto vijambhati. Vijambhanabhūmiyañca pana taruṇavacchako viya aparāparaṃ javati. Javato panassa sarīraṃ andhakāre paribbhamantaṃ alātaṃ viya khāyati.

He stretches by placing both hind feet evenly on a golden surface, or on a surface of silver, gems, crystal, or realgar; extending the front feet forward, pulling the back part of the body forward, bringing the front part forward, bending the back, lifting the neck, making a sound like thunder, striking his nostrils, and shaking off the dust clinging to his body. On the stretching ground, he runs to and fro like a young calf. And as he runs, his body appears in the darkness like a whirling firebrand.

Anuviloketīti [M 2.0262] kasmā anuviloketi? Parānuddayatāya. Tasmiṃ kira sīhanādaṃ nadante papātāvāṭādīsu visamaṭṭhānesu carantā hatthigokaṇṇamahiṃsādayo pāṇā papātepi āvāṭepi patanti, tesaṃ anuddayāya anuviloketi. Kiṃ panassa luddakammassa paramaṃsakhādino anuddayā nāma atthīti? Āma atthi. Tathā hesa “kiṃ me bahūhi ghātitehī”ti? Attano gocaratthāyapi [V 2.0253] khuddake pāṇe na gaṇhāti, evaṃ anuddayaṃ karoti. Vuttampicetaṃ – “māhaṃ kho khuddake pāṇe visamagate saṅghātaṃ āpādesi”nti (a. ni. 10.21).

He looks around. Why does he look around? Out of compassion for others. It is said that when he roars his lion's roar, creatures like elephants, wild oxen, and buffaloes, wandering in uneven places such as cliffs and pits, may fall into those cliffs or pits. He looks around out of compassion for them. But does this cruel one, a devourer of the flesh of others, really have compassion? Yes, he does. For he thinks, "What benefit is there for me from the many creatures killed?" For the sake of his own food, he does not seize small creatures; thus he shows compassion. And this was said: "May I not bring to destruction small creatures who move in uneven places." (AN 10.21)

Sīhanādaṃ nadatīti tikkhattuṃ tāva abhītanādaṃ nadati. Evañca panassa vijambhanabhūmiyaṃ ṭhatvā nadantassa saddo samantā tiyojanapadesaṃ ekaninnādaṃ karoti, tamassa ninnādaṃ sutvā tiyojanabbhantaragatā dvipadacatuppadagaṇā yathāṭhāne ṭhātuṃ na sakkonti. Gocarāya pakkamatīti āhāratthāya gacchati. Kathaṃ? So hi vijambhanabhūmiyaṃ ṭhatvā dakkhiṇato vā vāmato vā uppatanto usabhamattaṃ ṭhānaṃ gaṇhāti, uddhaṃ uppatanto cattāripi aṭṭhapi usabhāni uppatati, samaṭṭhāne ujukaṃ pakkhandanto soḷasausabhamattampi vīsatiusabhamattampi ṭhānaṃ pakkhandati, thalā vā pabbatā vā pakkhandanto saṭṭhiusabhamattampi asītiusabhamattampi ṭhānaṃ pakkhandati, antarāmagge rukkhaṃ vā pabbataṃ vā disvā taṃ pariharanto vāmato vā dakkhiṇato vā, usabhamattampi apakkamati. Tatiyaṃ pana sīhanādaṃ naditvā teneva [P 2.0285] saddhiṃ tiyojane ṭhāne paññāyati. Tiyojanaṃ gantvā nivattitvā ṭhito attanova nādassa anunādaṃ suṇāti. Evaṃ sīghena javena pakkamatīti.

He roars a lion's roar, first uttering a fearless roar three times. Standing in his stretching ground, his roar makes the three-yojana area all around resound as one. Hearing this roar, all two-legged and four-legged creatures within that three-yojana range cannot remain in their places. He proceeds to his feeding ground—that is, he goes for the sake of food. How? Standing in his stretching ground, when he leaps to the right or left, he covers a distance of one usabha. Leaping upward, he leaps four or even eight usabhas. Charging straight ahead on level ground, he charges a distance of sixteen or even twenty usabhas. Charging from a high place or a mountain, he charges a distance of sixty or even eighty usabhas. If he sees a tree or mountain in his path, he avoids it by moving to the left or right by as much as an usabha. After uttering the third lion's roar, he travels with that very sound for three yojanas. Having gone three yojanas and returned, standing in his original place, he still hears the echo of his own roar. Thus, he proceeds with swift speed.

Yebhuyyenāti pāyena. Bhayaṃ saṃvegaṃ santāsanti sabbaṃ cittutrāsasseva nāmaṃ. Sīhassa hi saddaṃ sutvā bahū sattā bhāyanti, appakā na bhāyanti. Ke pana teti? Samasīho hatthājānīyo assājānīyo usabhājānīyo purisājānīyo khīṇāsavoti. Kasmā panete na bhāyantīti? Samasīho nāma “jātigottakulasūrabhāvehi samānosmī”ti na bhāyati, hatthājānīyādayo attano sakkāyadiṭṭhibalavatāya na bhāyanti, khīṇāsavo sakkāyadiṭṭhipahīnattā na bhāyati.

'Mostly' (yebhuyyena) means for the most part. Fear, agitation, and terror are all names for mental fright. Upon hearing a lion's roar, many beings are afraid, but a few are not. Who are they? A lion of equal stature, a noble elephant, a noble horse, a noble bull, a noble man, and one whose cankers are destroyed. Why do they not fear? A lion of equal stature does not fear, thinking, "I am his equal in birth, lineage, clan, and courage." The noble elephants and others do not fear due to the strength of their identity-view. One whose cankers are destroyed does not fear because identity-view has been abandoned.

Bilāsayāti [M 2.0263] bile sayantā bilavāsino ahinakulagodhādayo. Dakāsayāti udakavāsino macchakacchapādayo. Vanāsayāti vanavāsino hatthiassagokaṇṇamigādayo. Pavisantīti “idāni āgantvā gaṇhissatī”ti maggaṃ olokentāva pavisanti. Daḷhehīti thirehi. Varattehīti cammarajjūhi. Mahiddhikotiādīsu vijambhanabhūmiyaṃ ṭhatvā dakkhiṇapassādīhi usabhamattaṃ, ujukaṃ vīsatiusabhamattādilaṅghanavasena mahiddhikatā, sesamigānaṃ adhipatibhāvena mahesakkhatā, samantā tiyojane saddaṃ sutvā palāyantānaṃ vasena mahānubhāvatā veditabbā.

'Hole-dwellers' (bilāsaya) means those who dwell in holes, such as snakes, mongooses, and iguanas. 'Water-dwellers' (dakāsaya) means those who dwell in water, such as fish and turtles. 'Forest-dwellers' (vanāsaya) means those who dwell in forests, such as elephants, horses, gokaṇṇa deer, and other deer. 'They enter' (pavisanti) means they enter while watching the path, thinking, "Now he will come and seize us." 'With strong' (daḷhehi) means with firm ones. 'With ropes' (varattehi) means with leather thongs. Regarding 'of great power' (mahiddhiko) and so forth: his great power is to be understood by his leaping a distance of one usabha to the right side and so forth, and by leaping straight ahead a distance of twenty usabhas and so forth; his great authority (mahesakkhatā) is by being the overlord of the remaining animals; his great might (mahānubhāvatā) is to be understood by the fact that creatures flee upon hearing his sound from three yojanas around.

Evameva [V 2.0254] khoti bhagavā tesu tesu suttesu tathā tathā attānaṃ kathesi. “Sīhoti kho, bhikkhave, tathāgatassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ arahato sammāsambuddhassā”ti (a. ni. 5.99; 10.21) imasmiṃ tāva sutte sīhasadisaṃ attānaṃ kathesi. “Bhisakko sallakattoti kho, sunakkhatta, tathāgatassetaṃ adhivacana”nti (ma. ni. 3.65) imasmiṃ vejjasadisaṃ. “Brāhmaṇoti, bhikkhave, tathāgatassetaṃ adhivacana”nti (a. ni. 8.85) imasmiṃ brāhmaṇasadisaṃ. “Puriso maggakusaloti kho, tissa, tathāgatassetaṃ adhivacana”nti (saṃ. ni. 3.84) imasmiṃ maggadesakapurisasadisaṃ. “Rājāhamasmi selā”ti (su. ni. 559) imasmiṃ rājasadisaṃ. “Sīhoti kho tathāgatassetaṃ [P 2.0286] adhivacana”nti (a. ni. 5.99; 10.21) imasmiṃ pana sutte sīhasadisameva katvā attānaṃ kathento evamāha.

Even so, indeed, in those various discourses the Blessed One spoke of himself in those various ways. 'A lion, monks, is a designation for the Tathāgata, the Arahant, the Perfectly Enlightened One.' (A. Ni. 5.99; 10.21) In this discourse, to begin with, he spoke of himself as being like a lion. 'A physician, a surgeon, Sunakkhatta, is a designation for the Tathāgata.' (Ma. Ni. 3.65) In this discourse, as being like a physician. 'A brahmin, monks, is a designation for the Tathāgata.' (A. Ni. 8.85) In this discourse, as being like a brahmin. 'A man skilled in the path, Tissa, is a designation for the Tathāgata.' (Saṃ. Ni. 3.84) In this discourse, as being like a man who shows the path. 'I am a king, Sela.' (Su. Ni. 559) In this discourse, as being like a king. 'A lion is a designation for the Tathāgata.' (A. Ni. 5.99; 10.21) However, in this discourse, speaking of himself by making himself only like a lion, he spoke thus.

Tatrāyaṃ sadisatā – sīhassa kañcanaguhādīsu vasanakālo viya hi tathāgatassa dīpaṅkarapādamūle katābhinīhārassa aparimitakālaṃ pāramiyo pūretvā pacchimabhave paṭisandhiggahaṇena ceva mātukucchito nikkhamanena ca dasasahassilokadhātuṃ kampetvā vuddhimanvāya dibbasampattisadisaṃ sampattiṃ anubhavamānassa tīsu pāsādesu nivāsakālo daṭṭhabbo. Sīhassa kañcanaguhādito nikkhantakālo viya tathāgatassa ekūnatiṃse saṃvacchare vivaṭena dvārena kaṇḍakaṃ āruyha channasahāyassa nikkhamitvā tīṇi rajjāni atikkamitvā anomānadītīre brahmunā dinnāni kāsāyāni [M 2.0264] paridahitvā pabbajitassa sattame divase rājagahaṃ gantvā tattha piṇḍāya caritvā paṇḍavagiripabbhāre katabhattakiccassa sammāsambodhiṃ patvā, paṭhamameva magadharaṭṭhaṃ āgamanatthāya yāva rañño paṭiññādānakālo.

Herein is the similarity: Just as the time of a lion's dwelling in golden caves and other such places, so should be regarded the time of the Tathāgata's dwelling in the three palaces—he who made his aspiration at the feet of Dīpaṅkara, fulfilled the perfections for an immeasurable time, and in his final existence, by taking rebirth and by emerging from his mother’s womb, shook the ten-thousandfold world-system, and having reached maturity, experienced a prosperity similar to divine fortune. Just as the time of a lion's departure from its golden cave and other such places, so should be regarded the period concerning the Tathāgata: who at twenty-nine years of age, with the gate opened, mounted Kaṇṭhaka, departed with Channa as his companion, crossed three kingdoms, donned the ochre robes given by a Brahmā on the bank of the Anomā river, and went forth; who on the seventh day went to Rājagaha, wandered there for alms, and performed his meal duties on the Paṇḍava mountain slope; and who, having attained perfect enlightenment, the time up until the giving of the promise to the king for his first visit to the country of Magadha.

Sīhassa vijambhanakālo viya tathāgatassa dinnapaṭiññassa āḷārakālāmaupasaṅkamanaṃ ādiṃ katvā yāva sujātāya dinnapāyāsassa ekūnapaṇṇāsāya piṇḍehi paribhuttakālo veditabbo. Sīhassa kesaravidhunanaṃ viya sāyanhasamaye sottiyena dinnā aṭṭha tiṇamuṭṭhiyo gahetvā dasasahassacakkavāḷadevatāhi thomiyamānassa gandhādīhi pūjiyamānassa tikkhattuṃ bodhiṃ padakkhiṇaṃ katvā bodhimaṇḍaṃ āruyha cuddasahatthubbedhe ṭhāne tiṇasantharaṃ santharitvā caturaṅgavīriyaṃ adhiṭṭhāya nisinnassa taṃkhaṇaṃyeva mārabalaṃ vidhamitvā tīsu yāmesu tisso vijjā visodhetvā anulomapaṭilomaṃ paṭiccasamuppādamahāsamuddaṃ yamakañāṇamanthanena manthentassa sabbaññutaññāṇe paṭividdhe tadanubhāvena dasasahassilokadhātukampanaṃ veditabbaṃ.

Just as the time of a lion's stretching, so should be understood the time concerning the Tathāgata, who had given his promise, beginning with his approach to Āḷāra Kālāma up until the time he consumed the milk-rice given by Sujātā in forty-nine morsels. Just as a lion shakes its mane, so should be understood the trembling of the ten-thousandfold world-system, which occurred by the power of the Tathāgata's omniscience upon its penetration, while he—in the evening, having received eight handfuls of grass from Sotthiya, being praised by the deities of the ten-thousandfold world-system, being honored with perfumes and such, having circumambulated the Bodhi tree three times, having ascended to the Bodhi-seat, having spread a grass mat on a place fourteen cubits in extent, having resolved upon the fourfold exertion and sat down—at that very moment dispelled Māra’s forces, purified the threefold knowledge in the three watches of the night, and churned the great ocean of dependent origination in forward and reverse order with the churning-stick of the twin knowledge.

Sīhassa [V 2.0255] catuddisāvilokanaṃ viya paṭividdhasabbaññutaññāṇassa sattasattāhaṃ bodhimaṇḍe viharitvā paribhuttamadhupiṇḍikāhārassa ajapālanigrodhamūle mahābrahmuno [P 2.0287] dhammadesanāyācanaṃ paṭiggahetvā tattha viharantassa ekādasame divase “sve āsāḷhipuṇṇamā bhavissatī”ti paccūsasamaye “kassa nu kho ahaṃ paṭhamaṃ dhammaṃ deseyya”nti? Āḷārudakānaṃ kālaṅkatabhāvaṃ ñatvā dhammadesanatthāya pañcavaggiyānaṃ olokanaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Sīhassa gocaratthāya tiyojanaṃ gamanakālo viya attano pattacīvaramādāya “pañcavaggiyānaṃ dhammacakkaṃ pavattessāmī”ti pacchābhatte ajapālanigrodhato vuṭṭhitassa aṭṭhārasayojanamaggaṃ gamanakālo.

Just as a lion surveys the four directions, so should be seen the looking towards the group of five for the purpose of teaching the Dhamma by the Tathāgata—who had penetrated omniscience, dwelt at the Bodhi-seat for seven weeks, partaken of the honey-cake meal, accepted the request of Mahābrahmā for a Dhamma teaching at the foot of the Ajapāla Nigrodha tree; and who, while dwelling there on the eleventh day at dawn, thought, 'Tomorrow will be the full moon of Āsāḷha. To whom should I first teach the Dhamma?', and having known that Āḷāra and Udaka had passed away. Just as the time of a lion’s going three yojanas for food, so should be understood the time of the Tathāgata's journey of eighteen yojanas, when, having taken his own bowl and robe, he arose from the Ajapāla Nigrodha after the meal with the thought, 'I shall set in motion the Wheel of Dhamma for the group of five.'

Sīhanādakālo viya tathāgatassa aṭṭhārasayojanamaggaṃ gantvā pañcavaggiye saññāpetvā acalapallaṅke nisinnassa dasahi cakkavāḷasahassehi sannipatitena devagaṇena parivutassa “dveme, bhikkhave, antā pabbajitena na sevitabbā”tiādinā (saṃ. ni. 5.1081; mahāva. 13) nayena dhammacakkappavattanakālo veditabbo. Imasmiñca pana pade desiyamāne tathāgatasīhassa dhammaghoso heṭṭhā avīciṃ upari bhavaggaṃ gahetvā dasasahassilokadhātuṃ [M 2.0265] paṭicchādesi. Sīhassa saddena khuddakapāṇānaṃ santāsaṃ āpajjanakālo viya tathāgatassa tīṇi lakkhaṇāni dīpetvā cattāri saccāni soḷasahākārehi saṭṭhiyā ca nayasahassehi vibhajitvā dhammaṃ kathentassa dīghāyukadevatānaṃ ñāṇasantāsassa uppattikālo veditabbo.

Like the time of a lion's roar, so should be understood the time of the Tathāgata's setting in motion the Wheel of Dhamma, when, having traveled the eighteen-yojana road, having convinced the group of five, he sat on the unshakeable seat, surrounded by a host of deities gathered from the ten thousand world-systems, and taught according to the method beginning with, 'Monks, these two extremes should not be pursued by one who has gone forth…' And while this passage was being taught, the Dhamma-sound of the Tathāgata-lion, reaching from Avīci below to the summit of existence above, covered the ten-thousandfold world-system. Just as the time when small creatures are struck with terror by a lion's roar, so should be understood the time of the arising of the terror of knowledge in the long-lived deities, when the Tathāgata was teaching the Dhamma, explaining the three characteristics and analyzing the four truths with their sixteen aspects and sixty thousand methods.

Yadāti yasmiṃ kāle. Tathāgatoti aṭṭhahi kāraṇehi bhagavā tathāgato – tathā āgatoti tathāgato, tathā gatoti tathāgato, tathalakkhaṇaṃ āgatoti tathāgato, tathadhamme yāthāvato abhisambuddhoti tathāgato, tathadassitāya tathāgato, tathāvāditāya tathāgato, tathākāritāya tathāgato. Abhibhavanaṭṭhena tathāgatoti. Tesaṃ vitthāro brahmajālavaṇṇanāyampi (dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.7) mūlapariyāyavaṇṇanāyampi (ma. ni. aṭṭha. 1.12) vuttoyeva. Loketi sattaloke. Uppajjatīti abhinīhārato paṭṭhāya yāva bodhipallaṅkā vā arahattamaggañāṇā vā uppajjati nāma[P 2.0288], arahattaphale pana patte uppanno nāma. Arahaṃ sammāsambuddhotiādīni visuddhimagge buddhānussatiniddese vitthāritāni.

Yadāti: at which time. Tathāgatoti: The Blessed One is the Tathāgata for eight reasons—He has come in the same way (tathā āgato), thus He is the Tathāgata; He has gone in the same way (tathā gato), thus He is the Tathāgata; He has arrived at the true characteristic (tathalakkhaṇaṃ āgato), thus He is the Tathāgata; He has directly awakened to the true Dhammas as they really are (tathadhamme yāthāvato abhisambuddho), thus He is the Tathāgata; because of seeing truly (tathadassitāya), He is the Tathāgata; because of speaking truly (tathāvāditāya), He is the Tathāgata; because of acting in accordance with his speech (tathākāritāya), He is the Tathāgata; and because of overcoming (abhibhavanaṭṭhena), He is the Tathāgata. Their detailed explanation has already been stated in the commentary on the Brahmajāla Sutta and in the commentary on the Mūlapariyāya Sutta. Loketi: in the world of beings. Uppajjatīti: from the moment of the great aspiration up to the Bodhi-seat or the knowledge of the path of Arahantship, it is called ‘arises’; but when the fruit of Arahantship is attained, it is called ‘arisen’. Arahaṃ sammāsambuddhoti and so forth are explained in detail in the Visuddhimagga in the section on the recollection of the Buddha.

Iti rūpanti idaṃ rūpaṃ ettakaṃ rūpaṃ, na ito bhiyyo rūpaṃ atthīti. Ettāvatā sabhāvato sarasato pariyantato paricchedato paricchindanato yāvatā cattāro ca mahābhūtā catunnañca [V 2.0256] mahābhūtānaṃ upādāyarūpaṃ, taṃ sabbaṃ dassitaṃ hoti. Iti rūpassa samudayoti ayaṃ rūpassa samudayo nāma. Ettāvatā hi “āhārasamudayo rūpasamudayo”tiādi sabbaṃ dassitaṃ hoti. Iti rūpassa atthaṅgamoti ayaṃ rūpassa atthaṅgamo. Imināpi “āhāranirodhā rūpanirodho”tiādi sabbaṃ dassitaṃ hoti. Iti vedanātiādīsupi eseva nayo.

Iti rūpanti: 'This is form, this much is form, there is no more form than this.' By this much, all that is form—namely, the four great elements and the form derived from the four great elements—is shown by its own nature, by its essential function, by its limit, by its delimitation, and by its circumscription. Iti rūpassa samudayoti: 'This is the origin of form.' By this much, all is shown, such as 'with the origin of nutriment, there is the origin of form.' Iti rūpassa atthaṅgamoti: 'This is the passing away of form.' By this too, all is shown, such as 'with the cessation of nutriment, there is the cessation of form.' In 'Iti vedanā' and so forth, this same method is to be applied.

Vaṇṇavantoti sarīravaṇṇena vaṇṇavanto. Dhammadesanaṃ sutvāti imaṃ pañcasu khandhesu paṇṇāsalakkhaṇapaṭimaṇḍitaṃ tathāgatassa dhammadesanaṃ sutvā. Yebhuyyenāti idha ke ṭhapeti? Ariyasāvake deve. Tesañhi khīṇāsavattā cittutrāsabhayampi na uppajjati, saṃviggassa yoniso padhānena pattabbaṃ [M 2.0266] pattatāya ñāṇasaṃvegopi. Itaresaṃ pana devānaṃ “tāso heso bhikkhū”ti aniccataṃ manasikarontānaṃ cittutrāsabhayampi, balavavipassanākāle ñāṇabhayampi uppajjati. Bhoti dhammālapanamattametaṃ. Sakkāyapariyāpannāti pañcakkhandhapariyāpannā. Iti tesaṃ sammāsambuddhe vaṭṭadosaṃ dassetvā tilakkhaṇāhataṃ katvā dhammaṃ desente ñāṇabhayaṃ nāma okkamati.

Vaṇṇavanto: endowed with the beauty of the body. Dhammadesanaṃ sutvā: having heard this Dhamma teaching of the Tathāgata, which is adorned with the fifty characteristics concerning the five aggregates. Yebhuyyenāti: Who are excluded here? The noble disciple devas. For them, since their taints are destroyed, the fear of mental terror does not arise; and for one who is spiritually agitated, since what is to be attained through proper exertion has been attained, the spiritual agitation of knowledge also does not arise. But for the other devas, while they are reflecting on impermanence, the fear of mental terror arises, and at the time of strong insight, the fear of knowledge also arises. Bhoti: this is merely an address to the Dhamma. Sakkāyapariyāpannāti: included in the five aggregates. Thus, when the Perfectly Enlightened One is teaching the Dhamma to them, having shown the fault of the round of existence and having struck it with the three characteristics, what is called the fear of knowledge descends upon them.

Abhiññāyāti jānitvā. Dhammacakkanti paṭivedhañāṇampi desanāñāṇampi. Paṭivedhañāṇaṃ nāma yena ñāṇena bodhipallaṅke nisinno cattāri saccāni soḷasahākārehi saṭṭhiyā ca nayasahassehi paṭivijjhi. Desanāñāṇaṃ nāma yena ñāṇena tiparivaṭṭaṃ dvādasākāraṃ dhammacakkaṃ pavattesi. Ubhayampi taṃ dasabalassa ure jātañāṇameva. Tesu [P 2.0289] idha desanāñāṇaṃ gahetabbaṃ. Taṃ panesa yāva aṭṭhārasahi brahmakoṭīhi saddhiṃ aññāsikoṇḍaññattherassa sotāpattiphalaṃ uppajjati, tāva pavatteti nāma. Tasmiṃ uppanne pavattitaṃ nāma hotīti veditabbaṃ. Appaṭipuggaloti sadisapuggalarahito. Yasassinoti parivārasampannā. Tādinoti lābhālābhādīhi ekasadisassa. Chaṭṭhaṃ.

Abhiññāyāti: having directly known. Dhammacakkanti: both the knowledge of penetration and the knowledge of teaching. The knowledge of penetration is that knowledge by which, seated on the Bodhi-throne, He penetrated the four truths with sixteen aspects and sixty thousand methods. The knowledge of teaching is that knowledge by which He set rolling the Dhamma-wheel of three turnings and twelve aspects. Both are indeed knowledge born in the breast of the Ten-Powered One. Among these, the knowledge of teaching should be taken here.
