As long as the fruit of stream-entry for the Elder Aññāsikoṇḍañña, together with eighteen crores of Brahmās, is yet to arise, it is said that He 'is setting it rolling'. When that has arisen, it should be understood that it 'has been set rolling'. Appaṭipuggaloti: a person without an equal. Yasassinoti: endowed with a retinue. Tādinoti: of one who is equanimous amidst gain and loss, and so forth. The sixth is finished.

7. Khajjanīyasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Khajjanīya Sutta

79. Sattame pubbenivāsanti na idaṃ abhiññāvasena anussaraṇaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ, vipassanāvasena pana pubbenivāsaṃ anussarante samaṇabrāhmaṇe sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Tenevāha – “sabbete pañcupādānakkhandhe anussaranti, etesaṃ vā aññatara”nti. Abhiññāvasena hi samanussarantassa [V 2.0257] khandhāpi upādānakkhandhāpi khandhapaṭibaddhāpi paṇṇattipi ārammaṇaṃ hotiyeva. Rūpaṃyeva anussaratīti evañhi anussaranto na aññaṃ kiñci sattaṃ vā puggalaṃ vā anussarati, atīte pana niruddhaṃ rūpakkhandhameva anussarati. Vedanādīsupi eseva nayoti. Suññatāpabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.

79. In the seventh (sutta), this phrase 'past abodes' is not stated with reference to recollection by means of supernormal knowledge; rather, it is stated with reference to those ascetics and brahmins who recollect past abodes by means of insight. Therefore, He said: 'All these recollect the five aggregates of clinging, or one of them.' For one who recollects by means of supernormal knowledge, the aggregates, the aggregates of clinging, what is connected with the aggregates, and concepts all indeed become objects. Indeed, one recollecting in this way does not recollect any other being or person; rather, one recollects only the aggregate of form that has ceased in the past. This same method applies to feeling and the others. The section on emptiness is finished.

Idāni suññatāya lakkhaṇaṃ dassetuṃ kiñca, bhikkhave, rūpaṃ vadethātiādimāha. Yathā hi naṭṭhaṃ goṇaṃ pariyesamāno puriso gogaṇe caramāne rattaṃ vā kāḷaṃ vā balībaddaṃ disvāpi na ettakeneva “ayaṃ mayhaṃ goṇo”ti sanniṭṭhānaṃ [M 2.0267] kātuṃ sakkoti. Kasmā? Aññesampi tādisānaṃ atthitāya. Sarīrapadese panassa sattisūlādilakkhaṇaṃ disvā “ayaṃ mayhaṃ santako”ti sanniṭṭhānaṃ hoti, evameva suññatāya kathitāyapi yāva suññatālakkhaṇaṃ na kathīyati, tāva sā akathitāva hoti, lakkhaṇe pana kathite kathitā nāma hoti. Goṇo viya hi suññatā, goṇalakkhaṇaṃ viya suññatālakkhaṇaṃ. Yathā goṇalakkhaṇe asallakkhite goṇo na suṭṭhu sallakkhito hoti, tasmiṃ pana sallakkhite so sallakkhito nāma hoti, evameva suññatālakkhaṇe akathite suññatā akathitāva hoti, tasmiṃ pana kathite sā kathitā nāma [P 2.0290] hotīti suññatālakkhaṇaṃ dassetuṃ kiñca, bhikkhave, rūpaṃ vadethātiādimāha.

Now, to show the characteristic of emptiness, He spoke the passage beginning with: 'And what, monks, do you say is form?' For it is like a man searching for a lost ox who, while it is moving in a herd of cattle, sees a red or black bullock, but cannot conclude just from that, 'This is my ox.' Why? Because others also have similar ones. But when he sees a mark on its body, such as from a spear or a stake, he concludes, 'This is my property.' In the same way, even when emptiness has been spoken of, as long as the characteristic of emptiness is not explained, it remains unexplained. But when the characteristic is explained, it is then truly explained. For emptiness is like the ox, and the characteristic of emptiness is like the mark on the ox. Just as when the mark on the ox is not perceived, the ox is not properly identified, but when that mark is perceived, it is then truly identified; in the same way, when the characteristic of emptiness is not explained, emptiness remains unexplained, but when it is explained, it is then truly explained. Therefore, to show the characteristic of emptiness, He spoke the passage beginning with: 'And what, monks, do you say is form?'

Tattha kiñcāti kāraṇapucchā, kena kāraṇena rūpaṃ vadetha, kena kāraṇenetaṃ rūpaṃ nāmāti attho. Ruppatīti khoti ettha itīti kāraṇuddeso, yasmā ruppati, tasmā rūpanti vuccatīti attho. Ruppatīti kuppati ghaṭṭīyati pīḷīyati, bhijjatīti attho. Sītenapi ruppatītiādīsu sītena tāva ruppanaṃ lokantarikaniraye pākaṭaṃ. Tiṇṇaṃ tiṇṇañhi cakkavāḷānaṃ antare ekeko lokantarikanirayo nāma hoti aṭṭhayojanasahassappamāṇo. Yassa neva heṭṭhā pathavī atthi, na upari candimasūriyadīpamaṇiāloko, niccandhakāro. Tattha nibbattasattānaṃ tigāvuto attabhāvo hoti, te vagguliyo viya pabbatapāde dīghaputhulehi nakhehi laggitvā avaṃsirā olambanti. Yadā saṃsappantā aññamaññassa hatthapāsāgatā honti, atha “bhakkho no laddho”ti? Maññamānā tattha byāvaṭā viparivattitvā lokasandhārake udake patanti, vāte paharantepi madhukaphalāni viya chijjitvā udake patanti, patitamattāva accantakhāre udake tattatele patitapiṭṭhapiṇḍi viya paṭapaṭāyamānā vilīyanti. Evaṃ sītena ruppanaṃ lokantarikaniraye [V 2.0258] pākaṭaṃ. Mahiṃsakaraṭṭhādīsupi himapātasītalesu padesesu etaṃ pākaṭameva. Tattha hi sattā sītena bhinnasarīrā jīvitakkhayampi pāpuṇanti.

Therein, 'kiñca' is a question about the cause. The meaning is: 'For what reason do you say "form"? For what reason is this called "form"?' In the phrase 'ruppatīti kho,' the particle 'iti' indicates the cause. The meaning is: 'Because it is afflicted (ruppati), therefore it is called "form" (rūpa).' 'Ruppati' means it is agitated, struck, oppressed, or broken. In the phrases beginning with 'sītenapi ruppati,' affliction by cold is first of all manifest in the inter-world hell. For between every three world-systems, there is one so-called inter-world hell. There is no earth below, nor is there the light of the moon, sun, lamps, or jewels above; it is perpetual darkness. The bodies of beings reborn there are three gāvutas in size. Like great bats, they cling by their long, thick nails to the foot of the mountain range and hang upside down. When, creeping about, they come within each other's reach, thinking, 'We have found food!', they struggle over it, turn over, and fall into the world-supporting water. Even when struck by the wind, they are cut off like madhuka fruits and fall into the water. The very moment they fall, they dissolve with a crackling sound in the extremely acrid water, like a lump of flour-paste fallen into boiling oil. Thus, affliction by cold is manifest in the inter-world hell. This is also manifest in places like the Mahiṃsaka country and others that are cold due to snowfall. For there, beings, their bodies shattered by the cold, even come to the end of their lives.

Uṇhena ruppanaṃ avīcimahāniraye pākaṭaṃ hoti. Jighacchāya ruppanaṃ pettivisaye ceva dubbhikkhakāle ca pākaṭaṃ. Pipāsāya ruppanaṃ kālakañjikādīsu pākaṭaṃ. Eko kira kālakañjikaasuro pipāsaṃ adhivāsetuṃ asakkonto [M 2.0268] yojanagambhīravitthāraṃ mahāgaṅgaṃ otari, tassa gatagataṭṭhāne udakaṃ chijjati, dhūmo uggacchati, tatte piṭṭhipāsāṇe caṅkamanakālo viya hoti. Tassa udakasaddaṃ sutvā ito cito ca vicarantasseva ratti vibhāyi. Atha naṃ pātova bhikkhācāraṃ [P 2.0291] gacchantā tiṃsamattā piṇḍacārikabhikkhū disvā “ko nāma tvaṃ sappurisā”ti? Pucchiṃsu. “Petohamasmi, bhante”ti. “Kiṃ pariyesasī”ti? “Pānīyaṃ, bhante”ti. “Ayaṃ gaṅgā paripuṇṇā, kiṃ tvaṃ na passasī”ti? “Na upakappati, bhante”ti. Tena hi gaṅgāpiṭṭhe nipajja, mukhe te pānīyaṃ āsiñcissāmā”ti. So vālikāpuḷine uttāno nipajji. Bhikkhū tiṃsamatte patte nīharitvā udakaṃ āharitvā tassa mukhe āsiñciṃsu. Tesaṃ tathā karontānaṃyeva velā upakaṭṭhā jātā. Tato “bhikkhācārakālo amhākaṃ sappurisa, kacci te assādamattā laddhā”ti āhaṃsu. Peto “sace me, bhante, tiṃsamattānaṃ ayyānaṃ tiṃsapattehi āsittaudakato aḍḍhapasatamattampi paragalaṃ gataṃ, petattabhāvato mokkho mā hotū”ti āha. Evaṃ pipāsāya ruppanaṃ pettivisaye pākaṭaṃ.

Affliction by heat is manifest in the great Avīci hell. Affliction by hunger is manifest in the realm of petas and during times of famine. Affliction by thirst is manifest among the Kālakañjika asuras and others. It is said that a certain Kālakañjika asura, unable to endure his thirst, entered the great river Ganges, which was a yojana deep and wide. Wherever he went, the water was cut off, smoke arose, and it became like the time for walking on a hot stone slab. As he was wandering here and there hearing the sound of water, the night dawned. Then, early in the morning, about thirty monks on their alms-round saw him and asked, 'Who are you, good sir?' 'I am a peta, venerable sir.' 'What are you searching for?' 'Drinking water, venerable sir.' 'Is this Ganges not full? Do you not see it?' 'It is of no use to me, venerable sir.' 'In that case, lie down on the bank of the Ganges; we will pour drinking water into your mouth.' He lay down supine on a sandbank. The monks brought out thirty bowls, fetched water, and poured it into his mouth. As they were doing so, their time for the alms-round drew near. Then they said, 'Good sir, it is our time for the alms-round. Have you had even a little relief?' The peta replied: 'Venerable sirs, if even half a pasata of the water poured from the thirty bowls of the thirty noble ones went down my throat, may I not be liberated from this peta existence!' Thus, affliction by thirst is manifest in the realm of petas.

Ḍaṃsādīhi ruppanaṃ ḍaṃsamakkhikādibahulesu padesesu pākaṭaṃ. Ettha ca ḍaṃsāti piṅgalamakkhikā. Makasāti makasāva. Vātāti kucchivātapiṭṭhivātādivasena veditabbā. Sarīrasmiñhi vātarogo uppajjitvā hatthapādapiṭṭhiādīni bhindati, kāṇaṃ karoti, khujjaṃ karoti, pīṭhasappiṃ karoti. Ātapoti sūriyātapo. Tena ruppanaṃ marukantārādīsu pākaṭaṃ. Ekā kira itthī marukantāre rattiṃ satthato ohīnā divā sūriye uggacchante vālikāya tappamānāya pāde ṭhapetuṃ asakkontī sīsato pacchiṃ otāretvā akkami. Kamena pacchiyā uṇhābhitattāya ṭhātuṃ asakkontī tassā upari sāṭakaṃ ṭhapetvā akkami. Tasmimpi santatte attano aṅkena gahitaputtakaṃ adhomukhaṃ nipajjāpetvā kandantaṃyeva akkamitvā saddhiṃ tena tasmiṃyeva ṭhāne uṇhābhitattā kālamakāsi.

Affliction by gadflies and so on is manifest in places with many gadflies, mosquitoes, and the like. Herein, 'gadflies' (ḍaṃsa) are yellow flies. 'Mosquitoes' (makasa) are simply mosquitoes. 'Wind' (vāta) should be understood by way of abdominal wind, back wind, and so on. For when a wind disease arises in the body, it afflicts the hands, feet, back, etc.; it makes one blind, makes one hunchbacked, or makes one crippled. 'Heat' (ātapa) is the sun's heat. Affliction by it is manifest in waterless wastelands and so on. It is said that a certain woman, left behind by her caravan in a waterless wasteland at night, was unable to place her feet on the sand as it was scorching when the sun rose during the day. So, taking the basket down from her head, she stepped on it. In due course, as the basket became intensely hot, she was unable to stand on it, so she placed her garment on top of it and stepped on that. When that also became hot, she laid the young son she was carrying on her hip face down, and while he was still crying, she stepped on him. Together with him, in that very place, she passed away, scorched by the heat.

Sarīsapāti [V 2.0259] ye keci dīghajātikā sarantā gacchanti. Tesaṃ samphassena ruppanaṃ āsīvisadaṭṭhakādīnaṃ vasena veditabbaṃ. Iti bhagavatā yāni imāni sāmaññapaccattavasena dhammānaṃ dve lakkhaṇāni, tesu rūpakkhandhassa tāva paccattalakkhaṇaṃ dassitaṃ. Rūpakkhandhasseva hi etaṃ, na vedanādīnaṃ[P 2.0292], tasmā paccattalakkhaṇanti [M 2.0269] vuccati. Aniccadukkhānattalakkhaṇaṃ pana vedanādīnampi hoti, tasmā taṃ sāmaññalakkhaṇanti vuccati.

'Creeping things' refers to any long-bodied species that move by crawling. Affliction by their contact should be understood by way of being bitten by a venomous snake and so on. Thus, of these two characteristics of phenomena—the general and the particular—the Blessed One has first shown the particular characteristic of the aggregate of form. For this affliction is the particular characteristic of the aggregate of form alone, not of feeling and so on; therefore, it is called the 'particular characteristic.' The characteristic of impermanence, suffering, and non-self, however, also pertains to feeling and so on; therefore, it is called the 'general characteristic.'

Kiñca, bhikkhave, vedanaṃ vadethātiādīsu purimasadisaṃ vuttanayeneva veditabbaṃ. Yaṃ pana purimena asadisaṃ, tassāyaṃ vibhāvanā – sukhampi vedayatīti sukhaṃ ārammaṇaṃ vedeti anubhavati. Parato padadvayepi eseva nayo. Kathaṃ panetaṃ ārammaṇaṃ sukhaṃ dukkhaṃ adukkhamasukhaṃ nāma jātanti? Sukhādīnaṃ paccayato. Svāyamattho “yasmā ca kho, mahāli, rūpaṃ sukhaṃ sukhānupatitaṃ sukhāvakkanta”nti imasmiṃ mahālisutte (saṃ. ni. 3.60) āgatoyeva. Vedayatīti ettha ca vedanāva vedayati, na añño satto vā puggalo vā. Vedanā hi vedayitalakkhaṇā, tasmā vatthārammaṇaṃ paṭicca vedanāva vedayatīti. Evamidha bhagavā vedanāyapi paccattalakkhaṇameva bhājetvā dassesi.

In the passage beginning, 'And what, bhikkhus, do you call feeling?' it should be understood in the same way as what was stated before. As for what is different from the preceding, this is the explanation: 'One feels pleasure' means one feels, one experiences a pleasant object. This same method applies to the two phrases that follow. But how does this object come to be called pleasant, painful, or neither-painful-nor-pleasant? Because it is a condition for pleasure and so on. This meaning is indeed found in the Mahāli Sutta: 'Because, Mahāli, form is pleasant, attended by pleasure, pervaded by pleasure.' And here, in 'one feels,' it is feeling itself that feels, not some other being or person. For feeling has the characteristic of experiencing. Therefore, dependent on the base and the object, it is feeling itself that feels. Thus, in this context, the Blessed One, having analyzed, showed the particular characteristic of feeling as well.

Nīlampi sañjānātīti nīlapupphe vā vatthe vā parikammaṃ katvā upacāraṃ vā appanaṃ vā pāpento sañjānāti. Ayañhi saññā nāma parikammasaññāpi upacārasaññāpi appanāsaññāpi vaṭṭati, nīlaṃ nīlanti uppajjanasaññāpi vaṭṭatiyeva. Pītakādīsupi eseva nayo. Idhāpi bhagavā sañjānanalakkhaṇāya saññāya paccattalakkhaṇameva bhājetvā dassesi.

'One perceives blue' means that having done the preliminary meditation practice on a blue flower or a blue cloth, one perceives while attaining either access concentration or absorption concentration. For this perception is applicable as preliminary perception, access perception, and absorption perception; the perception that arises as 'blue, blue' is also applicable. This same method should be understood for yellow and so on. Here too, the Blessed One, having analyzed, showed only the particular characteristic of perception, which has the characteristic of perceiving.

Rūpaṃ rūpattāya saṅkhatamabhisaṅkharontīti yathā yāgumeva yāguttāya, pūvameva pūvattāya pacati nāma, evaṃ paccayehi samāgantvā katabhāvena saṅkhatanti laddhanāmaṃ rūpameva rūpattāya yathā abhisaṅkhataṃ rūpaṃ nāma hoti, tathattāya rūpabhāvāya abhisaṅkharoti āyūhati sampiṇḍeti, nipphādetīti attho. Vedanādīsupi eseva nayo. Ayaṃ panettha saṅkhepo – attanā saha jāyamānaṃ rūpaṃ sampayutte ca vedanādayo dhamme abhisaṅkharoti nibbattetīti. Idhāpi bhagavā cetayitalakkhaṇassa saṅkhārassa paccattalakkhaṇameva bhājetvā dassesi.

'They form the conditioned for the sake of form-ness' means: just as one cooks what is to become gruel for the sake of it being gruel, or what is to become a cake for the sake of it being a cake, so too, conditions, having come together, form that very thing which has received the name 'conditioned' due to being made—that is, form—for the sake of its being form; they fashion it, strive for it, gather it, and produce it. This is the meaning. This same method applies to feeling and so on. Herein, this is the summary: a formation forms the form arising together with itself and the associated mental states such as feeling; it produces them. Here too, the Blessed One, having analyzed, showed only the particular characteristic of formations, which have the characteristic of volition.

Ambilampi [P 2.0293] vijānātīti ambaambāṭakamātuluṅgādiambilaṃ “ambila”nti vijānāti. Eseva [V 2.0260] nayo sabbapadesu. Api cettha tittakanti nimbapaṭolādinānappakāraṃ kaṭukanti pippalimaricādinānappakāraṃ. Madhuranti sappiphāṇitādinānappakāraṃ[M 2.0270]. Khārikanti vātiṅgaṇanāḷikera caturassavallivettaṅkurādinānappakāraṃ. Akhārikanti yaṃ vā taṃ vā phalajātaṃ kārapaṇṇādimissakapaṇṇaṃ. Loṇikanti loṇayāguloṇamacchaloṇabhattādinānappakāraṃ. Aloṇikantialoṇayāgualoṇamacchaaloṇabhattādinānappakāraṃ. Tasmā viññāṇanti vuccatīti yasmā imaṃ ambilādibhedaṃ aññamaññavisiṭṭhena ambilādibhāvena jānāti, tasmā viññāṇanti vuccatīti. Evamidhāpi bhagavā vijānanalakkhaṇassa viññāṇassa paccattalakkhaṇameva bhājetvā dassesi.

'One cognizes the sour' means one cognizes a sour substance such as a mango, hog plum, or citron as 'sour.' This same method applies in all instances. And herein, 'bitter' refers to various kinds such as neem and bitter gourd. 'Pungent' refers to various kinds such as long pepper and black pepper. 'Sweet' refers to various kinds such as ghee and molasses. 'Alkaline' refers to various kinds such as eggplant, coconut, winged bean, and rattan shoots. 'Non-alkaline' refers to any kind of fruit, or mixed leaves such as those of the kārab plant. 'Salty' refers to various kinds such as salty gruel, salted fish, and salty rice. 'Non-salty' refers to various kinds such as non-salty gruel, non-salted fish, and non-salty rice. 'Therefore it is called consciousness' means: because it cognizes this variety of sourness and so on, with each being distinct from the others, therefore it is called 'consciousness.' Thus, here too, the Blessed One, having analyzed, showed only the particular characteristic of consciousness, which has the characteristic of cognizing.

Yasmā pana ārammaṇassa ākārasaṇṭhānagahaṇavasena saññā pākaṭā hoti, tasmā sā cakkhudvāre vibhattā. Yasmā vināpi ākārasaṇṭhānā ārammaṇassa paccattabhedagahaṇavasena viññāṇaṃ pākaṭaṃ hoti, tasmā taṃ jivhādvāre vibhattaṃ. Imesaṃ pana saññāviññāṇapaññānaṃ asammohato sabhāvasallakkhaṇatthaṃ sañjānāti, vijānāti, pajānātīti ettha visesā veditabbā. Tattha upasaggamattameva viseso, jānātīti padaṃ pana aviseso. Tassapi jānanaṭṭhena viseso veditabbo. Saññā hi nīlādivasena ārammaṇasañjānanamattameva, aniccaṃ dukkhamanattāti lakkhaṇapaṭivedhaṃ pāpetuṃ na sakkoti. Viññāṇaṃ nīlādivasena ārammaṇañceva jānāti, aniccādivasena lakkhaṇapaṭivedhañca pāpeti, ussakkitvā pana maggapātubhāvaṃ pāpetuṃ na sakkoti. Paññā nīlādivasena ārammaṇampi jānāti, aniccādivasena lakkhaṇapaṭivedhampi pāpeti, ussakkitvā maggapātubhāvampi pāpeti.

Furthermore, since perception becomes evident by way of grasping the features and form of an object, it is analyzed at the eye-door. Since consciousness becomes evident by way of grasping the distinct individual nature of an object even without its features and form, it is analyzed at the tongue-door. Regarding this perception, consciousness, and wisdom, the distinctions among the terms sañjānāti (perceives), vijānāti (discerns), and pajānāti (understands) should be known for the sake of correctly cognizing their intrinsic nature without confusion. Therein, the distinction is merely in the prefix, while the word jānāti (knows) is common. Yet, a distinction should also be understood in the meaning of 'knowing.' Indeed, perception is merely the perceiving of an object by way of its being blue, etc.; it cannot bring about the penetration of the characteristics such as impermanence, suffering, and not-self. Consciousness both knows the object by way of its being blue, etc., and brings about the penetration of the characteristics such as impermanence; however, by striving, it cannot bring about the manifestation of the path. Wisdom both knows the object by way of its being blue, etc., brings about the penetration of the characteristics such as impermanence, and, by striving, also brings about the manifestation of the path.

Yathā hi heraññikaphalake kahāpaṇarāsimhi kate ajātabuddhidārako [P 2.0294] gāmikapuriso mahāheraññikoti tīsu janesu oloketvā ṭhitesu ajātabuddhidārako kahāpaṇānaṃ cittavicittacaturassamaṇḍalādibhāvameva jānāti, “idaṃ manussānaṃ upabhogaparibhogaṃ ratanasammata”nti na jānāti. Gāmikapuriso cittādibhāvañca jānāti, manussānaṃ upabhogaparibhogaratanasammatabhāvañca, “ayaṃ kūṭo, ayaṃ cheko, ayaṃ karaṭo, ayaṃ saṇho”ti na jānāti. Mahāheraññiko cittādibhāvampi ratanasammatabhāvampi kūṭādibhāvampi jānāti. Jānanto ca pana [M 2.0271] rūpaṃ disvāpi saddaṃ sutvāpi gandhaṃ ghāyitvāpi rasaṃ sāyitvāpi hatthena garulahubhāvaṃ upadhāretvāpi “asukagāme kato”tipi jānāti, “asukanigame asukanagare asukapabbatacchāyāya [V 2.0261] asukanadītīre kato”tipi, “asukācariyena kato”tipi jānāti. Evameva saññā ajātabuddhidārakassa kahāpaṇadassanaṃ viya nīlādivasena ārammaṇamattameva jānāti. Viññāṇaṃ gāmikapurisassa kahāpaṇadassanaṃ viya nīlādivasena ārammaṇampi jānāti, aniccādivasena lakkhaṇapaṭivedhampi pāpeti. Paññā mahāheraññikassa kahāpaṇadassanaṃ viya nīlādivasena ārammaṇampi jānāti, aniccādivasena lakkhaṇapaṭivedhampi pāpeti, ussakkitvā maggapātubhāvampi pāpeti.

For instance, when a pile of coins is placed on a money-changer's table, and three people—a boy with undeveloped intelligence, a villager, and a great money-changer—are observing it: the boy with undeveloped intelligence merely knows the coins' state of being variegated, multi-colored, square, round, and so on, but he does not know, 'This is considered a treasure by humans for their use and enjoyment.' The villager knows both their state of being variegated, etc., and their state of being considered a treasure for human use and enjoyment, but he does not know, 'This one is counterfeit, this one is genuine, this one is coarse, this one is fine.' The great money-changer, however, knows their state of being variegated, their state of being considered a treasure, and their state of being counterfeit, etc. And in knowing, even upon seeing their form, hearing their sound, smelling their scent, tasting their flavor, or assessing their heaviness or lightness with his hand, he knows, 'This was made in such-and-such a village,' or 'in such-and-such a market town, in such-and-such a city, in the shade of such-and-such a mountain, on the bank of such-and-such a river,' or 'by such-and-such a master craftsman.' In the same way, perception, like the boy with undeveloped intelligence seeing the coins, merely perceives the object by way of its being blue, etc. Consciousness, like the villager seeing the coins, both knows the object by way of its being blue, etc., and brings about the penetration of the characteristics such as impermanence. Wisdom, like the great money-changer seeing the coins, both knows the object by way of its being blue, etc., brings about the penetration of the characteristics such as impermanence, and, by striving, also brings about the manifestation of the path.

So pana nesaṃ viseso duppaṭivijjho. Tenāha āyasmā nāgaseno –

But this distinction among them is difficult to penetrate. Therefore, the Venerable Nāgasena said:

“Dukkaraṃ, mahārāja, bhagavatā katanti. Kiṃ, bhante nāgasena, bhagavatā dukkaraṃ katanti? Dukkaraṃ, mahārāja, bhagavatā kataṃ, imesaṃ arūpīnaṃ cittacetasikānaṃ dhammānaṃ ekārammaṇe vattamānānaṃ vavatthānaṃ akkhātaṃ ‘ayaṃ phasso, ayaṃ vedanā, ayaṃ saññā, ayaṃ cetanā, idaṃ citta’”nti (mi. pa. 2.7.16).

‘A difficult thing, great king, was done by the Blessed One.’ ‘What, venerable Nāgasena, is the difficult thing done by the Blessed One?’ ‘A difficult thing, great king, was done by the Blessed One, in that the distinction of these formless mental and mental-concomitant phenomena occurring with a single object was declared: “This is contact, this is feeling, this is perception, this is volition, this is consciousness.”’

Yathā hi tilatelaṃ sāsapatelaṃ madhukatelaṃ eraṇḍakatelaṃ vasātelanti imāni pañca telāni ekacāṭiyaṃ pakkhipitvā divasaṃ yamakamanthe hi manthetvā tato “idaṃ tilatelaṃ, idaṃ sāsapatela”nti ekekassa [P 2.0295] pāṭiyekkaṃ uddharaṇaṃ nāma dukkaraṃ, idaṃ tato dukkarataraṃ. Bhagavā pana sabbaññutaññāṇassa suppaṭividdhattā dhammissaro dhammarājā imesaṃ arūpīnaṃ dhammānaṃ ekārammaṇe vattamānānaṃ vavatthānaṃ akāsi. Pañcannaṃ mahānadīnaṃ samuddaṃ paviṭṭhaṭṭhāne “idaṃ gaṅgāya udakaṃ, idaṃ yamunāyā”ti evaṃ pāṭiyekkaṃ udakuddharaṇenāpi ayamattho veditabbo.

For instance, just as if sesame oil, mustard oil, madhuka oil, castor oil, and fat oil—these five oils—were poured into a single vessel and churned with a double churn for a day, it would be difficult to then extract each one separately, saying, ‘This is sesame oil, this is mustard oil’; this is even more difficult than that. The Blessed One, however, due to the thorough penetration of his omniscient knowledge, being the Lord of the Dhamma and the King of the Dhamma, established the distinction of these formless phenomena occurring with a single object. This meaning should also be understood through the analogy of separately drawing up the water at the place where the five great rivers have entered the ocean, saying, 'This is the water of the Ganges, this is the water of the Yamunā.'

Iti paṭhamapabbena suññataṃ, dutiyena suññatālakkhaṇanti dvīhi pabbehi anattalakkhaṇaṃ kathetvā idāni dukkhalakkhaṇaṃ dassetuṃ tatra, bhikkhavetiādimāha. Tattha khajjāmīti na rūpaṃ sunakho viya maṃsaṃ luñcitvā luñcitvā khādati, yathā pana kiliṭṭhavatthanivattho tatonidānaṃ pīḷaṃ sandhāya “khādati maṃ [M 2.0272] vattha”nti bhaṇati, evamidampi pīḷaṃ uppādentaṃ khādati nāmāti veditabbaṃ. Paṭipanno hotīti sīlaṃ ādiṃ katvā yāva arahattamaggā paṭipanno hoti. Yo panettha balavañāṇo tikkhabuddhi ñāṇuttaro yogāvacaro padhānabhūmiyaṃ vāyamanto khāṇunā vā kaṇṭakena vā viddho āvudhena vā pahaṭo byagghādīhi vā gahetvā khajjamāno taṃ vedanaṃ abbohārikaṃ [V 2.0262] katvā mūlakammaṭṭhānaṃ sammasanto arahattameva gaṇhāti, ayaṃ vedanāya nibbidāya virāgāya nirodhāya paṭipanno nāma vuccati pītamallatthero viya kuṭumbiyaputtamahātissatthero viya vattaniaṭaviyaṃ tiṃsamattānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ aññataro byagghamukhe nipannabhikkhu viya kaṇṭakena viddhatthero viya ca.

Thus, having taught emptiness in the first section and the characteristic of emptiness in the second—thereby explaining the characteristic of not-self in two sections—he now says, 'Therein, bhikkhus,' etc., to show the characteristic of suffering. Here, 'I am being devoured' does not mean that form devours by tearing at flesh like a dog. Rather, just as a person wearing a soiled garment, referring to the affliction caused by it, says, 'The cloth is devouring me,' so too should it be understood that this form, by producing affliction, is said to 'devour.' 'He is one who has undertaken the practice' means he is one who has undertaken the practice from virtue up to the path of Arahantship. Among these, a practitioner with strong wisdom, sharp intellect, and supreme knowledge, striving on the ground of exertion, though pierced by a stake or a thorn, struck by a weapon, or seized and devoured by a tiger or the like, by making that feeling of no account and contemplating the primary meditation subject, attains Arahantship itself. Such a one is called 'one who has undertaken the practice for disenchantment with, dispassion for, and cessation of feeling'—like the Elder Pītamalla, like the Elder Mahātissa, son of a householder, like a certain bhikkhu among thirty in the Vattaniya forest who lay in a tiger's mouth, and like the elder pierced by a thorn.

Dvādasasu kira bhikkhūsu ghaṇṭiṃ paharitvā araññe padhānamanuyuñjantesu eko sūriye atthaṅgatamatteyeva ghaṇṭiṃ paharitvā caṅkamaṃ oruyha caṅkamanto [P 2.0296] tiriyaṃ nimmathento tiṇapaṭicchannaṃ kaṇṭakaṃ akkami. Kaṇṭako piṭṭhipādena nikkhanto. Tattaphālena vinividdhakālo viya vedanā vattati. Thero cintesi – “kiṃ imaṃ kaṇṭakaṃ uddharāmi, udāhu pakatiyā vijjhitvā ṭhitakaṇṭaka”nti? Tassa evamahosi – “iminā kaṇṭakena viddhattā nirayādīsu bhayaṃ nāma natthi, pakatiyā vijjhitvā ṭhitakaṇṭakaṃyevā”ti. So taṃ vedanaṃ abbohārikaṃ katvā sabbarattiṃ caṅkamitvā vibhātāya rattiyā aññassa saññaṃ adāsi. So āgantvā “kiṃ, bhante”ti pucchi? “Kaṇṭakenamhi, āvuso, viddho”ti. “Kāya velāya, bhante”ti? “Sāyameva, āvuso”ti. “Kasmā na amhe pakkosittha, kaṇṭakaṃ uddharitvā tattha telampi siñceyyāmā”ti? “Pakatiyā vijjhitvā ṭhitakaṇṭakameva uddharituṃ vāyamimhā, āvuso”ti. “Sakkuṇittha, bhante, uddharitu”nti. “Ekadesamattena me, āvuso, uddhaṭo”ti. Sesavatthūni dīghamajjhimaṭṭhakathāsu (dī. ni. aṭṭha. 2.373; ma. ni. aṭṭha. 1.106) satipaṭṭhānasuttaniddese vitthāritāneva.

It is said that among twelve bhikkhus who, having struck the stone gong, were devoted to the meditative effort in the forest, one, having struck the stone gong just as the sun had set, descended to the walking path. While walking and shuffling sideways, he stepped on a thorn concealed by grass. The thorn exited through the top of his foot. A feeling arose as if being pierced through by a hot ploughshare. The elder thought: “Should I remove this thorn, or the thorn that has naturally pierced and remains?” To him it occurred thus: “From being pierced by this thorn, there is no fear of the hells and so on; I shall remove only the thorn that has naturally pierced and remains.” Making that feeling of no account, he walked the whole night, and at the break of dawn gave a sign to another bhikkhu. That bhikkhu came and asked, “What is it, venerable sir?” “I have been pierced by a thorn, friend.” “At what time, venerable sir?” “Just this evening, friend.” “Why did you not call us? We could have removed the thorn and applied oil there.” “I was endeavoring to remove the thorn that has naturally pierced and remains, friend.” “Were you able to remove it, venerable sir?” “It has been removed by me only in part, friend.” The remaining stories are elaborated in the commentaries to the Dīgha and Majjhima Nikāyas in the exposition of the Satipaṭṭhāna Sutta.

Taṃ kiṃ maññatha, bhikkhaveti kasmā āraddhaṃ? Imasmiṃ pabbe dukkhalakkhaṇameva kathitaṃ, na aniccalakkhaṇaṃ. Taṃ dassetuṃ idamāraddhaṃ. Tīṇi lakkhaṇāni samodhānetvā dassetumpi āraddhameva. Apacināti no ācinātīti vaṭṭaṃ vināseti, neva cināti. Pajahati na upādiyatīti tadeva vissajjeti, na [M 2.0273] gaṇhāti. Visineti na ussinetīti vikirati na sampiṇḍeti. Vidhūpeti na sandhūpetīti nibbāpeti na jālāpeti.

Why was the phrase, ‘What do you think, bhikkhus,’ undertaken? In this section, only the characteristic of suffering was taught, not the characteristic of impermanence. This was undertaken to show that. It was also undertaken to show the three characteristics combined. ‘It diminishes, it does not accumulate’ means it destroys the round of existence; it does not build it up. ‘It abandons, it does not take up’ means it relinquishes that very round; it does not grasp it. ‘It scatters, it does not consolidate’ means it disperses; it does not gather together. ‘It extinguishes, it does not kindle’ means it puts out the mass of fire of the threefold round; it does not cause it to blaze.

Evaṃ passaṃ, bhikkhaveti idaṃ kasmā āraddhaṃ? Vaṭṭaṃ vināsetvā ṭhitaṃ mahākhīṇāsavaṃ dassessāmīti āraddhaṃ. Ettakena vā ṭhānena vipassanā kathitā, idāni saha vipassanāya cattāro magge dassetuṃ idaṃ āraddhaṃ. Atha vā ettakena ṭhānena paṭhamamaggo kathito, idāni saha [V 2.0263] vipassanāya tayo magge dassetuṃ idamāraddhaṃ. Ettakena vā ṭhānena tīṇi maggāni kathitāni, idāni saha vipassanāya arahattamaggaṃ dassetumpi idaṃ āraddhameva.

Why was this phrase, ‘Seeing thus, bhikkhus,’ undertaken? It was undertaken with the thought, ‘I shall show the great one with taints destroyed who stands having destroyed the round of existence.’ Alternatively, by this much of the text, insight has been taught; now, this was undertaken to show the four paths together with insight. Or else, by this much of the text, the first path has been taught; now, this was undertaken to show the three higher paths together with insight. Alternatively, by this much of the text, the three paths have been taught; now, this was also indeed undertaken to show the path of arahantship together with insight.

Sapajāpatikāti [P 2.0297] saddhiṃ pajāpatinā devarājena. Ārakāva namassantīti dūratova namassanti, dūrepi ṭhitaṃ namassantiyeva āyasmantaṃ nītattheraṃ viya.

‘With Pajāpati’ means together with Pajāpati, the king of the devas. ‘From afar they pay homage’ means they pay homage from a distance; they indeed pay homage even to one standing far away, just as they did to the Venerable Nītatthera.

Thero kira pupphacchaḍḍakakulato nikkhamma pabbajito, khuraggeyeva arahattaṃ patvā cintesi – “ahaṃ ajjeva pabbajito ajjeva me pabbajitakiccaṃ matthakaṃ pattaṃ, catupaccayasantosabhāvanārāmamaṇḍitaṃ mahāariyavaṃsapaṭipadaṃ pūressāmī”ti. So paṃsukūlatthāya sāvatthiṃ pavisitvā coḷakaṃ pariyesanto vicari. Atheko mahābrahmā samāpattito vuṭṭhāya manussapathaṃ olokento theraṃ disvā – “ajjeva pabbajitvā ajjeva khuragge arahattaṃ patvā mahāariyavaṃsapaṭipadaṃ pūretuṃ coḷakaṃ pariyesatī”ti añjaliṃ paggayha namassamāno aṭṭhāsi. Tamañño mahābrahmā disvā “kaṃ namassasī”ti? Pucchi. Nītattheraṃ namassāmīti. Kiṃ kāraṇāti? Ajjeva pabbajitvā ajjeva khuragge arahattaṃ patvā mahāariyavaṃsapaṭipadaṃ pūretuṃ coḷakaṃ pariyesatīti. Sopi naṃ namassamāno aṭṭhāsi. Athañño, athaññoti sattasatā mahābrahmāno namassamānā aṭṭhaṃsu. Tena vuttaṃ –

The elder, it is said, having gone forth from a family of flower-disposers, attained arahantship at the very touch of the razor. He reflected: “Today I have gone forth, and today my task of going forth has reached its culmination. I will fulfill the practice of the great noble lineage, adorned with contentment with the four requisites and delight in development.” For the sake of a rag-robe, he entered Sāvatthī and wandered about searching for a piece of cloth. Then a certain great brahmā, rising from attainment and surveying the human world, saw the elder. Thinking, “Having gone forth just today and attained arahantship at the touch of the razor, he now seeks a piece of cloth to fulfill the practice of the great noble lineage,” he raised his joined palms and stood paying homage. Another great brahmā saw him and asked, “Whom do you pay homage to?” He replied, “I pay homage to the Elder Nīta.” The first asked, “For what reason?” He replied, “Because having gone forth just today and attained arahantship at the touch of the razor, he seeks a piece of cloth to fulfill the practice of the great noble lineage.” He too stood paying homage to him. Then another, and another, until seven hundred great brahmās stood paying homage. Therefore it is said:

“Tā devatā sattasatā uḷārā,

Brahmā vimānā abhinikkhamitvā;

Nītaṃ namassanti pasannacittā,

‘Khīṇāsavo gaṇhati paṃsukūlaṃ’”.

“Those seven hundred glorious devas, brahmās, emerging from the Brahmā mansions, with serene minds pay homage to Nīta: ‘The one with taints destroyed takes the rag-robe.’”

“Tā [M 2.0274] devatā sattasatā uḷārā,

Brahmā vimānā abhinikkhamitvā;

Nītaṃ namassanti pasannacittā,

‘Khīṇāsavo kayirati paṃsukūlaṃ’”.

“Those seven hundred glorious devas, brahmās, emerging from the Brahmā mansions, with serene minds pay homage to Nīta: ‘The one with taints destroyed makes the rag-robe.’”

“‘Khīṇāsavo [V 2.0264] dhovati paṃsukūlaṃ’;

‘Khīṇāsavo rajati paṃsukūlaṃ’;

‘Khīṇāsavo pārupati paṃsakūla’”nti.

“‘The one with taints destroyed washes the rag-robe;’ ‘The one with taints destroyed dyes the rag-robe;’ ‘The one with taints destroyed wears the rag-robe.’”

Iti bhagavā imasmiṃ sutte desanaṃ tīhi bhavehi vinivattetvā arahattassa kūṭaṃ gaṇhi. Desanāpariyosāne pañcasatā bhikkhū arahatte patiṭṭhahiṃsu. Sattamaṃ.

Thus the Blessed One, in this discourse, having turned the teaching away from the three states of existence, took arahantship as its pinnacle. At the conclusion of the teaching, five hundred bhikkhus were established in arahantship. The seventh discourse is finished.

8. Piṇḍolyasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Piṇḍolya Sutta

80. Aṭṭhame kismiñcideva pakaraṇeti kismiñcideva kāraṇe. Paṇāmetvāti nīharitvā. Kismiṃ pana kāraṇe ete bhagavatā paṇāmitāti? Ekasmiñhi [P 2.0298] antovasse bhagavā sāvatthiyaṃ vasitvā vutthavasso pavāretvā mahābhikkhusaṅghaparivāro sāvatthito nikkhamitvā janapadacārikaṃ caranto kapilavatthuṃ patvā nigrodhārāmaṃ pāvisi. Sakyarājāno “satthā āgato”ti sutvā pacchābhatte kappiyāni telamadhuphāṇitādīni ceva pānakāni ca kājasatehi gāhāpetvā vihāraṃ gantvā saṅghassa niyyātetvā satthāraṃ vanditvā paṭisanthāraṃ karontā ekamante nisīdiṃsu. Satthā tesaṃ madhuradhammakathaṃ kathento nisīdi. Tasmiṃ khaṇe ekacce bhikkhū senāsanaṃ paṭijagganti, ekacce mañcapīṭhādīni paññāpenti, sāmaṇerā appaharitaṃ karonti. Bhājanīyaṭṭhāne sampattabhikkhūpi atthi, asampattabhikkhūpi atthi. Sampattā asampattānaṃ lābhaṃ gaṇhantā, “amhākaṃ detha, amhākaṃ ācariyassa detha upajjhāyassa dethā”ti kathentā mahāsaddamakaṃsu. Satthā sutvā theraṃ pucchi “ke pana te, ānanda, uccāsaddā mahāsaddā kevaṭṭā maññe macchavilope”ti? Thero etamatthaṃ ārocesi. Satthā sutvā “āmisahetu, ānanda, bhikkhū mahāsaddaṃ karontī”ti āha. “Āma, bhante”ti. “Ananucchavikaṃ, ānanda, appatirūpaṃ. Na [M 2.0275] hi mayā kappasatasahassādhikāni cattāri asaṅkhyeyyāni cīvarādihetu pāramiyo pūritā, nāpi ime bhikkhū cīvarādihetu agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajitā, arahattahetu pabbajitvā anatthaṃ atthasadisaṃ asāraṃ sārasadisaṃ karonti, gacchānanda, te bhikkhū paṇāmehī”ti.

80. In the eighth [sutta], ‘regarding some matter’ means regarding some reason. ‘Having dismissed’ means having expelled. But for what reason were they dismissed by the Blessed One? For, on one occasion, the Blessed One, having resided in Sāvatthī during the rains-residence, having completed the rains-residence and performed the Pavāraṇā ceremony, departed from Sāvatthī with a great retinue of the Saṅgha of monks. While wandering on a tour of the countryside, he reached Kapilavatthu and entered the Banyan Tree Park. The Sakyan kings, hearing, “The Teacher has come,” after the midday meal, had permissible items such as oil, honey, and molasses, as well as beverages, brought by the hundreds of carrying-poles. They went to the monastery, presented them to the Saṅgha, paid homage to the Teacher, and, after exchanging courteous greetings, sat down to one side. The Teacher sat delivering a sweet Dhamma talk to them. At that moment, some monks were tidying the lodging, some were arranging couches and chairs, and the novices were clearing away the grass. At the place where provisions were distributed, some monks were present, and some were not. Those who were present, taking the shares for those who were not present, made a great noise, saying, “Give it to us! Give it to our teacher! Give it to our preceptor!” The Teacher heard this and asked the Elder, “Ānanda, what is that loud and great noise? It sounds like fishermen, I suppose, during a scramble for fish.” The Elder reported the matter. Having heard, the Teacher said, “Ānanda, the monks are making a great noise for the sake of material things.” “Yes, venerable sir.” “This is unseemly, Ānanda, it is unsuitable. For four incalculables and one hundred thousand aeons, I did not fulfill the perfections for the sake of robes and so on. Nor did these monks go forth from the household life into homelessness for the sake of robes and so on. Having gone forth for the sake of Arahantship, they make what is not the goal seem like the goal. Go, Ānanda, dismiss those monks.”

Pubbaṇhasamayanti dutiyadivase pubbaṇhasamayaṃ. Beluvalaṭṭhikāya mūleti taruṇabeluvarukkhamūle. Pabāḷhoti pabāhito. Pavāḷhotipi pāṭho, pavāhitoti attho. Ubhayampi nīhaṭabhāvameva [V 2.0265] dīpeti. Siyā aññathattanti pasādaññathattaṃ vā bhāvaññathattaṃ vā bhaveyya. Kathaṃ? “Sammāsambuddhena mayaṃ lahuke kāraṇe paṇāmitā”ti [P 2.0299] pasādaṃ mandaṃ karontānaṃ pasādaññathattaṃ nāma hoti. Saliṅgeneva titthāyatanaṃ pakkamantānaṃ bhāvaññathattaṃ nāma. Siyā vipariṇāmoti ettha pana “mayaṃ satthu ajjhāsayaṃ gaṇhituṃ sakkhissāmāti pabbajitā, naṃ gahetuṃ asakkontānaṃ kiṃ amhākaṃ pabbajjāyā”ti? Sikkhaṃ paccakkhāya hīnāyāvattanaṃ vipariṇāmoti veditabbo. Vacchassāti khīrūpakavacchassa. Aññathattanti milāyanaaññathattaṃ. Khīrūpako hi vaccho mātu adassanena khīraṃ alabhanto milāyati kampati pavedhati. Vipariṇāmoti maraṇaṃ. So hi khīraṃ alabhamāno khīrapipāsāya sussanto patitvā marati.

‘In the morning time’ means: on the second day, in the morning. ‘At the root of the Beluva staff’ means at the root of a young Beluva tree. ‘Banished’ (pabāḷho) means driven away (pabāhito). There is also the reading ‘cast off’ (pavāḷho), which means driven away (pavāhito). Both indicate the state of having been expelled. ‘There might be an alteration’ means there could be an alteration of faith or an alteration of state. How so? For those who think, “We were dismissed by the Perfectly Enlightened One for a trivial reason,” and whose faith thereby weakens, this is called an alteration of faith. For those who, while still in their own guise, go over to the abode of sectarians, it is called an alteration of state. As for ‘there might be a reversal’: here, it should be understood that a reversal is when, thinking, “We went forth thinking, ‘We will be able to grasp the Teacher’s intention,’ but since we are unable to grasp it, what is the use of our going forth?” they renounce the training and return to the lower life. ‘Of a calf’ means of a suckling calf. ‘An alteration’ means an alteration consisting of withering. For a suckling calf, not seeing its mother and not getting milk, withers, trembles, and shivers. ‘A reversal’ means death. For that calf, not getting milk, dries up from thirst for milk, collapses, and dies.

Bījānaṃ taruṇānanti udakena anuggahetabbānaṃ virūḷhabījānaṃ. Aññathattanti milāyanaññathattameva. Tāni hi udakaṃ alabhantāni milāyanti. Vipariṇāmoti vināso. Tāni hi udakaṃ alabhantāni sukkhitvā vinassanti, palālameva honti. Anuggahitoti āmisānuggahena ceva dhammānuggahena ca anuggahito. Anuggaṇheyyanti dvīhipi etehi anuggahehi anuggaṇheyyaṃ. Acirapabbajitā hi sāmaṇerā ceva daharabhikkhū ca cīvarādipaccayavekalle vā sati gelaññe vā satthārā vā ācariyupajjhāyehi vā āmisānuggahena ananuggahitā kilamantā na sakkonti sajjhāyaṃ vā manasikāraṃ vā kātuṃ, dhammānuggahena ananuggahitā uddesena ceva ovādānusāsaniyā ca parihāyamānā na sakkonti akusalaṃ parivajjetvā kusalaṃ bhāvetuṃ. Imehi pana dvīhi anuggahehi anuggahitā kāyena akilamantā sajjhāyamanasikāre pavattitvā yathānusiṭṭhaṃ paṭipajjamānā [M 2.0276] aparabhāge taṃ anuggahaṃ alabhantāpi teneva purimānuggahena laddhabalā sāsane patiṭṭhahanti, tasmā bhagavato evaṃ parivitakko udapādi.

‘Of young seeds’ means of sprouted seeds that ought to be supported with water. ‘An alteration’ means simply the alteration that is withering. For indeed, not receiving water, they wither. ‘A reversal’ means destruction. For indeed, not receiving water, they dry up and are destroyed; they become mere chaff. ‘Supported’ means supported by material support and by Dhamma support. ‘I would support them’ means I would support them with both these kinds of support. Indeed, recently ordained novices and young monks, if they are unsupported by material support from the Teacher or their teachers and preceptors when there is a lack of requisites such as robes or when they are ill, become weary and are unable to practice recitation or apply their minds. If they are unsupported by Dhamma support, declining in instruction, advice, and admonition, they are unable to avoid the unwholesome and cultivate the wholesome. But when they are supported by these two kinds of support, their bodies are not weary, and they can engage in recitation and application of mind. Practicing as they have been instructed, even if they do not receive that support in the future, they gain strength from that prior support and become established in the Teaching. Therefore, this reflection arose in the Blessed One.

Bhagavato purato pāturahosīti satthu cittaṃ ñatvā – “ime bhikkhū bhagavatā paṇāmitā, idāni nesaṃ anuggahaṃ kātukāmo evaṃ cintesi, kāraṇaṃ bhagavā cintesi, ahamettha ussāhaṃ janessāmī”ti purato pākaṭo ahosi. Santettha bhikkhūti idaṃ so mahābrahmā yathā nāma byatto sūdo yadeva [P 2.0300] ambilaggādīsu rasajātaṃ rañño ruccati, taṃ abhisaṅkhārena sādutaraṃ katvā punadivase upanāmeti, evameva attano byattatāya bhagavatā āhaṭaupamaṃyeva evametaṃ bhagavātiādivacanehi abhisaṅkharitvā bhagavantaṃ yācanto bhikkhusaṅghassa anuggahakaraṇatthaṃ vadati[V 2.0266]. Tattha abhinandatūti “mama santikaṃ bhikkhusaṅgho āgacchatū”ti. Evamassa āgamanaṃ sampiyāyamāno abhinandatu. Abhivadatūti āgatassa ca ovādānusāsaniṃ dadanto abhivadatu.

‘He appeared before the Blessed One’ means: knowing the Teacher’s mind—thinking, “These monks were dismissed by the Blessed One. Now, wishing to support them, he has considered a suitable reason. I will make an effort in this matter”—he became manifest before him. The great Brahmā spoke these words, ‘There are monks here.’ Just as a skilled cook, knowing which kind of flavor among sour flavors and so on pleases the king, prepares that flavor to be even more delicious through embellishment and presents it on the next day, just so, through his own skill, embellishing the very simile adduced by the Blessed One with words such as, “So it is, Blessed One,” he entreated the Blessed One, speaking for the sake of bringing support to the Saṅgha of monks. Therein, ‘let him rejoice’ means: ‘Let the Saṅgha of monks come to my presence.’ Thus, delighting in their arrival, let him rejoice. ‘Let him address them’ means: giving advice and instruction to those who have arrived, let him address them.

Paṭisallānāti ekībhāvā. Iddhābhisaṅkhāraṃ abhisaṅkhāsīti iddhiṃ akāsi. Ekadvīhikāyāti ekeko ceva dve dve ca hutvā. Sārajjamānarūpāti ottappamānasabhāvā bhāyamānā. Kasmā pana bhagavā tesaṃ tathā upasaṅkamanāya iddhimakāsīti? Hitapatthanāya. Yadi hi te vaggavaggā hutvā āgaccheyyuṃ, “bhagavā bhikkhusaṅghaṃ paṇāmetvā araññaṃ paviṭṭho ekadivasampi tattha vasituṃ nāsakkhi, vegeneva āgato”ti keḷimpi kareyyuṃ. Atha nesaṃ neva buddhagāravaṃ paccupaṭṭhaheyya, na dhammadesanaṃ sampaṭicchituṃ samatthā bhaveyyuṃ. Sabhayānaṃ pana sasārajjānaṃ ekadvīhikāya āgacchantānaṃ buddhagāravañceva paccupaṭṭhitaṃ bhavissati, dhammadesanañca sampaṭicchituṃ sakkhissantīti cintetvā tesaṃ hitapatthanāya tathārūpaṃ iddhiṃ akāsi.

“Seclusion” means being alone. “He performed the constructive effort of psychic power” means he created psychic power. “One or two at a time” means being one by one, or two by two. “Appearing abashed” means having a nature of being ashamed and fearful. But why did the Blessed One perform such a psychic feat for their approach? Out of a wish for their welfare. For if they had come in groups, they might have mocked, saying, “The Blessed One, having dismissed the Sangha of monks, entered the forest but could not stay there even for a single day—he returned in haste!” Then, neither would reverence for the Buddha arise in them, nor would they be capable of accepting the Dhamma teaching. But for those who came one or two at a time, fearful and abashed, reverence for the Buddha would arise, and they would be able to accept the Dhamma teaching. Reflecting thus, out of a wish for their welfare, the Blessed One performed such a psychic feat.

Nisīdiṃsūti tesu hi sārajjamānarūpesu āgacchantesu eko bhikkhu “mamaṃyeva satthā oloketi, maṃyeva maññe niggaṇhitukāmo”ti saṇikaṃ āgantvā vanditvā nisīdi, athañño athaññoti evaṃ pañcabhikkhusatāni nisīdiṃsu. Evaṃ nisinnaṃ pana bhikkhusaṅghaṃ sīdantare sannisinnaṃ mahāsamuddaṃ [P 2.0301] viya nivāte padīpaṃ viya ca niccalaṃ disvā satthā cintesi – “imesaṃ [M 2.0277] bhikkhūnaṃ kīdisī dhammadesanā vaṭṭatī”ti? Athassa etadahosi – “ime āhārahetu paṇāmitā, piṇḍiyālopadhammadesanāva nesaṃ sappāyā, taṃ dassetvā matthake tiparivaṭṭadesanaṃ desessāmi, desanāpariyosāne sabbe arahattaṃ pāpuṇissantī”ti. Atha nesaṃ taṃ dhammadesanaṃ desento antamidaṃ, bhikkhavetiādimāha.

“They sat down.” For as those who were appearing abashed arrived, one monk thought, “The Teacher is looking only at me; I think he wishes to rebuke me,” and slowly approached, paid homage, and sat down. Then another and another, thus five hundred monks sat down. Seeing the community of monks thus seated, motionless like the great ocean settled between the Sīdantara mountains and like a lamp in a windless place, the Teacher reflected: “What sort of Dhamma teaching is appropriate for these monks?” Then it occurred to him: “These were dismissed on account of food; a Dhamma teaching on morsels of almsfood is suitable for them. Having shown that, at the culmination I will teach the discourse with three revolutions. At the end of the teaching, all will attain arahantship.” Then, teaching them that Dhamma discourse, he spoke, beginning with “Monks, this is the lowest...”

Tattha antanti pacchimaṃ lāmakaṃ. Yadidaṃ piṇḍolyanti yaṃ evaṃ piṇḍapariyesanena jīvikaṃ kappentassa jīvitaṃ. Ayaṃ panettha padattho – piṇḍāya ulatīti piṇḍolo, piṇḍolassa kammaṃ piṇḍolyaṃ, piṇḍapariyesanena nipphāditajīvitanti attho. Abhisāpoti akkoso. Kupitā hi manussā attano paccatthikaṃ “cīvaraṃ nivāsetvā kapālaṃ gahetvā piṇḍaṃ pariyesamāno carissatī”ti akkosanti. Atha vā pana “kiṃ tuyhaṃ akātabbaṃ atthi, yo tvaṃ evaṃ balavā vīriyasampannopi hirottappaṃ pahāya kapaṇo viya piṇḍolo vicarasi pattapāṇī”ti? Evampi [V 2.0267] akkosantiyeva. Tañca kho etanti evaṃ taṃ abhisāpaṃ samānampi piṇḍolyaṃ. Kulaputtā upenti atthavasikāti mama sāsane jātikulaputtā ca ācārakulaputtā ca atthavasikā kāraṇavasikā hutvā kāraṇavasaṃ paṭicca upenti.

Here, “the lowest” (antaṃ) means the last, the base. That is, “alms-seeking” (piṇḍolyaṃ) is the life of one who maintains a living by searching for alms in this way. This is the word-analysis here: one who wanders for alms is an “alms-seeker” (piṇḍolo); the action of an alms-seeker is “alms-seeking” (piṇḍolyaṃ); the meaning is a life accomplished by searching for alms. “A curse” (abhisāpo) means abuse. For angry people abuse their enemy, saying, “Having put on the robe and taken up the bowl, you will wander searching for alms.” Or else: “What is there that you would not do, you who, though so strong and full of energy, have abandoned shame and moral fear and wander like a wretched person, an alms-seeker with bowl in hand?” In this way too they abuse. And this alms-seeking, even though it is such a curse, is what “Sons of good families undertake for a purpose” (kulaputtā upenti atthavasikā), meaning: in my dispensation, sons of good family by birth and sons of good family by conduct, being subject to a purpose, subject to a reason, undertake it depending on that reason.

Rājābhinītātiādīsu ye rañño santakaṃ khāditvā raññā bandhanāgāre bandhāpitā palāyitvā pabbajanti, te rājābhinītā nāma. Te hi raññā bandhanaṃ abhinītattā rājābhinītā nāma. Ye pana corehi aṭaviyaṃ gahetvā ekaccesu māriyamānesu ekacce “mayaṃ sāmi tumhehi vissaṭṭhā gehaṃ anajjhāvasitvā pabbajissāma, tattha yaṃ yaṃ buddhapūjādipuññaṃ karissāma, tato tumhākaṃ pattiṃ dassāmā”ti tehi vissaṭṭhā pabbajanti, te corābhinītā nāma. Tepi hi corehi māretabbataṃ abhinītāti corābhinītā nāma. Ye pana iṇaṃ gahetvā paṭidātuṃ asakkontā palāyitvā pabbajanti, te iṇaṭṭā nāma, iṇapīḷitāti attho[P 2.0302]. Iṇaṭṭhātipi pāṭho, iṇe ṭhitāti attho. Ye rājacorachātakarogabhayānaṃ aññatarena abhibhūtā upaddutā pabbajanti, te bhayaṭṭā nāma, bhayapīḷitāti attho. Bhayaṭṭhātipi pāṭho, bhaye [M 2.0278] ṭhitāti attho. Ājīvikāpakatāti ājīvikāya upaddutā abhibhūtā, puttadāraṃ posetuṃ asakkontāti attho. Otiṇṇāmhāti anto anupaviṭṭhā.

In the passage beginning with “driven by kings” (rājābhinītā): those who, having consumed the king's property, are bound in prison by the king, and having escaped, go forth—they are called “driven by kings.” For they were driven to imprisonment by the king; hence they are called “driven by kings.” Those who are seized by thieves in the forest, and while some are being killed, others plead, “Sirs, if we are released by you, we will not dwell at home but will go forth. Whatever merit we make there, such as the worship of the Buddha, we will share a portion of it with you,” and being released by them, go forth—they are called “driven by thieves” (corābhinītā). For they too were driven to the state of being killed by thieves; hence they are called “driven by thieves.” Those who, having taken a debt and being unable to repay it, run away and go forth—they are called “afflicted by debt” (iṇaṭṭā); the meaning is, oppressed by debt. There is also the reading iṇaṭṭhā, meaning “standing in debt.” Those who, being overwhelmed and afflicted by one or another of the fears of kings, thieves, famine, or disease, go forth—they are called “afflicted by fear” (bhayaṭṭā); the meaning is, oppressed by fear. There is also the reading bhayaṭṭhā, meaning “standing in fear.” “Afflicted by livelihood” (ājīvikāpakatā) means afflicted and overwhelmed by livelihood, being unable to support one's wife and children; that is the meaning. “Otiṇṇāmhā” means “entered within.”

So ca hoti abhijjhālūti idaṃ so kulaputto “dukkhassa antaṃ karissāmī”tiādivasena cittaṃ uppādetvā pabbajito, aparabhāge, taṃ pabbajjaṃ tathārūpaṃ kātuṃ na sakkoti, taṃ dassetuṃ vuttaṃ. Tattha abhijjhālūti parabhaṇḍānaṃ abhijjhāyitā. Tibbasārāgoti bahalarāgo. Byāpannacittoti pūtibhāvena vipannacitto. Paduṭṭhamanasaṅkappoti tikhiṇasiṅgo viya goṇo duṭṭhacitto. Muṭṭhassatīti bhattanikkhittakāko viya naṭṭhassati, idha kataṃ ettha nassati. Asampajānoti nippañño. Khandhādiparicchedarahito. Asamāhitoti caṇḍasote baddhanāvā viya upacārappanābhāvena asaṇṭhito. Vibbhantacittoti bandhāruḷhamago viya santamano. Pākatindriyoti yathā gihī puttadhītaro olokento asaṃvutindriyo hoti, evaṃ asaṃvutindriyo.

“And he is covetous” (so ca hoti abhijjhālu): this is said to show that a clansman, having generated the thought, “I will make an end of suffering,” and so forth, went forth, but later is unable to make that going-forth be of such a nature. Therein, “covetous” (abhijjhālu) means one who covets others' belongings. “With intense passion” (tibbasārāgo) means with strong passion. “With a corrupt mind” (byāpannacitto) means a mind ruined by way of putridity. “With a corrupt mental resolve” (paduṭṭhamanasaṅkappo) means having a corrupt mind, like a bull with sharp horns. “Of confused mindfulness” (muṭṭhassatī) means one whose mindfulness is lost, like a crow with its eyes fixed on a meal; what is done here is lost there. “Without clear comprehension” (asampajāno) means without wisdom, devoid of the discernment of the aggregates and so on. “Unconcentrated” (asamāhito) means unsteady due to the absence of access and absorption concentration, like a boat tied in a swift current. “With a bewildered mind” (vibbhantacitto) means with an agitated mind, like a deer driven into an enclosure. “With faculties in their natural state” (pākatindriyo) means with unrestrained faculties, just as a householder looking at his sons and daughters has unrestrained faculties.

Chavālātanti chavānaṃ daḍḍhaṭṭhāne alātaṃ. Ubhatopadittaṃ majjhe gūthagatanti pamāṇena aṭṭhaṅgulamattaṃ dvīsu ṭhānesu ādittaṃ majjhe gūthamakkhitaṃ. Neva gāmeti [P 2.0303] sace hi taṃ yuganaṅgalagopānasipakkhapāsakādīnaṃ [V 2.0268] atthāya upanetuṃ sakkā assa, gāme kaṭṭhatthaṃ phareyya. Sace khettakuṭiyaṃ kaṭṭhattharamañcakādīnaṃ atthāya upanetuṃ sakkā, araññe kaṭṭhatthaṃ phareyya. Yasmā pana ubhayathāpi na sakkā, tasmā evaṃ vuttaṃ. Gihibhogā ca parihīnoti yo agāre vasantehi gihīhi dāyajje bhājiyamāne bhogo laddhabbo assa, tato ca parihīno. Sāmaññatthañcāti ācariyupajjhāyānaṃ ovāde ṭhatvā pariyattipaṭivedhavasena pattabbaṃ sāmaññatthañca. Imañca pana upamaṃ satthā na dussīlassa vasena āhari, parisuddhasīlassa pana alasassa abhijjhādīhi dosehi upahatassa puggalassa imaṃ upamaṃ āhari.

'A firebrand from a funeral pyre' (chavālātanti) means a firebrand from the place where corpses are burned. 'Burning at both ends and smeared with dung in the middle' (ubhatopadittaṃ majjhe gūthagatanti) means a piece about eight finger-breadths long, burning at both ends and smeared with dung in the middle. 'It cannot be used in the village' (neva gāmeti)—indeed, if it could be brought for the purpose of a yoke, plow, roof-rafter, winnowing fan, or other such things, it would serve as firewood in the village. If it could be brought for the purpose of wood for a bed, etc., in a field-hut, it would serve as firewood in the forest. But since it cannot be used in either way, it is said thus. 'Deprived of householder’s wealth' (gihibhogā ca parihīnoti) means that he is deprived of the wealth that would have been received when the inheritance is divided by householders living at home. 'And the goal of the ascetic life' (sāmaññatthañcāti) means the goal of the ascetic life attainable by means of learning and realization, having abided in the advice of teachers and preceptors. The Teacher brought forth this simile not with respect to an immoral person, but with respect to a person of purified virtue who is lazy and afflicted by faults such as covetousness.

Tayome, bhikkhaveti kasmā āraddhaṃ? Imassa puggalassa chavālātasadisabhāvo neva mātāpitūhi kato, na ācariyupajjhāyehi, imehi pana pāpavitakkehi katoti dassanatthaṃ āraddhaṃ. Animittaṃ vā samādhinti [M 2.0279] vipassanāsamādhiṃ. So hi niccanimittādīnaṃ samugghātanena animittoti vuccati. Ettha ca cattāro satipaṭṭhānā missakā, animittasamādhi pubbabhāgo. Animittasamādhi vā missako, satipaṭṭhānā pubbabhāgāti veditabbā.

'Monks, these three' (tayome, bhikkhaveti)—why was this begun? It was begun to show that this person’s state of being like a funeral firebrand was not caused by his mother and father, nor by his teachers and preceptors, but by these evil thoughts. 'Or signless concentration' (animittaṃ vā samādhinti) means insight concentration. Indeed, it is called 'signless' because of its uprooting of the signs of permanence and so on. And here, the four foundations of mindfulness are mixed, while signless concentration is the preliminary part. Or, it should be understood that signless concentration is mixed, while the foundations of mindfulness are the preliminary part.

Dvemā, bhikkhave, diṭṭhiyoti idaṃ pana na kevalaṃ animittasamādhibhāvanā imesaṃyeva tiṇṇaṃ mahāvitakkānaṃ pahānāya saṃvattati, sassatucchedadiṭṭhīnampi pana samugghātaṃ karotīti dassanatthaṃ vuttaṃ. Na vajjavā assanti niddoso bhaveyyaṃ. Sesamettha uttānameva. Iti bhagavā imasmimpi sutte desanaṃ tīhi bhavehi vinivattetvā arahattena kūṭaṃ gaṇhi. Desanāvasāne pañcasatā bhikkhū saha paṭisambhidāhi arahattaṃ pāpuṇiṃsūti. Aṭṭhamaṃ.

'Monks, these are the two views' (dvemā, bhikkhave, diṭṭhiyoti)—this, however, is said to show that the development of signless concentration not only leads to the abandonment of these three great thoughts, but also brings about the uprooting of eternalist and annihilationist views. 'May I be without fault' (na vajjavā assanti) means 'I would be faultless.' The rest here is self-evident. Thus, in this discourse too, the Blessed One, having turned the teaching away from the three kinds of existence, brought it to a summit with arahantship. At the conclusion of the discourse, five hundred monks attained arahantship together with the analytical knowledges. The eighth.

9. Pālileyyasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Explanation of the Pālileyya Sutta

81. Navame [P 2.0304] cārikaṃ pakkāmīti kosambikānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ kalahakāle satthā ekadivasaṃ dīghītissa kosalarañño vatthuṃ āharitvā “na hi verena verāni, sammantīdha kudācana”ntiādīhi (dha. pa. 5) gāthāhi ovadati. Taṃdivasaṃ tesaṃ kalahaṃ karontānaṃyeva ratti vibhātā. Dutiyadivasepi bhagavā tameva vatthuṃ kathesi. Taṃdivasampi tesaṃ kalahaṃ karontānaṃyeva ratti vibhātā. Tatiyadivasepi bhagavā tameva vatthuṃ kathesi. Atha naṃ aññataro bhikkhu evamāha – “appossukko, bhante, bhagavā diṭṭhadhammasukhavihāraṃ anuyutto viharatu, mayametena bhaṇḍanena [V 2.0269] kalahena viggahena vivādena paññāyissāmā”ti. Satthā “pariyādiṇṇarūpacittā kho ime moghapurisā, na ime sakkā saññāpetu”nti cintetvā – “kiṃ mayhaṃ imehi, ekacāravāsaṃ vasissāmī”ti? So pātova sarīrapaṭijagganaṃ katvā kosambiyaṃ piṇḍāya caritvā kañcipi anāmantetvā ekova adutiyo cārikaṃ pakkāmi.

81. In the ninth section, 'set out on a journey' (cārikaṃ pakkāmīti): At the time of the quarrel of the Kosambi monks, one day the Teacher related the story of King Dīghīti of Kosala and advised them with verses such as: 'Hatreds are never appeased by hatred in this world' (Dhp 5). That very day, night fell while they were still quarreling. On the second day, the Blessed One again told the same story. That day too, night fell while they were still quarreling. On the third day, the Blessed One once more told the same story. Then a certain monk said to him: 'Venerable sir, let the Blessed One, being unconcerned, dwell devoted to a pleasant abiding in this very life. We ourselves will become known through this bickering, quarreling, dispute, and contention.' The Teacher, thinking, 'Indeed, these foolish men have minds whose nature is consumed; it is not possible to make them understand,' reflected, 'What use are these monks to me? I will live a solitary life.' So, early in the morning, having attended to his bodily needs, he went for alms in Kosambi and, without addressing anyone, set out on a journey all alone, without a companion.

Yasmiṃ, āvuso, samayeti idaṃ thero yasmāssa ajja bhagavā ekena bhikkhunā saddhiṃ pakkamissati, ajja dvīhi, ajja satena, ajja sahassena, ajja ekakovāti sabbo bhagavato cāro vidito pākaṭo paccakkho, tasmā āha.

'At which time, friend?' (yasmiṃ, āvuso, samayeti): The elder said this because to him, all the Blessed One’s conduct—that today the Blessed One would depart with one monk, today with two, today with a hundred, today with a thousand, today all alone—was known, evident, and directly experienced. Therefore, he said this.

Anupubbenāti [M 2.0280] gāmanigamapaṭipāṭiyā piṇḍāya caramāno ekacāravāsaṃ tāva vasamānaṃ bhikkhuṃ passitukāmo hutvā bālakaloṇakāragāmaṃ agamāsi. Tattha bhaguttherassa sakalapacchābhattañceva tiyāmarattiñca ekacāravāse ānisaṃsaṃ kathetvā punadivase tena pacchāsamaṇena piṇḍāya caritvā taṃ tattheva nivattetvā “samaggavāsaṃ vasamāne tayo kulaputte passissāmī”ti pācīnavaṃsamigadāyaṃ agamāsi. Tesampi sakalapacchābhattañceva tiyāmarattiñca ekacāravāse ānisaṃsaṃ kathetvā te tattheva nivattetvā ekakova pālileyya nagarābhimukho pakkamitvā anupubbena pālileyyanagaraṃ sampatto. Tena vuttaṃ – “anupubbena cārikaṃ caramāno yena pālileyyakaṃ, tadavasarī”ti.

'Gradually' (anupubbenāti): Wandering for alms in due order through villages and market towns, first, wishing to see a monk dwelling in solitude, he went to Bālakaloṇakāra village. There, after speaking about the benefits of solitary dwelling to Bhagu Thera for the entire post-meal period and throughout the three watches of the night, on the next day he wandered for alms with him as his attendant monk and then sent him back right there. Thinking, 'I will see the three clansmen living in harmony,' he went to the Pācīnavaṃsa deer park. To them too, he spoke about the benefits of solitary dwelling for the entire post-meal period and throughout the three watches of the night, then sent them back right there and set out all alone toward the city of Pāḷileyya. Gradually, he arrived at the city of Pāḷileyya. Therefore, it is said: 'Wandering on a journey gradually, he arrived at Pāḷileyya.'

Bhaddasālamūleti pālileyyavāsino bhagavato dānaṃ datvā [P 2.0305] pālileyyato avidūre rakkhitavanasaṇḍo nāma atthi, tattha bhagavato paṇṇasālaṃ katvā “ettha vasathā”ti paṭiññaṃ kāretvā vāsayiṃsu. Bhaddasālo pana tattheko manāpo laddhako sālarukkho. Bhagavā taṃ nagaraṃ upanissāya tasmiṃ vanasaṇḍe paṇṇasālasamīpe tasmiṃ rukkhamūle viharati. Tena vuttaṃ “bhaddasālamūle”ti.

'At the foot of the Bhaddasāla tree' (bhaddasālamūleti): The residents of Pāḷileyya, having given alms to the Blessed One, prepared a leaf-hut for him in a protected forest grove named Rakkhita not far from Pāḷileyya. Having had him make a promise, saying, 'Dwell here,' they had him stay. Now, there was a certain pleasing and splendid sāla tree there named Bhaddasāla. The Blessed One, depending on that town for alms, dwelled in that forest grove near the leaf-hut at the foot of that tree. Therefore, it is said: 'At the foot of the Bhaddasāla tree.'

Evaṃ viharante panettha tathāgate aññataro hatthināgo hatthinīhi hatthipotakādīhi gocarabhūmititthogāhanādīsu ubbāḷho yūthe ukkaṇṭhito “kiṃ me imehi hatthīhī”ti? Yūthaṃ pahāya manussapathaṃ gacchanto pālileyyakavanasaṇḍe bhagavantaṃ disvā ghaṭasahassena nibbāpitasantāpo viya nibbuto hutvā satthu santike aṭṭhāsi. So tato paṭṭhāya satthu vattapaṭivattaṃ karonto mukhadhovanaṃ [V 2.0270] deti, nhānodakaṃ āharati, dantakaṭṭhaṃ deti, pariveṇaṃ sammajjati, araññato madhurāni phalāphalāni āharitvā satthuno deti. Satthā paribhogaṃ karoti.

While the Tathāgata was dwelling thus here, a certain noble elephant, being harassed by female elephants, young elephants, and so on, in the grazing grounds, watering places, and so forth, became weary of the herd. Thinking, 'What use are these elephants to me?', he abandoned the herd. While going along a human path, he saw the Blessed One in the Pāḷileyyaka forest grove and, becoming as one whose torment has been extinguished by a thousand pots of water, he was pacified. He attended upon the Teacher. From that day on, performing the major and minor duties for the Teacher, he would offer water for washing the face, bring bathing water, offer a tooth-stick, sweep the dwelling place, and bringing sweet fruits, both great and small, from the forest, he would offer them to the Teacher. The Teacher made use of them.

Ekadivasaṃ satthā rattibhāgasamanantare caṅkamitvā pāsāṇaphalake nisīdi. Hatthīpi avidūre ṭhāne aṭṭhāsi. Satthā pacchato oloketvā na kiñci addasa, evaṃ purato ca ubhayapassesu ca. Athassa “sukhaṃ vatāhaṃ aññatra tehi bhaṇḍanakārakehi vasāmī”ti cittaṃ uppajji. Hatthinopi “mayā nāmitasākhaṃ aññe khādantā natthī”tiādīni cintetvā – “sukhaṃ vata ekakova [M 2.0281] vasāmi, satthu vattaṃ kātuṃ labhāmī”ti cittaṃ uppajji. Satthā attano cittaṃ oloketvā – “mama tāva īdisaṃ cittaṃ, kīdisaṃ nu kho hatthissā”ti tassāpi tādisameva disvā “sameti no citta”nti imaṃ udānaṃ udānesi –

One day, after walking meditation immediately following a watch of the night, the Teacher sat down on a stone slab. The elephant also waited in attendance in a suitable place nearby. The Teacher looked behind him but saw no one; likewise, he looked before him and to both sides but saw no one. Then the thought arose in him: 'Indeed, I dwell happily, apart from those quarrelsome monks.' The elephant, too, reflecting on such things as, 'There are no others who eat the branches I bend down,' thought: 'Indeed, I dwell happily all alone; I have the opportunity to perform my duties for the Teacher.' The Teacher, observing his own mind, thought: 'First, such a thought arises in me. What kind of thought, I wonder, will arise in the elephant?' Seeing that a similar thought had arisen in the elephant as well, he thought, 'Our minds are in accord,' and uttered this inspired utterance:

“Etaṃ nāgassa nāgena, īsādantassa hatthino;

Sameti cittaṃ cittena, yadeko ramatī vane”ti. (mahāva. 467);

The mind of the Nāga, the Buddha, accords with the mind of the nāga, the elephant with tusks like chariot-poles, in that each delights in dwelling alone in the forest.

Atha kho sambahulā bhikkhūti atha evaṃ tathāgate tattha viharante pañcasatā disāsu vassaṃvutthā bhikkhū. Yenāyasmā ānandoti “satthā kira bhikkhusaṅghaṃ paṇāmetvā araññaṃ paviṭṭho”ti attano dhammatāya satthu santikaṃ gantuṃ asakkontā yenāyasmā ānando, tenupasaṅkamiṃsu.

Regarding 'Then a large number of monks': While the Tathāgata was dwelling there in this way, five hundred monks who had completed the rains retreat in various regions, being unable to go into the Teacher's presence on their own accord upon hearing, 'It is said the Teacher has dismissed the Sangha of monks and entered the forest,' approached the Venerable Ānanda.

Anantarā [P 2.0306] āsavānaṃ khayoti maggānantaraṃ arahattaphalaṃ. Vicayasoti vicayena, tesaṃ tesaṃ dhammānaṃ sabhāvavicinanasamatthena ñāṇena paricchinditvāti attho. Dhammoti sāsanadhammo. Cattāro satipaṭṭhānātiādi ye ye koṭṭhāse paricchinditvā dhammo desito, tesaṃ pakāsanatthāya vuttaṃ. Samanupassanāti diṭṭhisamanupassanā. Saṅkhāro soti diṭṭhisaṅkhāro so. Tatojo so saṅkhāroti tato taṇhāto so saṅkhāro jāto. Taṇhāsampayuttesu cittesupi catūsu cittesu esa jāyati. Sāpi taṇhāti sā diṭṭhisaṅkhārassa paccayabhūtā taṇhā. Sāpi vedanāti sā taṇhāya paccayabhūtā vedanā. Sopi phassoti so vedanāya paccayo avijjāsamphasso. Sāpi avijjāti sā phassasampayuttā avijjā.

Regarding 'The destruction of the taints without interval': this is the fruit of Arahantship immediately following the path. Regarding 'this is investigation': the meaning is having discerned by means of the knowledge capable of investigating the intrinsic nature of those respective phenomena. Regarding 'Dhamma': this is the Dhamma of the Teaching. Regarding 'the four establishments of mindfulness,' and so on: this is said to make known those sections by which the textual Dhamma was delineated and taught. Regarding 'contemplation': this is the contemplation that is wrong view. Regarding 'that is a formation': that is a formation of view. Regarding 'from that, that formation arises': from that craving, that formation is born. Also in the four types of consciousness associated with craving, this formation supported by view arises. Regarding 'that craving too': that is the craving that is a condition for the formation supported by view. Regarding 'that feeling too': that is the feeling that is a condition for craving. Regarding 'that contact too': that is that contact associated with ignorance which is a condition for feeling. Regarding 'that ignorance too': that is that ignorance associated with contact.

No cassaṃ[V 2.0271], no ca me siyāti sace ahaṃ na bhaveyyaṃ, mama parikkhāropi na bhaveyya. Nābhavissaṃ, na me bhavissatīti sace pana āyatimpi ahaṃ na bhavissāmi, evaṃ mama parikkhāropi na bhavissati. Ettake ṭhāne bhagavā tena bhikkhunā gahitagahitadiṭṭhiṃ vissajjāpento āgato puggalajjhāsayenapi desanāvilāsenapi. Tatojo so saṅkhāroti taṇhāsampayuttacitte vicikicchāva natthi, kathaṃ vicikicchāsaṅkhāro taṇhāto jāyatīti? Appahīnattā. Yassa hi taṇhāya appahīnāya so [M 2.0282] uppajjati, taṃ sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Diṭṭhiyāpi eseva nayo labbhatiyeva catūsu hi cittuppādesu sampayuttadiṭṭhi nāma natthi. Yasmā pana taṇhāya appahīnattā sā uppajjati, tasmā taṃ sandhāya tatrāpi ayamattho yujjati. Iti imasmiṃ sutte tevīsatiyā ṭhānesu arahattaṃ pāpetvā vipassanā kathitā. Navamaṃ.

Regarding 'I should not be, and it should not be mine': If I were not to be, my requisites would also not be. Regarding 'I will not be, and it will not be mine': Furthermore, if in the future I will not be, then my requisites will also not be. At this point, the Blessed One proceeded, causing that monk to abandon the view he had grasped, both through consideration of the individual's disposition and through the elegance of the teaching. Regarding 'from that, that formation arises': In a mind associated with craving, there is no doubt. How then does a formation of doubt arise from craving? This is the objection. It arises because of non-abandonment. For a person in whom craving is unabandoned, that formation of doubt arises. This was said in reference to that. The very same method is also found in the case of view. Indeed, in the four types of consciousness rooted in greed and dissociated from view, there is no such thing as an associated view. However, because craving is unabandoned, that view arises. Therefore, in reference to that, this meaning is also applicable there. Thus, in this discourse, insight leading to Arahantship is taught in twenty-three instances. The ninth.

10. Puṇṇamasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Full-Moon Discourse

82. Dasame tadahuposathetiādi pavāraṇasutte vitthāritameva. Kiñcideva desanti kiñci kāraṇaṃ. Sake āsane nisīditvā puccha yadākaṅkhasīti kasmā evamāha? So kira bhikkhu pañcasatabhikkhuparivāro. Ācariye [P 2.0307] pana ṭhitake pucchante sace te bhikkhū nisīdanti, satthari gāravaṃ kataṃ hoti, ācariye agāravaṃ. Sace uṭṭhahanti, ācariye gāravaṃ kataṃ hoti, satthari agāravaṃ. Iti nesaṃ cittaṃ anekaggaṃ bhavissati, desanaṃ sampaṭicchituṃ na sakkhissanti. Tasmiṃ pana nisīditvā pucchante tesaṃ cittaṃ ekaggaṃ bhavissati, desanaṃ sampaṭicchituṃ sakkhissantīti ñatvā bhagavā evamāha. Ime nu kho, bhanteti ayaṃ thero pañcannaṃ bhikkhusatānaṃ ācariyo, pañcakkhandhamattampi nappajānātīti na vattabbo. Pañhaṃ pucchantena pana “ime pañcupādānakkhandhā, na aññe”ti evaṃ jānantena viya hutvā pucchituṃ na vaṭṭati, tasmā ajānanto viya pucchati. Tepi cassa antevāsikā “amhākaṃ ācariyo ‘ahaṃ jānāmī’ti na katheti, sabbaññutaññāṇena pana saddhiṃ saṃsanditvāva kathetī”ti sotabbaṃ saddhātabbaṃ maññissantītipi ajānanto viya pucchati.

82. In the tenth sutta, the passage beginning with 'on that Uposatha day' is explained in detail in the Pavāraṇā Sutta. As for the phrase `kiñcideva desaṃ`, it means 'for some reason'. Why did the Blessed One say, 'Having sat down in your own seat, ask what you wish'? It is said that monk had a retinue of five hundred monks. If, while their teacher was standing and asking, those monks were to sit down, respect would be shown to the Teacher (the Buddha), but disrespect would be shown to their teacher. If they were to stand up, respect would be shown to their teacher, but disrespect would be shown to the Teacher. Thus, their minds would become unfocused, and they would not be able to receive the teaching. But when that teacher asks while seated, their minds will become one-pointed, and they will be able to receive the teaching. Knowing this, the Blessed One spoke thus. Regarding the phrase 'Venerable sir, these...', it should not be said of this elder, who is the teacher of five hundred monks, 'He does not understand even the five aggregates.' However, it is not proper for one asking a question to ask as if knowing, 'These are the five aggregates of clinging, and no others.' Therefore, he asks as if not knowing. And his disciples, too, will think, 'Our teacher does not say, "I know," but speaks only after comparing it with the knowledge of omniscience,' and thus they will consider it worth listening to and worth believing. For this reason also, he asks as if not knowing.

Chandamūlakāti taṇhāchandamūlakā. Na kho bhikkhu taññeva upādānaṃ te pañcupādānakkhandhāti yasmā chandarāgamattaṃ pañcakkhandhā na hoti, tasmā idaṃ vuttaṃ. Yasmā pana sahajātato vā ārammaṇato vā khandhe muñcitvā upādānaṃ natthi, tasmā nāpi aññatra pañcahi [V 2.0272] upādānakkhandhehi upādānanti vuttaṃ. Taṇhāsampayuttasmiñhi citte vattamāne taṃcittasamuṭṭhānarūpaṃ rūpakkhandho, ṭhapetvā taṃ taṇhaṃ sesā arūpadhammā cattāro khandhāti sahajātatopi khandhe muñcitvā upādānaṃ natthi. Upādānassa pana rūpādīsu aññataraṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā uppajjanato ārammaṇatopi pañcakkhandhe muñcitvā upādānaṃ natthi. Chandarāgavemattatāti chandarāganānattaṃ. Evaṃ kho bhikkhūti evaṃ rūpārammaṇassa chandarāgassa [M 2.0283] vedanādīsu aññataraṃ ārammaṇaṃ akaraṇato siyā chandarāgavemattatā. Khandhādhivacananti [P 2.0308] khandhāti ayaṃ paññatti. Ayaṃ pana anusandhi na ghaṭiyati, kiñcāpi na ghaṭiyati, sānusandhikāva pucchā, sānusandhikaṃ vissajjanaṃ. Ayañhi thero tesaṃ tesaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ ajjhāsayena pucchati, satthāpi tesaṃ tesaṃ ajjhāsayeneva vissajjeti. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānameva. Dasamaṃ.

'Rooted in desire' means rooted in the desire that is craving. 'It is not, monk, that that very clinging is the five aggregates of clinging.' Because the five aggregates are not mere desire and lust, this was said. But because, apart from the aggregates, there is no clinging—either by way of co-nascence or by way of object—it was therefore said, 'Nor is there clinging apart from the five aggregates of clinging.' For when a mind associated with craving is occurring, the form originating from that mind is the aggregate of form; and, setting aside that craving, the remaining immaterial phenomena are the four aggregates. Thus, even by way of co-nascence, there is no clinging apart from the aggregates. Furthermore, since clinging arises by taking one of form and so on as its object, by way of object also there is no clinging apart from the five aggregates. 'The difference in desire and lust' means the diversity of desire and lust. 'Thus, monks...'—thus, because desire and lust that has form as its object does not take one of feeling and so on as its object, there would be a difference in desire and lust. 'The designation "aggregates"' means this concept 'aggregates'. But this connection does not fit. Although it does not fit, the question is indeed connected, and the answer is connected. For this elder asks according to the disposition of those various monks, and the Teacher also answers according to their disposition. The remainder is clear everywhere. The tenth [sutta].

Imassa ca pana vaggassa ekekasmiṃ sutte pañcasatā pañcasatā bhikkhū arahattaṃ pattāti.

Furthermore, in this chapter, in each and every sutta, five hundred monks attained Arahantship.

Khajjanīyavaggo aṭṭhamo.

The Khajjanīya Chapter, the Eighth.

9. Theravaggo

9. The Chapter of Elders

1. Ānandasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Ānanda Sutta

83. Theravaggassa paṭhame mantāṇiputtoti, mantāṇiyā nāma brāhmaṇiyā putto. Upādāyāti āgamma ārabbha sandhāya paṭicca. Asmīti hotīti asmīti evaṃ pavattaṃ taṇhāmānadiṭṭhipapañcattayaṃ hoti. Daharoti taruṇo. Yuvāti yobbanena samannāgato. Maṇḍanakajātikoti maṇḍanakasabhāvo maṇḍanakasīlo. Mukhanimittanti mukhapaṭibimbaṃ. Tañhi parisuddhaṃ ādāsamaṇḍalaṃ paṭicca paññāyati. Kiṃ pana taṃ olokayato sakamukhaṃ paññāyati, paramukhanti? Yadi sakaṃ bhaveyya, parammukhaṃ hutvā paññāyeyya, atha parassa bhaveyya, vaṇṇādīhi asadisaṃ hutvā paññāyeyya. Tasmā na taṃ attano, na parassa, ādāsaṃ pana nissāya nibhāsarūpaṃ nāma taṃ paññāyatīti vadanti. Atha yaṃ udake paññāyati, taṃ kena kāraṇenāti[V 2.0273]? Mahābhūtānaṃ visuddhatāya. Dhammo me abhisamitoti mayā ñāṇena catusaccadhammo abhisamāgato, sotāpannosmi jātoti kathesi. Paṭhamaṃ.

83. In the first [sutta] of the Chapter of Elders, ‘Mantāṇiputta’ means: the son of the brahmin woman named Mantāṇī. ‘Upādāya’ means: having approached, concerning, with reference to, depending on. ‘Asmīti hoti’ means: the threefold proliferation of craving, conceit, and views, which occurs as 'I am', arises. ‘Dahara’ means: young. ‘Yuvā’ means: endowed with youth. ‘Maṇḍanakajātika’ means: of an ornamental nature, habitually adorning. ‘Mukhanimitta’ means: the reflection of the face. For it appears depending on a perfectly clean mirror-disc. When one looks at it, does one's own face appear, or another's? If it were one's own, it would appear facing away; if it were another's, it would appear dissimilar in complexion and so on. Therefore, it is not one's own, nor is it another's; rather, they say, depending on the mirror, a so-called radiant form appears. The first [sutta].

2. Tissasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Tissa Sutta

84. Dutiye [M 2.0284][P 2.0309] madhurakajāto viyāti sañjātagarubhāvo viya akammañño. Disāpi meti ayaṃ puratthimā ayaṃ dakkhiṇāti evaṃ disāpi mayhaṃ na pakkhāyanti, na pākaṭā hontīti vadati. Dhammāpi maṃ na paṭibhantīti pariyattidhammāpi mayhaṃ na upaṭṭhahanti, uggahitaṃ sajjhāyitaṃ na dissatīti vadati. Vicikicchāti no mahāvicikicchā. Na hi tassa “sāsanaṃ niyyānikaṃ nu kho, na nu kho”ti vimati uppajjati. Evaṃ panassa hoti “sakkhissāmi nu kho samaṇadhammaṃ kātuṃ, udāhu pattacīvaradhāraṇamattameva karissāmī”ti.

84. In the second [sutta], ‘madhurakajāto viya’ means: as if having become heavy, the body becomes unwieldy. He says: ‘Even the directions do not appear to me thus: “This is the east, this is the south.” They are not manifest.’ He says: ‘Even the doctrinal teachings do not come to my mind; what has been learned and recited does not appear.’ ‘Vicikicchā’ means: it is not great uncertainty.
