For the doubt, ‘Is the teaching liberating, or is it not liberating?’ does not arise for him. Rather, this occurs to him: ‘Will I be able to accomplish the ascetic's duties, or will I only accomplish the mere wearing of the bowl and robes?’

Kāmānametaṃ adhivacananti yathā hi ninnaṃ pallalaṃ olokentassa dassanarāmaṇeyyakamattaṃ atthi, yo panettha otarati, taṃ caṇḍamīnākulatāya ākaḍḍhitvā anayabyasanaṃ pāpeti, evamevaṃ pañcasu kāmaguṇesu cakkhudvārādīnaṃ ārammaṇe rāmaṇeyyakamattaṃ atthi, yo panettha gedhaṃ āpajjati, taṃ ākaḍḍhitvā nirayādīsu eva pakkhipanti. Appassādā hi kāmā bahudukkhā bahupāyāsā, ādīnavo ettha bhiyyoti imaṃ atthavasaṃ paṭicca “kāmānametaṃ adhivacana”nti vuttaṃ. Ahamanuggahenāti ahaṃ dhammāmisānuggahehi [P 2.0310] anuggaṇhāmi. Abhinandīti sampaṭicchi. Na kevalañca abhinandi, imaṃ pana satthu santikā assāsaṃ labhitvā ghaṭento vāyamanto katipāhena arahatte patiṭṭhāsi. Dutiyaṃ.

‘This is a designation for sensual pleasures.’ Just as a low-lying pool may appear delightful to one who gazes upon it, but whoever descends into it is dragged away by the fierce fish and brought to ruin and disaster, so too in the case of the five strands of sensual pleasure, there is merely the quality of being a delightful object for the eye-door and so on. But whoever falls into greed for them is dragged away and cast down into hell and the like. For sensual pleasures are of little satisfaction, of much suffering, of much distress. Herein, the danger is greater. Depending on this meaning, it was said: ‘This is a designation for sensual pleasures.’ ‘Ahamanuggahenāti’ means: I will support with the support of the Dhamma and material things. ‘Abhinandi’ means: he accepted well. And he did not merely rejoice. Rather, having received this comfort from the Teacher, striving and making an effort, within just a few days he was established in Arahantship. The second [sutta].

3. Yamakasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Yamaka Sutta

85. Tatiye diṭṭhigatanti sace hissa evaṃ bhaveyya “saṅkhārā uppajjanti ceva nirujjhanti ca, saṅkhārappavattameva appavattaṃ hotī”ti, diṭṭhigataṃ nāma na bhaveyya, sāsanāvacarikaṃ ñāṇaṃ bhaveyya. Yasmā panassa “satto ucchijjati vinassatī”ti ahosi, tasmā diṭṭhigataṃ nāma jātaṃ. Thāmasā parāmāsāti diṭṭhithāmena ceva diṭṭhiparāmāsena ca.

85. In the third [sutta], as for 'a wrong view': If it were thus for him: "Formations arise and cease, and the very process of formations does not occur again," it would not be what is called a wrong view; it would be knowledge pertaining to the Dispensation. But because for him there was the view, "A being is annihilated, perishes," therefore what is called a wrong view arose. As for 'firmly grasping': by the power of view and by the grasping of view.

Yenāyasmā [V 2.0274] sāriputtoti yathā nāma paccante kupite taṃ vūpasametuṃ asakkontā rājapurisā senāpatissa vā rañño vā santikaṃ gacchanti, evaṃ diṭṭhigatavasena tasmiṃ there kupite taṃ vūpasametuṃ asakkontā te bhikkhū yena dhammarājassa dhammasenāpati āyasmā sāriputto[M 2.0285], tenupasaṅkamiṃsu. Evaṃbyākhoti tesaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ santike viya therassa sammukhā paggayha vattuṃ asakkonto olambantena hadayena “evaṃbyākho”ti āha. Taṃ kiṃ maññasi, āvusoti? Idaṃ thero tassa vacanaṃ sutvā, “nāyaṃ attano laddhiyaṃ dosaṃ passati, dhammadesanāya assa taṃ pākaṭaṃ karissāmī”ti cintetvā tiparivaṭṭaṃ desanaṃ desetuṃ ārabhi.

As for 'Where the Venerable Sāriputta was': Just as, for example, when a border region is in revolt, the king's men, being unable to pacify it, go to the presence of the general or the king, so too, when that elder was in revolt by way of his wrong view, those monks, being unable to pacify it, approached the place where the Venerable Sāriputta, the Dhamma-general of the Dhamma-King, was. As for 'Thus I declare': Being unable to speak firmly in the elder's presence, upholding his view as he had in the presence of those monks, with a drooping heart he said, 'Thus I declare.' As for 'What do you think, friend?': The elder, having heard his statement, thought, 'This one does not see the fault in his own view; by means of a Dhamma discourse I will make it apparent to him,' and he began to deliver a discourse with a triple round.

Taṃ kiṃ maññasi, āvuso yamaka, rūpaṃ tathāgatoti idaṃ kasmā āraddhaṃ? Anuyogavattaṃ dāpanatthaṃ. Tiparivaṭṭadesanāvasānasmiñhi thero sotāpanno jāto. Atha naṃ anuyogavattaṃ dāpetuṃ “taṃ kiṃ maññasī”tiādimāha? Tathāgatoti [P 2.0311] satto. Rūpaṃ vedanā saññā saṅkhārā viññāṇanti ime pañcakkhandhe sampiṇḍetvā “tathāgato”ti samanupassasīti pucchati. Ettha ca te, āvusoti idaṃ therassa anuyoge bhummaṃ. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – ettha ca te ettake ṭhāne diṭṭheva dhamme saccato thirato satte anupalabbhiyamāneti. Sace taṃ, āvusoti idametaṃ aññaṃ byākarāpetukāmo pucchati. Yaṃ dukkhaṃ taṃ niruddhanti yaṃ dukkhaṃ, tadeva niruddhaṃ, añño satto nirujjhanako nāma natthi, evaṃ byākareyyanti attho.

As for 'What do you think, friend Yamaka, is form the Tathāgata?': Why was this begun? For the purpose of making him undertake the duty of being investigated. Indeed, at the conclusion of the discourse with a triple round, the elder became a stream-enterer. Then, to make him undertake the duty of being investigated, he said, 'What do you think?' and so on. By 'Tathāgata' is meant a being. He asks: 'Do you, having lumped together these five aggregates—form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness—repeatedly regard them as the “Tathāgata”?' As for 'And here, friend': this is a locative indicator in the elder's investigation. This is what is said: And here, in this line of questioning, while for you a being is not found in truth and steadfastness in this very life. As for 'If, friend...': he asks this desiring to make him declare the other fruit, that is, Arahantship. As for 'Whatever is suffering, that has ceased': The meaning is, 'Whatever is suffering, that very thing has ceased; there is no other being whatsoever that ceases. Thus I would declare.'

Etasseva atthassāti etassa paṭhamamaggassa. Bhiyyosomattāya ñāṇāyāti atirekappamāṇassa ñāṇassa atthāya, sahavipassanakānaṃ upari ca tiṇṇaṃ maggānaṃ āvibhāvatthāyāti attho. Ārakkhasampannoti antoārakkhena ceva bahiārakkhena ca samannāgato. Ayogakkhemakāmoti catūhi yogehi khemabhāvaṃ anicchanto. Pasayhāti pasayhitvā abhibhavitvā. Anupakhajjāti anupavisitvā.

As for 'for the sake of this very meaning': of this first path. As for 'for the sake of knowledge in a greater measure': the meaning is for the sake of knowledge of exceeding measure, and for the purpose of the manifestation of the three higher paths together with insight. As for 'endowed with protection': endowed with both internal and external protection. As for 'not desiring security from the yogas': not desiring the state of security from the four bonds. As for 'having overpowered': having overpowered and overwhelmed. As for 'having entered': having approached.

Pubbuṭṭhāyītiādīsu dūratova āgacchantaṃ disvā āsanato paṭhamataraṃ vuṭṭhātīti pubbuṭṭhāyī. Tassa āsanaṃ datvā tasmiṃ nisinne pacchā nipatati nisīdatīti, pacchānipātī. Pātova vuṭṭhāya “ettakā kasituṃ gacchatha, ettakā vapitu”nti vā sabbapaṭhamaṃ vuṭṭhātīti pubbuṭṭhāyī. Sāyaṃ sabbesu attano attano vasanaṭṭhānaṃ gatesu gehassa samantato ārakkhaṃ saṃvidhāya dvārāni thaketvā sabbapacchā nipajjanatopi pacchānipātī. “Kiṃ karomi[M 2.0286], ayyaputta[V 2.0275]? Kiṃ karomi ayyaputtā”ti? Mukhaṃ olokento kiṃkāraṃ paṭisāvetīti kiṃkārapaṭissāvī. Manāpaṃ caratīti manāpacārī. Piyaṃ vadatīti piyavādī. Mittatopi naṃ saddaheyyāti mitto me ayanti saddaheyya. Vissāsaṃ āpajjeyyāti ekato pānabhojanādiṃ karonto vissāsiko bhaveyya. Saṃvissatthoti suṭṭhu vissattho.

In 'one who rises early' and so forth: 'One who rises early' (pubbuṭṭhāyī) is so called because, seeing his master coming from afar, he rises from his seat before him. 'One who lies down afterward' (pacchānipātī) is so called because, having given him a seat, he sits down after he is seated. Alternatively, 'one who rises early' is so called because he rises early in the morning and, giving instructions such as, 'So many of you, go to plow; so many, go to sow,' he rises first of all. 'One who lies down afterward' is also so called because, in the evening, after everyone has gone to their own dwelling places, he arranges protection all around the house, fastens the doors, and lies down last of all. 'One who is ready to do what is to be done' (kiṃkārapaṭissāvī) is so called because, looking at his master's face, he is ready to hear the call to service, thinking, 'What shall I do, young master? What shall I do, young master?' 'One of agreeable conduct' (manāpacārī) is so called because he conducts himself in a pleasing manner. 'One of pleasant speech' (piyavādī) is so called because he speaks pleasant words. As for 'one should trust him as a friend': one should trust him, thinking, 'This is my friend.' As for 'one should place confidence in him': by drinking and eating together and so forth, one would become intimate. As for 'one who is thoroughly confided in' (saṃvissaṭṭho): one who is very much confided in.

Evameva khoti ettha idaṃ opammasaṃsandanaṃ – bālagahapatiputto viya hi vaṭṭasannissitakāle assutavā puthujjano[P 2.0312], vadhakapaccāmitto viya abaladubbalā pañcakkhandhā, vadhakapaccāmittassa “bālagahapatiputtaṃ upaṭṭhahissāmī”ti upagatakālo viya paṭisandhikkhaṇe upagatā pañcakkhandhā, tassa hi “na me ayaṃ sahāyo, vadhakapaccatthiko aya”nti ajānanakālo viya vaṭṭanissitaputhujjanassa pañcakkhandhe “na ime mayha”nti agahetvā “mama rūpaṃ, mama vedanā, mama saññā, mama saṅkhārā, mama viññāṇa”nti gahitakālo, vadhakapaccatthikassa “mitto me aya”nti gahetvā sakkārakaraṇakālo viya “mama ime”ti gahetvā pañcannaṃ khandhānaṃ nhāpanabhojanādīhi sakkārakaraṇakālo, “ativissattho me aya”nti ñatvā sakkāraṃ karontasseva asinā sīsacchindanaṃ viya vissatthassa bālaputhujjanassa tikhiṇehi bhijjamānehi khandhehi jīvitapariyādānaṃ veditabbaṃ.

As for 'Just so indeed': here is the connection with the simile. The uninstructed ordinary person at the time of being dependent on the round of existence is like the foolish householder's son. The five weak and feeble aggregates are like the murderous enemy. The five aggregates that have come near at the moment of rebirth-linking are like the time when the murderous enemy approaches, thinking, 'I will attend upon the foolish householder's son.' The time when the ordinary person dependent on the round, not grasping the five aggregates as 'these are not mine,' grasps them as 'my form, my feeling, my perception, my formations, my consciousness' is like the time when the son does not know, 'This is not my companion; this is a murderous enemy.' The time of making offerings to the five aggregates with bathing, feeding, and so on, having grasped them as 'these are mine,' is like the time of making offerings to the enemy, having grasped him as 'this is my friend.' The exhaustion of the life faculty of the trustful, foolish ordinary person by the sharp, breaking-up aggregates should be understood as being like the cutting off of the head with a sword of the very one who is making offerings, having come to think, 'This one is my intimate.'

Upetīti upagacchati. Upādiyatīti gaṇhāti. Adhiṭṭhātīti adhitiṭṭhati. Attā meti ayaṃ me attāti. Sutavā ca kho, āvuso, ariyasāvakoti yathā pana paṇḍito gahapatiputto evaṃ upagataṃ paccatthikaṃ “paccatthiko me aya”nti ñatvā appamatto tāni tāni kammāni kāretvā anatthaṃ pariharati, atthaṃ pāpuṇāti, evaṃ sutavā ariyasāvakopi “na rūpaṃ attato samanupassatī”tiādinā nayena pañcakkhandhe ahanti vā mamanti vā agahetvā, “paccatthikā me ete”ti ñatvā rūpasattakaarūpasattakādivasena vipassanāya yojetvāva tatonidānaṃ dukkhaṃ parivajjetvā aggaphalaṃ arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti. Sesamettha uttānameva. Tatiyaṃ.

As for 'Approaches' means 'comes near.' 'Clings' means 'grasps.' 'Determines' means 'resolves.' 'Self is mine' means 'this is my self.' As for 'But, friend, the instructed noble disciple': just as a wise householder's son, knowing an approaching adversary as 'this is my adversary,' remains vigilant, has various tasks done, avoids harm, and achieves benefit, so too the instructed noble disciple, not grasping the five aggregates as 'I' or 'mine' in the way beginning with 'he does not regard form as self,' knowing 'these are my adversaries,' applies insight by way of the sevenfold contemplation of form, the sevenfold contemplation of the formless, and so on, and thereby avoids suffering originating therefrom and attains the supreme fruit of arahantship. The rest here is clear. The third.

4. Anurādhasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Anurādha Sutta

86. Catutthe araññakuṭikāyanti tasseva vihārassa paccante paṇṇasālāyaṃ. Taṃ tathāgatoti tumhākaṃ satthā tathāgato taṃ sattaṃ [M 2.0287] tathāgataṃ. Aññatra imehīti tassa kira evaṃ ahosi [V 2.0276] “ime sāsanassa paṭipakkhā paṭivilomā, yathā ime bhaṇanti, na evaṃ satthā paññāpessati, aññathā paññāpessatī”ti. Tasmā evamāha. Evaṃ vutte te aññatitthiyāti evaṃ therena attano ca paresañca samayaṃ ajānitvā vutte ekadesena sāsanasamayaṃ jānantā [P 2.0313] therassa vāde dosaṃ dātukāmā te aññatitthiyā paribbājakā āyasmantaṃ anurādhaṃ etadavocuṃ.

86. In the fourth, 'in a forest hut' means in a leaf hut at the edge of that very monastery. 'That Tathāgata' means your teacher, the Tathāgata; that being, the Tathāgata. 'Apart from these'—it seems he thought thus: 'These are opponents of the teaching, contrary to it. The Teacher will not declare it as they say; he will declare it otherwise.' Therefore, he spoke thus. 'When this was said, those other sectarians' means: when the elder spoke thus, not knowing his own and others’ tenets, those other sectarians, the wanderers—who knew the tenet of the Dispensation in part and wished to find fault with the elder’s statement—said this to the Venerable Anurādha.

Taṃ kiṃ maññasi anurādhāti satthā tassa kathaṃ sutvā cintesi – “ayaṃ bhikkhu attano laddhiyaṃ dosaṃ na jānāti, kārako panesa yuttayogo, dhammadesanāya eva naṃ jānāpessāmī”ti tiparivaṭṭaṃ desanaṃ desetukāmo “taṃ kiṃ maññasi, anurādhā”tiādimāha. Athassa tāya desanāya arahattappattassa anuyogavattaṃ āropento taṃ kiṃ maññasi, anurādha? Rūpaṃ tathāgatotiādimāha. Dukkhañceva paññapemi, dukkhassa ca nirodhanti vaṭṭadukkhañceva vaṭṭadukkhassa ca nirodhaṃ nibbānaṃ paññapemi. Dukkhanti vā vacanena dukkhasaccaṃ gahitaṃ. Tasmiṃ gahite samudayasaccaṃ gahitameva hoti, tassa mūlattā. Nirodhanti vacanena nirodhasaccaṃ gahitaṃ. Tasmiṃ gahite maggasaccaṃ gahitameva hoti tassa upāyattā. Iti pubbe cāhaṃ, anurādha, etarahi ca catusaccameva paññapemīti dasseti. Evaṃ imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭavivaṭṭameva kathitaṃ. Catutthaṃ.

“What do you think, Anurādha?”: The Teacher, having heard his words, thought: “This monk does not know the fault in his own view, yet he is a practitioner, engaged in the endeavor. I will make him understand through a Dhamma discourse.” Wishing to teach a discourse with three revolutions, he said, “What do you think, Anurādha?” and so on. Then, imposing upon him who had attained Arahantship through that discourse the practice of inquiry, he said, “What do you think, Anurādha? Is form the Tathāgata?” and so on. ‘I make known only suffering and the cessation of suffering’ means: I make known the suffering of the round of existence and its cessation, Nibbāna. Or, by the word ‘suffering,’ the truth of suffering is grasped. When that is grasped, the truth of the origin is also grasped, because it is its root. By the word ‘cessation,’ the truth of cessation is grasped. When that is grasped, the truth of the path is also grasped, because it is its means. Thus he shows: “Anurādha, both previously and now, I make known only the Four Truths.” In this way, in this sutta, only the round of existence and the escape from the round are discussed. The fourth.

5. Vakkalisuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Vakkali Sutta

87. Pañcame kumbhakāranivesaneti kumbhakārasālāyaṃ. Thero kira vutthavasso pavāretvā bhagavantaṃ dassanāya āgacchati. Tassa nagaramajjhe mahāābādho uppajji, pādā na vahanti. Atha naṃ mañcakasivikāya kumbhakārasālaṃ āhariṃsu. Sā ca sālā tesaṃ kammasālā, na nivesanasālā. Taṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ “kumbhakāranivesane viharatī”ti. Bāḷhagilānoti adhimattagilāno. Samadhosīti samantato adhosi, calanākārena apacitiṃ dassesi. Vattaṃ kiretaṃ bāḷhagilānenapi buḍḍhataraṃ disvā uṭṭhānākārena apaciti dassetabbā. Tena pana “mā cali mā calī”ti vattabbo. Santimāni āsanānīti buddhakālasmiñhi ekassapi bhikkhuno [M 2.0288] vasanaṭṭhāne [P 2.0314] “sace satthā āgacchissati, idha nisīdissatī”ti āsanaṃ paññattameva hoti antamaso phalakamattampi paṇṇasanthāramattampi. Khamanīyaṃ yāpanīyanti kacci dukkhaṃ khamituṃ iriyāpathaṃ vā yāpetuṃ sakkāti pucchati. Paṭikkamantīti nivattanti. Abhikkamantīti adhigacchanti[V 2.0277]. Paṭikkamosānanti paṭikkamo etāsaṃ. Sīlato na upavadatīti sīlaṃ ārabbha sīlabhāvena na upavadati. Cirapaṭikāhanti cirapaṭiko ahaṃ, cirato paṭṭhāya ahanti attho. Pūtikāyenāti attano suvaṇṇavaṇṇampi kāyaṃ bhagavā dhuvapaggharaṇaṭṭhena evamāha. Yo kho, vakkali, dhammanti idha bhagavā “dhammakāyo kho, mahārāja, tathāgato”ti vuttaṃ dhammakāyataṃ dasseti. Navavidho hi lokuttaradhammo tathāgatassa kāyo nāma.

87. In the fifth, 'in the potter's dwelling' means in the potter's hall. It is said that the elder, having completed the rains-residence and performed the Pavāraṇā ceremony, was coming to see the Blessed One. In the middle of the city, a great illness arose in him, and his feet could not carry him. Then they brought him to the potter's hall on a bed-palanquin. And that hall was their workshop, not a dwelling hall. With reference to that, it was said, 'he dwells in the potter's dwelling.' 'Severely ill' means extremely ill. 'He trembled' means he trembled all over, showing reverence by the act of moving. It is said that this is the custom: even one who is severely ill, upon seeing one who is more senior, should show reverence by the gesture of rising. But that senior one should say, 'Do not move, do not move.' 'There are seats' means: indeed, in the Buddha's time, in the dwelling place of even a single monk, a seat was always prepared with the thought, 'If the Teacher comes, he will sit here'—at the very least a mere plank or a mere spread of leaves. 'Is it bearable, is it sustainable?' means: he asks, 'Is it possible to endure the pain or to maintain the posture?' 'They recede' means they turn back. 'They advance' means they attain. 'The receding of them' means their receding. 'Does not reproach on account of virtue' means: he does not reproach based on virtue, in the manner of virtue. 'Cirapaṭikāhaṃ' is to be parsed as 'cirapaṭiko ahaṃ'; the meaning is 'I, for a long time,' or 'I, from a long time ago.' 'With this putrid body' means: the Blessed One, on account of its nature of constantly oozing, spoke thus of his own body, even though it was of a golden hue. In 'He who, Vakkali, [sees] the Dhamma,' here the Blessed One shows the state of being the Dhamma-body which was spoken of thus: 'The Tathāgata, O great king, is indeed the Dhamma-body.' For the nine-fold supramundane Dhamma is called the body of the Tathāgata.

Idāni therassa tiparivaṭṭadhammadesanaṃ ārabhanto taṃ kiṃ maññasītiādimāha. Kāḷasilāti kāḷasilāvihāro. Vimokkhāyāti maggavimokkhatthāya. Suvimutto vimuccissatīti arahattaphalavimuttiyā vimutto hutvā vimuccissati. Tā kira devatā “yena nīhārena iminā vipassanā āraddhā, anantarāyena arahattaṃ pāpuṇissatī”ti ñatvā evamāhaṃsu. Apāpakanti alāmakaṃ. Satthaṃ āharesīti thero kira adhimāniko ahosi. So samādhivipassanāhi vikkhambhitānaṃ kilesānaṃ samudācāraṃ apassanto “khīṇāsavomhī”ti saññī hutvā “kiṃ me iminā dukkhena jīvitena? Satthaṃ āharitvā marissāmī”ti tikhiṇena satthena kaṇṭhanāḷaṃ chindi. Athassa dukkhā vedanā uppajji. So tasmiṃ khaṇe attano puthujjanabhāvaṃ ñatvā avissaṭṭhakammaṭṭhānattā sīghaṃ mūlakammaṭṭhānaṃ ādāya sammasanto arahattaṃ pāpuṇitvāva kālamakāsi. Paccavekkhaṇā panassa ca kathaṃ ahosīti? Khīṇāsavassa ekūnavīsati [P 2.0315] paccavekkhaṇā na sabbāva avassaṃ laddhabbā, tikhiṇenāpi pana asinā sīse chijjante ekaṃ dve ñāṇāni avassaṃ uppajjanti.

Now, beginning the Dhamma-discourse with its three revolutions for the elder, he said, 'What do you think?' and so on. 'At Kāḷasilā' refers to the Kāḷasilā monastery. 'For liberation' means for the sake of liberation by the path. 'Well-liberated, he will be liberated' means, having been liberated by the liberation of the fruit of Arahantship, he will be liberated. It is said that those deities, knowing, 'By whichever method this one has undertaken insight, he will attain Arahantship without obstruction,' spoke thus. 'Not evil' means not defective. 'He brought a knife' means: it is said that the elder had become conceited. Not seeing the manifestation of defilements suppressed by concentration and insight, he perceived, 'I am one whose cankers are destroyed,' and thought, 'What use is this painful life to me? I will bring a knife and die.' With a sharp knife, he cut his windpipe. Then, a painful feeling arose in him. At that moment, realizing his status as a worldling, because his meditation subject had not been abandoned, he quickly took up the root meditation subject, and while contemplating, he attained Arahantship and only then passed away. But how did his reviewing occur? For one whose cankers are destroyed, all nineteen reviewings need not necessarily be obtained. Yet, even when the head is being severed by a sharp sword, one or two knowledges must inevitably arise.

Vivattakkhandhanti parivattakkhandhaṃ. Semānanti sayamānaṃ. Thero kira uttānako nipanno satthaṃ āhari. Tassa sarīraṃ yathāṭhitameva ahosi. Sīsaṃ pana dakkhiṇapassena parivattitvā aṭṭhāsi. Ariyasāvakā hi yebhuyyena dakkhiṇapasseneva kālaṃ karonti. Tenassa sarīraṃ yathāṭhitaṃyeva ahosi. Sīsaṃ pana dakkhiṇapassena parivattitvā ṭhitaṃ. Taṃ sandhāya vivattakkhandho nāma jātotipi vadanti. Dhūmāyitattanti dhūmāyanabhāvaṃ[M 2.0289]. Timirāyitattanti timirāyanabhāvaṃ. Dhūmavalāhakaṃ viya timiravalāhakaṃ viya cāti attho. Pañcamaṃ.

'With neck turned' means with neck turned over. 'Lying down' means lying down. The Elder, it is said, lay on his back and brought the knife. His body remained just as it was. The head, however, turned to the right side and remained so. For noble disciples, for the most part, pass away on their right side. Therefore his body remained just as it was, but his head, having turned to the right side, remained so. With reference to that, they also say that he came to be called 'one with a turned neck'. 'A state of smokiness' means the state of emitting smoke. 'A state of darkness' means the state of being dark. The meaning is 'like a cloud of smoke and like a cloud of darkness'. The Fifth.

6. Assajisuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Assaji Sutta

88. Chaṭṭhe kassapakārāmeti kassapaseṭṭhinā kārite ārāme. Kāyasaṅkhāreti assāsapassāse[V 2.0278]. So hi te catutthajjhānena passambhitvā passambhitvā vihāsi. Evaṃ hotīti idāni taṃ samādhiṃ appaṭilabhantassa evaṃ hoti. No cassāhaṃ parihāyāmīti kacci nu kho ahaṃ sāsanato na parihāyāmi? Tassa kira ābādhadosena appitappitā samāpatti parihāyi, tasmā evaṃ cintesi. Samādhisārakā samādhisāmaññāti samādhiṃyeva sārañca sāmaññañca maññanti. Mayhaṃ pana sāsane na etaṃ sāraṃ, vipassanāmaggaphalāni sāraṃ. So tvaṃ samādhito parihāyanto kasmā cintesi “sāsanato parihāyāmī”ti. Evaṃ theraṃ assāsetvā idānissa tiparivaṭṭaṃ dhammadesanaṃ ārabhanto taṃ kiṃ maññasītiādimāha. Athassa tiparivaṭṭadesanāvasāne arahattaṃ pattassa satatavihāraṃ dassento so sukhaṃ ce vedanaṃ vedayatītiādimāha[P 2.0316]. Tattha anabhinanditāti pajānātīti sukhavedanāya tāva abhinandanā hotu, dukkhavedanāya kathaṃ hotīti? Dukkhaṃ patvā sukhaṃ pattheti, yadaggena sukhaṃ pattheti, tadaggena dukkhaṃ patthetiyeva. Sukhavipariṇāmena hi dukkhaṃ āgatameva hotīti evaṃ dukkhe abhinandanā veditabbā. Sesaṃ pubbe vuttanayamevāti. Chaṭṭhaṃ.

88. In the sixth: 'Kassapakārāma' means the monastery built by the wealthy merchant Kassapa. 'Bodily formation' means the in-breaths and out-breaths. For he, having repeatedly tranquilized them with the fourth jhāna, dwelt therein. 'Thus it is' means: now, for one not attaining that concentration, it happens thus. 'I hope I am not declining' means: 'Could it be that I am not declining from the Dispensation?' For him, it is said, due to the fault of his illness, the attainment he repeatedly entered into declined; therefore, he thought thus. 'Those who consider concentration the essence and the ascetic life' means they regard concentration itself as both the essence and the ascetic life. But in my Dispensation, this is not the essence; insight, the path, and its fruition are the essence. You are declining from concentration, so why do you think, 'I am declining from the Dispensation'? Thus, having comforted the elder, now, beginning the Dhamma discourse with its three rounds for him, he spoke the words beginning with, 'What do you think of this?' Then, at the end of the discourse with its three rounds, showing the constant abiding of him who had attained Arahantship, he spoke the words beginning with, 'If he feels a pleasant feeling...'. Therein, regarding 'he understands it as not being delighted in': let there be delighting in a pleasant feeling, but how can there be delighting in a painful feeling? Having experienced pain, one longs for pleasure. To the extent that one longs for pleasure, to that same extent one indeed longs for pain. For through the changing nature of pleasure, pain inevitably comes. Thus, delighting in pain should be understood in this way. The rest is in the same way as stated before. The Sixth.

7. Khemakasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Khemaka Sutta

89. Sattame attaniyanti attano parikkhārajātaṃ. Asmīti adhigatanti asmīti evaṃ pavattā taṇhāmānā adhigatā. Sandhāvanikāyāti punappunaṃ gamanāgamanena. Upasaṅkamīti badarikārāmato gāvutamattaṃ ghositārāmaṃ agamāsi. Dāsakatthero pana catukkhattuṃ gamanāgamanena taṃdivasaṃ dviyojanaṃ addhānaṃ āhiṇḍi. Kasmā pana taṃ therā pahiṇiṃsu? Vissutassa dhammakathikassa santikā dhammaṃ suṇissāmāti. Sayaṃ kasmā na gatāti? Therassa vasanaṭṭhānaṃ araññaṃ sambādhaṃ, tattha saṭṭhimattānaṃ therānaṃ ṭhātuṃ vā nisīdituṃ vā okāso natthīti na gatā. “Idhāgantvā amhākaṃ dhammaṃ kathetū”tipi [M 2.0290] kasmā pana na pahiṇiṃsūti? Therassa ābādhikattā. Atha kasmā punappunaṃ pahiṇiṃsūti? Sayameva ñatvā amhākaṃ kathetuṃ āgamissatīti. Theropi tesaṃ ajjhāsayaṃ ñatvāva agamāsīti.

89. In the seventh: 'belonging to oneself' means one's own requisites. 'The conceit "I am" has been acquired' means that the craving and conceit that proceed as 'I am' have been acquired. 'Running back and forth' means by repeatedly going and coming. 'Approached' means he went from the Badarikārāma to the Ghositārāma, a distance of about a gāvuta. The elder Dāsaka, however, by going and coming four times that day, travelled a distance of two yojanas. Why then did the elders send him? Thinking, 'We will hear the Dhamma from the renowned Dhamma preacher.' Why did they not go themselves? The elder’s dwelling place was a cramped forest hermitage, and there was no space there for about sixty elders to stand or to sit, so they did not go. Why then did they not send the message, 'Come here and teach the Dhamma to us'? Because of the elder's illness. Then why did they send repeatedly? Thinking, 'Knowing on his own, he will come to teach us.' The elder, too, having understood their intention, went.

Na khvāhaṃ, āvuso, rūpanti yo hi rūpameva asmīti vadati, tena itare cattāro khandhā paccakkhātā honti. Yo aññatra rūpā vadati, tena rūpaṃ paccakkhātaṃ hoti. Vedanādīsupi eseva nayo. Therassa pana samūhato pañcasupi khandhesu asmīti adhigato, tasmā [V 2.0279] evamāha. Hotevāti hotiyeva. Anusahagatoti sukhumo. Ūseti chārikākhāre. Khāreti ūsakhāre. Sammadditvāti temetvā khādetvā.

Regarding 'I do not, friend, [say] I am form': for whoever says, 'I am form itself,' by him the other four aggregates are rejected. Whoever speaks of being apart from form, by him form is rejected. This same method applies to feeling and so on. For the elder, however, the conceit 'I am' was acquired with regard to the five aggregates collectively; therefore, he spoke thus. 'It exists' means it exists indeed. 'Accompanied by' means subtle. 'Lye' means ash-lye. 'Alkali' means salt-lye. 'Having scrubbed' means having moistened and scoured.

Evameva [P 2.0317] khoti ettha idaṃ opammasaṃsandanaṃ – kiliṭṭhavatthaṃ viya hi puthujjanassa cittācāro, tayo khārā viya tisso anupassanā, tīhi khārehi dhotavatthaṃ viya desanāya madditvā ṭhito anāgāmino cittācāro, anusahagato ūsādigandho viya arahattamaggavajjhā kilesā, gandhakaraṇḍako viya arahattamaggañāṇaṃ gandhakaraṇḍakaṃ āgamma anusahagatānaṃ ūsagandhādīnaṃ samugghāto viya arahattamaggena sabbakilesakkhayo, gandhaparibhāvitavatthaṃ nivāsetvā chaṇadivase antaravīthiyaṃ sugandhagandhino vicaraṇaṃ viya khīṇāsavassa sīlagandhādīhi dasa disā upavāyantassa yathākāmacāro.

Just so, indeed: here is the application of the simile. The conduct of an ordinary person's mind is like a soiled cloth. The three contemplations are like the three kinds of lye. The conduct of a non-returner's mind, established after being subdued by the teaching, is like a cloth washed with the three kinds of lye. The defilements to be abandoned by the path of Arahantship are like the lingering smell of lye and so on. The knowledge of the path of Arahantship is like a perfume box. The complete destruction of all defilements by the path of Arahantship is like the eradication of the lingering smell of lye and so on by means of a perfume box. The free movement of one whose taints are destroyed, whose fragrance of virtue and other qualities spreads to the ten directions, is like the wandering of one fragrant with sweet scents in the inner city streets on a festival day, having worn a cloth imbued with perfume.

Ācikkhitunti kathetuṃ. Desetunti pakāsetuṃ. Paññāpetunti jānāpetuṃ. Paṭṭhapetunti patiṭṭhāpetuṃ. Vivaritunti vivaṭaṃ kātuṃ. Vibhajitunti suvibhattaṃ kātuṃ. Uttānīkātunti uttānakaṃ kātuṃ. Saṭṭhimattānaṃ therānanti te kira therena kathitakathitaṭṭhāne vipassanaṃ paṭṭhapetvā uparūpari sammasantā desanāpariyosāne arahattaṃ pāpuṇiṃsu. Theropi aññena nīhārena akathetvā vipassanāsahagatacitteneva kathesi. Tasmā sopi arahattaṃ pāpuṇi. Tena vuttaṃ – “saṭṭhimattānaṃ therānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ anupādāya āsavehi cittāni vimucciṃsu āyasmato khemakassa cā”ti. Sattamaṃ.

By 'to declare' is meant 'to state'. By 'to teach' is meant 'to make clear'. By 'to make known' is meant 'to cause to know'. By 'to establish' is meant 'to found'. By 'to open up' is meant 'to make open'. By 'to analyze' is meant 'to make well-divided'. By 'to make plain' is meant 'to make evident'. Regarding the sixty elders: it is said that those elders, having established insight at each and every point taught by the elder and contemplating it stage by stage, attained Arahantship at the conclusion of the discourse. The elder, too, did not teach in any other manner, but taught only with a mind accompanied by insight. Therefore, he too attained Arahantship. For that reason it was said: 'The minds of about sixty elder monks were liberated from the taints through non-clinging, and that of the Venerable Khemaka as well.' The Seventh.

8. Channasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Channa Sutta

90. Aṭṭhame [M 2.0291] āyasmā channoti tathāgatena saddhiṃ ekadivase jāto mahābhinikkhamanadivase saddhiṃ nikkhamitvā puna aparabhāge satthu santike pabbajitvā “amhākaṃ buddho amhākaṃ dhammo”ti evaṃ makkhī ceva paḷāsī ca hutvā sabrahmacārīnaṃ pharusavācāya saṅghaṭṭanaṃ karonto thero. Avāpuraṇaṃ ādāyāti kuñcikaṃ gahetvā. Vihārena vihāraṃ upasaṅkamitvāti ekaṃ vihāraṃ pavisitvā tato aññaṃ, tato aññanti evaṃ tena tena vihārena taṃ taṃ vihāraṃ upasaṅkamitvā. Etadavoca ovadantu manti kasmā evaṃ mahantena ussāhena tattha tattha gantvā etaṃ avocāti? Uppannasaṃvegatāya. Tassa hi parinibbute satthari dhammasaṅgāhakattherehi pesito āyasmā [V 2.0280] ānando kosambiṃ [P 2.0318] gantvā brahmadaṇḍaṃ adāsi. So dinne brahmadaṇḍe sañjātapariḷāho visaññībhūto patitvā puna saññaṃ labhitvā vuṭṭhāya ekassa bhikkhuno santikaṃ gato, so tena saddhiṃ kiñci na kathesi. Aññassa santikaṃ agamāsi, sopi na kathesīti evaṃ sakalavihāraṃ vicaritvā nibbinno pattacīvaraṃ ādāya bārāṇasiṃ gantvā uppannasaṃvego tattha tattha gantvā evaṃ avoca.

90. In the eighth sutta, the Venerable Channa was born on the same day as the Tathāgata and went forth together with him on the day of the Great Renunciation. Later, having taken ordination in the presence of the Teacher, the elder, having become both a denigrator and a rival with the attitude 'Our Buddha, our Dhamma,' caused friction among his fellow monks with harsh speech. 'Taking the opener' means taking the key. 'He went from monastery to monastery' means having entered one monastery, then another, and from there yet another, he thus approached each and every monastery. He said this: 'Let them advise me!' Why, with such great effort, did he go here and there and say this? Because of the arising of spiritual urgency. For when the Teacher had attained Parinibbāna, the Venerable Ānanda was sent by the elder compilers of the Dhamma to Kosambī to impose the Brahma-penalty on him. When the Brahma-penalty was imposed, he became greatly agitated, fell down unconscious, and then, regaining consciousness, he rose and approached one monk, but that monk did not speak with him at all. He went to another, but that one also did not speak. Having wandered the entire monastery in this way, he became disheartened. Taking his bowl and robe, he went to Bārāṇasī. There, with spiritual urgency having arisen, he went from place to place and said this.

Sabbe saṅkhārā aniccāti sabbe tebhūmakasaṅkhārā aniccā. Sabbe dhammā anattāti sabbe catubhūmakadhammā anattā. Iti sabbepi te bhikkhū theraṃ ovadantā aniccalakkhaṇaṃ anattalakkhaṇanti dveva lakkhaṇāni kathetvā dukkhalakkhaṇaṃ na kathayiṃsu. Kasmā? Evaṃ kira nesaṃ ahosi – “ayaṃ bhikkhu vādī dukkhalakkhaṇe paññāpiyamāne rūpaṃ dukkhaṃ…pe… viññāṇaṃ dukkhaṃ, maggo dukkho, phalaṃ dukkhanti ‘tumhe dukkhappattā bhikkhū nāmā’ti gahaṇaṃ gaṇheyya, yathā gahaṇaṃ gahetuṃ na sakkoti, evaṃ niddosamevassa katvā kathessāmā”ti dveva lakkhaṇāni kathayiṃsu.

‘All formations are impermanent’ means all formations in the three planes are impermanent. ‘All dhammas are not-self’ means all dhammas in the four planes are not-self. Thus, all those monks, while advising the elder, spoke only of the two characteristics—the characteristic of impermanence and the characteristic of not-self—and did not speak of the characteristic of suffering. Why? It is said their thought was this: ‘This monk is quick to argue. When the characteristic of suffering is being explained—that form is suffering… consciousness is suffering, the path is suffering, the fruit is suffering—he might grasp the wrong view, thinking, “You are monks who have attained suffering!” So that he would not be able to grasp it wrongly, we will teach in a way that is faultless for him.’ Thus, they spoke only of the two characteristics.

Paritassanā upādānaṃ uppajjatīti paritassanā ca upādānañca uppajjati. Paccudāvattati mānasaṃ, atha ko carahi me attāti yadi rūpādīsu ekopi anattā, atha ko nāma me attāti evaṃ paṭinivattati “mayhaṃ mānasa”nti. Ayaṃ kira thero paccaye apariggahetvā vipassanaṃ paṭṭhapesi, sāssa dubbalavipassanā attagāhaṃ pariyādātuṃ asakkuṇantī saṅkhāresu suññato [M 2.0292] upaṭṭhahantesu “ucchijjissāmi vinassissāmī”ti ucchedadiṭṭhiyā ceva paritassanāya ca paccayo ahosi. So ca attānaṃ pāpate papatantaṃ viya disvā, “paritassanā upādānaṃ uppajjati, paccudāvattati mānasaṃ, atha ko carahi me attā”ti āha. Na kho panevaṃ dhammaṃ passato hotīti catusaccadhammaṃ passantassa evaṃ na hoti. Tāvatikā vissaṭṭhīti tattako vissāso. Sammukhā metanti thero tassa vacanaṃ sutvā, “kīdisā nu kho imassa dhammadesanā sappāyā”ti? Cintento tepiṭakaṃ [P 2.0319] buddhavacanaṃ vicinitvā kaccānasuttaṃ (saṃ. ni. 2.15) addasa “idaṃ āditova diṭṭhiviniveṭhanaṃ katvā majjhe buddhabalaṃ dīpetvā saṇhasukhumapaccayākāraṃ pakāsayamānaṃ gataṃ, idamassa desessāmī”ti dassento “sammukhā meta”ntiādimāha. Aṭṭhamaṃ.

‘Terror and clinging arise’ means that both terror and clinging to views arise. ‘The mind turns back, thinking, “Then what indeed is my self?”’ means: if even one thing among form and so on is not-self, then what is my self? In this way, my mind turns back. It seems this elder established insight meditation without having comprehended the conditions. His weak insight, being unable to completely overcome the grasping of self, became a condition for both the annihilationist view—‘I will be annihilated, I will perish!’—and for terror, when the formations appeared as empty. And he, seeing himself as if falling from a cliff, said: ‘Terror and clinging arise; the mind turns back, thinking, “Then what indeed is my self?”’ ‘But it is not so for one who sees the Dhamma’ means: for one who sees the Dhamma of the Four Truths, it is not so. ‘There is that much tranquility’ means there is that much trust. ‘Face to face with me…’—the elder, having heard his words and thinking, ‘What kind of Dhamma teaching would be suitable for him?’ searched through the Tipiṭaka, the word of the Buddha, and saw the Kaccāna Sutta. He saw: ‘This discourse, right from the beginning, serves to disentangle wrong views; in the middle, it reveals the Buddha’s power; and it proceeds by explaining the gentle and subtle dependent origination. I will teach this to him.’ Intending to teach it, he said the passage beginning, ‘Face to face with me…’. The eighth.

9-10. Rāhulasuttādivaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the Rāhula Sutta and So Forth

91-92. Navamadasamāni [V 2.0281] rāhulasaṃyutte (saṃ. ni. 2.188) vuttatthāneva. Kevalaṃ hetāni ayaṃ theravaggoti katvā idhāgatānīti. Navamadasamāni.

The ninth and tenth suttas have the same meaning as stated in the Rāhula Saṃyutta. They were brought here by the compilers simply because they considered, 'This is the Chapter on the Elders.' The ninth and tenth suttas.

Theravaggo navamo.

The Ninth Chapter: The Elders.

10. Pupphavaggo

10. The Chapter on Flowers

1. Nadīsuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Discourse on the River

93. Pupphavaggassa paṭhame pabbateyyāti pabbate pavattā. Ohārinīti sote patitapatitāni tiṇapaṇṇakaṭṭhādīni heṭṭhāhārinī. Dūraṅgamāti nikkhantaṭṭhānato paṭṭhāya catupañcayojanasatagāminī. Sīghasotāti caṇḍasotā. Kāsātiādīni sabbāni tiṇajātāni. Rukkhāti eraṇḍādayo dubbalarukkhā. Te naṃ ajjholambeyyunti te tīre jātāpi onamitvā aggehi udakaṃ phusantehi adhiolambeyyuṃ, upari lambeyyunti attho. Palujjeyyunti samūlamattikāya saddhiṃ sīse pateyyuṃ. So tehi ajjhotthaṭo vālukamattikodakehi mukhaṃ pavisantehi mahāvināsaṃ pāpuṇeyya.

1. In the first sutta of the Chapter on the Flower, 'pabbateyyā' means originating in the mountains. 'Ohārinī' means carrying downwards the grass, leaves, sticks, and so forth that have fallen into the stream. 'Dūraṅgamā' means traveling four or five hundred yojanas from its place of origin. 'Sīghasotā' means having a violent current. 'Kāsa' and so forth are all types of grass. 'Rukkhā' refers to weak trees such as castor-oil plants. 'Te naṃ ajjholambeyyuṃ' means that though growing on the bank, they would bend down and, with their tips touching the water, would hang over it; this is the meaning. 'Palujjeyyuṃ' means they would fall on his head along with the soil with its roots. That man, being overwhelmed by them, with sand, soil, and water entering his mouth, would come to great destruction.

Evameva [M 2.0293] khoti ettha sote patitapuriso viya vaṭṭasannissito bālaputhujjano daṭṭhabbo, ubhatotīre kāsādayo viya dubbalapañcakkhandhā, “ime gahitāpi maṃ tāretuṃ na sakkhissantī”ti tassa purisassa ajānitvā gahaṇaṃ viya ime khandhā “na mayhaṃ sahāyā”ti bālaputhujjanassa ajānitvā catūhi gāhehi gahaṇaṃ, gahitagahitānaṃ palujjanattā purisassa byasanappatti viya catūhi gāhehi gahitānaṃ khandhānaṃ vipariṇāme bālaputhujjanassa sokādibyasanappatti veditabbā. Paṭhamaṃ.

93. Just so, in this case, the foolish ordinary person, dependent on the cycle of existence, should be understood as being like a man who has fallen into a stream. The five weak aggregates should be understood as being like the kāsa grass and so forth on both banks. Just as that man, not knowing, 'Even if I grasp these, they will not be able to save me,' grasps them, so too should be understood the grasping of the foolish ordinary person with the four kinds of grasping, not knowing, 'These aggregates are not my companions.' Just as the man meets with disaster because whatever he grasps breaks away, so too, due to the change in the aggregates grasped with the four kinds of grasping, the foolish ordinary person's attainment of disaster, such as sorrow, should be known. The first sutta is finished.

2. Pupphasuttavaṇṇanā

Commentary on the Discourse on the Flower

94. Dutiye [V 2.0282][P 2.0320] vivadatīti “aniccaṃ dukkhaṃ anattā asubha”nti yathāsabhāvena vadantena saddhiṃ “niccaṃ sukhaṃ attā subha”nti vadanto vivadati. Lokadhammoti khandhapañcakaṃ. Tañhi lujjanasabhāvattā lokadhammoti vuccati. Kinti karomīti kathaṃ karomi? Mayhañhi paṭipattikathanameva bhāro, paṭipattipūraṇaṃ pana kulaputtānaṃ bhāroti dasseti. Imasmiṃ sutte tayo lokā kathitā. “Nāhaṃ, bhikkhave, lokenā”ti ettha hi sattaloko kathito, “atthi, bhikkhave, loke lokadhammo”ti ettha saṅkhāraloko, “tathāgato loke jāto loke saṃvaḍḍho”ti ettha okāsaloko kathito. Dutiyaṃ.

94. In the second discourse, vivadati means 'he disputes'; that is, one who says, 'It is permanent, pleasant, self, attractive,' disputes with one who speaks according to its true nature, saying, 'It is impermanent, suffering, non-self, unattractive.' Lokadhammo means the five aggregates. For they are called lokadhammo because they have the nature of perishing. Kinti karomi means 'What can I do?' This shows: 'Indeed, for me, the burden is only to explain the practice, but to fulfill the practice is the burden of the sons of good family.' In this discourse, three worlds are taught. In the passage, 'Bhikkhus, I do not dispute with the world,' the world of beings is taught. In the passage, 'Bhikkhus, there exists in the world a worldly phenomenon,' the world of formations is taught. In the passage, 'The Tathāgata was born in the world, grew up in the world,' the world of space is taught. The second is finished.

3. Pheṇapiṇḍūpamasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Discourse on the Simile of the Lump of Foam

95. Tatiye gaṅgāya nadiyā tīreti ayujjhapuravāsino aparimāṇabhikkhuparivāraṃ cārikaṃ caramānaṃ tathāgataṃ attano nagaraṃ sampattaṃ disvā ekasmiṃ gaṅgāya nivattanaṭṭhāne mahāvanasaṇḍamaṇḍitappadese satthu vihāraṃ katvā adaṃsu. Bhagavā tattha viharati. Taṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ “gaṅgāya nadiyā tīre”ti. Tatra kho bhagavā bhikkhū āmantesīti tasmiṃ vihāre vasanto bhagavā sāyanhasamayaṃ gandhakuṭito nikkhamitvā gaṅgātīre paññattavarabuddhāsane nisinno gaṅgāya nadiyā āgacchantaṃ mahantaṃ pheṇapiṇḍaṃ disvā, “mama sāsane pañcakkhandhanissitaṃ ekaṃ dhammaṃ kathessāmī”ti cintetvā parivāretvā nisinne bhikkhū āmantesi.

95. In the third discourse, on the bank of the river Ganges refers to this: the residents of Ayujjhapura, seeing the Tathāgata traveling on tour with an immeasurable retinue of bhikkhus arriving at their city, built a monastery for the Teacher in a place adorned with a great forest grove at a bend of the Ganges River, and they gave it. The Blessed One dwelt there. It was with reference to this that it was said, 'on the bank of the river Ganges.' There, the Blessed One addressed the bhikkhus means that while dwelling in that monastery, the Blessed One, emerging from his fragrant chamber in the evening, sat on the excellent Buddha-seat that had been prepared on the bank of the Ganges. Seeing a large lump of foam coming down the river Ganges, he thought, 'I will teach a certain teaching in my dispensation based on the five aggregates.' Then he addressed the bhikkhus who were seated surrounding him.

Mahantaṃ [M 2.0294] pheṇapiṇḍanti uṭṭhānuṭṭhāne badarapakkappamāṇato paṭṭhāya anusotāgamanena anupubbena pavaḍḍhitvā pabbatakūṭamattaṃ jātaṃ, yattha udakasappādayo anekapāṇayo nivasanti, evarūpaṃ mahantaṃ pheṇapiṇḍaṃ. Āvaheyyāti āhareyya. So panāyaṃ pheṇapiṇḍo uṭṭhitaṭṭhānepi bhijjati[P 2.0321], thokaṃ gantvāpi, ekadviyojanādivasena dūraṃ gantvāpi, antarā pana abhijjantopi mahāsamuddaṃ patvā avassameva bhijjati. Nijjhāyeyyāti olokeyya. Yoniso upaparikkheyyāti kāraṇena upaparikkheyya. Kiñhi siyā, bhikkhave, pheṇapiṇḍe sāroti, bhikkhave, pheṇapiṇḍamhi sāro nāma kiṃ bhaveyya? Vilīyitvā viddhaṃseyyeva.

A great lump of foam means a great lump of foam of this kind: starting from the size of a ripe jujube fruit at each place it arises, it gradually grows by flowing downstream until it becomes as large as a mountain peak, a place where water snakes and many other creatures dwell. Āvaheyya means 'he should bring it.' But this lump of foam breaks apart even at the place where it arises, or after going a short distance, or after traveling far for one or two leagues and so forth. Yet, even if it does not break apart along the way, upon reaching the great ocean, it inevitably breaks apart. Nijjhāyeyya means 'he should look at it.' Yoniso upaparikkheyya means 'he should investigate it with reason.' For what substance, bhikkhus, could there be in a lump of foam? means, 'Bhikkhus, what could be called a substance in a lump of foam?' It would just dissolve and be destroyed.

Evameva [V 2.0283] khoti yathā pheṇapiṇḍo nissāro, evaṃ rūpampi niccasāradhuvasāraattasāravirahena nissārameva. Yathā ca so “iminā pattaṃ vā thālakaṃ vā karissāmī”ti gahetuṃ na sakkā, gahitopi tamatthaṃ na sādheti, bhijjati eva, evaṃ rūpampi niccanti vā dhuvanti vā ahanti vā mamanti vā gahetuṃ na sakkā, gahitampi na tathā tiṭṭhati, aniccaṃ dukkhaṃ anattā asubhaññeva hotīti evaṃ pheṇapiṇḍasadisameva hoti. Yathā vā pana pheṇapiṇḍo chiddāvachiddo anekasandhighaṭito bahūnaṃ udakasappādīnaṃ pāṇānaṃ āvāso, evaṃ rūpampi chiddāvachiddaṃ anekasandhighaṭitaṃ, kulavasenevettha asīti kimikulāni vasanti, tadeva tesaṃ sūtigharampi vaccakuṭipi gilānasālāpi susānampi, na te aññattha gantvā gabbhavuṭṭhānādīni karonti, evampi pheṇapiṇḍasadisaṃ.

Just so means that just as a lump of foam is without substance, so too is form without substance, being devoid of the substance of permanence, the substance of stability, and the substance of self. And just as that lump of foam cannot be grasped with the thought, 'With this, I will make a bowl or a dish,' and even if grasped, it does not accomplish that purpose but simply breaks apart—so too, form cannot be grasped as 'permanent' or 'stable.' Even if grasped, it does not remain so; it is impermanent, suffering, non-self, and just foul. Thus, it is truly like a lump of foam. Or, just as a lump of foam is full of holes and gaps, joined together at many seams, and is a dwelling for many creatures like water snakes, so too is form full of holes and gaps and joined together at many seams. By way of classification, eighty families of worms dwell herein; this very body is their delivery room, their latrine, their sick-bay, and their charnel ground. They do not go elsewhere to perform gestation, birth, and so on. In this way too, it is like a lump of foam.

Yathā ca pheṇapiṇḍo ādito badarapakkamatto hutvā anupubbena pabbatakūṭamattopi hoti, evaṃ rūpampi ādito kalalamattaṃ hutvā anupubbena byāmamattampi gomahiṃsahatthiādīnaṃ vasena pabbatakūṭādimattaṃ hoti macchakacchapādīnaṃ vasena anekayojanasatapamāṇampi, evampi pheṇapiṇḍasadisaṃ. Yathā ca pheṇapiṇḍo uṭṭhitamattopi bhijjati, thokaṃ gantvāpi, dūraṃ gantvāpi, samuddaṃ patvā pana avassameva bhijjati, evamevaṃ rūpampi kalalabhāvepi bhijjati abbudādibhāvepi, antarā pana abhijjamānampi vassasatāyukānaṃ vassasataṃ patvā avassameva bhijjati, maraṇamukhe cuṇṇavicuṇṇaṃ hoti, evampi pheṇapiṇḍasadisaṃ.

And just as a lump of foam, initially the size of a ripe jujube fruit, gradually becomes as large as a mountain peak, so too does form—initially the size of a mere embryonic cell—gradually grow to be a fathom in size; in the case of cows, buffaloes, elephants, and so forth, it becomes the size of a mountain peak and the like; and in the case of fish, turtles, and so forth, it can be many hundreds of yojanas in size. In this way too, it is like a lump of foam. And just as a lump of foam breaks apart just as it arises, or after going a short distance, or after going far—but upon reaching the ocean, it inevitably breaks apart—so too does form break apart even in the state of an embryonic cell, or in the state of an abbuda and so forth. Even if it does not break apart in between, for those with a hundred-year lifespan, upon reaching one hundred years, it inevitably breaks apart. In the mouth of death, it is crushed to powder. In this way too, it is like a lump of foam.

Kiñhi [M 2.0295][P 2.0322] siyā, bhikkhave, vedanāya sārotiādīsu vedanādīnaṃ pubbuḷādīhi evaṃ sadisatā veditabbā. Yathā hi pubbuḷo asāro evaṃ vedanāpi. Yathā ca so abalo agayhūpago, na sakkā taṃ gahetvā phalakaṃ vā āsanaṃ vā kātuṃ, gahitopi bhijjateva, evaṃ vedanāpi abalā agayhūpagā, na sakkā niccāti vā dhuvāti vā gahetuṃ, gahitāpi na tathā tiṭṭhati, evaṃ agayhūpagatāyapi vedanā pubbuḷasadisā. Yathā pana tasmiṃ tasmiṃ udakabindumhi pubbuḷo uppajjati ceva bhijjati ca, na ciraṭṭhitiko hoti, evaṃ vedanāpi uppajjati ceva bhijjati ca, na ciraṭṭhitikā hoti. Ekaccharakkhaṇe koṭisatasahassasaṅkhā uppajjitvā nirujjhati. Yathā ca pubbuḷo udakatalaṃ, udakabinduṃ, udakajallaṃ, saṅkaḍḍhitvā puṭaṃ katvā gahaṇavātañcāti cattāri kāraṇāni paṭicca uppajjati, evaṃ vedanāpi vatthuṃ ārammaṇaṃ kilesajallaṃ phassasaṅghaṭṭanañcāti cattāri kāraṇāni paṭicca uppajjati. Evampi vedanā pubbuḷasadisā.

In such passages as, For what, bhikkhus, could be the substance in feeling?, the similarity of feeling and the other aggregates to a bubble and the other similes should be understood in this way. For just as a bubble is without substance, so too is feeling. And just as a bubble is weak and cannot be grasped, and one cannot make a plank or a seat by taking it—even if grasped, it simply bursts—so too is feeling weak and cannot be grasped. One cannot take it as 'permanent' or 'stable,' and even if grasped, it does not remain so. Thus, in its ungraspability, feeling is similar to a bubble. Furthermore, just as a bubble arises and bursts with each and every drop of water, not lasting long, so too does feeling arise and burst, not lasting long. In a single finger-snap, feelings numbering a hundred thousand crores arise and cease. And just as a bubble arises dependent on four causes—the water’s surface, a drop of water, water scum, and entrapped air—so too does feeling arise dependent on four causes: the physical basis, the object, the scum of defilements, and the impact of contact. In this way too, feeling is similar to a bubble.

Saññāpi [V 2.0284] asārakaṭṭhena marīcisadisā. Tathā agayhūpagaṭṭhena. Na hi sakkā taṃ gahetvā pivituṃ vā nhāyituṃ vā bhājanaṃ vā pūretuṃ. Apica yathā marīci vipphandati, sañjātūmivegā viya khāyati, evaṃ nīlasaññādibhedā saññāpi nīlādianubhavanatthāya phandati vipphandati. Yathā ca marīci mahājanaṃ vippalambheti “puṇṇavāpi viya puṇṇanadī viya dissatī”ti vadāpeti, evaṃ saññāpi vippalambheti, “idaṃ nīlakaṃ subhaṃ sukhaṃ nicca”nti vadāpeti. Pītakādīsupi eseva nayo. Evaṃ saññā vippalambhanenāpi marīcisadisā.

Perception, in being insubstantial, is like a mirage. So too in being ungraspable. For it cannot be taken to drink from, to bathe in, or to fill a vessel. Moreover, just as a mirage flickers, appearing as if with the speed of a generated wave, so too perception, differentiated as blue perception and so forth, flickers and trembles for the sake of experiencing blue and the like for a person whose craving is not abandoned. And just as a mirage deceives the masses, making them say, 'It appears like a full pond or a full river,' so too perception deceives, making one say, 'This is blue, beautiful, pleasant, permanent.' The same method applies to yellow and so forth. Thus, in its deceptiveness, perception is also like a mirage.

Akukkukajātanti anto asañjātaghanadaṇḍakaṃ. Saṅkhārāpi asārakaṭṭhena kadalikkhandhasadisā, tathā agayhūpagaṭṭhena[P 2.0323]. Yatheva hi kadalikkhandhato kiñci gahetvā na sakkā gopānasiādīnaṃ atthāya upanetuṃ, upanītampi na tathā hoti, evaṃ saṅkhārāpi na sakkā niccādivasena gahetuṃ, gahitāpi na tathā honti. Yathā ca kadalikkhandho bahupattavaṭṭisamodhāno hoti, evaṃ saṅkhārakkhandho bahudhammasamodhāno. Yathā ca kadalikkhandho nānālakkhaṇo. Aññoyeva hi bāhirāya pattavaṭṭiyā vaṇṇo, añño tato abbhantaraabbhantarānaṃ, evameva saṅkhārakkhandhepi [M 2.0296] aññadeva phassassa lakkhaṇaṃ, aññā cetanādīnaṃ, samodhānetvā pana saṅkhārakkhandhova vuccatīti evampi saṅkhārakkhandho kadalikkhandhasadiso.

`Akukkukajātaṃ` means that inside, a solid core has not formed. Formations are like a banana trunk in being insubstantial, and similarly, in being unfit for use. Just as it is not possible to take anything from a banana trunk for the purpose of rafters and so forth, and even if taken, it does not serve that purpose, so too formations cannot be grasped in terms of permanence and so forth, and even if grasped, they are not as they have been grasped. And just as a banana trunk is a collection of many layered sheaths, so too the aggregate of formations is a collection of many phenomena. And just as a banana trunk has diverse characteristics—for the color of the outer sheath is one, and that of the inner ones is another—so too in the aggregate of formations, the characteristic of contact is one, and that of volition and the like is another. Yet, when combined, it is simply called the aggregate of formations. Thus, in this way too, the aggregate of formations is like a banana trunk.

Cakkhumā purisoti maṃsacakkhunā ceva paññācakkhunā cāti dvīhi cakkhūhi cakkhumā. Maṃsacakkhumpi hissa parisuddhaṃ vaṭṭati apagatapaṭalapiḷakaṃ, paññācakkhumpi asārabhāvadassanasamatthaṃ. Viññāṇampi asārakaṭṭhena māyāsadisaṃ, tathā agayhūpagaṭṭhena. Yathā ca māyā ittarā lahupaccupaṭṭhānā, evaṃ viññāṇaṃ. Tañhi tatopi ittaratarañceva lahupaccupaṭṭhānatarañca. Teneva hi cittena puriso āgato viya gato viya ṭhito viya nisinno viya hoti. Aññadeva ca āgamanakāle cittaṃ, aññaṃ gamanakālādīsu. Evampi viññāṇaṃ māyāsadisaṃ. Māyā ca mahājanaṃ vañceti, yaṃkiñcideva “idaṃ suvaṇṇaṃ rajataṃ muttā”ti gāhāpeti, viññāṇampi mahājanaṃ vañceti. Teneva hi cittena āgacchantaṃ viya gacchantaṃ viya ṭhitaṃ viya nisinnaṃ viya katvā gāhāpeti. Aññadeva ca āgamane cittaṃ, aññaṃ gamanādīsu. Evampi viññāṇaṃ māyāsadisaṃ.

By 'a man with eyes' is meant one with two eyes: the physical eye and the eye of wisdom. His physical eye is also suitable, being pure and free from the defects of cataracts and blemishes; and the eye of wisdom is also suitable, being capable of seeing the nature of substancelessness. Consciousness is like a magic trick in being insubstantial, and similarly, in being ungraspable. And just as a magic trick is transient and quick to appear, so too is consciousness. For it is even more transient and quicker to appear than that. Indeed, by that very mind, a person appears as if coming, as if going, as if standing, or as if sitting. Yet the mind at the time of coming is different from that at the time of going and so forth. In this way too, consciousness is like a magic trick. And a magic trick deceives the masses, making them grasp whatever it may be as 'This is gold, silver, or pearls'; so too consciousness deceives the masses. For by that very mind, it makes them grasp themselves as if coming, as if going, as if standing, or as if sitting. Yet the mind at the time of coming is different from that at the time of going and so forth. In this way too, consciousness is like a magic trick.

Bhūripaññenāti saṇhapaññena ceva vipulavitthatapaññena ca. Āyūti jīvitindriyaṃ. Usmāti [V 2.0285] kammajatejodhātu. Parabhattanti nānāvidhānaṃ kimigaṇādīnaṃ bhattaṃ hutvā. Etādisāyaṃ santānoti etādisī ayaṃ paveṇī matakassa [P 2.0324] yāva susānā ghaṭṭīyatīti. Māyāyaṃ bālalāpinīti yvāyaṃ viññāṇakkhandho nāma, ayaṃ bālamahājanalapāpanikamāyā nāma. Vadhakoti dvīhi kāraṇehi ayaṃ khandhasaṅkhāto vadhako aññamaññaghātanenapi, khandhesu sati vadho paññāyatītipi. Ekā hi pathavīdhātu bhijjamānā sesadhātuyo gahetvāva bhijjati, tathā āpodhātuādayo. Rūpakkhandho ca bhijjamāno arūpakkhandhe gahetvāva bhijjati, tathā arūpakkhandhesu vedanādayo saññādike. Cattāropi cete vatthurūpanti evaṃ aññamaññavadhanenettha vadhakatā veditabbā. Khandhesu pana sati vadhabandhanacchedādīni sambhavanti, evaṃ etesu sati vadhabhāvatopi vadhakatā veditabbā. Sabbasaṃyoganti sabbaṃ dasavidhampi saṃyojanaṃ. Accutaṃ padanti nibbānaṃ. Tatiyaṃ.

By 'one of great wisdom' is meant one with subtle wisdom and with vast, extensive wisdom. By 'life' is meant the life faculty. By 'heat' is meant the heat element produced by kamma. By 'food for others' is meant becoming food for various kinds of creatures, such as swarms of worms and so forth. By 'such is this continuity' is meant that this succession of the deceased is of such a nature, continuing up to the charnel ground. By 'this magic trick that makes fools babble' is meant this so-called aggregate of consciousness, which is a magic trick that causes the great mass of fools to babble. By 'killer' is meant that this existence, designated as the aggregates, is a killer for two reasons: through mutual destruction, and because when the aggregates exist, killing is evident. For when one earth element breaks apart, it breaks only by taking the remaining elements with it; similarly with the water element and so forth. The form aggregate, when breaking apart, breaks by taking the formless aggregates with it; similarly, among the formless aggregates, feeling and the like break by taking perception and the like with them. And all four of these formless aggregates also break by taking the physical basis with them. Thus, in this way, their nature as killers should be understood here through mutual destruction. Moreover, when the aggregates exist, killing, bondage, cutting, and so forth occur; thus, their nature as killers should also be understood from the fact that killing exists when they are present. By 'all fetters' is meant all ten kinds of fetters. By 'the deathless state' is meant Nibbāna. The third.

4-6. Gomayapiṇḍasuttādivaṇṇanā

4-6. Commentary on the Gomayapiṇḍa Sutta, etc.

96-98. Catutthe [M 2.0297] sassatisamanti sinerumahāpathavīcandimasūriyādīhi sassatīhi samaṃ. Parittaṃ gomayapiṇḍanti appamattakaṃ madhukapupphappamāṇaṃ gomayakhaṇḍaṃ. Kuto panānenetaṃ laddhanti. Paribhaṇḍakaraṇatthāya ābhatato gahitanti eke. Atthassa pana viññāpanatthaṃ iddhiyā abhisaṅkharitvā hatthāruḷhaṃ katanti veditabbanti. Attabhāvapaṭilābhoti paṭiladdhaattabhāvo. Na yidaṃ brahmacariyavāso paññāyethāti ayaṃ maggabrahmacariyavāso nāma na paññāyeyya. Maggo hi tebhūmakasaṅkhāre vivaṭṭento uppajjati. Yadi ca ettako attabhāvo nicco bhaveyya, maggo uppajjitvāpi saṅkhāravaṭṭaṃ vivaṭṭetuṃ na sakkuṇeyyāti brahmacariyavāso na paññāyetha.

In the fourth, by 'equal to the eternal' is meant for a period of time equal to that of eternal things such as Mount Sineru, the great earth, the moon, the sun, and so on. 'A small lump of cow dung' means a tiny piece of cow dung, about the size of a madhuka flower. From where did he get this? Some say it was taken from what was brought for the purpose of smearing. But it should be understood that for the purpose of making the meaning known, he created it by psychic power and made it appear in his hand. 'Acquisition of individual existence' means the obtained individual existence. 'Nor would this holy life be discerned' means that this holy life of the path would not be discerned. For the path arises by undoing the formations of the three realms. And if even such a small individual existence were permanent, the path, even if it arose, would not be able to undo the round of formations—thus, the holy life would not be discerned.

Idāni sace koci saṅkhāro nicco bhaveyya, mayā mahāsudassanarājakāle anubhūtā sampatti niccā bhaveyya, sāpi ca aniccāti taṃ dassetuṃ bhūtapubbāhaṃ bhikkhu rājā ahosintiādimāha. Tattha kusāvatīrājadhānippamukhānīti kusāvatīrājadhānī tesaṃ nagarānaṃ pamukhā, sabbaseṭṭhāti attho. Sāramayānīti [P 2.0325] rattacandanasāramayāni. Upadhānaṃ pana sabbesaṃ suttamayameva. Goṇakatthatānīti caturaṅgulādhikalomena kāḷakojavena atthatāni, yaṃ mahāpiṭṭhiyakojavoti vadanti. Paṭakatthatānīti ubhatolomena uṇṇāmayena setakambalena atthatāni. Paṭalikatthatānīti ghanapupphena uṇṇāmayaattharaṇena atthatāni. Kadalimigapavarapaccattharaṇānīti kadalimigacammamayena [V 2.0286] uttamapaccattharaṇena atthatāni. Taṃ kira paccattharaṇaṃ setavatthassa upari kadalimigacammaṃ attharitvā sibbetvā karonti. Sauttaracchadānīti saha uttaracchadena, upari baddhena rattavitānena saddhinti attho. Ubhatolohitakūpadhānīti sīsūpadhānañca pādūpadhānañcāti pallaṅkānaṃ ubhatolohitakūpadhānāni. Vejayantarathappamukhānīti ettha vejayanto nāma tassa rañño ratho, yassa cakkānaṃ indanīlamaṇimayā nābhi, sattaratanamayā arā, pavāḷamayā nemi, rajatamayo akkho, indanīlamaṇimayaṃ upakkharaṃ, rajatamayaṃ kubbaraṃ. So tesaṃ rathānaṃ pamukho aggo. Dukūlasandānānīti dukūlasantharāni. Kaṃsūpadhāraṇānīti rajatamayadohabhājanāni. Vatthakoṭisahassānīti yathārucitaṃ [M 2.0298] paribhuñjissatīti nhatvā ṭhitakāle upanītavatthāneva sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Bhattābhihāroti abhiharitabbabhattaṃ.

Now, if any conditioned formation were permanent, the prosperity I experienced during the time of King Mahāsudassana would be permanent. But since it too is impermanent, to demonstrate this, He spoke the words beginning with, 'Formerly, monks, I was a king…' Therein, 'headed by the royal capital of Kusāvatī' means that the royal capital Kusāvatī was the foremost among those cities, the very best of all; this is the meaning. 'Made of heartwood' means made of red sandalwood heartwood. The cushions, however, were all made only of embroidered cloth. 'Spread with long-fleeced rugs' means spread with a black rug with fleece longer than four fingers, which they call a 'large-backed rug.' 'Spread with woolen cloth' means spread with a white woolen blanket with fleece on both sides. 'Spread with figured cloth' means spread with a woolen spread with dense floral patterns. 'With the finest antelope-hide coverings' means spread with a supreme covering made of the hide of the kadali antelope. It is said that this covering is made by spreading a kadali antelope hide over a white cloth and sewing it. 'With a canopy above' means together with a canopy, that is, together with a red canopy fastened above; this is the meaning. 'With red cushions at both ends' means with a head-cushion and a foot-cushion; thus, the couches had red cushions at both ends. 'Headed by the Vejayanta chariot': herein, Vejayanta was the name of that king's chariot, of which the hubs of the wheels were made of sapphire, the spokes were made of the seven kinds of gems, the rim was made of coral, the axle was made of silver, the chassis was made of sapphire, and the pole was made of silver. It was the foremost, the chief among those chariots. 'With fine cloth fastenings' means spread with fine cloth. 'With bronze supports' means with silver milking vessels. 'Myriads of garments': this was said in reference to the garments brought when he stood after bathing, to be used as he pleased. 'The presentation of the meal' means the meal to be presented.

Yamahaṃ tena samayena ajjhāvasāmīti yattha vasāmi, taṃ ekaññeva nagaraṃ hoti, avasesesu puttadhītādayo ceva dāsamanussā ca vasiṃsu. Pāsādakūṭāgārādīsupi eseva nayo. Pallaṅkādīsu ekaṃyeva sayaṃ paribhuñjati, sesā puttādīnaṃ paribhogā honti. Itthīsu ekāva paccupaṭṭhāti, sesā parivāramattā honti. Velāmikāti khattiyassa vā brāhmaṇiyā, brāhmaṇassa vā khattiyāniyā kucchismiṃ jātā. Paridahāmīti [P 2.0326] ekaṃyeva dussayugaṃ nivāsemi, sesāni parivāretvā vicarantānaṃ asītisahassādhikānaṃ soḷasannaṃ purisasatasahassānaṃ hontīti dasseti. Bhuñjāmīti paramappamāṇena nāḷikodanamattaṃ bhuñjāmi, sesaṃ parivāretvā vicarantānaṃ cattālīsasahassādhikānaṃ aṭṭhannaṃ purisasatasahassānaṃ hotīti dasseti. Ekathālipāko hi dasannaṃ janānaṃ pahoti.

Regarding 'In which I lived at that time': wherever I lived, that was only one city; in the remaining cities lived my sons, daughters, and my servants and people. This same principle should be understood for the palaces, gabled mansions, and so on. Of the couches and so on, I myself used only one; the rest were for the use of my sons and others. Among the women, only one attended me; the rest were merely the retinue. 'Velāmikā' means one born in the womb of a Brahmin woman to a Khattiya man, or in the womb of a Khattiya woman to a Brahmin man. 'I wear': I wear only one pair of cloths; He shows that the remaining cloths were for the one million, six hundred and eighty thousand men who moved about in the retinue. 'I eat': I eat at most a measure of one nāḷi of cooked rice; He shows that the remainder was for the eight hundred and forty thousand men who moved about in the retinue. For one pot of cooked food is sufficient for ten people.

Iti imaṃ mahāsudassanakāle sampattiṃ dassetvā idāni tassā aniccataṃ dassento iti kho bhikkhūtiādimāha. Tattha vipariṇatāti pakatijahanena nibbutapadīpo viya apaṇṇattikabhāvaṃ gatā. Evaṃ aniccā kho bhikkhu saṅkhārāti evaṃ hutvāabhāvaṭṭhena aniccā. Ettāvatā bhagavā yathā nāma puriso satahatthubbedhe campakarukkhe nisseṇiṃ bandhitvā abhiruhitvā campakapupphaṃ ādāya nisseṇiṃ muñcanto otareyya, evamevaṃ nisseṇiṃ bandhanto viya anekavassakoṭisatasahassubbedhaṃ mahāsudassanasampattiṃ āruyha sampattimatthake ṭhitaṃ aniccalakkhaṇaṃ ādāya nisseṇiṃ muñcanto viya otiṇṇo. Evaṃ addhuvāti evaṃ udakapubbuḷādayo viya dhuvabhāvarahitā. Evaṃ anassāsikāti evaṃ supinake pītapānīyaṃ viya anulittacandanaṃ viya ca [V 2.0287] assāsavirahitā. Iti imasmiṃ sutte aniccalakkhaṇaṃ kathitaṃ. Pañcame sabbaṃ vuttanayameva. Chaṭṭhaṃ tathā bujjhanakassa ajjhāsayena vuttaṃ. Catutthādīni.

Thus, having shown this prosperity during the time of Mahāsudassana, now, in order to show its impermanence, He spoke the words beginning with, 'Thus, monks…' Therein, 'have been transformed' means they have gone to a state of being undesignatable, like an extinguished lamp, by abandoning their natural state. 'Thus, monks, conditioned formations are impermanent': thus, they are impermanent in the sense of ceasing to be after having been. By this much, the Blessed One is like a man who, having fixed a ladder to a campaka tree a hundred cubits high, climbs up, takes a campaka flower, and descends while detaching the ladder; just so, like one fixing a ladder, He ascended to the great prosperity of Mahāsudassana, which was as high as many hundred-thousand koṭis of years, and having taken the characteristic of impermanence that stood at the peak of that prosperity, He descended like one detaching the ladder. 'Thus, they are unstable': thus, they are devoid of a stable state, like water bubbles and so on. 'Thus, they are unconsoling': thus, they are devoid of solace, like drinking a beverage or being anointed with sandalwood in a dream. Thus, in this sutta, the characteristic of impermanence has been taught. In the fifth, everything is in the same way as has been stated. The sixth was spoken according to the disposition of one who is to awaken. The fourth and so on.

7. Gaddulabaddhasuttavaṇṇanā

7. The Commentary on the Sutta on Being Tied by a Leash

99. Sattame yaṃ mahāsamuddoti yasmiṃ samaye pañcame sūriye uṭṭhite mahāsamuddo ussussati. Dukkhassa antakiriyanti cattāri saccāni appaṭivijjhitvā avijjāya nivutānaṃyeva sataṃ vaṭṭadukkhassa antakiriyaṃ paricchedaṃ [M 2.0299] na vadāmi. Sā gaddulabaddhoti gaddulena baddhasunakho. Khīleti [P 2.0327] pathaviyaṃ ākoṭite mahākhīle. Thambheti nikhaṇitvā ṭhapite thambhe. Evameva khoti ettha sunakho viya vaṭṭanissito bālo, gaddulo viya diṭṭhi, thambho viya sakkāyo, gaddularajjuyā thambhe upanibaddhasunakhassa thambhānuparivattanaṃ viya diṭṭhitaṇhāya sakkāye baddhassa puthujjanassa sakkāyānuparivattanaṃ veditabbaṃ. Sattamaṃ.

99. In the seventh, regarding 'the great ocean': this refers to the time when the fifth sun arises and the great ocean dries up. 'The ending of suffering': I do not speak of the ending or limitation of the suffering of the round of existence for beings who are shrouded in ignorance, not having penetrated the four truths. 'Tied by a leash': this means a dog bound by a leash. 'At a stake': at a large stake driven into the ground. 'At a post': at a post that has been dug in and set up. 'Just so': herein, the fool who is dependent on the round of existence should be understood as being like the dog; wrong view is like the leash; personal existence is like the post. Just as a dog tied to a post by a leash circles around that same post, so too should it be understood that an ordinary person, bound to personal existence by wrong view and craving, revolves around that same personal existence. The seventh.

8. Dutiyagaddulabaddhasuttavaṇṇanā

8. The Commentary on the Second Gaddulabaddha Sutta

100. Aṭṭhame tasmāti yasmā diṭṭhigaddulanissitāya taṇhārajjuyā sakkāyathambhe upanibaddho vaṭṭanissito bālaputhujjano sabbiriyāpathesu khandhapañcakaṃ nissāyeva pavattati, yasmā vā dīgharattamidaṃ cittaṃ saṃkiliṭṭhaṃ rāgena dosena mohena, tasmā. Cittasaṃkilesāti sunhātāpi hi sattā cittasaṃkileseneva saṃkilissanti, malaggahitasarīrāpi cittassa vodānattā visujjhanti. Tenāhu porāṇā –

100. In the eighth sutta, regarding 'therefore' (tasmā): It is because the foolish ordinary person, dependent on the round of existence, is bound to the pillar of personal identity by the rope of craving, which is dependent on the leash of wrong view, and thus proceeds solely by relying on the five aggregates in all postures. Or it is because this mind has for a long time been defiled by lust, hatred, and delusion; therefore. Regarding 'defilement of mind': Indeed, beings are defiled by the defilement of mind even when well-bathed; though their bodies are covered with dirt, they become purified through the purity of the mind. Hence, the ancients said:

“Rūpamhi saṃkiliṭṭhamhi, saṃkilissanti māṇavā;

Rūpe suddhe visujjhanti, anakkhātaṃ mahesinā.

“When form is defiled, beings are defiled; when form is pure, they are purified—this was not declared by the Great Seer.

“Cittamhi saṃkiliṭṭhamhi, saṃkilissanti māṇavā;

Citte suddhe visujjhanti, iti vuttaṃ mahesinā”ti.

“When the mind is defiled, beings are defiled; when the mind is pure, they are purified—this was declared by the Great Seer.”

Caraṇaṃ nāma cittanti vicaraṇacittaṃ. Saṅkhā nāma brāhmaṇapāsaṇḍikā honti, te paṭakoṭṭhakaṃ [V 2.0288] katvā tattha nānappakārā sugatiduggativasena sampattivipattiyo lekhāpetvā, “imaṃ kammaṃ katvā idaṃ paṭilabhati, idaṃ katvā ida”nti dassentā taṃ cittaṃ gahetvā vicaranti. Citteneva cittitanti cittakārena cintetvā katattā cittena cintitaṃ nāma. Cittaññeva cittataranti tassa cittassa upāyapariyesanacittaṃ tatopi cittataraṃ. Tiracchānagatā pāṇā citteneva cittitāti kammacitteneva cittitā. Taṃ pana kammacittaṃ ime vaṭṭakatittirādayo “evaṃ cittā bhavissāmā”ti āyūhantā nāma natthi. Kammaṃ pana yoniṃ upaneti, yonimūlako tesaṃ cittabhāvo. Yoniupagatā [P 2.0328] hi sattā taṃtaṃyonikehi sadisacittāva honti. Iti yonisiddho cittabhāvo, kammasiddhā yonīti veditabbā.

Regarding 'a wandering picture is called a painting' (caraṇaṃ nāma cittaṃ): This refers to a painting for wandering with. There are heretical brahmins called Saṅkha; they make a picture-scroll and, having caused to be drawn there various kinds of prosperity and adversity according to good and bad destinations, they wander about carrying that painting (cittaṃ), showing: 'Having done this kamma, one obtains this; having done that, one obtains that.' Regarding 'painted by the mind alone' (citteneva cittitaṃ): Because it is made by a painter (cittakāra) after having conceived it, it is called 'conceived by the mind'. Regarding 'the mind itself is more variegated' (cittaññeva cittataraṃ): The mind that seeks the means for that painting is even more variegated than the painting itself. Regarding 'animals are variegated by the mind alone': They are made variegated by kamma-consciousness alone. But these quails, partridges, and so on, do not strive, thinking, 'We shall be variegated in this way.' Rather, kamma leads to a rebirth-destination, and their variegated nature is rooted in that rebirth-destination. For beings who have gone to a particular rebirth-destination come to have a variegated nature similar to those of that respective rebirth-destination. Thus, it should be understood that the variegated nature is established by the rebirth-destination, and the rebirth-destination is established by kamma.

Apica [M 2.0300] cittaṃ nāmetaṃ sahajātaṃ sahajātadhammacittatāya bhūmicittatāya vatthucittatāya dvāracittatāya ārammaṇacittatāya kammanānattamūlakānaṃ liṅganānattasaññānānattavohāranānattādīnaṃ anekavidhānaṃ cittānaṃ nipphādanatāyapi tiracchānagatacittato cittatarameva veditabbaṃ.

Furthermore, this which is called 'mind' should be known as even more variegated than the variegated nature of an animal, because of the variegated nature of its co-arisen states, the variegated nature of its plane, the variegated nature of its physical base, the variegated nature of its door, the variegated nature of its object, and also because of its producing many kinds of variegated phenomena—such as the variety of gender, the variety of perception, the variety of designation, and so forth—which are rooted in the variety of kamma.

Rajakoti vatthesu raṅgena rūpasamuṭṭhāpanako. So pana acheko amanāpaṃ rūpaṃ karoti, cheko manāpaṃ dassanīyaṃ, evameva puthujjano akusalacittena vā ñāṇavippayuttakusalena vā cakkhusampadādivirahitaṃ virūpaṃ samuṭṭhāpeti, ñāṇasampayuttakusalena cakkhusampadādisampannaṃ abhirūpaṃ. Aṭṭhamaṃ.

Regarding 'dyer' (rajaka): This is one who produces forms on cloths by means of dye. An unskilled one produces an unpleasant form, while a skilled one produces a pleasant, lovely one. In the same way, an ordinary person, with an unwholesome mind or with a wholesome mind dissociated from knowledge, generates a deformed form lacking the excellence of the eye and so forth; but with a wholesome mind associated with knowledge, he generates a beautiful form endowed with the excellence of the eye and so forth. The eighth is finished.

9. Vāsijaṭasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Vāsijaṭa Sutta

101. Navame seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, kukkuṭiyā aṇḍānīti imā kaṇhapakkhasukkapakkhavasena dve upamā vuttā. Tāsu kaṇhapakkhaupamā atthassa asādhikā, itarā sādhikāti. Sukkapakkhaupamāya evaṃ attho veditabbo – seyyathāti opammatthe nipāto, apīti sambhāvanatthe. Ubhayenāpi seyyathā nāma, bhikkhaveti dasseti. Kukkuṭiyā aṇḍāni aṭṭha vā dasa vā dasa vā dvādasa vāti ettha pana kiñcāpi kukkuṭiyā vuttappakārato ūnādhikānipi aṇḍāni honti, vacanasiliṭṭhatāya pana evaṃ vuttaṃ. Evañhi loke siliṭṭhavacanaṃ hoti. Tānassūti tāni assu, tāni bhaveyyunti attho. Kukkuṭiyā sammā adhisayitānīti tāya ca janettiyā kukkuṭiyā pakkhe pasāretvā tesaṃ upari sayantiyā sammā [V 2.0289] adhisayitāni. Sammā pariseditānīti kālena kālaṃ utuṃ gaṇhāpentiyā suṭṭhu samantato seditāni usmīkatāni. Sammā paribhāvitānīti kālena kālaṃ suṭṭhu samantato bhāvitāni, kukkuṭagandhaṃ gāhāpitānīti attho. Kiñcāpi tassā kukkuṭiyāti tassā kukkuṭiyā iminā tividhakiriyākaraṇena appamādaṃ katvā kiñcāpi na evaṃ icchā upajjeyya. Atha [P 2.0329] kho bhabbāva teti atha kho te kukkuṭapotakā vuttanayena sotthinā abhinibbhijjituṃ bhabbāva. Te hi yasmā tāya kukkuṭiyā evaṃ tīhākārehi tāni aṇḍāni paripāliyamānāni na pūtīni [M 2.0301] honti, yo nesaṃ allasineho, sopi pariyādānaṃ gacchati, kapālaṃ tanukaṃ hoti, pādanakhasikhā ca mukhatuṇḍakañca kharaṃ hoti, sayampi pariṇāmaṃ gacchanti, kapālassa tanuttā bahi āloko anto paññāyati, tasmā “ciraṃ vata mayaṃ saṅkuṭitahatthapādā sambādhe sayimhā, ayañca bahi āloko dissati, ettha dāni no sukhavihāro bhavissatī”ti nikkhamitukāmā hutvā kapālaṃ pādena paharanti, gīvaṃ pasārenti, tato taṃ kapālaṃ dvedhā bhijjati. Atha te pakkhe vidhunantā taṃkhaṇānurūpaṃ viravantā nikkhamantiyeva, nikkhamitvā ca gāmakkhettaṃ upasobhayamānā vicaranti.

9. In the ninth sutta, these two similes are given by way of the dark phase and the bright phase: 'Just as, O monks, a hen's eggs...' Among them, the simile of the dark phase does not accomplish the purpose, whereas the other does. Thus, the meaning of the simile of the bright phase should be understood as follows: 'Seyyathā' is a particle in the sense of a simile; 'api' is in the sense of conjecture. By both, it indicates: 'Just as, O monks...' As for 'a hen's eggs, eight or ten or ten or twelve,' although a hen may have fewer or more eggs than the number stated, it is said this way for the sake of verbal smoothness. For in the world, speech becomes smooth in this way. 'Tānassu' is to be divided as 'tāni assu,' meaning 'they might be.' 'Properly sat upon by the hen' means they would be properly sat upon by that mother hen as she lies on them, having spread her wings over them. 'Properly incubated' means they would be well warmed all around, as she causes them to absorb her body heat from time to time. 'Properly pervaded' means they would be well pervaded all around from time to time, meaning they are caused to take on the hen's scent. 'Even though for that hen...'—although for that hen, having been diligent in performing these three actions, such a wish might not arise. 'Then they are indeed capable'—then those chicks are indeed capable of safely breaking out in the manner described. For since those eggs, being cared for by that hen in these three ways, do not become rotten, and whatever wet moisture they have is dried up, the shell becomes thin, the tips of their claws and beak become hard, and they themselves reach maturity. Due to the thinness of the shell, the light outside becomes apparent inside. Therefore, thinking, 'For a long time, indeed, we have lain with folded limbs in a confined space, and now this light outside is seen; here now will be our pleasant abiding,' they become desirous of emerging. They strike the shell with a foot and stretch their necks, and then that shell breaks in two. Then, shaking their wings and chirping in a manner appropriate to that moment, they emerge indeed. And having emerged, they roam about, beautifying the village fields.

Evameva khoti idaṃ opammasampaṭipādanaṃ. Taṃ evaṃ atthena saṃsanditvā veditabbaṃ – tassā kukkuṭiyā aṇḍesu tividhakiriyākaraṇaṃ viya hi imassa bhikkhuno bhāvānuyogaṃ anuyuttakālo, kukkuṭiyā tividhakiriyāsampādanena aṇḍānaṃ apūtibhāvo viya bhāvanānuyogamanuyuttassa bhikkhuno tividhānupassanāsampādanena vipassanāñāṇassa aparihāni, tassā tividhakiriyākaraṇena allasinehapariyādānaṃ viya tassa bhikkhuno tividhānupassanāsampādanena bhavattayānugatanikantisinehapariyādānaṃ, aṇḍakapālānaṃ tanubhāvo viya tassa bhikkhuno avijjaṇḍakosassa tanubhāvo, kukkuṭapotakānaṃ pādanakhasikhamukhatuṇḍakānaṃ thaddhakharabhāvo viya bhikkhuno vipassanāñāṇassa tikkhakharavippasanna sūrabhāvo, kukkuṭapotakānaṃ pariṇāmakālo viya bhikkhuno vipassanāñāṇassa pariṇāmakālo vaḍḍhitakālo gabbhaggahaṇakālo, kukkuṭapotakānaṃ pādanakhasikhāya vā mukhatuṇḍakena vā aṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā pakkhe papphoṭetvā sotthinā abhinibbhidākālo viya tassa bhikkhuno vipassanāñāṇagabbhaṃ gaṇhāpetvā vicarantassa tajjātikaṃ utusappāyaṃ vā bhojanasappāyaṃ vā puggalasappāyaṃ vā dhammassavanasappāyaṃ vā labhitvā ekāsane nisinnasseva vipassanaṃ [P 2.0330] vaḍḍhentassa anupubbādhigatena arahattamaggena avijjaṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā abhiññāpakkhe papphoṭetvā sotthinā arahattapattakālo veditabbo. Yathā pana kukkuṭapotakānaṃ [V 2.0290] pariṇatabhāvaṃ ñatvā mātāpi aṇḍakosaṃ bhindati, evaṃ tathārūpassa bhikkhuno ñāṇaparipākaṃ ñatvā satthāpi –

‘Just so...’—this is the application of the simile.
