It should be understood by connecting it with the meaning in this way: Just as there is the mother hen’s threefold action upon the eggs, so there is this monk’s time of being devoted to the practice of meditation. Just as through the hen’s accomplishment of the threefold action there is the non-rotting of the eggs, so through the devoted monk’s accomplishment of the three contemplations there is the non-decline of insight-knowledge. Just as through her threefold action there is the drying up of the wet moisture, so for that monk, through the accomplishment of the three contemplations, there is the drying up of the moisture of attachment and craving that follows along with the three kinds of existence. Just as there is the thinness of the eggshells, so for that monk there is the thinness of the eggshell of ignorance. Just as there is the firmness and hardness of the chicks’ claw-tips and beaks, so for the monk there is the sharpness, hardness, clarity, and valor of the insight-knowledge. Just as there is the time of the chicks’ maturing, so for the monk there is the time of the insight-knowledge’s maturing, the time of its growth, and the time of conceiving the embryo. Just as there is the time when the chicks, having broken the eggshell with the tips of their claws or with their beaks and flapped their wings, break out safely, so should be understood the time of attaining Arahantship for that monk who, having caused insight-knowledge to conceive the embryo of the path and while wandering about, upon finding suitable weather, food, persons, or a Dhamma teaching, while sitting on a single seat and developing insight, breaks the eggshell of ignorance with the successively attained path of Arahantship, flaps the wings of the higher knowledges, and safely attains Arahantship. Furthermore, just as the mother hen, knowing the chicks have matured, also breaks the eggshell, so too the Teacher, knowing the maturity of such a monk’s knowledge—

“Ucchinda [M 2.0302] sinehamattano, kumudaṃ sāradikaṃva pāṇinā;

Santimaggameva brūhaya, nibbānaṃ sugatena desita”nti. (dha. pa. 285) –

‘Cut off your own craving, as one plucks an autumn water lily with the hand. Cultivate only the path to peace—Nibbāna taught by the Sugata.’

Ādinā nayena obhāsaṃ pharitvā gāthāya avijjaṇḍakosaṃ paharati. So gāthāpariyosāne avijjaṇḍakosaṃ bhinditvā arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti. Tato paṭṭhāya yathā te kukkuṭapotakā gāmakkhettaṃ upasobhayamānā tattha vicaranti, evaṃ ayampi mahākhīṇāsavo nibbānārammaṇaṃ phalasamāpattiṃ appetvā saṅghārāmaṃ upasobhayamāno vicarati.

By this method, having suffused the area with radiance, the Teacher strikes the eggshell of ignorance with a verse. At the conclusion of the verse, that monk, having broken the eggshell of ignorance, attains Arahantship. From that time onwards, just as those chicks wander about there adorning the village fields, so too this great one whose taints are destroyed, having entered the fruition attainment that has Nibbāna as its object, wanders about adorning the monastery.

Palagaṇḍassāti vaḍḍhakissa. So hi olambakasaṅkhātaṃ palaṃ dhāretvā dārūnaṃ gaṇḍaṃ haratīti palagaṇḍoti vuccati. Vāsijaṭeti vāsidaṇḍakassa gahaṇaṭṭhāne. Ettakaṃ vata me ajja āsavānaṃ khīṇanti pabbajitassa hi pabbajjāsaṅkhepena uddesena paripucchāya yoniso manasikārena vattapaṭipattiyā ca niccakālaṃ āsavā khīyanti. Evaṃ khīyamānānaṃ pana tesaṃ “ettakaṃ ajja khīṇaṃ, ettakaṃ hiyyo”ti evamassa ñāṇaṃ na hotīti attho. Imāya upamāya vipassanāyānisaṃso dīpito. Hemantikenāti hemantasamayena. Paṭippassambhantīti thirabhāvena parihāyanti.

Of a `palagaṇḍa` means of a carpenter. For he is called a `palagaṇḍa` because, holding a plumb line called `olambaka`, he removes the knots from wood. `Vāsijaṭe` means at the place for grasping the adze handle. Regarding 'Just this much of my taints is destroyed today': for one who has gone forth, the taints are constantly being destroyed through the practice of going forth, learning the Pāli, learning the commentary, wise attention, and the fulfillment of duties. However, as they are being destroyed in this way, the knowledge does not arise for him, 'This much was destroyed today, this much yesterday.' By this simile, the benefit of insight is shown. `Hemantikena` means in the winter season. `Paṭippassambhanti` means they diminish from a state of being firm.

Evameva khoti ettha mahāsamuddo viya sāsanaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ, nāvā viya yogāvacaro, nāvāya mahāsamudde pariyādānaṃ viya imassa bhikkhuno ūnapañcavassakāle ācariyupajjhāyānaṃ santike vicaraṇaṃ, nāvāya mahāsamuddodakena khajjamānānaṃ bandhanānaṃ tanubhāvo viya bhikkhuno pabbajjāsaṅkhepena uddesaparipucchādīhi ceva saṃyojanānaṃ tanubhāvo, nāvāya thale ukkhittakālo [P 2.0331] viya bhikkhuno nissayamuccakassa kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvā araññe vasanakālo, divā vātātapena saṃsussanaṃ viya vipassanāñāṇena taṇhāsnehasaṃsussanaṃ, rattiṃ himodakena temanaṃ viya kammaṭṭhānaṃ nissāya uppannena pītipāmojjena cittatemanaṃ, rattindivaṃ vātātapena ceva himodakena ca parisukkhaparitintānaṃ bandhanānaṃ dubbalabhāvo viya ekadivasaṃ utusappāyādīni laddhā vipassanāñāṇapītipāmojjehi saṃyojanānaṃ bhiyyosomattāya dubbalabhāvo, pāvussakamegho viya arahattamaggañāṇaṃ[V 2.0291], meghavuṭṭhiudakena nāvāya bandhe pūtibhāvo viya āraddhavipassakassa [M 2.0303] rūpasattakādivasena vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhentassa okkhāyamāne pakkhāyamāne kammaṭṭhāne ekadivasaṃ utusappāyādīni laddhā ekapallaṅkena nisinnassa arahattaphalādhigamo, pūtibandhanāvāya kañci kālaṃ ṭhānaṃ viya khīṇasaṃyojanassa arahato mahājanaṃ anuggaṇhantassa yāvatāyukaṃ ṭhānaṃ, pūtibandhanāvāya anupubbena bhijjitvā apaṇṇattikabhāvūpagamo viya khīṇāsavassa upādiṇṇakkhandhabhedena anupādisesāya nibbānadhātuyā parinibbutassa apaṇṇattikabhāvūpagamoti imāya upamāya saṃyojanānaṃ dubbalatā dīpitā. Navamaṃ.

In the same way, here the Teaching should be seen as like the great ocean; the yogi practitioner as like a boat; the boat’s wearing away in the great ocean as like this monk’s wandering near his teachers and preceptors during the time of less than five years; the weakening of the boat’s bonds being gnawed by the great ocean’s water as like the weakening of the monk’s fetters through the practice of going forth, learning the Pāli, learning the commentary, and so on; the boat’s time of being hauled onto land as like the monk’s time of dwelling in the forest after taking up a meditation subject, having become released from dependence; drying by day from wind and sun as like the drying up of the moisture of craving through insight knowledge; moistening at night by dew as like the mind’s moistening through joy and gladness arisen in dependence on the meditation subject; the weakness of the bonds, repeatedly dried and moistened day and night by wind, sun, and dew, as like the fetters’ greater weakness when, having obtained suitable conditions for a day, they are weakened still further by insight knowledge, joy, and gladness; the monsoon rain cloud as like the knowledge of the path to Arahantship; the rotting of the boat’s bonds from the great rain’s water as like the attainment of the fruit of Arahantship for one who has commenced insight meditation—developing insight through the sevenfold materiality and so on, while the meditation subject becomes clear and firm—when, having obtained suitable conditions for a day, he sits in one posture; the boat with rotten bonds remaining for some time as like the Arahant whose fetters are destroyed remaining for the extent of his life while assisting the great populace; the boat with rotten bonds gradually breaking up and reaching a state of non-designation as like the one whose taints are destroyed, who has attained Parinibbāna in the Nibbāna-element without residue upon the breakup of the appropriated aggregates, reaching a state of non-designation. Through this simile, the weakness of the fetters is shown. The Ninth.

10. Aniccasaññāsuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Discourse on the Perception of Impermanence

102. Dasame aniccasaññāti aniccaṃ aniccanti bhāventassa uppannasaññā. Pariyādiyatīti khepayati. Sabbaṃ asmimānanti navavidhaṃ asmimānaṃ. Mūlasantānakānīti santānetvā ṭhitamūlāni. Mahānaṅgalaṃ viya hi aniccasaññā, khuddānukhuddakāni mūlasantānakāni viya kilesā, yathā kassako kasanto naṅgalena tāni padāleti, evaṃ yogī aniccasaññaṃ bhāvento aniccasaññāñāṇena kilese padāletīti idamettha opammasaṃsandanaṃ.

102. In the tenth discourse, `aniccasaññā` (perception of impermanence) is the perception that arises in one who develops the meditation 'impermanent, impermanent.' `Pariyādiyati` (it is worn away) means it destroys. `Sabbaṃ asmimānaṃ` (all conceit 'I am') means the ninefold conceit 'I am'. `Mūlasantānakāni` means roots that are established by spreading out. For the perception of impermanence is like a great plow, and the defilements are like small and tiny spreading roots. Just as a farmer, while plowing, tears them apart with the plow, so the yogi, while developing the perception of impermanence, tears apart the defilements with the knowledge associated with the perception of impermanence. This is the connection of the simile here.

Odhunātīti heṭṭhā dhunāti. Niddhunātīti papphoṭeti. Nicchoṭetīti [P 2.0332] papphoṭetvā chaḍḍeti. Idhāpi pabbajāni viya kilesā, lāyanaṃ nicchoṭanaṃ viya aniccasaññāñāṇanti iminā atthena upamā saṃsandetabbā.

`Odhunāti` (shakes down) means shakes downwards. `Niddhunāti` (shakes off) means beats. `Nicchoṭeti` (shakes out) means having beaten, one discards. Here too, the defilements should be seen as like thatch-grass, and the knowledge associated with the perception of impermanence should be seen as like the reaping and the discarding. The simile should be connected in this sense.

Vaṇṭacchinnāyāti tiṇhena khurappena vaṇṭacchinnāya. Tadanvayāni bhavantīti taṃ ambapiṇḍiṃ anugacchanti, tassā patamānāya ambāni bhūmiyaṃ patanti. Idhāpi ambapiṇḍi viya kilesā, tiṇhakhurappo viya aniccasaññā, yathā khurappena chinnāya ambapiṇḍiyā sabbāni ambāni bhūmiyaṃ patanti, evaṃ aniccasaññāñāṇena kilesānaṃ mūlabhūtāya avijjāya chinnāya sabbakilesā samugghātaṃ gacchantīti, idaṃ opammasaṃsandanaṃ.

`Vaṇṭacchinnāya` (cut at the stalk) means when cut at the stalk with a sharp crescent-tipped arrow. `Tadanvayāni bhavanti` (they follow accordingly) means they follow that cluster of mangoes; as that cluster of mangoes falls, the mangoes fall to the ground. Here too, the defilements should be seen as like the cluster of mangoes, and the perception of impermanence as like a sharp crescent-tipped arrow. Just as when the cluster of mangoes is cut by the crescent-tipped arrow, all the mangoes fall to the ground, so when ignorance, which is the root of the defilements, is cut by the knowledge associated with the perception of impermanence, all defilements are completely uprooted. This is the connection of the simile.

Kūṭaṅgamāti kūṭaṃ gacchanti. Kūṭaninnāti kūṭaṃ pavisanabhāvena kūṭe ninnā. Kūṭasamosaraṇāti kūṭe samosaritvā ṭhitā. Idhāpi kūṭaṃ viya aniccasaññā, gopānasiyo viya [V 2.0292] catubhūmakakusaladhammā, yathā sabbagopānasīnaṃ [M 2.0304] kūṭaṃ aggaṃ, evaṃ kusaladhammānaṃ aniccasaññā aggā. Nanu ca aniccasaññā lokiyā, sā lokiyakusalānaṃ tāva aggaṃ hotu, lokuttarānaṃ kathaṃ agganti? Tesampi paṭilābhakaraṇatthena agganti veditabbā. Iminā upāyena sabbāsu upamāsu opammasaṃsandanaṃ veditabbaṃ. Purimāhi panettha tīhi aniccasaññāya kiccaṃ, pacchimāhi balanti. Dasamaṃ.

`Kūṭaṅgamā` means they go to the peak. `Kūṭaninnā` means they are inclined to the peak by way of entering it. `Kūṭasamosaraṇā` means having converged at the peak, they stand. Here too, the perception of impermanence should be seen as like the peak, and the wholesome states of the four planes as like the rafters. Just as the peak is the highest of all rafters, so the perception of impermanence is the highest among wholesome states. But is not the perception of impermanence mundane? Let it be the highest among mundane wholesome states; how can it be the highest among supramundane ones? It should be known as the highest of those too, in the sense that it is the cause for their attainment. By this method, the connection of the simile should be understood in all the similes. Herein, by the first three the function of the perception of impermanence is shown, and by the latter ones its power. The Tenth.

Pupphavaggo dasamo.

The Flower Chapter, the Tenth.

Majjhimapaṇṇāsako samatto.

The Middle Fifty is complete.

11. Antavaggo

11. The Chapter on Ends

1. Antasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Explanation of the Discourse on Ends

103. Antavaggassa paṭhame antāti koṭṭhāsā. Idaṃ suttaṃ catusaccavasena pañcakkhandhe yojetvā antoti vacanena bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayavasena vuttaṃ. Paṭhamaṃ.

103. In the first of the Chapter on Ends, 'ends' (antā) means parts. This discourse, connecting the five aggregates by way of the Four Truths, was spoken according to the disposition of those who are to understand by means of the word 'end.' The First.

2-3. Dukkhasuttādivaṇṇanā

2–3. Explanation of the Discourses Beginning with the Discourse on Suffering

104-105. Dutiyampi pañcakkhandhe catusaccavasena yojetvā dukkhanti bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayena kathitaṃ. Tatiyampi [P 2.0333] tatheva sakkāyoti bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayena kathitaṃ. Dutiyatatiyāni.

The second discourse, connecting the five aggregates by way of the Four Truths, was also spoken according to the disposition of those who are to understand by means of 'suffering.' The third was likewise spoken according to the disposition of those who are to understand by means of 'personality.' The Second and Third.

4. Pariññeyyasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Explanation of the Discourse on What Is to Be Fully Understood

106. Catutthe pariññeyyeti parijānitabbe samatikkamitabbe. Pariññanti samatikkamaṃ. Pariññātāvinti tāya pariññāya parijānitvā samatikkamitvā ṭhitaṃ. Rāgakkhayotiādīhi nibbānaṃ dassitaṃ. Catutthaṃ.

106. In the fourth, 'to be fully understood' means to be comprehended and to be transcended. 'Full understanding' means transcendence. 'Having fully understood' means one stands firm, having comprehended and transcended by that full understanding. By 'the destruction of lust,' etc., Nibbāna is shown. The Fourth.

5-10. Samaṇasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-10. Explanation of the Discourses Beginning with the Discourse on the Ascetic

107-112. Pañcamādīsu [V 2.0293] catūsu cattāri saccāni kathitāni. Navamadasamesu kilesappahānanti. Pañcamādīni.

In the four suttas beginning with the fifth, the four truths are spoken of. In the ninth and tenth, the abandoning of defilements is spoken of. The suttas beginning with the fifth are finished.

Antavaggo ekādasamo.

The Chapter on Ends, the eleventh, is finished.

12. Dhammakathikavaggo

12. The Chapter on the Dhamma-Speaker

1-2. Avijjāsuttādivaṇṇanā

1-2. Explanation of the Discourses Beginning with the Discourse on Ignorance

113-114. Dhammakathikavaggassa paṭhame [M 2.0305] ettāvatā ca avijjāgato hotīti yāvatā imāya catūsu saccesu aññāṇabhūtāya avijjāya samannāgato, ettāvatā avijjāgato hotīti attho. Dutiyepi eseva nayo. Paṭhamadutiyāni.

In the first discourse of the Chapter on the Dhamma-Speaker, 'to this extent one has gone to ignorance' has this meaning: to the extent that one is endowed with this ignorance, which is the state of not knowing regarding the four truths, to that extent one has gone to ignorance. In the second discourse as well, this same method should be understood. The First and Second.

3. Dhammakathikasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Explanation of the Discourse on the Dhamma-Speaker

115. Tatiye paṭhamena dhammakathiko, dutiyena sekhabhūmi, tatiyena asekhabhūmīti evaṃ dhammakathikaṃ pucchitena visesetvā dve bhūmiyo kathitā. Tatiyaṃ.

115. In the third, by the first section, the Dhamma-speaker is explained; by the second, the stage of a trainee is explained; by the third, the stage of one beyond training is explained. Thus, the one who was asked, having gone beyond the question about the Dhamma-speaker, explained two stages. The Third.

4. Dutiyadhammakathikasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Explanation of the Second Discourse on the Dhamma-Speaker

116. Catutthe tissannampi pucchānaṃ tīṇi vissajjanāni kathitāni. Catutthaṃ.

116. In the fourth, three answers to all three questions have been given. The Fourth.

5-9. Bandhanasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-9. Explanation of the Discourses Beginning with the Discourse on Bondage

117-121. Pañcame atīradassīti tīraṃ vuccati vaṭṭaṃ, taṃ na passati. Apāradassīti pāraṃ vuccati nibbānaṃ, taṃ na passati. Baddhoti kilesabandhanena baddho hutvā jīyati ca mīyati [V 2.0294] ca asmā lokā paraṃ lokaṃ gacchatīti. Imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭadukkhaṃ kathitanti. Chaṭṭhādīni uttānatthāneva. Pañcamādīni.

In the fifth, regarding 'one who does not see the near shore': the round of existence is called the 'near shore'; one does not see it. Regarding 'one who does not see the far shore': Nibbāna is called the 'far shore'; one does not see it. 'Bound' means: being bound by the bond of defilements, one grows old and dies, and goes from this world to the next. Thus, in this discourse, the suffering of the round is spoken of. The discourses beginning with the sixth have an obvious meaning. The discourses beginning with the fifth are finished.

10. Sīlavantasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Discourse about the Virtuous

122. Dasame [P 2.0334] aniccatotiādīsu hutvā abhāvākārena aniccato, paṭipīḷanākārena dukkhato, ābādhaṭṭhena rogato, antodosaṭṭhena gaṇḍato, tesaṃ tesaṃ gaṇḍānaṃ paccayabhāvena vā khaṇanaṭṭhena vā sallato dukkhaṭṭhena aghato, visabhāgamahābhūtasamuṭṭhānaābādhapaccayaṭṭhena ābādhato, asakaṭṭhena parato, palujjanaṭṭhena palokato[M 2.0306], sattasuññataṭṭhena suññato, attābhāvena anattato. Evamettha “aniccato palokato”ti dvīhi padehi aniccamanasikāro, “suññato anattato”ti dvīhi anattamanasikāro, sesehi dukkhamanasikāro vuttoti veditabbo. Sesamettha uttānameva. Dasamaṃ.

122. In the tenth sutta, regarding 'as impermanent' and so on: 'as impermanent' because of its nature of not existing long after having arisen; 'as suffering' because of its nature of oppression; 'as a disease' because of its nature of afflicting; 'as a boil' because of its nature of internal corruption; 'as a dart' because of its nature of piercing; 'as misery' because of its being a state of suffering; 'as an affliction' in the sense of being a condition for illness arising from the discordant great elements; 'as other' in the sense of not being one's own; 'as perishable' because of its nature of falling apart; 'as empty' in the sense of being void of a being; 'as not-self' because of the absence of a self. Thus, it should be understood that here, by the two terms 'as impermanent' and 'as perishable,' the contemplation of impermanence is taught; by the two terms 'as empty' and 'as not-self,' the contemplation of not-self is taught; and by the remaining terms, the contemplation of suffering is taught. The rest here is clear in meaning. The Tenth.

11. Sutavantasuttavaṇṇanā

11. Commentary on the Discourse about the Learned

123. Tathā ekādasame. Dasamasmiñhi “sīlavatā”ti catupārisuddhisīlaṃ vuttaṃ, idha sutavatāti kammaṭṭhānasutaṃ idameva nānākaraṇaṃ. Ekādasamaṃ.

123. Similarly in the eleventh sutta. For in the tenth, by 'one who is virtuous' (sīlavatā), the fourfold purity of virtue is taught; here, by 'one who is learned' (sutavatā), the learning of meditation subjects is taught. This alone is the difference. The Eleventh.

12-13. Kappasuttādivaṇṇanā

12-13. Commentary on the Kappa Sutta and Others

124-125. Dvādasamaterasamāni rāhulovādasadisānevāti. Dvādasamaterasamāni.

The twelfth and thirteenth suttas are just like the discourse in the Rāhulovāda Saṃyutta. The Twelfth and Thirteenth.

Dhammakathikavaggo dvādasamo.

The Chapter on the Dhamma-Preacher is the twelfth.

13. Avijjāvaggo

13. The Chapter on Ignorance

1-10. Samudayadhammasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-10. Commentary on the Discourse on Phenomena Subject to Arising and Others

126-135. Avijjāvaggo [V 2.0295] uttānatthova. Imasmiñhi vagge sabbasuttesu catusaccameva kathitaṃ.

The Chapter on Ignorance is clear in meaning. For in this chapter, in all the suttas, only the Four Truths are taught.

Avijjāvaggo terasamo.

The Chapter on Ignorance is the thirteenth.

14. Kukkuḷavaggo

14. The Chapter on Hot Embers

1-13. Kukkuḷasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-13. Commentary on the Discourse on Hot Embers and Others

136-149. Kukkuḷavaggassa paṭhame kukkuḷanti santattaṃ ādittaṃ chārikarāsiṃ viya mahāpariḷāhaṃ. Imasmiṃ sutte dukkhalakkhaṇaṃ kathitaṃ, sesesu aniccalakkhaṇādīni. Sabbāni cetāni pāṭiyekkaṃ puggalajjhāsayena kathitānīti.

In the first sutta of the Chapter on Hot Embers, 'hot embers' (kukkuḷa) means intensely hot, ablaze, like a heap of hot ashes—a great conflagration. In this sutta, the characteristic of suffering is taught; in the remaining suttas, the characteristics of impermanence and so on are taught. And all these suttas are taught individually according to the disposition of the person.

Kukkuḷavaggo cuddasamo.

The Chapter on Hot Embers is the fourteenth.

15. Diṭṭhivaggo

15. The Chapter on Views

1-9. Ajjhattasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-9. Commentary on the Discourse on the Internal and Others

150-158. Diṭṭhivaggassa [M 2.0307][P 2.0335] paṭhame kiṃ upādāyāti kiṃ paṭicca. Dutiye kiṃ abhinivissāti [V 2.0296] kiṃ abhinivisitvā, paccayaṃ katvāti attho. Tatiyādīsu diṭṭhītiādīni puggalajjhāsayena vuttāni. Paṭhamādīni.

In the first sutta of the Chapter on Views, 'kiṃ upādāya' means 'depending on what' (kiṃ paṭicca). In the second, 'kiṃ abhinivissa' means 'having clung to what,' that is, 'having made it a condition.' In the third and following suttas, 'views' and so on are taught according to the disposition of the individual. The first and following suttas are finished.

10. Ānandasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Explanation of the Ānanda Sutta

159. Dasame upasaṅkamīti aññe bhikkhū pañcakkhandhakammaṭṭhānaṃ kathāpetvā yuñjitvā ghaṭetvā arahattaṃ patvā satthu santike aññaṃ byākaronte disvā “ahampi pañcakkhandhakammaṭṭhānaṃ kathāpetvā yuñjanto ghaṭento, arahattaṃ patvā aññaṃ byākarissāmī”ti cintetvā upasaṅkami. Satthā pana attano dharamānakāle therassa uparimaggattayavajjhānaṃ kilesānaṃ pahānaṃ apassantopi “imassa cittaṃ gaṇhissāmī”ti kathesi. Tassāpi ekaṃ dve vāre manasi katvāva buddhupaṭṭhānavelā jātāti gantabbaṃ hoti. Itissa cittaṃ sampahaṃsamāno vimuttiparipācanīyadhammova so kammaṭṭhānānuyogo jātoti. Dasamaṃ.

159. In the tenth sutta, 'he approached' (upasaṅkami) means that seeing other bhikkhus, after having had the meditation subject of the five aggregates explained, applied themselves, strove, attained the fruit of Arahantship, and then declared their final knowledge in the presence of the Teacher, he thought, 'I too, having had the meditation subject of the five aggregates explained, applying myself, striving, will attain the fruit of Arahantship and declare my final knowledge,' and so he approached. The Teacher, however, though not seeing the abandonment of the defilements to be abandoned by the three higher paths in the elder during his lifetime, thought, 'I will grasp his mind,' and spoke. For him also, it should be understood that after having attended to it only once or twice, the time for attending upon the Buddha arose. Therefore, that application to the meditation subject became a condition that gladdens his mind and ripens deliverance. The tenth is finished.

Diṭṭhivaggo pannarasamo.

The Chapter on Views is the fifteenth.

Uparipaṇṇāsako samatto.

The Uparipaṇṇāsaka is complete.

Khandhasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Khandha Saṃyutta is concluded.

2. Rādhasaṃyuttaṃ

2. The Rādhasaṃyutta

1. Paṭhamavaggo

1. The First Vagga

1. Mārasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Explanation of the Māra Sutta.

160. Rādhasaṃyuttassa [V 2.0297][M 2.0308] paṭhame māro vā assāti maraṇaṃ vā bhaveyya. Māretā vāti māretabbo vā. Yo vā pana mīyatīti yo vā pana marati. Nibbidatthanti nibbidāñāṇatthaṃ. Nibbānatthāti phalavimutti nāmesā anupādānibbānatthāti attho. Accayāsīti [P 2.0336] atikkantosi. Nibbānogadhanti nibbāne patiṭṭhitaṃ. Idaṃ maggabrahmacariyaṃ nāma nibbānabbhantare vussati, na nibbānaṃ atikkamitvāti attho. Nibbānapariyosānanti nibbānaṃ assa pariyosānaṃ, nipphatti niṭṭhāti attho. Paṭhamaṃ.

160. In the first of the Rādhasaṃyutta: `māro vā assa` means 'it might be death.' `Māretā vā` means 'it might be one who is to be killed.' `Yo vā pana mīyati` means 'whatever being dies.' `Nibbidatthaṃ` is for the purpose of the knowledge of disenchantment. `Nibbānatthaṃ`: the meaning is that this, named the liberation of fruition, has the purpose of Nibbāna without clinging. `Accayāsi` means 'you have passed beyond.' `Nibbānogadhaṃ` means 'established in Nibbāna.' The meaning is that this noble practice called the path is lived within Nibbāna; it does not transcend Nibbāna. `Nibbānapariyosānaṃ`: the meaning is that Nibbāna is its end, its completion, its final limit. The first is finished.

2-10. Sattasuttādivaṇṇanā

2-10. Commentary on the Discourse on a Being and Others

161-169. Dutiye satto sattoti laggapucchā. Tatra satto tatra visattoti tatra laggo tatra vilaggo. Paṃsvāgārakehīti paṃsugharakehi. Keḷāyantīti kīḷanti. Dhanāyantīti dhanaṃ viya maññanti. Mamāyantīti “mama idaṃ, mama ida”nti mamattaṃ karonti, aññassa phusitumpi na denti. Vikīḷaniyaṃ karontīti “niṭṭhitā kīḷā”ti te bhindamānā kīḷāvigamaṃ karonti. Tatiye bhavanettīti bhavarajju. Catutthaṃ uttānameva. Pañcamādīsu catūsu cattāri saccāni kathitāni, dvīsu kilesappahānanti. Dutiyādīni.

In the second sutta, `satto, satto` is a question concerning clinging. `Tatra satto tatra visatto` means 'clinging there, variously clinging there.' `Paṃsvāgārakehi` means 'with little dust-houses.' `Keḷāyanti` means 'they play.' `Dhanāyanti` means 'they consider them as wealth.' `Mamāyanti` means 'they make them their own,' thinking, 'This is mine, this is mine.' `Vikīḷaniyaṃ karonti` means 'they make an end of the play' by breaking them, thinking, 'the play is finished.' In the third sutta, `bhavanetti` means 'the rope of existence.' The fourth is straightforward. In the four suttas beginning with the fifth, the four truths are taught; in two, the abandoning of defilements is taught. The second and following are finished.

Paṭhamo vaggo.

The First Vagga is finished.

2. Dutiyavaggo

2. The Second Chapter

1-12. Mārasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-12. Commentary on the Māra Sutta, etc.

170-181. Dutiyavaggassa [V 2.0298] paṭhame māro, māroti maraṇaṃ pucchati. Yasmā pana rūpādivinimuttaṃ maraṇaṃ nāma natthi, tenassa bhagavā rūpaṃ kho, rādha, mārotiādimāha[M 2.0309]. Dutiye māradhammoti maraṇadhammo. Etenupāyena sabbattha attho veditabboti.

In the first sutta of the second chapter, by 'Māra, Māra,' he asks about death. However, since there is no such thing as death apart from form and so forth, for that reason the Blessed One said to him, 'Form, indeed, Rādha, is Māra,' and so on. In the second sutta, 'māradhammo' means 'of the nature of death.' By this method, the meaning should be understood everywhere.

Dutiyo vaggo.

The Second Chapter.

3-4. Āyācanavaggādi

3-4. The Chapter on Entreaty, etc.

1-11. Mārādisuttaekādasakavaṇṇanā

1-11. Commentary on the Eleven Suttas Beginning with Māra

182-205. Tato [P 2.0337] paraṃ uttānatthameva. Ayañhi rādhatthero paṭibhāniyatthero nāma. Tathāgatassa imaṃ theraṃ disvā sukhumaṃ kāraṇaṃ upaṭṭhāti. Tenassa bhagavā nānānayehi dhammaṃ deseti. Evaṃ imasmiṃ rādhasaṃyutte ādito dve vaggā pucchāvasena desitā, tatiyo āyācanena, catuttho upanisinnakakathāvasena. Sakalampi panetaṃ rādhasaṃyuttaṃ therassa vimuttiparipācanīyadhammavaseneva gahitanti veditabbaṃ.

Thereafter, the meaning is straightforward. This Elder Rādha was indeed the Elder of Ready Wit. When the Tathāgata saw this Elder, a subtle reason occurred to him. Therefore, the Blessed One taught him the Dhamma in various ways. Thus, in this Rādhasaṃyutta, the first two chapters were taught by way of questions, the third by way of an entreaty, and the fourth by way of a talk to one sitting near. However, this entire Rādhasaṃyutta should be understood as having been compiled solely by way of the Dhamma that ripens the Elder's deliverance.

Rādhasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Rādhasaṃyutta is concluded.

3. Diṭṭhisaṃyuttaṃ

3. The Connected Discourses on Views

1. Sotāpattivaggo

1. The Chapter on Stream-Entry

1. Vātasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Sutta on Wind

206. Diṭṭhisaṃyutte [V 2.0299][M 2.0310] na vātā vāyantītiādīsu evaṃ kira tesaṃ diṭṭhi – “yepi ete rukkhasākhādīni bhañjantā vātā vāyanti, na ete vātā, vātaleso nāmeso, vāto pana esikatthambho viya pabbatakūṭaṃ viya ca ṭhito. Tathā yāpi etā tiṇakaṭṭhādīni vahantiyo nadiyo sandanti, na ettha udakaṃ sandaki, udakaleso nāmesa, udakaṃ pana esikatthambho viya pabbatakūṭaṃ viya ca ṭhitaṃ. Yāpimā gabbhiniyo vijāyantīti ca vuccanti, kiñcāpi tā milātudarā honti, gabbho pana na nikkhamati, gabbhaleso nāmeso, gabbho pana esikatthambho viya pabbatakūṭaṃ viya ca ṭhito. Yepi ete candimasūriyā udenti vā apenti vā, neva te udenti na apenti, candimasūriyaleso nāmesa, candimasūriyā pana esikatthambho viya pabbatakūṭaṃ viya ca ṭhitā”ti.

206. In the Connected Discourses on Views, regarding the passage beginning 'the winds do not blow,' and so forth, such, it is said, was their view: 'These winds that blow, breaking tree branches and so forth, are not winds; this is merely a semblance of wind. The wind, however, stands like a city-post or a mountain peak. Likewise, these rivers that flow carrying grass and wood—water does not flow therein; this is merely a semblance of water. The water, however, stands like a city-post or a mountain peak. Also, these pregnant women who are said to give birth, although their bellies become slack, the fetus does not come out; this is merely a semblance of a fetus. The fetus, however, stands like a city-post or a mountain peak. And these sun and moon that rise or set, they neither rise nor set; this is merely a semblance of the sun and moon. The sun and moon, however, stand like city-posts or mountain peaks.'

2-4. Etaṃmamasuttādivaṇṇanā

2-4. Commentary on the 'This is Mine' Sutta, and so forth.

207-209. Diṭṭhantiādīsu diṭṭhaṃ rūpāyatanaṃ. Sutaṃ saddāyatanaṃ. Mutaṃ gandhāyatanaṃ rasāyatanaṃ phoṭṭhabbāyatanaṃ. Tañhi patvā gahetabbato mutanti ca vuttaṃ. Avasesāni sattāyatanāni viññātaṃ nāma. Pattanti pariyesitvā vā apariyesitvā vā pattaṃ. Pariyesitanti pattaṃ vā apattaṃ [P 2.0338] vā pariyesitaṃ. Anuvicaritaṃ manasāti cittena anusañcaritaṃ. Lokasmiñhi pariyesitvā pattampi atthi, pariyesitvā nopattampi, apariyesitvā pattampi, apariyesitvā nopattampi. Tattha pariyesitvā pattaṃ pattaṃ nāma, pariyesitvā nopattaṃ pariyesitaṃ nāma[V 2.0300]. Apariyesitvā pattañca apariyesitvā nopattañca manasānuvicaritaṃ nāma. Atha vā pariyesitvā pattampi apariyesitvā pattampi pattaṭṭhena pattaṃ nāma, pariyesitvā nopattameva pariyesitaṃ nāma, apariyesitvā nopattaṃ manasānuvicaritaṃ nāma. Sabbaṃ vā etaṃ manasā anuvicaritameva.

207-209. In the passages beginning with 'what is seen,' and so forth: 'What is seen' is the form-base. 'What is heard' is the sound-base. 'What is sensed' refers to the smell-base, the taste-base, and the tangible-base—for these are called 'sensed' because they are to be apprehended upon contact. The remaining seven bases are called 'what is cognized.' 'Obtained' is what is obtained either after seeking or without seeking. 'Sought' is what has been sought, whether obtained or not. 'Pondered by the mind' means repeatedly traversed by the mind. For in the world, there is that which is obtained after seeking, that which is not obtained after seeking, that which is obtained without seeking, and that which is not obtained without seeking. Among these, what is obtained after seeking is called 'obtained'; what is not obtained after seeking is called 'sought'; what is obtained without seeking and what is not obtained without seeking are called 'pondered by the mind.' Alternatively, what is obtained after seeking and what is obtained without seeking are both called 'obtained' in the sense of having been obtained; only what is not obtained after seeking is called 'sought'; what is not obtained without seeking is called 'pondered by the mind.' Or, all of this is simply 'pondered by the mind.'

5. Natthidinnasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Explanation of the Natthidinna Sutta

210. Natthi [M 2.0311] dinnantiādīsu natthi dinnanti dinnassa phalābhāvaṃ sandhāya vadanti. Yiṭṭhaṃ vuccati mahāyāgo. Hutanti paheṇakasakkāro adhippeto. Tampi ubhayaṃ phalābhāvameva sandhāya paṭikkhipanti. Sukatadukkaṭānanti sukatadukkatānaṃ, kusalākusalānanti attho. Phalaṃ vipākoti yaṃ phalanti vā vipākoti vā vuccati, taṃ natthīti vadanti. Natthi ayaṃ lokoti paraloke ṭhitassa ayaṃ loko natthi. Natthi paro lokoti idha loke ṭhitassapi paro loko natthi, sabbe tattha tattheva ucchijjantīti dassenti. Natthi mātā natthi pitāti tesu sammāpaṭipattimicchāpaṭipattīnaṃ phalābhāvavasena vadanti. Natthi sattā opapātikāti cavitvā uppajjanakasattā nāma natthīti vadanti. Natthi loke samaṇabrāhmaṇāti loke sammāpaṭipannā samaṇabrāhmaṇā nāma natthīti vadanti.

210. In 'there is no given,' etc.: 'There is no given' is said with reference to the non-existence of the fruit of giving. `Yiṭṭhaṃ` is called a great sacrifice. By `hutaṃ` is intended honorific offerings to guests. They reject both of these with reference to the very non-existence of fruit. `Sukatadukkaṭānaṃ` means of good and bad deeds, that is, of wholesome and unwholesome kamma. 'Fruit, result' means whatever is called 'fruit' or 'result'—they say that does not exist. 'This world does not exist' means for one established in the other world, this world does not exist. 'The other world does not exist' means for one established in this world, the other world does not exist; they show that all beings are annihilated right there where they are. 'There is no mother, there is no father' is said by way of the non-existence of fruit from right and wrong conduct towards them. 'There are no spontaneously arisen beings' means they say there are no beings who, having passed away, arise. 'There are no ascetics or brahmins in the world' means they say there are no ascetics or brahmins in the world who are of right practice.

Cātumahābhūtikoti catumahābhūtamayo. Pathavī pathavīkāyanti ajjhattikā pathavīdhātu bāhiraṃ pathavīdhātuṃ. Anupetīti anuyāti. Anupagacchatīti tasseva vevacanaṃ, anugacchatītipi attho. Ubhayenāpi upeti upagacchatīti dassenti. Āpādīsupi eseva nayo. Indriyānīti manacchaṭṭhāni indriyāni. Saṅkamantīti [P 2.0339] ākāsaṃ pakkhandanti. Āsandipañcamāti nipannamañcena pañcamā, mañco ceva, cattāro mañcapāde gahetvā ṭhitā cattāro purisā cāti attho. Yāva āḷāhanāti yāva susānā. Padānīti “ayaṃ evaṃ sīlavā ahosi, evaṃ dussīlo”tiādinā nayena pavattāni guṇāguṇapadāni. Sarīrameva vā ettha padānīti adhippetaṃ. Kāpotakānīti kapotakavaṇṇāni, pārāvatapakkhavaṇṇānīti attho. Bhassantāti bhasmantā. Ayameva vā pāḷi. Āhutiyoti yaṃ paheṇakasakkārādibhedaṃ dinnadānaṃ, sabbaṃ taṃ chārikāvasānameva hoti, na tato paraṃ phaladāyakaṃ hutvā gacchatīti attho. Dattupaññattanti [V 2.0301] dattūhi bālamanussehi paññattaṃ. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – bālehi abuddhīhi paññattamidaṃ dānaṃ, na paṇḍitehi. Bālā denti, paṇḍitā gaṇhantīti dassenti.

`Cātumahābhūtiko` means composed of the four great elements. `Pathavī pathavīkāyaṃ` (anupeti): the internal earth element (goes to) the external earth element. `Anupeti` means it follows. `Anupagacchati` is a synonym for that; it also means it follows along. By both terms, they show that it approaches, it goes to. This same method applies to the water element and so on. `Indriyāni` means the faculties with the mind as the sixth. `Saṅkamanti` means they leap into space. `Āsandipañcamā` means five with the bier; the meaning is the bier itself, and the four men standing holding the four legs of the bier. `Yāva āḷāhanā` means up to the charnel ground. `Padāni` refers to the terms of qualities and faults that have occurred in the manner of, 'This one was so virtuous; this one was so unvirtuous,' etc. Alternatively, here by `padāni` the body itself is intended. `Kāpotakāni` means dove-colored, that is, the color of a pigeon's wing. `Bhassantā` means ending in ashes. Or this (`bhasmantā`) is the Pāli reading itself. `Āhutiyo` means whatever gift is given, such as honorific offerings to guests and so on—all that giving has only ashes as its end; it does not proceed beyond that to become fruit-bearing. This is the meaning. `Dattupaññattaṃ` means prescribed by fools, by foolish people. This is what is said: This giving is prescribed by fools, by those without wisdom, not by the wise. Fools give, the wise receive—this is what they show.

6. Karotosuttavaṇṇanā

6. Explanation of the Karoto Sutta

211. Karototi [M 2.0312] sahatthā karontassa. Kārayatoti āṇattiyā kārentassa. Chindatoti paresaṃ hatthādīni chindantassa. Chedāpayatoti parehi chedāpentassa. Pacatoti daṇḍena pīḷentassa. Pacāpayatoti parehi daṇḍādinā pīḷāpentassa. Socato socāpayatoti parassa bhaṇḍaharaṇādīhi sokaṃ sayaṃ karontassāpi parehi kārentassāpi. Kilamato kilamāpayatoti āhārupacchedabandhanāgārapavesanādīhi sayaṃ kilamentassapi parehi kilamāpentassapi. Phandato phandāpayatoti paraṃ phandantaṃ phandanakāle sayampi phandato parempi phandāpayato. Pāṇamatipātayatoti [P 2.0340] pāṇaṃ hanantassapi hanāpentassapi. Evaṃ sabbattha karaṇakārāpanavaseneva attho veditabbo.

211. Karoto means: for one who does with his own hand. Kārayato means: for one who causes to be done by command. Chindato means: for one who cuts off the hands, etc., of others. Chedāpayato means: for one who causes them to be cut off by others. Pacato means: for one who torments with punishment. Pacāpayato means: for one who causes them to be tormented by others with punishment, etc. Socato socāpayatoti means: for one who himself causes sorrow by such things as stealing another's belongings, and for one who causes it to be done by others. Kilamato kilamāpayatoti means: for one who himself causes exhaustion by such things as cutting off food, imprisonment, and confinement in a house, and for one who causes it to be done by others. Phandato phandāpayatoti means: for one who himself trembles and for one who causes another to tremble. Pāṇamatipātayatoti means: for one who destroys life and for one who causes life to be destroyed. In this way, the meaning should be understood everywhere according to the distinction between doing and causing to be done.

Sandhinti gharasandhiṃ. Nillopanti mahāvilopaṃ. Ekāgārikanti ekameva gharaṃ parivāretvā vilumpanaṃ. Paripanthe tiṭṭhatoti āgatāgatānaṃ acchindanatthaṃ magge tiṭṭhato. Karoto na karīyati pāpanti yaṃkiñci pāpaṃ karomīti saññāya karotopi pāpaṃ na karīyati, natthi pāpaṃ. Sattā pana karomāti evaṃsaññino hontīti dīpenti. Khurapariyantenāti khuraneminā, khuradhārasadisapariyantena vā. Ekamaṃsakhalanti ekamaṃsarāsiṃ. Puñjanti tasseva vevacanaṃ. Tatonidānanti ekamaṃsakhalakaraṇanidānaṃ.

Sandhiṃ means: house-breaking. Nillopaṃ means: a great robbery, the plundering of an entire village. Ekāgārikaṃ means: the looting of just one house after surrounding it. Paripanthe tiṭṭhatoti means: for one who stands on the road in order to seize those who come and go. Karoto na karīyati pāpanti means: even for one who acts with the perception, 'I am doing an evil deed,' no evil is done; there is no evil. However, beings have the perception, 'We are doing evil'—thus they explain. Khurapariyantenāti means: with a wheel-like weapon having a rim like a razor, or with an edge like a razor blade. Ekamaṃsakhalanti means: a single heap of flesh. Puñjanti is a synonym for that very term. Tatonidānanti means: having the making of that single heap of flesh as its cause.

Dakkhiṇanti dakkhiṇatīre manussā kakkhaḷā dāruṇā, te sandhāya hanantotiādi vuttaṃ. Uttaranti uttaratīre saddhā honti pasannā buddhamāmakā dhammamāmakā saṅghamāmakā, te sandhāya dadantotiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha yajantoti mahāyāgaṃ karonto. Damenāti indriyadamena uposathakammena. Saṃyamenāti sīlasaṃyamena. Saccavajjenāti saccavacanena. Āgamoti āgamanaṃ, pavattīti attho. Sabbathāpi pāpapuññānaṃ kiriyameva paṭikkhipanti.

By Dakkhiṇaṃ is meant: on the southern bank, the people are harsh and cruel; with reference to them, 'killing,' and so forth is said. By Uttaraṃ is meant: on the northern bank, they are faithful, confident, devoted to the Buddha, devoted to the Dhamma, and devoted to the Saṅgha; with reference to them, 'giving,' and so forth is said. Therein, by yajanto is meant performing a great sacrifice. By Damena is meant by the taming of the senses and by the Uposatha observance. By Saṃyamena is meant by restraint of virtue. By Saccavajjena is meant by truthful speech. By Āgamo is meant that there is no coming, that is, no occurrence. In every way, they reject the very doing of evil and merit.

7. Hetusuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Discourse on Causes

212. Natthi [V 2.0302][M 2.0313] hetu natthi paccayoti ettha paccayoti hetuvevacanameva. Ubhayenāpi vijjamānameva kāyaduccaritādīnaṃ saṃkilesapaccayaṃ, kāyasucaritādīnañca visuddhipaccayaṃ paṭikkhipanti. Natthi balanti yamhi attano bale patiṭṭhitā ime sattā devattampi mārattampi brahmattampi sāvakabodhimpi paccekabodhimpi sabbaññutampi pāpuṇanti, taṃ balaṃ paṭikkhipanti. Natthi vīriyantiādīni sabbāni aññamaññavevacanāneva. “Idaṃ no vīriyena, idaṃ purisathāmena, idaṃ purisaparakkamena [P 2.0341] patta”nti, evaṃ pavattavacanapaṭikkhepakaraṇavasena panetāni visuṃ ādiyanti.

212. In the phrase 'There is no cause, there is no condition,' the word 'condition' is just a synonym for 'cause.' With both terms, they reject the cause of defilement for bodily misconduct and so on, and the cause of purification for good bodily conduct and so on, even though these actually exist. In 'There is no strength,' they reject that strength of one's own, established in which these beings attain the state of a deva, the state of Māra, the state of a brahmā, the enlightenment of a disciple, the enlightenment of a solitary enlightened one, and omniscience. 'There is no energy,' and so on—all these are just synonyms for one another. However, they take these terms separately by way of rejecting statements that proceed thus: 'This was attained by our energy,' 'This, by manly strength,' 'This, by manly exertion.'

Sabbe sattāti oṭṭhagoṇagadrabhādayo anavasese pariggaṇhanti. Sabbe pāṇāti ekindriyo pāṇo, dvindriyo pāṇotiādivasena vadanti. Sabbe bhūtāti aṇḍakosavatthikosesu bhūte sandhāya vadanti. Sabbe jīvāti sāliyavagodhumādayo sandhāya vadanti. Tesu hi te viruhanabhāvena jīvasaññino. Avasā abalā avīriyāti tesaṃ attano vaso vā balaṃ vā vīriyaṃ vā natthi. Niyatisaṅgatibhāvapariṇatāti ettha niyatīti niyatatā. Saṅgatīti channaṃ abhijātīnaṃ tattha tattha gamanaṃ. Bhāvoti sabhāvoyeva. Evaṃ niyatiyā ca saṅgatiyā ca bhāvena ca pariṇatā nānappakārataṃ pattā. Yena hi yathā bhavitabbaṃ, so tatheva bhavati. Yena na bhavitabbaṃ, so na bhavatīti dassenti. Chasvevābhijātīsūti chasu eva abhijātīsu ṭhatvā sukhañca dukkhañca paṭisaṃvedenti, aññā sukhadukkhabhūmi natthīti dassenti.

By 'All beings' they include all without exception, such as camels, oxen, and donkeys. By 'All living things' they speak by classifying them as one-facultied living things, two-facultied living things, and so on. By 'All creatures' they speak with reference to creatures in eggshells and wombs. By 'All life' they speak with reference to rice, barley, wheat, and so on, for in these, because of their nature of growing, they have the perception of life. By 'Without power, without strength, without energy' is meant that for them there is no power, strength, or energy of their own. Regarding 'Transformed by destiny, association, and nature'—here 'destiny' means fixedness. 'Association' is the going to various states among the six classes. 'Nature' is just inherent nature. Thus, transformed by destiny, association, and nature, they attain various states. They show that by whatever way a thing is to be, so it becomes; and by whatever way it is not to be, so it does not become. By 'Only in the six classes' is meant that, standing only in these six classes, they experience pleasure and pain; they show there is no other plane of neither-pain-nor-pleasure.

8-10. Mahādiṭṭhisuttādivaṇṇanā

8-10. Commentary on the Mahādiṭṭhi Discourse, etc.

213-215. Akaṭāti akatā. Akaṭavidhāti akatavidhānā, “evaṃ karohī”ti kenaci kāritāpi na hontīti attho. Animmitāti iddhiyāpi na nimmitā. Animmātāti animmāpitā. “Animmitabbā”tipi pāṭho, na nimmitabbāti attho. Vañjhāti vañjhapasuvañjhatālādayo viya aphalā kassaci ajanakā. Pabbatakūṭaṃ viya ṭhitāti kūṭaṭṭhā. Esikaṭṭhāyino viya hutvā ṭhitāti esikaṭṭhāyiṭṭhitā, yathā sunikhāto esikatthambho niccalo tiṭṭhati, evaṃ ṭhitāti attho. Na [P 2.0342] iñjantīti esikatthambho viya ṭhitattā [M 2.0314] na calanti. Na vipariṇamantīti [V 2.0303] pakatiṃ na vijahanti. Na aññamaññaṃ byābādhentīti aññamaññaṃ na upahananti. Nālanti na samatthā. Pathavīkāyotiādīsu pathavīyeva pathavīkāyo, pathavīsamūho vā. Sattannaṃtveva kāyānanti yathā muggarāsiādīsu pahaṭaṃ satthaṃ muggarāsiādīnaṃ antareneva pavisati, evaṃ sattannaṃ kāyānaṃ antarena chiddena vivarena satthaṃ pavisati. Tattha “ahaṃ imaṃ jīvitā voropemī”ti kevalaṃ saññāmattameva hotīti dassenti.

213-215. By 'Akaṭā' is meant not made. By 'Akaṭavidhā' is meant not arranged to be made; the meaning is that they are not caused to be made by anyone saying, 'Do it like this.' By 'Animmitā' is meant not created even by supernatural power. By 'Animmātā' is meant not caused to be created. There is also the reading 'animmitabbā,' meaning not to be created. By 'Vañjhā' is meant barren, like a barren cow or a fruitless palm tree, fruitless and producing nothing for anyone. By 'Kūṭaṭṭhā' is meant they stand like a mountain peak. By 'Esikaṭṭhāyiṭṭhitā' is meant they stand like a city gate-post; the meaning is that just as a firmly planted gate-post stands unmoving, so they stand. By 'Na iñjanti' is meant they do not stir; because they stand like a gate-post, they do not move. By 'Na vipariṇamanti' is meant they do not abandon their natural state. By 'Na aññamaññaṃ byābādhenti' is meant they do not harm one another. By 'Nālaṃ' is meant they are not able. In 'pathavīkāyo' and so on, 'pathavīkāyo' is the earth element itself, or a collection of earth. By 'Sattannaṃtveva kāyānaṃ' is meant that just as a weapon struck into a pile of beans enters only through the space between the beans, so too a weapon enters through the gap, the opening, between the seven bodies. Therein, they show that the thought, 'I deprive this one of life,' is merely a perception.

Yonipamukhasatasahassānīti pamukhayonīnaṃ uttamayonīnaṃ cuddasasatasahassāni aññāni ca saṭṭhisatāni aññāni ca chasatāni pañca ca kammuno satānīti pañcakammasatāni cāti kevalaṃ takkamattakena niratthakadiṭṭhiṃ dīpenti. Pañca ca kammāni tīṇi ca kammānītiādīsupi eseva nayo. Keci panāhu “pañca kammānīti pañcindriyavasena gaṇhanti, tīṇīti kāyakammādivasenā”ti. Kamme ca aḍḍhakamme cāti ettha panassa kāyakammavacīkammāni kammanti laddhi, manokammaṃ upaḍḍhakammanti. Dvaṭṭhipaṭipadāti dvāsaṭṭhipaṭipadāti vadanti. Dvaṭṭhantarakappāti ekasmiṃ kappe catusaṭṭhi antarakappā nāma honti, ayaṃ pana aññe dve ajānanto evamāha.

By 'Yonipamukhasatasahassāni' is meant fourteen hundred thousand principal births, which are superior births, plus another sixty hundred, and another six hundred; and by 'pañca ca kammuno satāni' is meant five hundred actions. Thus, merely by way of conjecture, they reveal a worthless view. In 'five actions, three actions,' and so on, this same method applies. Some, however, say: they take 'five actions' by way of the five faculties, and 'three' by way of bodily action and so on. Regarding 'in a full action and in a half action,' their doctrine is that bodily and verbal actions are 'full action,' and mental action is 'half action.' By 'Dvaṭṭhipaṭipadā' they say is meant sixty-two practices. By 'Dvaṭṭhantarakappā' is meant that in one eon there are sixty-four intermediate eons, but this one, not knowing the other two, spoke thus.

Chaḷābhijātiyoti kaṇhābhijāti nīlābhijāti lohitābhijāti haliddābhijāti sukkābhijāti paramasukkābhijātīti imā cha abhijātiyo vadanti. Tattha orabbhikā sūkarikā sākuṇikā māgavikā luddā macchaghātakā corā coraghātakā bandhanāgārikā, ye vā panaññepi keci kurūrakammantā, ayaṃ kaṇhābhijātīti vadanti. Bhikkhū nīlābhijātīti vadanti. Te kira catūsu paccayesu kaṇṭake pakkhipitvā khādanti, “bhikkhū [P 2.0343] ca kaṇṭakavuttikā”ti (a. ni. 6.57) ayaṃ hissa pāḷi eva. Atha vā kaṇṭakavuttikā eva nāma eke pabbajitāti vadanti. Lohitābhijāti nāma nigaṇṭhā ekasāṭakāti vadanti. Ime kira purimehi dvīhi paṇḍaratarā. Gihī odātavasanā acelakasāvakā haliddābhijātīti vadanti. Evaṃ attano paccayadāyake nigaṇṭhehipi jeṭṭhakatare karonti. Ājīvakā ājīviniyo ayaṃ sukkābhijātīti vadanti. Te kira purimehi catūhi paṇḍaratarā[M 2.0315]. Nando vaccho, kiso saṃkicco, makkhali gosālo paramasukkābhijātīti vadanti. Te kira sabbehi paṇḍaratarā.

'Chaḷābhijātiyo' refers to the six classes they speak of: the black class, the blue class, the red class, the yellow class, the white class, and the supremely white class. Among them, sheep-butchers, pig-butchers, fowlers, deer-hunters, cruel hunters, fishermen, thieves, executioners of thieves, jailers, and any others who perform cruel deeds—these they call the black class. Monks, they call the blue class. It is said that they partake of the four requisites having inserted the 'thorns'; for this is his Pāḷi text: 'monks with a thorny practice.' Alternatively, they say there are certain ascetics outside the dispensation who are simply called 'those of thorny practice.' The red class, they say, are the Nigaṇṭhas who wear a single garment. It is said these are purer than the first two. Householders who wear white clothes and are disciples of the naked ascetics, they call the yellow class. Thus they make their own supporters of requisites superior even to the Nigaṇṭhas. The Ājīvakas and Ājīvinīs—these they call the white class. It is said they are purer than the previous four. Nanda Vaccha, Kisa Saṅkicca, and Makkhali Gosāla, they call the supremely white class. It is said they are the purest of all.

Aṭṭha [V 2.0304] purisabhūmiyoti mandabhūmi khiḍḍābhūmi vīmaṃsakabhūmi ujugatabhūmi sekhabhūmi samaṇabhūmi jānanabhūmi pannabhūmīti imā aṭṭha purisabhūmiyoti vadanti. Tattha jātadivasato paṭṭhāya satta divase sambādhaṭṭhānato nikkhantattā sattā mandā honti momūhā, ayaṃ mandabhūmīti vadanti. Ye pana duggatito āgatā honti, te abhiṇhaṃ rodanti ceva viravanti ca, sugatito āgatā taṃ anussaritvā anussaritvā hasanti, ayaṃ khiḍḍābhūmi nāma. Mātāpitūnaṃ hatthaṃ vā pādaṃ vā mañcaṃ vā pīṭhaṃ vā gahetvā bhūmiyaṃ padanikkhipanaṃ vīmaṃsakabhūmi nāma. Padasā gantuṃ samatthakālo ujugatabhūmi nāma. Sippāni sikkhanakālo sekhabhūmi nāma. Gharā nikkhamma pabbajanakālo samaṇabhūmi nāma. Ācariyaṃ sevitvā jānanakālo jānanabhūmi nāma. “Bhikkhu ca pannako jino na kiñci āhā”ti evaṃ alābhiṃ samaṇaṃ pannabhūmīti vadanti.

The eight stages of a person’s life are called the dull stage, the playful stage, the investigative stage, the upright stage, the learner's stage, the ascetic stage, the knowing stage, and the fallen stage. These are called the eight stages of a person’s life. From the day of birth for seven days, beings are dull and bewildered because they have emerged from a confined place; this is called the dull stage. Those who come from an unfortunate destination cry and wail frequently, while those who come from a fortunate destination laugh, remembering it again and again; this is called the playful stage. Taking hold of the mother's or father's hand or foot, or a bed or a seat, and placing a foot on the ground is called the investigative stage. The time when one is capable of going on foot is called the upright stage. The time of learning skills is called the learner's stage. The time of leaving home and going forth is called the ascetic stage. The time of knowing after attending upon a teacher is called the knowing stage. Regarding the saying, 'A monk, fallen and a victor, said nothing,' they call a renunciate with little gain the 'fallen stage'.

Ekūnapaññāsa [P 2.0344] ājīvakasateti ekūnapaññāsa ājīvavuttisatāni. Paribbājakasateti paribbājakapabbajjāsatāni. Nāgavāsasateti nāgamaṇḍalasatāni. Vīse indriyasateti vīsa indriyasatāni. Tiṃse nirayasateti tiṃsa nirayasatāni. Rajodhātuyoti rajaokiraṇaṭṭhānāni. Hatthapiṭṭhipādapiṭṭhādīni sandhāya vadati. Satta saññīgabbhāti oṭṭhagoṇagadrabhaajapasumigamahiṃse sandhāya vadati. Satta asaññīgabbhāti sāliyavagodhumamuggakaṅguvarakakudrūsake sandhāya vadati. Nigaṇṭhigabbhāti gaṇṭhimhi jātagabbhā, ucchuveḷunaḷādayo sandhāya vadati. Satta devāti bahū devā, so pana sattāti vadati. Manussāpi anantā, so sattāti vadati. Satta pesācāti pisācā mahantamahantā, sattāti vadati. Sarāti mahāsarā. Kaṇṇamuṇḍa-rathakāra-anotatta-sīhappapāta-chaddanta-mucalinda-kuṇāladahe gahetvā vadati.

'Forty-nine hundreds of Ājīvakas' means forty-nine hundred Ājīvaka modes of livelihood. 'Hundreds of wanderers' means hundreds of wanderer-renunciations. 'Hundreds of Nāga abodes' means hundreds of Nāga realms. 'Twenty hundreds of faculties' means two thousand faculties. 'Thirty hundreds of hells' means three thousand hells. 'Dust elements' means places where dust is scattered. He speaks with reference to the backs of hands, backs of feet, and so on. 'Seven conscious wombs': he speaks with reference to camels, oxen, donkeys, goats, sheep, deer, and buffaloes. 'Seven unconscious wombs': he speaks with reference to rice, barley, wheat, mung beans, foxtail millet, Italian millet, and kudrūsaka grains. 'Nigaṇṭha wombs' means those with seeds born in nodes; he speaks with reference to sugarcane, bamboo, reeds, and the like. 'Seven gods': gods are many, but he says 'seven'. Humans too are countless, but he says 'seven'. 'Seven pesācas': pesācas are very numerous, but he says 'seven'. 'Lakes' means great lakes. He speaks by taking into account the Kaṇṇamuṇḍa, Rathakāra, Anotatta, Sīhappapāta, Chaddanta, Mucalinda, and Kuṇāla lakes.

Pavuṭāti gaṇṭhikā. Papātāti mahāpapātā. Papātasatānīti khuddakapapātasatāni. Supināti mahāsupinā. Supinasatānīti khuddakasupinasatāni. Mahākappinoti mahākappānaṃ. Ettha ekamhā mahāsarā vassasate [M 2.0316] vassasate kusaggena ekaṃ udakabinduṃ nīharitvā sattakkhattuṃ tamhi sare nirudake kate eko mahākappoti vadati. Evarūpānaṃ mahākappānaṃ caturāsītisatasahassāni khepetvā bāle ca paṇḍite ca dukkhassantaṃ karontīti ayamassa laddhi. Paṇḍitopi kira antarāvisujjhituṃ na sakkoti, bālopi tato uddhaṃ na gacchati.

'Pavuṭā' means 'knots'. 'Papātā' means 'great precipices'. 'Hundreds of precipices' means 'hundreds of small precipices'. 'Dreams' means 'great dreams'. 'Hundreds of dreams' means 'hundreds of small dreams'. 'Of great eons' means 'of great eons'. Here, it is said that one great eon is when, from one great lake, a single drop of water is removed with the tip of a kusa grass blade every hundred years, and that lake is made waterless seven times. Having expended eighty-four hundred thousand such great eons, both the fool and the wise make an end of suffering—this is his doctrine. It is said that the wise cannot become purified in the interim, and the fool does not go beyond that.

Sīlena [V 2.0305][P 2.0345] vāti acelakasīlena vā aññena vā yena kenaci. Vatenāti tādiseneva vatena. Tapenāti tapokammena. Aparipakkaṃ paripāceti nāma yo “ahaṃ paṇḍito”ti antarā visujjhati. Paripakkaṃ phussa phussa byantīkaroti nāma yo “ahaṃ bālo”ti vuttaparimāṇakālaṃ atikkamitvā yāti. Hevaṃ natthīti evaṃ natthi. Tañhi ubhayampi na sakkā kātunti dīpeti. Doṇamiteti doṇena mitaṃ viya. Sukhadukkheti sukhadukkhaṃ. Pariyantakateti vuttaparimāṇena kālena katapariyanto. Natthi hāyanavaḍḍhaneti natthi hāyanavaḍḍhanāni, na saṃsāro paṇḍitassa hāyati, na bālassa vaḍḍhatīti attho. Ukkaṃsāvakaṃseti ukkaṃsāvakaṃsā. Hāyanavaḍḍhanānamevetaṃ vevacanaṃ. Idāni tamatthaṃ upamāya sādhento seyyathāpi nāmātiādimāha. Tattha suttaguḷeti veṭhetvā katasuttaguḷe. Nibbeṭhiyamānameva paletīti pabbate vā rukkhagge vā ṭhatvā khittaṃ suttappamāṇena nibbeṭhiyamānameva gacchati, sutte khīṇe tattheva tiṭṭhati, na gacchati evameva bālā ca paṇḍitā ca kālavasena nibbeṭhiyamānā sukhadukkhaṃ palenti, yathāvuttena kālena atikkamantīti dasseti.

'By virtue' means by the virtue of naked ascetics or by any other virtue whatsoever. 'By practice' means by such a practice. 'By austerity' means by the performance of austerity. He who is purified in the interim, thinking, 'I am wise,' is said to ripen the unripe. He who, thinking, 'I am a fool,' goes on, having exceeded the stated period of time, is said to make an end of the ripe by experiencing it again and again. 'It is not so' means that this is not so. For it is shown that both cannot be done. 'Measured by a doṇa' means as if measured with a doṇa. 'Pleasure and pain' means pleasure and pain. 'Having a limit' means having a limit made by the stated period of time. 'There is no decrease or increase' means there are no decreases or increases; the meaning is that the cycle of existence neither decreases for the wise nor increases for the fool. 'Exaltation and debasement' means exaltations and debasements. This is just another term for decrease and increase. Now, demonstrating that meaning with a simile, he says, 'Just as, for example,' and so on. There, 'a ball of thread' means a ball of thread made by winding. 'It runs on only as it is unwound' means that when a ball of thread is thrown while standing on a mountain or in a treetop, it goes only as far as the length of the thread as it is unwound; when the thread is exhausted, it stops right there and does not go on. In the same way, he shows that fools and the wise, being unwound by the power of time, run their course through pleasure and pain and, by means of the stated time, pass beyond.

11-18. Antavāsuttādivaṇṇanā

11-18. The Commentary on the Antavā Sutta, etc.

216-223. Antavā lokoti ekato vaḍḍhitanimittaṃ lokoti gāhena vā takkena vā uppannadiṭṭhi. Anantavāti sabbato vaḍḍhitaṃ appamāṇanimittaṃ lokoti gāhena vā takkena vā uppannadiṭṭhi. Taṃ jīvaṃ taṃ sarīranti jīvañca sarīrañca ekamevāti uppannadiṭṭhi. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti. Imāni tāva sotāpattimaggavasena aṭṭhārasa veyyākaraṇāni ekaṃ gamanaṃ.

216-223. 'The world is finite' is the view that has arisen, either through grasping or through reasoning, that the kasiṇa-sign developed on one side is 'the world'. 'The world is infinite' is the view that has arisen, either through grasping or through reasoning, that the immeasurable kasiṇa-sign developed on all sides is 'the world'. 'The life-principle is the same as the body' is the view that has arisen that the life-principle and the body are one and the same. The rest is clear everywhere. These are the eighteen explanations by way of the path of stream-entry, making one passage.

2. Dutiyagamanādivaggavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Chapter on the Second Passage, etc.

224-301. Dutiyaṃ [M 2.0317] gamanaṃ dukkhavasena vuttaṃ. Tatrāpi aṭṭhāraseva veyyākaraṇāni, tato parāni “rūpī attā hotī”tiādīni aṭṭha veyyākaraṇāni, tehi saddhiṃ taṃ dutiyapeyyāloti vutto.

224-301. The second passage is spoken by way of suffering. Therein also are just eighteen explanations. After those are the eight explanations beginning with 'the self has form,' etc. Together with those, it is called the 'second peyyāla' (repetition series).

Tattha [P 2.0346] rūpīti ārammaṇameva “attā”ti gahitadiṭṭhi. Arūpīti jhānaṃ “attā”ti gahitadiṭṭhi[V 2.0306]. Rūpī ca arūpī cāti ārammaṇañca jhānañca “attā”ti gahitadiṭṭhi. Neva rūpī nārūpīti takkamattena gahitadiṭṭhi. Ekantasukhīti lābhītakkījātissarānaṃ uppannadiṭṭhi. Jhānalābhinopi hi atīte ekantasukhaṃ attabhāvaṃ manasikaroto evaṃ diṭṭhi uppajjati. Takkinopi “yathā etarahi ahaṃ ekantasukhī, evaṃ samparāyepi bhavissāmī”ti uppajjati. Jātissarassapi sattaṭṭhabhave sukhitabhāvaṃ passantassa evaṃ uppajjati, ekantadukkhītiādīsupi eseva nayo.

Herein, 'having form' is the view that grasps the meditation object (kasiṇa) itself as the self. 'Formless' is the view that grasps jhāna as the self. 'Having form and formless' is the view that grasps both the meditation object and jhāna as the self. 'Neither having form nor formless' is the view grasped by mere reasoning. 'Exclusively happy' is the view that arises for a jhāna-attainer, a reasoner, or one who remembers past lives. Indeed, for a jhāna-attainer, such a view arises when reflecting on a past existence that was exclusively happy. For a reasoner, it arises thus: 'Just as I am now exclusively happy, so will I be in the future.' For one who remembers past lives, such a view arises when seeing a state of happiness in seven or eight existences. This same method should be understood in the cases of 'exclusively suffering', and so on.

Tatiyapeyyālo aniccadukkhavasena tehiyeva chabbīsatiyā suttehi vutto, catutthapeyyālo tiparivaṭṭavasenāti.

The third peyyāla (repetition series) is spoken by way of impermanence and suffering through those same twenty-six suttas; the fourth peyyāla is by way of the three rounds.

Diṭṭhisaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Diṭṭhi Saṃyutta is concluded.

4. Okkantasaṃyuttaṃ

4. The Okkanta Saṃyutta

1-10. Cakkhusuttādivaṇṇanā

1–10. Commentary on the Cakkhu Sutta and so forth.

302-311. Okkantasaṃyutte [V 2.0307] adhimuccatīti saddhādhimokkhaṃ paṭilabhati. Okkanto sammattaniyāmanti paviṭṭho ariyamaggaṃ. Abhabbo ca tāva kālaṃ kātunti iminā uppanne magge phalassa anantarāyataṃ dīpeti. Uppannasmiñhi magge phalassa antarāyakaraṇaṃ nāma natthi. Tenevāha – “ayañca puggalo sotāpattiphalasacchikiriyāya paṭipanno assa, kappassa ca uḍḍayhanavelā assa, neva tāva kappo uḍḍayheyya, yāvāyaṃ puggalo na sotāpattiphalaṃ sacchikaroti, ayaṃ vuccati puggalo ṭhitakappī”ti (pu. pa. 17). Mattaso nijjhānaṃ khamantīti pamāṇato olokanaṃ khamanti. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

302–311. In the Okkanta Saṃyutta, 'adhimuccati' (is resolved) means one obtains faith-resolution. 'Okkanto sammattaniyāmaṃ' (entered the certainty of rightness) means one has entered the noble path. By the phrase 'abhabbo ca tāva kālaṃ kātuṃ' (and is incapable of passing away until then), it shows the non-obstruction of the fruit when the path has arisen. Indeed, when the path has arisen, there is nothing that can cause an obstruction to the fruit. For that reason it is said: “And should this person be practicing for the realization of the fruit of stream-entry, and it were the time for the aeon to be consumed by fire, the aeon would not be consumed by fire so long as this person has not realized the fruit of stream-entry. This person is called one for whom the aeon stands” (cf. Puggalapaññatti p. 17). 'Mattaso nijjhānaṃ khamanti' (they endure scrutiny in due measure) means they endure examination in due measure. The rest is clear everywhere.

Okkantasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Okkanta Saṃyutta is concluded.

5. Uppādasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Uppāda Saṃyutta

312-321. Uppādasaṃyutte [V 2.0308][P 2.0347][M 2.0318] sabbaṃ pākaṭameva.

312–321. In the Uppāda Saṃyutta, everything is clear.

Uppādasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Uppāda Saṃyutta is concluded.

6. Kilesasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Kilesa Saṃyutta

322-331. Kilesasaṃyutte [V 2.0309] cittasseso upakkilesoti kataracittassa? Catubhūmakacittassa. Tebhūmakacittassa tāva hotu, lokuttarassa kathaṃ upakkileso hotīti? Uppattinivāraṇato. So hi tassa uppajjituṃ appadānena upakkilesoti veditabbo. Nekkhammaninnanti navalokuttaradhammaninnaṃ. Cittanti samathavipassanācittaṃ. Abhiññā sacchikaraṇīyesu dhammesūti paccavekkhaṇañāṇena abhijānitvā sacchikātabbesu chaḷabhiññādhammesu, ekaṃ dhammaṃ vā gaṇhantena nekkhammanti gahetabbaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

322–331. In the Kilesa Saṃyutta, regarding the phrase “this is a defilement of the mind” (cittasseso upakkileso), of which mind is it a defilement? Of the four-plane mind (catubhūmakacitta). Let it be so for the three-plane mind (tebhūmakacitta); but how can it be a defilement of the supramundane mind (lokuttaracitta)? By preventing its arising. For it should be understood as a defilement because it does not allow the arising of that supramundane mind. Nekkhammaninnaṃ means inclined toward the nine supramundane dhammas. Cittaṃ means the mind of serenity and insight. Abhiññā sacchikaraṇīyesu dhammesūti means in the six dhammas of direct knowledge to be realized, having been directly known by means of reviewing knowledge. Alternatively, taking it as one dhamma, 'renunciation' (nekkhamman) is to be understood. The rest is clear throughout.

Kilesasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Kilesa Saṃyutta is concluded.

7. Sāriputtasaṃyuttaṃ

7. The Sāriputta Saṃyutta

1-9. Vivekajasuttādivaṇṇanā

1–9. Commentary on the Vivekaja Sutta and so forth.

332-340. Sāriputtasaṃyuttassa [V 2.0310] paṭhame na evaṃ hotīti ahaṅkāramamaṅkārānaṃ pahīnattā evaṃ na hoti. Dutiyādīsupi eseva nayo. Paṭhamādīni.

332–340. In the first discourse of the Sāriputta Saṃyutta, na evaṃ hoti (it is not thus) means it is not thus because 'I-making' (ahaṅkāra) and 'my-making' (mamaṅkāra) have been abandoned. The same method applies in the second and subsequent discourses. The first and so forth.

10. Sucimukhīsuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Sucimukhī Sutta

341. Dasame sucimukhīti evaṃnāmikā. Upasaṅkamīti theraṃ abhirūpaṃ dassanīyaṃ suvaṇṇavaṇṇaṃ samantapāsādikaṃ disvā “iminā saddhiṃ parihāsaṃ karissāmī”ti upasaṅkami. Atha therena tasmiṃ vacane paṭikkhitte “idānissa [M 2.0319] vādaṃ āropessāmī”ti maññamānā tena hi, samaṇa, ubbhamukho bhuñjasīti āha. Disāmukhoti [P 2.0348] catuddisāmukho, catasso disā olokentoti attho. Vidisāmukhoti catasso vidisā olokento.

341. In the tenth discourse, Sucimukhī means a woman named thus. Upasaṅkami (approached) means seeing the elder, who was handsome, pleasing to see, golden-hued, and inspiring confidence all around, she thought, “I will jest with him,” and approached him. Then, when the elder rejected her words, thinking, “Now I will bring a charge against him,” she said, “Well then, ascetic, do you eat facing upward?” Disāmukho means facing the four directions, that is, looking in the four directions. Vidisāmukho means looking at the four intermediate directions.

Vatthuvijjātiracchānavijjāyāti vatthuvijjāsaṅkhātāya tiracchānavijjāya. Vatthuvijjā nāma lābuvatthu-kumbhaṇḍavatthu-mūlakavatthu-ādīnaṃ vatthūnaṃ phalasampattikāraṇakālajānanupāyo. Micchājīvena jīvikaṃ kappentīti teneva vatthuvijjātiracchānavijjāsaṅkhātena micchājīvena jīvikaṃ kappenti, tesaṃ vatthūnaṃ sampādanena pasannehi manussehi dinne paccaye paribhuñjantā jīvantīti attho. Adhomukhāti vatthuṃ oloketvā bhuñjamānavasena adhomukhā bhuñjanti nāma. Evaṃ sabbattha yojanā kātabbā. Api cettha nakkhattavijjāti “ajja imaṃ nakkhattaṃ iminā nakkhattena gantabbaṃ, iminā idañcidañca kātabba”nti evaṃ jānanavijjā. Dūteyyanti dūtakammaṃ, tesaṃ tesaṃ sāsanaṃ gahetvā tattha tattha gamanaṃ. Pahiṇagamananti ekagāmasmiṃyeva ekakulassa sāsanena aññakulaṃ [V 2.0311] upasaṅkamanaṃ. Aṅgavijjāti itthilakkhaṇapurisalakkhaṇavasena aṅgasampattiṃ ñatvā “tāya aṅgasampattiyā idaṃ nāma labbhatī”ti evaṃ jānanavijjā. Vidisāmukhāti aṅgavijjā hi taṃ taṃ sarīrakoṭṭhāsaṃ ārabbha pavattattā vidisāya pavattā nāma, tasmā tāya vijjāya jīvikaṃ kappetvā bhuñjantā vidisāmukhā bhuñjanti nāma. Evamārocesīti “dhammikaṃ samaṇā”tiādīni vadamānā sāsanassa niyyānikaṃ guṇaṃ kathesi. Tañca paribbājikāya kathaṃ sutvā pañcamattāni kulasatāni sāsane otariṃsūti.

The phrase vatthuvijjātiracchānavijjāya means the low art (tiracchānavijjā) known as the science of sites (vatthuvijjā). The science of sites (vatthuvijjā) is the method of knowing the time and means to achieve success for sites such as those for gourds, pumpkins, radishes, and so forth. Micchājīvena jīvikaṃ kappenti (they make a living by wrong livelihood) means they make a living by means of this very low art known as the science of sites. The meaning is that they live by enjoying the requisites given by people who are pleased by the fulfillment of those sites. Adhomukhā (facing downward) means they eat with their faces turned downward, in the manner of eating while looking at the site. This explanation should be applied everywhere. Here also, the science of stars (nakkhattavijjā) is the knowledge that allows one to know, for example, “Today is this constellation; one should travel under this constellation; this and that should be done under this constellation.” Dūteyyaṃ (messengering) means the work of a messenger, which is going here and there after receiving instructions from various people. Pahiṇagamanaṃ (sent and going) means approaching another household in the same village after receiving instructions from one household. Aṅgavijjā (the science of limbs) is the knowledge that allows one to know the perfection of limbs through the marks of women and men, and to know that “such and such will be obtained due to this perfection of limbs.” Vidisāmukhā (facing the intermediate directions) refers to the science of limbs, which operates concerning various parts of the body and thus is said to operate in the intermediate directions. Therefore, those who make a living by this science and eat are said to eat “facing the intermediate directions.” Evamārocesi (thus she reported) means that speaking words like “the ascetics are righteous,” she explained the liberating qualities of the teaching. And upon hearing the words of the female wanderer, five hundred families entered the teaching.

Sāriputtasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Sāriputta Saṃyutta is concluded.

8. Nāgasaṃyuttaṃ

8. The Connected Discourses on Nāgas

1. Suddhikasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Suddhika Sutta

342. Nāgasaṃyutte [V 2.0312] aṇḍajāti aṇḍe jātā. Jalābujāti vatthikose jātā. Saṃsedajāti saṃsede jātā. Opapātikāti upapatitvā [M 2.0320] viya jātā. Idañca pana suttaṃ aṭṭhuppattiyā [P 2.0349] vuttaṃ. Bhikkhūnañhi “kati nu kho nāgayoniyo”ti kathā udapādi. Atha bhagavā puggalānaṃ nāgayonīhi uddharaṇatthaṃ nāgayoniyo āvikaronto imaṃ suttamāha.

8. In the Connected Discourses on Nāgas, aṇḍajāti means born from an egg. Jalābujāti means born from a womb. Saṃsedajāti means born from moisture. Opapātikāti means born as if having arisen. This first discourse, however, was spoken due to an occasion that had arisen. For a discussion arose among the monks: “How many, indeed, are the births of nāgas?” Then the Blessed One, making the nāga-births manifest for the purpose of delivering individuals from the round of rebirths, spoke this discourse.

2-50. Paṇītatarasuttādivaṇṇanā

2-50. Commentary on the Paṇītatara Sutta, etc.

343-391. Dutiyādīsu vossaṭṭhakāyāti ahituṇḍikaparibuddhaṃ agaṇetvā vissaṭṭhakāyā. Dvayakārinoti duvidhakārino, kusalākusalakārinoti attho. Sacajja mayanti sace ajja mayaṃ. Sahabyataṃ upapajjatīti sahabhāvaṃ āpajjati. Tatrassa akusalaṃ upapattiyā paccayo hoti, kusalaṃ upapannānaṃ sampattiyā. Annanti khādanīyabhojanīyaṃ. Pānanti yaṃkiñci pānakaṃ. Vatthanti nivāsanapārupanaṃ. Yānanti chattupāhanaṃ ādiṃ katvā yaṃkiñci gamanapaccayaṃ. Mālanti yaṃkiñci sumanamālādipupphaṃ. Gandhanti yaṃkiñci candanādigandhaṃ. Vilepananti yaṃkiñci chavirāgakaraṇaṃ. Seyyāvasathapadīpeyyanti mañcapīṭhādiseyyaṃ ekabhūmikādiāvasathaṃ vaṭṭitelādipadīpūpakaraṇañca detīti attho. Tesañhi dīghāyukatāya ca vaṇṇavantatāya ca sukhabahulatāya ca patthanaṃ katvā imaṃ dasavidhaṃ dānavatthuṃ datvā taṃ sampattiṃ anubhavituṃ tattha nibbattanti. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

1. In the second and so forth suttas, 'those with a body let loose' (vossaṭṭhakāya) means those whose bodies are let loose, disregarding the torment of a snake charmer. 'One who does both' means one who performs two kinds of actions, that is, one who performs both wholesome and unwholesome deeds. 'If today we...' means 'if today we...' 'They are reborn together' means they attain a state of companionship. There, unwholesome actions become the condition for rebirth, while wholesome actions lead to the attainment of prosperity for those who are reborn. 'Food' refers to edibles and consumables. 'Drink' means any kind of beverage. 'Clothing' includes garments and coverings. 'Vehicles' refers to any means of travel, beginning with umbrellas and sandals. 'Garlands' mean any kind of floral adornments like flower garlands. 'Scents' refer to any fragrances like sandalwood. 'Ointments' mean any substances that enhance the skin's complexion. 'Beds, dwellings, and lamps' means giving beds, chairs, and other furnishings, single-story dwellings, and lighting equipment like wicks and oil. For by aspiring to long life, beauty, and abundant happiness, they give these ten kinds of gifts and are reborn there to experience that prosperity. The rest is clear throughout.

Nāgasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

342. The Commentary on the Connected Discourses on Nāgas is concluded.

9. Supaṇṇasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Connected Discourses on Supaṇṇas

392-437. Supaṇṇasaṃyutte [V 2.0313] pattānaṃ vaṇṇavantatāya garuḷā supaṇṇāti vuttā. Idhāpi paṭhamasuttaṃ purimanayeneva aṭṭhuppattiyaṃ vuttaṃ. Harantīti uddharanti. Uddharamānā ca pana te attanā hīne vā same vā uddharituṃ sakkonti, na [P 2.0350] attanā paṇītatare. Sattavidhā hi anuddharaṇīyanāgā nāma paṇītatarā kambalassatarā dhataraṭṭhā sattasīdantaravāsino pathaviṭṭhakā pabbataṭṭhakā vimānaṭṭhakāti. Tatra aṇḍajādīnaṃ jalābujādayo paṇītatarā[M 2.0321], te tehi anuddharaṇīyā. Kambalassatarā pana nāgasenāpatino, te yattha katthaci disvā yo koci supaṇṇo uddharituṃ na sakkoti. Dhataraṭṭhā pana nāgarājāno, tepi koci uddharituṃ na sakkoti. Ye pana sattasīdantare mahāsamudde vasanti, te yasmā katthaci vikampanaṃ kātuṃ na sakkā, tasmā koci uddharituṃ na sakkoti. Pathaviṭṭhakādīnaṃ nilīyanokāso atthi, tasmā tepi uddharituṃ na sakkoti. Ye pana mahāsamudde ūmipiṭṭhe vasanti, te yo koci samo vā paṇītataro vā supaṇṇo uddharituṃ sakkoti. Sesaṃ nāgasaṃyutte vuttanayamevāti.

In the Connected Discourses on Supaṇṇas, the garuḷas are called supaṇṇas because of the beautiful color of the feathers on their wings. Here too, the first sutta was spoken due to an occasion that had arisen, in the same way as before. Haranti means they carry away. While carrying, they can carry away those who are inferior or equal to themselves, but not those superior to them. Indeed, there are seven kinds of un-carryable nāgas: those who are more excellent, the Kambalassatara nāgas, the Dhataraṭṭha nāgas, those dwelling within the seven Sīdantara oceans, those dwelling within the earth, those dwelling within mountains, and those dwelling in mansions. Among them, the womb-born and so forth are more excellent than the egg-born and so forth; the latter cannot carry away the former. The Kambalassatara, for their part, are nāga generals; no supaṇṇa, upon seeing them anywhere, is able to carry them away. The Dhataraṭṭha, for their part, are nāga kings; them too, no one is able to carry away. As for those who dwell in the great ocean between the seven Sīdā mountain ranges, because they cannot be made to tremble anywhere, no one is able to carry them away. The earth-dwelling nāgas and so forth have a place to hide, therefore them too, no one is able to carry away. But as for those who dwell on the surface of the waves in the great ocean, any supaṇṇa who is their equal or is more excellent is able to carry them away. The rest is in the same way as was stated in the Connected Discourses on Nāgas.

Supaṇṇasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Connected Discourses on Supaṇṇas is concluded.

10. Gandhabbakāyasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Gandhabbakāya Saṃyutta

438-549. Gandhabbakāyasaṃyutte [V 2.0314] mūlagandhe adhivatthāti yassa rukkhassa mūle gandho atthi, taṃ nissāya nibbattā. So hi sakalopi rukkho tesaṃ upakappati. Sesapadesupi eseva nayo. Gandhagandheti mūlādigandhānaṃ gandhe. Yassa hi rukkhassa sabbesampi mūlādīnaṃ gandho atthi, so idha gandho nāma. Tassa gandhassa gandhe, tasmiṃ adhivatthā. Idha mūlādīni sabbāni tesaṃyeva upakappanti. So dātā hoti mūlagandhānanti so kāḷānusārikādīnaṃ mūlagandhānaṃ dātā hoti. Evaṃ sabbapadesu attho veditabbo. Evañhi sarikkhadānampi datvā patthanaṃ ṭhapenti, asarikkhadānampi. Taṃ dassetuṃ so annaṃ detītiādi dasavidhaṃ dānavatthu vuttaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

In the Gandhabbasaṃyutta, regarding 'residing in the root-fragrance': they are born depending on the root-fragrance of a tree in whose root there is fragrance. Indeed, that entire tree is suitable for them. This same method should be understood in the remaining terms. 'In the fragrance of fragrances' means in the collective fragrance of the fragrances of the roots and so on. Indeed, for any tree of which all its parts, from the root onwards, have fragrance, that collective fragrance is here called 'fragrance of fragrances'. They dwell exercising mastery in that fragrance. Here, all parts, from the root onwards, are suitable for them. 'He is a giver of root-fragrances' means he is a giver of root-fragrances such as black eaglewood and so on. In this way, the meaning should be understood in all terms. Indeed, thus they make an aspiration, having given a similar gift, and also a dissimilar gift. To show that, the ten kinds of objects of giving are stated, beginning with 'he gives food'. The rest is clear everywhere.

Gandhabbakāyasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Gandhabbakāya Saṃyutta is concluded.

11. Valāhakasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā

11. The Commentary on the Valāhaka Saṃyutta

550-606. Valāhakasaṃyutte [V 2.0315][P 2.0351] valāhakakāyikāti valāhakanāmake devakāye uppannā ākāsacārikadevā. Sītavalāhakāti sītakaraṇavalāhakā. Sesapadesupi eseva nayo. Cetopaṇidhimanvāyāti cittaṭṭhapanaṃ āgamma. Sītaṃ hotīti yaṃ vassāne vā hemante [M 2.0322] vā sītaṃ hoti, taṃ utusamuṭṭhānameva. Yaṃ pana sītepi atisītaṃ, gimhe ca uppannaṃ sītaṃ, taṃ devatānubhāvena nibbattaṃ sītaṃ nāma. Uṇhaṃ hotīti yaṃ gimhāne uṇhaṃ, taṃ utusamuṭṭhānikaṃ pākatikameva. Yaṃ pana uṇhepi atiuṇhaṃ, sītakāle ca uppannaṃ uṇhaṃ, taṃ devatānubhāvena nibbattaṃ uṇhaṃ nāma. Abbhaṃ hotīti abbhamaṇḍapo hoti. Idhāpi yaṃ vassāne ca sisire ca abbhaṃ uppajjati, taṃ utusamuṭṭhānikaṃ pākatikameva. Yaṃ pana abbheyeva atiabbhaṃ, sattasattāhampi candasūriye chādetvā ekandhakāraṃ karoti, yañca cittavesākhamāsesu abbhaṃ, taṃ devatānubhāvena uppannaṃ abbhaṃ nāma. Vāto hotīti yo tasmiṃ tasmiṃ utumhi uttaradakkhiṇādipakativāto hoti, ayaṃ utusamuṭṭhānova. Yopi pana rukkhakkhandhādipadālano ativāto nāma atthi, ayañceva, yo ca aññopi akālavāto, ayaṃ devatānubhāvanibbatto nāma. Devo vassatīti yaṃ vassike cattāro māse vassaṃ, taṃ utusamuṭṭhānameva. Yaṃ pana vasseyeva ativassaṃ, yañca cittavesākhamāsesu vassaṃ, taṃ devatānubhāvanibbattaṃ nāma.

In the Valāhakasaṃyutta, 'of the Valāhaka host' means devas who travel in the sky, born in the host of devas named Valāhaka. 'Cool-cloud devas' means Valāhakas who cause coolness. This same method should be understood in the remaining terms. 'Following the mind's resolve' means depending on the establishment of thought. 'It becomes cold' refers to the cold that occurs in the rainy season or winter; that arises from the season itself. But whatever extreme cold there is even in the cold season, and the cold that arises in the hot season, that is called cold produced by the power of devas. 'It becomes hot' refers to the heat in the hot season; that is natural, arising from the season. But whatever extreme heat there is even in the hot season, and the heat that arises in the cold season, that is called heat produced by the power of devas. 'There is a cloud' means a canopy of clouds appears. Here too, whatever cloud arises in the rainy season and the cool season, that is natural, arising from the season. But whatever excessive cloudiness there is even during the cloudy season, which, covering the moon and sun for seven times seven days, creates a solid darkness, and whatever cloud there is in the months of Citta and Vesākha, that is called a cloud that has arisen by the power of devas.
